# Proposed non-orthopaedic audits (401)

Designed from current standards and gaps in the corpus. Each has a data template in `templates/`. Not yet run anywhere.

## General and emergency surgery

**NNA-001. What proportion of adults admitted with suspected non-variceal upper GI bleeding do not receive a proton pump inhibitor before their endoscopy?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG141 rec 1.4.3 (2012, updated 2016): "Do not offer acid-suppression drugs (proton pump inhibitors or H2-receptor antagonists) before endoscopy to patients with suspected non-variceal upper gastrointestinal bleeding." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg141/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No PPI or H2-receptor antagonist given (oral or IV) between arrival and the start of endoscopy, on the drug chart. Patients already on a regular oral PPI who simply continue it are recorded separately and do not fail.. Target: ≥80% receive no new pre-endoscopy acid suppression. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 3 months.
- Change: Remove IV PPI from the ED and surgical admission order set for GI bleeding, and add a 'no PPI before endoscopy (NICE 1.4.3)' line to the GI bleed proforma.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-001.csv`
- Pitfalls: Gastroenterology or ED consultants may still favour pre-scope PPI → agree the standard with both leads before cycle 1 and cite NICE 1.4.3.; Patients on a regular PPI are counted as failures → record them separately and agree the rule in advance.; Variceal and non-variceal cases get mixed up → decide exclusion on the endoscopy findings and cirrhosis history.; The order set change is bypassed by free-text prescribing → check pharmacy intervention logs in cycle 2.; Gains fade when ED rotates → make the order set the default, not the teaching.
- Pearls: Recruit the endoscopy lead early; they can pull the case list in minutes.; Pair the audit with PPI after endoscopy when stigmata are seen, so the message is 'later, not never'.; Ask pharmacy to flag new IV PPI in suspected bleeds for 4 weeks.; Show the cost of IV PPI infusions alongside the result.

**NNA-002. What proportion of haemodynamically stable adults admitted with upper GI bleeding have endoscopy within 24 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG141 rec 1.3.2 (2012, updated 2016): "Offer endoscopy within 24 hours of admission to all other patients with upper gastrointestinal bleeding." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg141/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Endoscopy start time is within 24 hours of the admission time (ED decision to admit or ward arrival, whichever is recorded first).. Target: ≥85% within 24 hours. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 3 months.
- Change: A named daily 'bleed slot' on the endoscopy list, booked by the admitting team through an electronic request that timestamps the referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-002.csv`
- Pitfalls: Admission time is defined differently between cycles → fix one definition in writing before cycle 1.; Weekend admissions dominate breaches and small numbers skew the rate → report weekday and weekend rates separately.; Endoscopy managers object to 'unused' slots → release the slot to the waiting list at a fixed time if no bleed is booked.; Patients scoped late for good reasons are counted as failures → record the reason and report them separately.
- Pearls: Recruit an endoscopy nurse manager as co-author.; Record the Blatchford score at the same time; low scores may suit outpatient scopes and free up slots.; Keep a live list of bleeds awaiting endoscopy on the ward handover sheet.; Show bed-days lost per delayed case to win management support.

**NNA-003. What proportion of adults treated for a perforated peptic ulcer are tested for H. pylori and, if positive, given eradication therapy?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG184 recs 1.7.1 and 1.9.1 (2014, updated 2019): "1.7.1 Offer H pylori eradication therapy to people who have tested positive for H pylori and who have peptic ulcer disease. ... 1.9.1 Test for H pylori using a carbon‑13 urea breath test or a stool antigen test, or laboratory-based serology where its performance has been locally validated." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg184/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: An H. pylori test (serology, breath, stool antigen or biopsy) is sent during admission or planned in the discharge letter with a named owner, and, if positive, eradication therapy is prescribed.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible cases over the last 18 months (expect 20–40 in a DGH); NELA extract helps identify operative cases.
- Change: Add 'H. pylori serology sent / eradication prescribed' to the emergency laparotomy postoperative order set and discharge template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-003.csv`
- Pitfalls: Breath and stool tests give false negatives on PPI → use locally validated serology during admission.; Small numbers make percentages jumpy → report counts and extend the window.; Results return after discharge and no one acts → name the consultant team as owner in the discharge letter.; Gastroenterology may own the result → agree who prescribes before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Ask the lab to add H. pylori serology to a 'perforated ulcer' order panel.; Recruit the upper GI consultant who runs the follow-up clinic.; Check the GP record for eradication if the hospital record is blank.; Track gastric ulcers for a 6–8 week OGD at the same time.

**NNA-004. What proportion of adults given intravenous antibiotics for acute diverticulitis have a documented review of the IV antibiotics within 48 hours or after their CT, whichever is sooner?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG147 rec 1.3.11 (2019): "Review intravenous antibiotics within 48 hours or after scanning if sooner (see recommendation 1.3.5) and consider stepping down to oral antibiotics where possible." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng147/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A dated entry in the notes or drug chart by a prescriber stating the IV antibiotic decision (continue with reason, switch to oral, or stop) within 48 hours of the first IV dose, or within 24 hours of the CT report if that is sooner.. Target: ≥90% reviewed within the standard. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 4 months.
- Change: Add a 48-hour 'IV antibiotic review: continue / oral / stop' box to the EPMA antibiotic order that must be completed on the surgical ward round.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-004.csv`
- Pitfalls: 'Continue antibiotics' written without mention of route is counted inconsistently → define what counts as a review before cycle 1.; Weekend admissions skew the timing → report weekday and weekend separately.; Microbiology may object to earlier oral switch in abscesses → agree exceptions (abscess ≥3 cm, sepsis) with them first.; The EPMA prompt is clicked through → spot-check 10 cases for a real decision.
- Pearls: Recruit the antimicrobial pharmacist; they can pull EPMA data fast.; Record length of stay to show the benefit.; Put the NG147 oral options table on the ward round checklist.; Hand the dashboard to the stewardship team so it survives rotation.

**NNA-005. What proportion of adults admitted with acute pancreatitis are allowed to eat, or have a documented reason for withholding food, within 24 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG104 rec 1.2.5 (2018, updated 2020): "Ensure that people with acute pancreatitis are not made 'nil‑by‑mouth' and do not have food withheld unless there is a clear reason for this (for example, vomiting)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng104/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: By 24 hours after admission the patient is on oral diet or free fluids progressing to diet, or the notes record a clear reason for withholding food (for example vomiting, ileus, planned procedure).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Replace the default 'NBM' line in the pancreatitis clerking proforma with 'Diet: eat as tolerated unless vomiting / ileus / procedure (state reason)'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-005.csv`
- Pitfalls: Nurses keep patients nil by mouth 'just in case' → brief the ward sisters before the change.; Cases waiting for ERCP or cholecystectomy are counted as failures → accept a documented planned procedure as a reason.; Food charts are incomplete → use the ward round entry as the primary source.; Seniors disagree about early feeding → share the NICE rationale at the audit meeting.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward dietitian as co-lead.; Add a bedside sign option 'eat as tolerated'.; Measure length of stay as a balancing measure.; Get the consultant on-call rota to sign off the proforma change.

**NNA-006. What proportion of adults with acute pancreatitis not caused by gallstones or alcohol have serum calcium and triglycerides measured during the admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG104 rec 1.2.2 (2018, updated 2020): "If gallstones and alcohol have been excluded as potential causes of a person's acute pancreatitis, investigate other possible causes such as: metabolic causes (such as hypercalcaemia or hyperlipidaemia) prescription drugs microlithiasis hereditary causes autoimmune pancreatitis ampullary or pancreatic tumours anatomical anomalies (pancreas divisum)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng104/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Both adjusted calcium and triglycerides are measured during the index admission.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive eligible cases over the last 12 months.
- Change: An electronic 'pancreatitis cause' blood panel (calcium, triglycerides, IgG4) available from the admission order set.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-006.csv`
- Pitfalls: Triglycerides taken late are falsely low after fasting → take on admission and note timing.; Alcohol history is vague → define 'alcohol excluded' before cycle 1.; Numbers are small → extend the look-back window.; The lab rejects triglyceride requests without a reason → add the indication to the panel.
- Pearls: Recruit a biochemist to create the panel.; Include IgG4 and drug review in the same prompt.; Check whether GPs were asked to repeat tests after discharge.; Flag idiopathic cases for HPB review at discharge.

**NNA-007. What proportion of adults who had potentially curative surgery for colorectal cancer have CEA and CT chest, abdomen and pelvis at the planned intervals over the first 3 years?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG151 rec 1.6.1 (2020, updated 2021): "For people who have had potentially curative surgical treatment for non-metastatic colorectal cancer, offer follow-up for detection of local recurrence and distant metastases for the first 3 years. Follow-up should include serum carcinoembryonic antigen (CEA) and CT scan of the chest, abdomen and pelvis." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Every CEA and CT due by the local schedule was done within 6 weeks of its due date up to the audit date (or 3 years after surgery).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 patients operated on 3 years ago (full schedule) from the cancer database.
- Change: Automatic surveillance tracking: each patient is loaded onto an electronic recall list at the end of treatment, with CEA and CT requests triggered by the cancer nurse team.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-007.csv`
- Pitfalls: Local schedules differ from NICE → state the local schedule and audit against it.; Scans done at another trust are missed → check image-sharing systems.; Patients who opt out are counted as failures → record choice as an exclusion.; Recall lists depend on one nurse → give a deputy access.
- Pearls: Recruit the colorectal CNS team; they hold the follow-up list.; Audit the full 3-year cohort, not recent patients.; Report recurrences found by surveillance to show value.; Link to the Personalised Stratified Follow-Up system if the trust has one.

**NNA-008. What proportion of adults having sphincter-preserving rectal cancer surgery have documented pre-operative information about low anterior resection syndrome?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG151 rec 1.6.2 (2020, updated 2021): "Give information on low anterior resection syndrome (LARS) to people who will potentially have sphincter-preserving surgery. Advise them to seek help from primary care if they think they have symptoms of LARS, such as: increased frequency of stool urgency with or without incontinence of stool feeling of incomplete emptying of the bowels fragmentation of stool (passing small amounts little and often) difficulty in differentiating between gas and stool." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Clinic letter, CNS record or consent form documents that LARS was discussed or a LARS leaflet given before surgery.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive cases over the last 12 months.
- Change: Add 'LARS explained and leaflet given' to the colorectal CNS pre-operative checklist and to the anterior resection consent template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-008.csv`
- Pitfalls: Discussion happens but is not written down → accept any written record and agree sources before cycle 1.; Surgeons see it as consent detail, not audit → frame it as a NICE quality item.; Small numbers → extend the window or pool with a neighbour unit.; Leaflet stock runs out → keep an electronic copy linked in the checklist.
- Pearls: Recruit the colorectal CNS lead; they own the pre-operative visit.; Use the national LARS leaflet so no one has to write one.; Add the LARS score to the first follow-up clinic template.; Ask two patients to comment on the leaflet.

**NNA-009. What proportion of women under 50 with triple-negative breast cancer are offered BRCA1/2 genetic testing?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG101 rec 1.3.6 (2018, updated 2025): "Offer genetic testing for BRCA1 and BRCA2 mutations to women under 50 years with triple-negative breast cancer, including those with no family history of breast or ovarian cancer (also see the recommendations on genetic testing in the NICE guideline on familial breast cancer)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng101/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Clinic letter or MDT outcome records that BRCA testing was offered (mainstream test sent or genetics referral made), or that the patient declined.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All eligible cases over the last 2 years (expect 20–40 in a DGH unit).
- Change: Add an automatic 'TNBC <50: BRCA test offered?' field to the breast MDT outcome form, completed at the diagnostic MDT.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-009.csv`
- Pitfalls: Some units route testing through genetics with long waits → record the date offered, not only the date tested.; Small numbers → use a 2-year window and report counts.; Oncology offers the test and it is missed in surgical records → check oncology letters too.; Eligibility criteria widen over time → state the NICE criterion used in both cycles.
- Pearls: Recruit a breast CNS trained in mainstream consent.; Check the regional genetics criteria at the same time.; Report the proportion with results before surgery; it drives surgical choice.; Hand the MDT field to the MDT coordinator so it survives rotation.

**NNA-010. What proportion of postmenopausal women with node-positive early invasive breast cancer are offered adjuvant bisphosphonates?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG101 rec 1.12.1 (2018, updated 2025): "Offer bisphosphonates (zoledronic acid or sodium clodronate) as adjuvant therapy to postmenopausal women with node-positive invasive breast cancer." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng101/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: MDT outcome or oncology letter records that adjuvant zoledronic acid or sodium clodronate was offered (started or declined), or a documented contraindication (for example dental work pending).. Target: ≥90% offered. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients from the last 12 months.
- Change: Add 'Adjuvant bisphosphonate: yes / no (reason)' to the postoperative MDT outcome template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-010.csv`
- Pitfalls: Menopausal status is not recorded → agree a definition (age, periods, FSH) before cycle 1.; Oncologists may make the decision outside the MDT → include oncology letters.; Dental delays postpone starts → record the dental check as a separate step.; Off-label status worries prescribers → cite the NICE note on off-label use.
- Pearls: Recruit a breast oncologist as co-lead.; Pair with the DEXA pathway so bone care is one conversation.; Use the MDT coordinator to pull node-positive cases.; Report both 'offered' and 'started'.

**NNA-011. What proportion of pre-operative breast MRIs in newly diagnosed breast cancer have a NICE indication recorded on the request?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG101 recs 1.2.2 and 1.2.3 (2018, updated 2025): "1.2.2 Do not routinely use MRI of the breast as part of the preoperative assessment of people with biopsy-proven invasive breast cancer or ductal carcinoma in situ (DCIS). 1.2.3 Offer MRI of the breast as part of preoperative assessment to people with invasive breast cancer: if the extent of disease is not clear from clinical examination, mammography and ultrasound assessment for planning treatment if accurate mammographic assessment is difficult because of breast density to assess the tumour size if breast-conserving surgery is being considered for invasive lobular cancer." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng101/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The MRI request or MDT outcome states one NICE indication (unclear extent, dense breasts, lobular cancer for conservation) or another agreed local indication (for example neoadjuvant therapy, occult primary).. Target: ≥90% with a valid indication. Sample: 40 consecutive pre-operative breast MRIs over the last 4 months.
- Change: Make 'MRI indication' a mandatory drop-down on the breast MRI request, listing the NICE and agreed local indications.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-011.csv`
- Pitfalls: Radiologists and surgeons disagree on local indications → agree the list at the MDT first.; Requests are made verbally at MDT → use the MDT outcome as a second source.; Neoadjuvant cases inflate MRI use → record them as an agreed indication.; The drop-down becomes a tick box → spot-check 10 cases against the MDT discussion.
- Pearls: Recruit a breast radiologist as co-author.; Record whether MRI changed the plan; it answers the value question.; Track time from MRI request to surgery as a balancing measure.; Re-audit yearly via the radiology system.

**NNA-012. What proportion of people treated for breast cancer have their annual surveillance mammogram within 13 months of the previous one?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG101 rec 1.15.1 (2018, updated 2025): "Offer annual mammography for 5 years to all people who have had or are being treated for breast cancer, including DCIS. For women, continue annual mammography past 5 years until they enter the NHS Breast Screening Programme (NHSBSP) in England or the Breast Test Wales Screening Programme (BTWSP) in Wales." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng101/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Each surveillance mammogram due in the audit period was done within 13 months of the previous one (or of the end of surgery for the first).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 patients diagnosed 2–4 years ago, randomly selected from the breast MDT database.
- Change: Add each patient to an electronic surveillance recall list in the breast imaging system at the end of treatment, with automatic invitations.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-012.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients discharged to GP-led follow-up are missed → include open-access follow-up patients.; Scans done at another site are not visible → check shared PACS.; DNAs are counted as failures → record them and check re-invitation.; Recall lists get switched off after IT upgrades → name an owner.
- Pearls: Recruit the breast imaging superintendent.; Sample randomly across years to show where gaps happen.; Report any cancers found by surveillance.; Check that patients reaching 5 years are moved to screening.

**NNA-013. What proportion of adults with an incidental abdominal aortic aneurysm of 3.0 cm or more on imaging are referred to the vascular service within the NICE time frame?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG156 rec 1.1.5 (2020): "Refer people with an AAA that is 5.5 cm or larger to a regional vascular service, to be seen within 2 weeks of diagnosis. Refer people with an AAA that is 3.0 cm to 5.4 cm to a regional vascular service, to be seen within 12 weeks of diagnosis." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng156/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Seen by the vascular service (or in the AAA surveillance programme) within 2 weeks for ≥5.5 cm or within 12 weeks for 3.0–5.4 cm, from the date of the report.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive cases found by a radiology report text search ('aneurysm', 'aortic') over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Radiology adds a coded 'AAA ≥3 cm' alert that automatically copies the report to the vascular service's referral inbox.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-013.csv`
- Pitfalls: Text search misses reports with odd wording → search several terms and check a sample by hand.; Referrals go to the hub hospital and are hard to trace → get access to the hub clinic system.; Frail patients are appropriately not referred → accept a documented decision.; Radiology IT change takes months → start with a manual weekly list.
- Pearls: Recruit a vascular nurse specialist from the hub.; Report sizes: the ≥5.5 cm group matters most.; Ask the radiology lead to add a standard sentence to reports.; Share the result with ED, whose CTs find many aneurysms.

**NNA-014. What proportion of aortic ultrasound reports state the inner-to-inner maximum anterior-posterior diameter?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG156 rec 1.1.10 (2020): "When measuring aortic size with ultrasound, report the inner-to-inner maximum anterior-posterior aortic diameter, in accordance with the NHS AAA screening programme. Clearly document any additional measurements taken." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng156/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The report gives a maximum anterior-posterior diameter and states it is inner-to-inner.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive aortic ultrasound reports over the last 3 months.
- Change: A structured aortic ultrasound report template with 'Maximum AP diameter (inner-to-inner): __ cm' as a required field.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-014.csv`
- Pitfalls: Sonographers measure correctly but do not state the method → the template field fixes this.; Vascular lab scans use outer-to-outer by habit → agree one method with the vascular lab.; Reports from outsourced providers differ → include them and share results.; Staff see it as criticism → present it as aligning with the screening programme.
- Pearls: Recruit the ultrasound lead sonographer.; Keep the audit to report text only; it is quick.; Pair with the incidental AAA referral audit.; Re-audit after 3 months to check the template holds.

**NNA-015. What proportion of adults newly diagnosed with intermittent claudication in vascular clinic are offered a supervised exercise programme?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG147 rec 1.5.1 (2012, updated 2020): "Offer a supervised exercise programme to all people with intermittent claudication." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg147/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The clinic letter records a referral to a supervised exercise programme, or that it was offered and declined, or a documented reason it is unsuitable.. Target: ≥90% offered. Sample: 40 consecutive new patients over the last 4 months.
- Change: Add 'Supervised exercise programme: referred / declined / unsuitable (reason)' to the vascular new-patient clinic template, with a one-click referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-015.csv`
- Pitfalls: No programme exists locally → audit anyway; the result supports a business case.; Advice to 'walk more' is counted as a pass → define supervised programme before cycle 1.; Patients seen at spoke sites are missed → include all clinic sites.; Attendance is low even with referral → measure it as a secondary outcome.
- Pearls: Recruit the vascular physiotherapist who runs the class.; Record distance to the nearest programme.; Use the audit to support a virtual or community option.; Share with GP leads to build referral routes.

**NNA-016. What proportion of adults having major abdominal surgery lasting longer than the half-life of the prophylactic antibiotic receive a timely repeat dose?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG125 rec 1.2.16 (2019, updated 2020): "Before giving antibiotic prophylaxis, take into account the timing and pharmacokinetics (for example, the serum half-life) and necessary infusion time of the antibiotic. Give a repeat dose of antibiotic prophylaxis when the operation is longer than the half-life of the antibiotic given." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng125/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A repeat dose is recorded on the anaesthetic chart within 30 minutes of the redosing interval in the local guideline (for example 4 hours for cefuroxime or co-amoxiclav).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible cases over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a 'Redose antibiotic due at __:__' line to the WHO checklist time-out, completed by the anaesthetist, with the time shown on the theatre whiteboard.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-016.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper anaesthetic charts are hard to read → pilot 5 charts first.; Guideline intervals differ between antibiotics → list them in the template.; Anaesthetists may see it as surgical audit → co-lead with an anaesthetic trainee.; Long cases are few → include all specialties using abdominal surgery lists.
- Pearls: Recruit an anaesthetic trainee as co-lead.; Use the whiteboard time as a visible cue.; Report SSI rates from surveillance alongside.; Get the theatre manager to add the line to the checklist.

**NNA-017. What proportion of adults on the general surgical wards who are able to take oral medicines receive paracetamol orally rather than intravenously on the day after surgery?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG180 rec 1.6.6 (2020): "Do not offer intravenous paracetamol unless the person cannot take oral medicine." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng180/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: On postoperative day 1, every paracetamol dose given is oral (or rectal) in a patient documented as able to take oral fluids or medicines.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive postoperative patients over 2 weeks (point prevalence on ward rounds is acceptable).
- Change: Change the postoperative EPMA order set so oral paracetamol is the default and IV paracetamol needs a reason with a 24-hour auto-stop.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-017.csv`
- Pitfalls: Oral intake status is poorly recorded → use the fluid chart and ward round note together.; Anaesthetists prescribe IV paracetamol in recovery → involve them in the order set change.; Nausea is used as a blanket reason → accept only documented vomiting.; Gains fade after the pharmacist leaves → rely on the order set, not reminders.
- Pearls: Recruit the surgical ward pharmacist.; Show the cost difference per day.; Collect data on two ward rounds a week for speed.; Pair with oral ibuprofen for a single analgesia message.

**NNA-018. What proportion of adults after elective abdominal or hernia surgery are prescribed regular oral ibuprofen for the first 24 hours unless contraindicated?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG180 rec 1.6.7 (2020): "Offer oral ibuprofen to manage immediate postoperative pain of all severities (including pain after dental surgery) unless the person has had surgery for hip fracture (see the recommendations on analgesia in the NICE guideline on hip fracture)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng180/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Regular oral ibuprofen (or another oral NSAID) is prescribed for the first 24 hours after surgery, or a contraindication is documented (for example AKI, eGFR <30, active GI bleeding, NSAID allergy, surgeon request after anastomosis).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive elective cases over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Add regular oral ibuprofen to the elective general surgery postoperative order set, with a contraindication checklist that removes it when ticked.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-018.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgeons worry about anastomotic leak → agree colorectal exceptions before cycle 1.; Renal function is not checked → build eGFR into the checklist.; Day-case patients are prescribed on discharge only → count TTO prescriptions too.; Opioid use goes up if NSAIDs are missed → record opioid doses as a balance.
- Pearls: Recruit a pain team nurse.; Use EPMA reports to pull data quickly.; Start with day-case lists, where the change is least contested.; Report AKI rates to reassure colleagues.

**NNA-019. What proportion of adults referred on the suspected lower GI cancer pathway (excluding rectal or anal mass or anal ulceration) have a FIT result available at triage?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG12 recs 1.3.1 and 1.3.2 (2015, updated 2026): "1.3.1 ... People with a rectal mass, an unexplained anal mass or unexplained anal ulceration do not need to be offered FIT before referral is considered. [2023] 1.3.2 Refer adults using a suspected cancer pathway referral for colorectal cancer if they have a FIT result of at least 10 micrograms of haemoglobin per gram of faeces. [2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: A quantitative FIT result is on the referral or in the pathology system at the time of straight-to-test triage.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive referrals over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Make a FIT result a required field on the electronic lower GI referral form, with referral returned to the GP with a FIT kit if blank (safety-netted for strong clinical concern).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-019.csv`
- Pitfalls: GPs object to returned referrals → agree the rule and safety-net with the primary care lead first.; FIT done after referral is counted as a pass → use 'available at triage' as the fixed time.; Patients who cannot return a sample are penalised → allow a documented exception.; Local form versions vary → check the form in use in both cycles.
- Pearls: Recruit the colorectal cancer pathway navigator.; Pull data weekly from the referral system.; Report conversion rates by FIT band to show value.; Share results with the cancer alliance, which may fund changes.

**NNA-020. What proportion of adults needing immediate emergency laparotomy have their pre-operative CT reported by a senior radiologist within one hour of the scan?** (new)
- Standard: NELA Principal Standards (February 2025), CT scanning and reporting, citing RCS The High-Risk General Surgical Patient 2018: "For patients requiring immediate surgery CT scanning should be undertaken immediately. CT results should be reported by a senior radiologist (ST3 and above) within one hour of being undertaken, regardless of urgency of request." https://www.nela.org.uk/s/NELAStandards_10Feb2025.pdf
- Pass: CT report (preliminary or final) issued by a radiologist ST3 or above within 60 minutes of scan completion.. Target: ≥85% (NELA green). Sample: 40 consecutive eligible NELA cases over the last 6 months.
- Change: A 'NELA urgent' flag on the CT request that sends the scan to the top of the reporting worklist and prompts a phone call to the surgical registrar.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-020.csv`
- Pitfalls: NELA fields are incomplete → cross-check times on RIS.; Outsourced reports lack reporter grade → ask the provider for grade data.; Radiology feels blamed → co-present with a radiology trainee.; Night-time cases dominate breaches → report by time of day.
- Pearls: Recruit the NELA lead and a radiology registrar.; Use the NELA extract to find cases in minutes.; Include surgeon–radiologist discussion as a second measure.; Show time from report to theatre alongside.

**NNA-021. What proportion of adults having emergency laparotomy with suspected sepsis receive antibiotics within one hour of the first qualifying NEWS2 score?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NELA Principal Standards (February 2025), infection management, citing AoMRC 2022 and RCS HRGSP 2018: "Patients with a suspected “surgical” source of infection and NEWS2 of 5+, or >=3 in any one variable, should receive antimicrobials within 1 hour of recorded NEWS2 abnormality." https://www.nela.org.uk/s/NELAStandards_10Feb2025.pdf
- Pass: First antibiotic dose administered within 60 minutes of the first recorded NEWS2 ≥5 (or a single parameter scoring 3).. Target: ≥85% (NELA green), aiming for 90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible NELA cases over the last 6 months.
- Change: A 'surgical sepsis' PGD allowing ED and SAU nurses to give first-dose antibiotics from the trust guideline once NEWS2 ≥5 with suspected abdominal source.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-021.csv`
- Pitfalls: Prescription time is used instead of administration time → use administration only.; NEWS2 is recorded late → use the first observations timestamp.; ED and surgery each think the other owns antibiotics → agree ownership in the pathway.; PGD approval takes time → start with a pre-printed stat prescription.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED sepsis nurse.; Record where the patient was at the trigger point.; Present individual delayed cases anonymously.; Use NELA data so cycle 2 needs no extra collection.

**NNA-022. What proportion of adults having emergency laparotomy have a mortality risk score documented at the end of surgery?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NELA Principal Standards (February 2025), risk assessment, citing RCS HRGSP 2018 p37: "Patients should have their risk of in-hospital mortality reassessed and documented at the end of surgery, using risk prediction tools and clinical judgement. Patients with an end-of-operation risk of death of 5%+, by any measure, should be admitted directly to critical care." https://www.nela.org.uk/s/NELAStandards_10Feb2025.pdf
- Pass: A numerical mortality risk (NELA, P-POSSUM or similar) is written on the operation note, anaesthetic record or immediate postoperative plan.. Target: ≥85% (NELA green). Sample: 40 consecutive NELA cases over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'End-of-surgery NELA risk: __%' field to the emergency laparotomy operation note template, completed at sign-out.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-022.csv`
- Pitfalls: The score is entered in NELA later but not in notes → count only contemporaneous documentation.; Surgeons and anaesthetists each expect the other to do it → name the anaesthetist at sign-out.; Intraoperative data are missing → allow a documented clinical estimate.; Template fields become default values → spot-check against NELA calculations.
- Pearls: Recruit the NELA anaesthetic lead.; Add the NELA calculator link to the op note.; Report the proportion of ≥5% cases reaching critical care alongside.; Build it into the WHO sign-out question.

**NNA-023. What proportion of adults with bowel perforation or ischaemia managed without surgery who die in hospital receive direct input from the palliative care team?** (new)
- Standard: NELA NoLap Standards of Care (from April 2024), end-of-life care, citing RCS Caring for Patients Nearing the End of Life: "The surgeon should seek to discuss the patient’s case with relevant professionals to ascertain: • the appropriate treatment options at the end of life available to the patient • the patients’ wishes, preferences and beliefs to inform their best interests. A formal consultation with the palliative care team is particularly useful in this context." https://www.nela.org.uk/s/NELA-No-Lap_Standards_from-April-2024_V3-1-52mm.pdf
- Pass: A palliative care team member reviewed the patient in person (or by documented direct consultation) before death.. Target: ≥85% (NELA green). Sample: All eligible cases over the last 12 months (expect 20–40 in a DGH).
- Change: An automatic palliative care referral prompt in the NoLap proforma when the consultant records a non-operative decision.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-023.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients die within hours, before referral is possible → record time from decision to death and analyse short survivors separately.; Palliative teams are small out of hours → accept telephone advice documented in notes.; Case finding misses non-operated patients → use the NoLap ICD-10 algorithm and M&M lists.; Surgeons feel judged about the decision → keep the audit on care, not the decision.
- Pearls: Recruit a palliative care consultant as co-lead.; Use the NELA NoLap extract for case finding.; Include the end-of-life care plan as a second measure.; Share anonymised family feedback if available.

## Surgical specialties

**NNA-024. What proportion of adults with a CT-confirmed ureteric stone and ongoing pain, or a stone unlikely to pass, have surgical treatment (ureteroscopy or lithotripsy) within 48 hours of diagnosis?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG118 rec 1.5.4 (2019): "Offer surgical treatment (including SWL) to adults with ureteric stones and renal colic within 48 hours of diagnosis or readmission, if: pain is ongoing and not tolerated or the stone is unlikely to pass." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng118/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Ureteroscopy or shockwave lithotripsy started within 48 hours of the diagnostic CT (or of readmission). A stent or nephrostomy alone does not count, except in obstructed infected kidneys, which are excluded.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible adults over the last 3 months (extend to 6 months in low-volume units).
- Change: Ring-fence a daily 'hot stone' slot (ureteroscopy list or lithotripsy session) that the urology registrar books directly from the morning trauma/take meeting.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-024.csv`
- Pitfalls: Clock start is disputed (ED arrival, CT, urology review) → fix it as CT report time and keep it the same in both cycles.; Cases miscounted when a stent is the only procedure → record the first procedure separately and count stents as fails unless infection is present.; Small numbers per month in small units → extend the look-back rather than shortening the window.; Theatre managers resist ring-fenced time → show stent change episodes and readmissions avoided, not just the percentage.; Gains fade when the rota changes → put the hot slot in the theatre template, not in a consultant's goodwill.
- Pearls: Recruit the stone lead and the theatre manager before cycle 1.; Agree 'unlikely to pass' (e.g. ≥10 mm or failed 4-week trial) before collecting.; Log delay reasons; they show which resource to ask for.; Pair with the stent database to show downstream stent burden.

**NNA-025. What proportion of men catheterised for acute urinary retention are prescribed an alpha blocker before their trial without catheter?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG97 rec 1.7.2 (2010, updated 2015): "Offer an alpha blocker to men for managing acute urinary retention before removal of the catheter. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg97/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Alpha blocker (e.g. tamsulosin 400 micrograms) prescribed from the day of catheterisation or before the trial without catheter, on the drug chart, discharge prescription or GP letter; or a contraindication documented. Men already taking an alpha blocker pass if it is continued.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive men over the last 3 months.
- Change: Build an 'acute urinary retention' order set in ED and urology e-prescribing that includes tamsulosin 400 micrograms once daily and books the trial without catheter.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-025.csv`
- Pitfalls: ED doctors discharge before prescribing → put the order set in ED, not only on the ward.; Alpha blocker recorded only in the GP letter → accept the discharge prescription as evidence and check both.; Mixing postoperative retention into the sample lowers the rate → apply the exclusions from the start.; Clinic TWOC outcomes missing → record TWOC outcome but do not make it a pass criterion.; Order set ignored after rotation → make it the default pathway item and brief new ED starters.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED consultant and the urology nurse specialist who runs the TWOC clinic.; Pull cases from the TWOC clinic list; it captures most retention episodes.; Show TWOC success by alpha blocker status; it persuades sceptics.; Agree the dose and the start day before cycle 1.

**NNA-026. What proportion of patients whose first TURBT shows high-risk non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer have a second TURBT within 6 weeks?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG2 rec 1.3.5 (2015): "If the first TURBT shows high-risk non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer, offer another TURBT as soon as possible and no later than 6 weeks after the first resection." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng2/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Second TURBT performed ≤42 days after the first TURBT.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 12 months (expect 30–50 in a DGH).
- Change: Make the MDT outcome for high-risk NMIBC automatically list the re-TURBT with a 'must be done by' date (day 42) on the waiting list, tracked by the cancer tracker.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-026.csv`
- Pitfalls: Pathology or MDT delay uses up most of the 6 weeks → record each step date so the bottleneck is visible.; Differences in 'high risk' definitions → use the NG2 risk table and state it in the method.; Small numbers → use a 12-month look-back and all sites of the Trust.; Surgeons object when a patient chose delay → record patient choice separately and report it.; Waiting-list validation removes the date → ask the cancer tracker to flag re-TURBTs weekly.
- Pearls: Recruit the bladder cancer lead and the MDT coordinator first.; Report median days and the 42-day rate together.; Link to theatre scheduling so re-TURBTs get protected slots.; Include residual tumour and upstaging rates to show why it matters.

**NNA-027. What proportion of men starting androgen deprivation therapy for prostate cancer are offered a supervised exercise programme?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG131 rec 1.4.19 (2019, updated 2021; rec from 2014): "Offer people who are starting or having androgen deprivation therapy supervised resistance and aerobic exercise at least twice a week for 12 weeks to reduce fatigue and improve quality of life. [2014]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng131/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Clinic letter or nurse specialist record documents an offer of, or referral to, a supervised exercise programme within 3 months of the first ADT dose.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive men who started ADT in the last 6 months.
- Change: Add a 'Supervised exercise programme offered: accepted / declined' line to the ADT start clinic template with a one-click referral to the local programme.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-027.csv`
- Pitfalls: No local programme exists → map options (physiotherapy, Macmillan, leisure centre schemes) before cycle 1 and report the service gap if none.; ADT started by GPs or oncology is missed → take the case list from the injection record, not only urology clinics.; Offers made verbally are not written → the template line solves this; audit written evidence only.; Clinicians see it as optional → quote the 'Offer' wording and fatigue data.
- Pearls: Recruit the prostate CNS team; they deliver most ADT starts.; Agree what counts as 'supervised' with the local service.; Give men a leaflet with the referral; uptake rises.; Record acceptance to show real demand to commissioners.

**NNA-028. What proportion of women seen for the first time in a continence clinic with urinary incontinence or overactive bladder have a completed bladder diary of at least 3 days?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG123 rec 1.3.13 (2019; rec from 2006): "Use bladder diaries in the initial assessment of women with urinary incontinence or overactive bladder. Encourage women to complete a minimum of 3 days of the diary covering variations in their usual activities, such as both working and leisure days. [2006]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng123/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A bladder diary covering ≥3 days is recorded or scanned in the notes by the end of the first specialist appointment.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive new women over the last 2 months.
- Change: Have the booking system send a 3-day bladder diary with every new continence appointment letter, with instructions to bring it.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-028.csv`
- Pitfalls: Diaries brought but not scanned → count a diary summarised in the letter as present.; Booking team cannot add inserts → ask for an electronic letter template change or a text link.; Different clinics use different diaries → agree one 3-day format.; Women forget to bring it → add a reminder in the pre-appointment text.
- Pearls: Recruit the continence nurse lead and outpatient booking manager.; Record whether a diary was sent; it separates system failure from patient factors.; Measure decisions made at first visit to show time saved.

**NNA-029. What proportion of children having grommets for otitis media with effusion have a hearing test about 6 weeks after surgery?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG233 rec 1.6.11 (2023): "Perform a postoperative hearing test 6 weeks after surgery for OME, and: If the hearing loss has resolved, discharge and: advise parents and carers to seek a reassessment by the audiology service involved in their child's care if they are concerned about a possible recurrence of OME-related hearing loss at a later date or consider a 1-year follow up with a hearing test if there are concerns a potential recurrence of hearing loss could be missed or consider an individualised follow-up plan if the child has an increased risk of unrecognised OME with hearing loss (for example, children with a learning disability or craniofacial anomalies)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng233/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Age-appropriate hearing test (pure tone or play audiometry, or visual reinforcement audiometry) documented 4–10 weeks after grommet insertion.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive children operated on 3–6 months ago.
- Change: Add 'Audiology at 6 weeks post-op' as a default item on the paediatric grommet listing form, so the audiology request is generated at listing.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-029.csv`
- Pitfalls: Audiology capacity limits → agree slot numbers with audiology before the change.; Tests done in community audiology are invisible → check community records or letters.; Did-not-attend counted as failure → record DNA separately and report both rates.; Window disputes → fix 4–10 weeks before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric audiology lead early.; Book the test at listing, not at discharge.; Include follow-up plans (discharge, 1-year test) as a secondary measure.

**NNA-030. What proportion of children listed for grommets for otitis media with effusion had hearing reassessed at least 3 months after the first test showing hearing loss?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG233 rec 1.3.1 and 1.3.2 (2023): "1.3.1 In bilateral OME (in both ears) with hearing loss, reassess hearing after 3 months. Where the OME with hearing loss is unilateral (in one ear), consider reassessment of hearing after 3 months. ... 1.3.2 In children who are experiencing hearing difficulties that significantly affect day-to-day living, consider intervening earlier than the 3‑month reassessment ..." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng233/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Two hearing assessments ≥3 months apart before listing; or a documented reason for earlier intervention (significant day-to-day impact, or a condition such as Down syndrome or cleft palate).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive children listed in the last 6 months.
- Change: Add two required fields to the grommet listing form: dates of both hearing tests, or an 'early intervention reason' box.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-030.csv`
- Pitfalls: Clinicians dispute the rule for severe impact → the early-intervention box keeps judgement while making it visible.; Tests from community audiology missed → check referral letters for dates.; Unilateral cases counted as fails → report bilateral and unilateral separately.; Listing form changes need IT approval → start with a paper sticker if needed.
- Pearls: Agree with consultants which conditions justify early surgery before cycle 1.; Show how many children resolved while waiting; it supports the rule.; Recruit the waiting-list manager to enforce the fields.

**NNA-031. What proportion of people who have a PET-CT after radical chemoradiotherapy for head and neck cancer have it 3 to 6 months after treatment ends?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG36 rec 1.4.3 (2016, updated 2018): "For people having an FDG PET-CT scan after chemoradiotherapy, perform the scan 3 to 6 months after chemoradiotherapy has finished. [2018]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng36/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: PET-CT date is 12–26 weeks after the last radiotherapy fraction.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 12 months (expect 30–50).
- Change: Have the oncology end-of-treatment summary generate the PET-CT request with a 'not before' date of 12 weeks and a 'not after' date of 26 weeks.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-031.csv`
- Pitfalls: PET capacity is regional → share the data with the PET provider early.; Scans booked early to 'catch' recurrence → quote the false-positive risk.; Small numbers → use a 12-month look-back across the network.; Last fraction date hard to find → take it from the radiotherapy system.
- Pearls: Recruit a head and neck oncologist and the MDT coordinator.; Report equivocal scans by timing; early scans often cause them.; Keep the same date rule in both cycles.

**NNA-032. What proportion of smokers newly diagnosed with head and neck cancer are offered referral to a stop-smoking service at diagnosis?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG36 rec 1.1.3 (2016, updated 2018): "Offer help to people to stop smoking, in line with NICE's guideline on tobacco: preventing uptake, promoting quitting and treating dependence. [2016]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng36/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Referral to a stop-smoking service (or opt-out referral) documented within 2 weeks of the diagnosis appointment.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new diagnoses who smoke, over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add an opt-out stop-smoking referral field to the CNS diagnosis/holistic needs template that emails the Trust stop-smoking service automatically.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-032.csv`
- Pitfalls: Status recorded at diagnosis but not updated → capture the status on the day of diagnosis.; Clinicians feel it adds distress at bad news → allow referral within 2 weeks via the CNS.; Stop-smoking service capacity → agree the referral route before cycle 1.; Former smokers counted as smokers → define 'current' as smoked in the last month.
- Pearls: Recruit the head and neck CNS team and the Trust tobacco dependency lead.; Opt-out wording lifts uptake more than asking.; Measure quit status at treatment start as an outcome.

**NNA-033. What proportion of people with newly diagnosed head and neck cancer have their need for enteral nutrition assessed and recorded before treatment starts?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG36 rec 1.8.1 (2016, updated 2018): "Assess people's need for enteral nutrition at diagnosis, including prophylactic tube placement. The multidisciplinary team should take into account: performance status and social factors nutritional status (weight loss, high or low BMI, ability to meet estimated nutritional needs) tumour stage tumour site pre‑existing dysphagia impact of planned treatment (such as radiation treatment volume and dose‑fractionation, concomitant chemotherapy, and extent and site of surgery). [2016]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng36/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: MDT outcome or dietitian record documents a decision on enteral nutrition (prophylactic tube, reactive tube, or not needed) before the first treatment date.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new patients over the last 4 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Nutrition and tube plan' field to the head and neck MDT outcome form, completed by the MDT dietitian.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-033.csv`
- Pitfalls: Dietitian not present at MDT → allow a dietetic clinic entry before treatment to count.; Decision recorded only as 'dietitian to see' → define that as a fail.; Mixed OMFS and ENT pathways → include both and report by team.; Unplanned tube counts are small → report them descriptively.
- Pearls: Recruit the head and neck dietitian as co-lead.; Agree the three allowed decisions before cycle 1.; Record weight at diagnosis; it strengthens the case for the field.

**NNA-034. What proportion of people with early (T1–T2, N0) oral cavity cancer have sentinel lymph node biopsy rather than elective neck dissection?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG36 rec 1.3.5 (2016, updated 2018): "Offer sentinel lymph node biopsy instead of elective neck dissection to people with early oral cavity cancer (grade T1 to T2, N0), unless they need cervical access at the same time (for example, free‑flap reconstruction). [2016]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng36/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: SLNB performed; or elective neck dissection with documented need for cervical access (e.g. free-flap reconstruction).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 24 months (expect 30–40 in a head and neck centre).
- Change: Add a default 'SLNB' option to the MDT outcome form for cT1–2N0 oral cancer, with a required reason if elective neck dissection is chosen.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-034.csv`
- Pitfalls: No nuclear medicine or sentinel node pathway locally → treat this as a service gap and escalate.; Small numbers → use 24 months and all network sites.; Surgeons prefer neck dissection → the reason box keeps choice visible and discussable.; Staging changes on pathology → classify by clinical stage at MDT.
- Pearls: Recruit the OMFS head and neck lead and nuclear medicine.; Report morbidity (shoulder, length of stay) alongside the rate.; Agree the cervical access exception wording first.

**NNA-035. What proportion of patients having surgical removal of impacted wisdom teeth have a NICE-listed indication documented before surgery?** (new)
- Standard: NICE TA1 recs 1.1, 1.3 and 1.4 (2000): "1.1 The practice of prophylactic removal of pathology-free impacted third molars should be discontinued in the NHS. ... 1.3 Surgical removal of impacted third molars should be limited to patients with evidence of pathology. Such pathology includes unrestorable caries, non-treatable pulpal and/or periapical pathology, cellulitis, abscess and osteomyelitis, internal/external resorption of the tooth or adjacent teeth, fracture of tooth, disease of follicle including cyst/tumour, tooth/teeth impeding surgery or reconstructive jaw surgery, and when a tooth is involved in or within the field of tumour resection. 1.4 ... Second or subsequent episodes should be considered the appropriate indication for surgery." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ta1/chapter/1-Guidance
- Pass: Clinic letter, listing form or consent names a TA1 indication (listed pathology, or two or more episodes of pericoronitis, or one particularly severe episode).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over the last 2 months.
- Change: Replace free text on the wisdom tooth listing (TCI) form with a mandatory tick-box list of TA1 indications plus 'other (specify)'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-035.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgeons cite reasons outside TA1 (e.g. distal caries risk in the second molar) → record these as 'other' and discuss them rather than failing silently.; Indication in the referral but not the letter → accept any pre-operative OMFS document.; Mixed operators (dental core trainees, staff grades) → report by grade to target teaching.; The form becomes a tick-box habit → spot-check 10 notes against radiographs.
- Pearls: Recruit the OMFS clinical lead and the day-surgery booking team.; Agree how to classify 'other' reasons before cycle 1.; Include pericoronitis episode counts; they are the most common grey area.

**NNA-036. What proportion of people newly diagnosed with melanoma have vitamin D measured at diagnosis in secondary care?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG14 rec 1.2.1 (2015, updated 2022): "Measure vitamin D levels at diagnosis in secondary care in all people with melanoma. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng14/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D result dated within 3 months of the histological diagnosis.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new melanomas over the last 4 months.
- Change: Add vitamin D to the 'new melanoma' results-clinic order set used by the skin cancer CNS.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-036.csv`
- Pitfalls: Tests ordered by GPs are invisible → check the shared pathology system.; Split care between dermatology and plastics → include both and report by team.; Low results not acted on → add supplementation advice as a secondary field.; Laboratory rejects 'non-standard' requests → confirm the test code first.
- Pearls: Recruit the skin cancer CNS; they run results clinics.; Use the diagnosis date from histology to avoid disputes.; Share the result template with GPs for follow-up.

**NNA-037. What proportion of patients attending the emergency department with a burn that meets national referral thresholds are referred to a specialised burn service?** (new)
- Standard: National Network for Burn Care, National Burn Care Referral Guidance, section 3 (2012; hosted by the British Burn Association): "The suggested minimum threshold for referral into specialised burn care services can be summarised as: All burns ≥2% TBSA in children or ≥3% in adults All full thickness burns All circumferential burns Any burn not healed in 2 weeks Any burn with suspicion of non-accidental injury should be referred to a Burn Unit/Centre for expert assessment within 24 hours" https://www.britishburnassociation.org/national-burn-care-referral-guidance/
- Pass: Referral to, or documented discussion with, a specialised burn service (facility, unit or centre) for a burn meeting any threshold, during the ED attendance or within 24 hours.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive qualifying attendances over the last 3 months.
- Change: Introduce an ED burns proforma with a Lund and Browder chart and a 'refer if' box that links to the burns network referral portal.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-037.csv`
- Pitfalls: TBSA not recorded, so eligibility unclear → report 'TBSA missing' separately and count as unassessable.; Coding misses burns → add a free-text search for 'burn' and 'scald'.; Paediatric burns go to a different ED → include both or state the scope.; Referrals by phone leave no record → ask the burns service for its referral log.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED consultant and the burns network outreach nurse.; Use the network's own referral portal data for cross-checking.; Measure Lund and Browder use; it drives correct TBSA.

**NNA-038. What proportion of patients with a hand injury or burn seen by plastic surgery have tetanus prophylaxis managed in line with UKHSA guidance?** (under-audited)
- Standard: UKHSA, Guidance on the management of suspected tetanus cases and the assessment and management of tetanus-prone wounds, sections 7.2–7.3 and Table 4 (updated July 2026): "If the wound, burn or injury fulfils the criteria for a high-risk tetanus-prone wound, intramuscular tetanus immunoglobulin (IM-TIG ) should be considered (see Table 4 and section 6). ... A reinforcing dose of tetanus-containing vaccine is recommended (see Table 4)." https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/tetanus-advice-for-health-professionals/guidance-on-the-management-of-suspected-tetanus-cases-and-the-assessment-and-management-of-tetanus-prone-wounds
- Pass: Immunisation status and wound category (clean, tetanus-prone, high-risk tetanus-prone) documented, and vaccine and/or immunoglobulin given when Table 4 requires it.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Embed a three-step tetanus box (status → wound category → action from Table 4) in the hand trauma and burns clerking proformas.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-038.csv`
- Pitfalls: ED already gave vaccine but did not record status → check ED administration records.; Status unknown in adults → Table 4 treats uncertain status as not adequately primed; teach that rule.; Immunoglobulin supply → confirm the local HNIG/IM-TIG product and route first.; Guidance updates between cycles → use the same version for both cycles and note the date.
- Pearls: Recruit the hand trauma nurse lead and ED pharmacist.; Print Table 4 on the back of the proforma.; Use the GP summary care record to confirm status quickly.

**NNA-039. What proportion of patients admitted with a human or animal bite on intravenous antibiotics are reviewed for a switch to oral antibiotics within 48 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG184 rec 1.2.3 (2020): "If intravenous antibiotics are given, review within 48 hours and consider switching to oral antibiotics if possible." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng184/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Documented antibiotic review (continue IV with reason, or switch to oral) within 48 hours of the first IV dose.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions over the last 6 months.
- Change: Set IV antibiotics on the bite admission order set to a 48-hour review stop date in e-prescribing.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-039.csv`
- Pitfalls: Theatre washout delays the review → a documented 'continue IV until washout' counts as review.; Paper charts hide times → take times from the administration record.; Small numbers in summer-heavy seasonality → use 6 months.; Stop dates overridden → audit overrides in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit the antimicrobial pharmacist.; Report length of stay and IV days as balancing measures.; Agree first dose time as the clock start.

**NNA-040. What proportion of patients having uncomplicated cataract surgery avoid an in-person review on the day after surgery?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG77 rec 1.9.3 (2017): "Do not offer in‑person, first-day review to people after uncomplicated cataract surgery." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng77/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No face-to-face appointment on the first post-operative day (telephone contact is allowed).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive uncomplicated cataract operations over the last month.
- Change: Set the cataract post-operative booking template to default 'no day-1 visit; nurse phone call; 4-week review', with a surgeon override box for complicated cases.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-040.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgeons keep day-1 reviews for reassurance → show unplanned attendance rates as a safety measure.; Complications coded late → use the operation record, not coding.; Some day-1 visits are for trainee cases → decide before cycle 1 if these are allowed.; Telephone calls not recorded → add a phone-call field to the nurse template.
- Pearls: Recruit the cataract lead and the outpatient booking manager.; Report clinic slots freed; managers support it.; Track unplanned attendance to reassure consultants.

**NNA-041. What proportion of adults newly diagnosed with non-advanced chronic open angle glaucoma are offered 360° selective laser trabeculoplasty as first treatment?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG81 rec 1.4.17 (2017, updated 2022): "Offer 360° SLT to people with newly diagnosed COAG that is not advanced (excluding cases associated with pigment dispersion syndrome). For people with advanced COAG see the section on treatment for people with advanced COAG and recommendation 1.4.27. To help inform their decision, tell people: that having 360° SLT can delay the need for eye drops and can reduce but does not remove the chance they will be needed at all how long it may take for their IOP to improve after the procedure about 360° SLT‑specific side effects and complications and how long they are likely to last that a second 360° SLT procedure may be needed at a later date. [2022]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng81/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Clinic letter documents that 360° SLT was offered as first treatment (accepted or declined).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new COAG diagnoses over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add a 'First-line SLT offered: accepted / declined / not suitable (reason)' field to the glaucoma new-patient letter template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-041.csv`
- Pitfalls: Laser capacity limits offers → record capacity delays separately.; Severity grading varies → use the Hodapp or local staging agreed in advance.; Offers made verbally are unrecorded → template field fixes this.; Optometrist-led clinics use different letters → update all templates.
- Pearls: Recruit the glaucoma lead and laser nurse.; Report acceptance rates to plan laser sessions.; Include both consultant and optometrist clinics.

**NNA-042. What proportion of eyes with newly confirmed wet age-related macular degeneration receive their first anti-VEGF injection within 14 days of referral to the macular service?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG82 rec 1.4.10 (2018): "For eyes with confirmed late AMD (wet active) for which antiangiogenic treatment is recommended (see the section on pharmacological management of AMD), offer treatment as soon as possible (within 14 days of referral to the macular service)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng82/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First anti-VEGF injection ≤14 days after the referral is received by the macular service.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new treated eyes over the last 3 months.
- Change: Introduce ring-fenced 'assess and inject same day' new-patient slots booked directly at referral triage.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-042.csv`
- Pitfalls: Referral receipt date not recorded → use the e-referral timestamp.; Injector capacity (nurse injectors) → plan staffing before the change.; Patient-choice delays counted as fails → record them separately.; Second-eye conversions dilute the sample → exclude them.
- Pearls: Recruit the medical retina lead and nurse injector lead.; Report median days as well as the 14-day rate.; Triage referrals daily, including Fridays.

**NNA-043. What proportion of adults admitted for head injury observation have neurological observations recorded at the NICE minimum frequency for the first 6 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.9.11 (2023; rec from 2003): "Carry out and record observations on a half-hourly basis until there is a GCS score of 15. Observations for people with a GCS score of 15 should start after the initial assessment in the emergency department and the minimum frequency should be: half-hourly for 2 hours, then 1 hourly for 4 hours, then 2 hourly. [2003]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: All scheduled neurological observations in the first 6 hours recorded within 15 minutes of their due time (half-hourly for 2 hours then hourly for 4 hours if GCS 15; half-hourly until GCS 15 otherwise).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions over the last 2 months.
- Change: Build a head injury observation schedule into the electronic observations system that sets due times automatically and alerts when overdue.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-043.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper charts hide exact times → use e-obs timestamps or accept charted times.; Patients spread over many wards → take the case list from the head injury admission code.; Nursing staff feel blamed → present with nursing leads and focus on the system.; Frequency resets after deterioration are complex → record deterioration and schedule reset as notes.
- Pearls: Co-lead with a ward nurse manager.; Agree the 15-minute tolerance before cycle 1.; Show missed deterioration cases (anonymised) to motivate.

**NNA-044. What proportion of people with suspected metastatic spinal cord compression have an MRI of the spine within 24 hours of suspicion?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG234 rec 1.5.2 (2023): "Offer an MRI scan to people with suspected MSCC (see recommendation 1.3.2), to be done: as soon as possible (and always within 24 hours) at the local hospital or appropriate centre with direct access to imaging facilities. Transfer to a tertiary centre for MRI should only be done if local MRI is not possible. [2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng234/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: MRI spine completed ≤24 hours after the first documented clinical suspicion of MSCC (or MSCC coordinator contact, whichever is earlier).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive referrals over the last 4 months.
- Change: Create a protected MSCC MRI pathway: coordinator-requested scans go straight to the urgent slot with automatic radiographer alert, including out of hours.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-044.csv`
- Pitfalls: Suspicion time is vague → use the first note mentioning cord compression.; Out-of-hours radiography staffing → agree cover with radiology before the change.; Referrals come from many teams → use the coordinator log as the single source.; Scans at other sites → include transfer times.
- Pearls: Recruit the acute oncology lead and MSCC coordinator.; Record dexamethasone as a secondary measure; it is often missed.; Report by hour of referral; night referrals show the gap.

**NNA-045. What proportion of people discharged after aneurysmal subarachnoid haemorrhage have a follow-up care plan with specialist contact details in their discharge letter?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG228 recs 1.4.1 and 1.4.2 (2022): "1.4.1 Agree and document a plan for follow-up care with the person after their aneurysmal subarachnoid haemorrhage. Give a paper copy of the plan to the person (and their family or carers if appropriate) and include details of who to contact at the specialist centre for ongoing advice and support. 1.4.2 Include the follow-up care plan in the person's medical record and discharge correspondence." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng228/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Discharge letter contains a follow-up plan (clinic and/or imaging) and a named contact route at the specialist centre (e.g. SAH nurse phone number).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive discharges over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'SAH follow-up plan and contact' section to the neurosurgical electronic discharge template, prefilled with the SAH nurse contact.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-045.csv`
- Pitfalls: Repatriated patients get letters from the local hospital → audit the neurosurgical transfer letter.; Plan decided later at MDT → allow 'to be confirmed by letter by date X' with a contact.; Template fields ignored → make them mandatory in the e-discharge system.; Small numbers → use 6 months.
- Pearls: Recruit the SAH or vascular nurse specialist.; Prefill the contact to save junior time.; Include paper copy given as a secondary measure.

**NNA-046. What proportion of patients discharged after coronary artery bypass grafting are prescribed atorvastatin 80 mg?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG238 recs 1.7.2 and 1.7.3 (2023): "1.7.2 Offer atorvastatin 80 mg to people with CVD, whatever their cholesterol level, unless the person meets the criteria in recommendation 1.7.3. ... 1.7.3 Offer a lower dose of atorvastatin if any of the following apply: it could react with other drugs there is a high risk of adverse effects the person would prefer to take a lower dose." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng238/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Atorvastatin 80 mg on the discharge prescription; or a lower dose or other lipid therapy with a documented 1.7.3 reason.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive discharges over the last 2 months.
- Change: Make atorvastatin 80 mg the default line in the post-CABG discharge order set, with an 'alternative dose because' box.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-046.csv`
- Pitfalls: Pre-operative statin simply continued → count continuation of a lower dose without reason as a fail.; Interactions (e.g. with some antiarrhythmics) → accept documented pharmacist advice.; Surgeons defer to cardiologists → agree the default with both before cycle 1.; Reason boxes filled with 'n/a' → spot-check reasons.
- Pearls: Recruit the cardiac surgical pharmacist.; Pull prescriptions from the e-discharge system in bulk.; Feed back to the cardiac rehab team to reinforce.

**NNA-047. What proportion of patients already taking a beta-blocker before cardiac surgery have it restarted by the end of the first postoperative day?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG196 rec 1.10.2 (2021; rec from 2006, amended 2014): "In people having cardiothoracic surgery who are already on beta‑blocker therapy, continue this treatment unless contraindications develop (such as postoperative bradycardia or hypotension). [2006, amended 2014]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng196/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Beta-blocker administered by the end of postoperative day 1 (drug chart administration record), or a contraindication documented (bradycardia, hypotension, pacing dependence, inotropes).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 6 weeks.
- Change: Add 'restart home beta-blocker on POD1 unless HR <60, SBP <100, paced or on inotropes' as a default line in the post-cardiac surgery ICU order set.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-047.csv`
- Pitfalls: Epicardial pacing or inotropes make many ineligible → record contraindications and report the eligible denominator.; Medicines reconciliation incomplete → confirm pre-operative use from the GP summary care record.; ICU and ward teams hand over poorly → put the default in the ICU order set so it travels.; Postoperative AF numbers are small → treat as descriptive.
- Pearls: Recruit a cardiac intensivist and the ICU pharmacist.; Agree the haemodynamic thresholds before cycle 1.; Show AF rates by restart status.

**NNA-048. What proportion of patients having coronary artery bypass grafting after a myocardial infarction are referred to cardiac rehabilitation before discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG185 rec 1.8.1 (2020; rec from 2007): "All people (regardless of their age) should be given advice about and offered a cardiac rehabilitation programme with an exercise component. [2007]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng185/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Referral to a cardiac rehabilitation programme sent before discharge (referral record or discharge letter).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 3 months.
- Change: Generate the cardiac rehabilitation referral automatically from the cardiac surgery discharge summary (opt-out), sent to the service closest to the patient's home.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-048.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients from other regions need referral to distant services → keep a regional service list in the template.; Rehabilitation teams refer later from clinic → record any later referral separately.; Opt-out referrals rejected for incomplete data → include minimum dataset fields.; Extending to all CABG changes the denominator → keep the MI group for the re-audit.
- Pearls: Recruit the cardiac rehabilitation lead nurse.; Check the NACR submission for the same patients.; Measure uptake (first session attended) later as an outcome.

**NNA-049. What proportion of curative lung cancer resections include sampling of both hilar and mediastinal lymph nodes?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG122 rec 1.5.3 (2019, updated 2024; rec from 2011): "Perform hilar and mediastinal lymph node sampling or en bloc resection for all people having surgery with curative intent. [2011]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng122/chapter/Management
- Pass: Pathology report shows at least one hilar (N1) station and at least one mediastinal (N2) station sampled.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive curative resections over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a nodal station checklist to the thoracic operation note template and supply pre-labelled specimen pots for each station.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-049.csv`
- Pitfalls: Nodes sent together as one pot cannot be mapped → the labelled pots fix this.; Wedge resections in frail patients → report by resection type.; Pathology reports use different station labels → agree IASLC station names with pathology.; Surgeons see the pass bar as low → add station 7 and ≥3 N2 stations as secondary measures.
- Pearls: Recruit a thoracic pathologist as co-lead.; Keep the pass criterion simple in cycle 1; add ESTS detail later.; Report upstaging (N1/N2 found) to show benefit.

## Anaesthesia, perioperative care and intensive care

**NNA-050. What proportion of adults having non-orthopaedic surgery in an operating theatre with a risk of bleeding receive intravenous tranexamic acid just before the start of surgery?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG24 recs 1.3.1 and 1.3.3 (2015, updated February 2026): "1.3.1 Offer tranexamic acid to adults having surgery in an operating theatre if: there is any risk of bleeding and the procedure will breach the skin or mucous membranes. [2026] 1.3.3 When using tranexamic acid for adults having surgery, administer it just before the start of surgery. Typically give 1 g by slow intravenous injection. [2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Reducing-requirement-for-blood-transfusion-for-people-having-surgery
- Pass: Tranexamic acid (typically 1 g IV) recorded on the anaesthetic chart between induction and knife-to-skin, or a documented contraindication or reason for omission.. Target: ≥85% of eligible adults. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible adults from the theatre system over the last 4 weeks (about 8 per specialty).
- Change: Add a default 'Tranexamic acid 1 g IV at induction (omit if contraindicated: reason ___)' line to the electronic anaesthetic chart template for all adult theatre cases.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-050.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgeons worry about thrombosis in cancer or vascular patients → agree a short contraindication list with haematology before cycle 1 and count a documented reason as a pass.; Tranexamic acid given at closure is recorded but not at induction → record the time and report timing separately from 'given at all'.; Minor procedures swamp the sample → define 'risk of bleeding' locally (e.g. any procedure with a group and save) and keep the same definition in both cycles.; Default chart line ignored by locums → add it to the Sign In so the ODP asks every case.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion practitioner; the 2026 NICE update is on their audit list.; Pull cases from the theatre system by specialty so one audit covers several teams.; Ask pharmacy to stock 1 g tranexamic acid in every anaesthetic room drug drawer.; Name a consultant anaesthetist lead who will keep the chart default after the trainee rotates.

**NNA-051. In adults on surgical wards after an operation, what proportion of intravenous paracetamol doses are given only when the patient cannot take oral medicine?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG180 rec 1.6.6 (2020): "Do not offer intravenous paracetamol unless the person cannot take oral medicine." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng180/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Every IV paracetamol dose after return to the ward was given while the patient was nil by mouth, vomiting, or had another documented reason they could not take oral medicine..
