Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive postoperative inpatients prescribed IV paracetamol over 2 weeks (EPMA report).
- Change: Add an EPMA rule: IV paracetamol prescriptions expire after 24 hours and require the indication 'cannot take oral' to renew, with a one-click switch to oral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-051.csv`
- Pitfalls: Eating status not recorded → accept any documented oral medicine given at the same time as proof oral route was possible.; Anaesthetists prescribe IV paracetamol in recovery 'regular' → agree with the anaesthetic department that PACU prescriptions are 'once only' or 24 hours.; Nurses give IV because it is quicker on rounds → involve ward sisters in the change.; Pharmacy switch not sustained after rotation → put the rule in EPMA, not in teaching.
- Pearls: Ask pharmacy for an EPMA report of IV paracetamol by ward; it takes minutes.; Show the cost difference per dose at the first presentation.; Recruit a ward pharmacist as co-lead.; Lock the 'cannot take oral' definition before cycle 1.

**NNA-052. In adults having general or regional anaesthesia for more than 30 minutes, what proportion have their temperature recorded before induction and at least every 30 minutes until the end of surgery?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG65 rec 1.3.1 (2008, updated 2016): "The patient's temperature should be measured and documented before induction of anaesthesia and then every 30 minutes until the end of surgery. [2008]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg65/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A temperature before induction and no gap longer than 30 minutes between readings until the end of surgery on the anaesthetic chart.. Target: ≥90% of cases. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible cases across two theatre suites over 2 weeks.
- Change: Set the electronic anaesthetic record to prompt a temperature entry at induction and every 30 minutes (or link a continuous temperature probe to the monitor feed), with a 36.0°C alert.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-052.csv`
- Pitfalls: Monitor records temperature but it is not exported to the chart → check that the data feed is on before cycle 1.; Tympanic infrared readings are indirect estimates (NICE 1.1.6) → record the site and report it separately.; Short cases with one reading distort results → apply the >30-minute rule in both cycles.; Staff see it as box-ticking → show the recovery hypothermia rate alongside compliance.
- Pearls: Recruit an ODP lead; ODPs usually place the probe.; Audit one theatre suite first if paper charts are mixed with electronic ones.; Record the lowest temperature too; it makes the case for warming equipment.; Ask the theatre educator to add the probe to the room set-up checklist.

**NNA-053. What proportion of adults sent from the ward or emergency department for surgery have a temperature recorded in the hour before they leave?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG65 rec 1.2.2 (2008, updated 2016): "The patient's temperature should be measured and documented in the hour before they leave the ward or emergency department. [2008]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg65/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A temperature documented within 60 minutes before the time the patient left the ward or emergency department for theatre.. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over 2 weeks from two surgical wards and ED.
- Change: Add 'Temperature in last hour ___°C (time ___); if <36.0°C start warming' as a mandatory field on the pre-operative transfer checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-053.csv`
- Pitfalls: Departure time not recorded → use the theatre 'patient arrived in reception' time minus porter time, and keep the same rule in both cycles.; Ward nurses object to extra observations → the field replaces a free-text line on the existing checklist.; Emergency patients sent urgently → accept 'clinical urgency' documented as an exclusion.; Gains fade after rotation → make the field mandatory in the electronic checklist.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward sister and a theatre reception nurse.; Theatre reception can refuse to accept a checklist without the temperature during weeks 5–12.; Report how many patients were below 36.0°C; it shows why it matters.; Agree the time source before cycle 1.

**NNA-054. What proportion of discharges from critical care to a general ward between 22:00 and 07:00 are reported as adverse incidents?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG50 rec 1.14 (2007): "After the decision to transfer a patient from a critical care area to the general ward has been made, he or she should be transferred as early as possible during the day. Transfer from critical care areas to the general ward between 10:00pm and 7:00am should be avoided whenever possible, and should be documented as an adverse incident if it occurs." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg50/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A ward transfer between 22:00 and 07:00 has a matching incident report (Datix or equivalent).. Target: ≥90% of night discharges reported; secondary: fall in night discharges. Sample: All ward discharges over the last 3 months (typically 150–250), with every 22:00–07:00 transfer (expect 10–40) checked for an incident report.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Discharge time 22:00–07:00? Incident number ___' field to the ICU electronic discharge form, and a nightly report of such discharges to the nurse in charge.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-054.csv`
- Pitfalls: Nurses feel blamed by incident reporting → frame reports as capacity data for the board, not individual error.; Decision time and actual time confused → record both; the standard is about actual transfer.; Small numbers → use all discharges over 3 months, not a sample.; Bed managers push back → invite them to the presentation and show readmission data.
- Pearls: ICNARC case mix data already hold discharge times; ask the ICNARC data clerk.; Recruit the ICU matron as co-lead.; Report time from decision to discharge; it shows the day-time delays that cause night moves.; Keep the report monthly after the audit.

**NNA-055. In adults admitted to critical care from a ward or the emergency department, what proportion have a documented admission decision involving both the referring consultant and the critical care consultant?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG50 rec 1.13 (2007): "If the team caring for the patient considers that admission to a critical care area is clinically indicated, then the decision to admit should involve both the consultant caring for the patient on the ward and the consultant in critical care." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg50/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The ICU admission note or referral record names both the parent-team consultant and the critical care consultant as involved in the decision.. Target: ≥90% of unplanned admissions. Sample: 40 consecutive unplanned admissions over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add two mandatory fields to the ICU referral/admission proforma: 'Parent consultant spoken to: name/time' and 'ICU consultant decision: name/time'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-055.csv`
- Pitfalls: Night calls happen by phone and are not written → a timed name field fixes documentation, not behaviour; audit out-of-hours separately.; Parent consultants feel policed → present as shared decision-making support, with parent-team representation.; Referral through outreach blurs who spoke to whom → record the parent consultant contact explicitly.; Definitions drift → lock the exclusion list before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit an acute medicine consultant; most ward admissions are medical.; Check that the ICU admission proforma is the source of truth before cycle 1.; Report the proportion with a treatment escalation plan at the same time; it is often the real gap.; Name an ICU consultant sponsor.

**NNA-056. In adults in critical care at risk of long-term problems, what proportion have rehabilitation goals agreed and documented within 4 days of admission or before critical care discharge, whichever is sooner?** (new)
- Standard: NICE QS158 statement 1 (2017), from NICE CG83 rec 1.4: "Adults in critical care at risk of morbidity have their rehabilitation goals agreed within 4 days of admission to critical care or before discharge from critical care, whichever is sooner." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs158/chapter/Quality-statement-1-Rehabilitation-goals
- Pass: Short-term and medium-term rehabilitation goals documented (by any member of the critical care MDT) by day 4 of admission, or before discharge if the stay was shorter.. Target: ≥80% of at-risk patients. Sample: 40 consecutive at-risk admissions over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a 'Rehabilitation goals (short-term / medium-term) – agreed on ___' box to the ICU daily ward-round template that becomes mandatory on day 3.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-056.csv`
- Pitfalls: Physiotherapists record goals in a separate system → agree before cycle 1 which records count.; Sedated patients cannot agree goals → count family involvement or documented reason, as NICE allows.; At-risk definition changes between cycles → write the criteria on the data sheet.; Weekend admissions missed → report weekday and weekend admissions separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the lead ICU physiotherapist as co-lead.; Use the NCEPOD 2025 audit toolkit fields where they match.; Present with a patient story from the follow-up clinic.; Add the box to the day-3 ward round so it is part of routine care.

**NNA-057. In adults at risk of long-term problems who move from critical care to a general ward, what proportion have a written handover that includes their rehabilitation programme?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS158 statement 2 (2017), from NICE CG83 rec 1.12 and CG50 rec 1.15: "Adults at risk of morbidity have a formal handover of care, including their agreed individualised structured rehabilitation programme, when they transfer from critical care to a general ward." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs158/chapter/Quality-statement-2-Transfer-from-critical-care-to-a-general-ward
- Pass: The ICU discharge summary or handover document contains the rehabilitation goals and the plan (therapy frequency, mobility level, referrals) for the ward team.. Target: ≥85% of at-risk discharges. Sample: 40 consecutive at-risk ward discharges over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Rehabilitation plan for ward' section (goals, mobility, therapy frequency, referrals, psychological needs) to the ICU electronic discharge summary.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-057.csv`
- Pitfalls: Handover is verbal between physiotherapists → count only written handover and say so.; Summary written by a junior doctor without therapy input → build the section so the physiotherapist can complete it.; Night discharges skip the summary → cross-check with audit 'night discharges'.; Section filled with 'see physio notes' → define what counts as a plan.
- Pearls: Recruit ICU and ward physiotherapists together.; Check 48-hour ward therapy review as an outcome; it shows impact.; Use the same at-risk list as the rehabilitation-goals audit.; Ask the EPR team for a template change early; it can take weeks.

**NNA-058. In adults who stayed in critical care for more than 4 days and were at risk of long-term problems, what proportion are reviewed 2 to 3 months after critical care discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE QS158 statement 4 (2017), from NICE CG83 recs 1.1 and 1.23: "Adults who stayed in critical care for more than 4 days and were at risk of morbidity have a review 2 to 3 months after discharge from critical care." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs158/chapter/Quality-statement-4-Follow-up-after-critical-care-discharge
- Pass: A face-to-face or video review by a critical care follow-up service (or equivalent) between 8 and 13 weeks after ICU discharge.. Target: ≥75% of eligible patients reviewed at 2–3 months. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients discharged from ICU 3–6 months ago.
- Change: Make an automatic follow-up clinic referral from the ICU discharge form for every patient with a stay over 4 days, with an opt-out reason.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-058.csv`
- Pitfalls: No follow-up clinic exists → first measure referrals and present the gap to the business planning meeting.; Outcome lags by 3 months → audit referral rate at re-audit and attendance later.; Patients die or move after discharge → exclude and report them separately.; Clinic capacity fills → agree nurse-led or virtual review models with the ICU lead.
- Pearls: Recruit the ICU follow-up nurse or the lead physiotherapist.; Ask ICUsteps or the patient group for a patient voice at the presentation.; The NCEPOD 2025 audit toolkit fits this audit.; Separate 'not offered' from 'did not attend'.

**NNA-059. In adults who had a critical care stay and received rehabilitation on the ward, what proportion are given their own copy of the critical care discharge summary before leaving hospital?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG83 rec 1.22 (2009): "Give patients the following information before their discharge to home or community care. Also give the information to their family and/or carer, if the patient agrees. ... Give the patient their own copy of the critical care discharge summary." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg83/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The hospital discharge record, discharge checklist or letter documents that the patient (or family, with consent) was given a copy of the critical care discharge summary.. Target: ≥80% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible hospital discharges over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add 'Copy of critical care discharge summary given to patient: yes/no' to the ward discharge checklist, triggered when the EPR shows a critical care stay.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-059.csv`
- Pitfalls: ICU summary written in jargon → pair the change with a lay-language section.; Ward nurses do not know the patient was in ICU → use an EPR trigger.; Giving a copy is not documented even when done → the checklist tick fixes this.; Low numbers per ward → sample hospital-wide.
- Pearls: Recruit the ICU follow-up nurse.; Check GP receipt at the same time; it is often missing.; Ask a former patient to review the summary wording.; Keep the checklist item after the audit.

**NNA-060. At pre-operative assessment, what proportion of adults aged over 80 or with diabetes have a lying-to-standing or sitting-to-standing blood pressure recorded?** (new)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists and British and Irish Hypertension Society, Measurement and management of adult blood pressure in the peri-operative period (Anaesthesia 2026), recommendation 6: "Patients at risk of postural hypotension including those who are symptomatic, aged > 80 y or who are diabetic, should be screened with a supine-to-stand blood pressure assessment or a sit-to-stand assessment (if more pragmatic)." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.70082
- Pass: A lying (or sitting) and standing blood pressure pair documented at the pre-assessment visit, with action recorded if the drop is ≥20/10 mmHg.. Target: ≥85% of eligible patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible pre-assessment attendances over 2 weeks.
- Change: Add an auto-triggered 'Postural BP (lying/standing)' field to the pre-assessment template when age >80 or diabetes is entered.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-060.csv`
- Pitfalls: Clinic time is short → standing BP adds 3 minutes; show the falls risk data.; Telephone pre-assessments cannot measure it → exclude them and report the proportion.; Drops found but not acted on → add a 'drop found: action' field.; Automated cuffs give wrong readings on standing → agree a method (1 and 3 minutes) before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the pre-assessment nurse lead.; Ask the falls team to support the presentation.; Report the number of significant drops found; it motivates staff.; Use the same eligibility list in both cycles.

**NNA-061. In adults taking an ACE inhibitor or angiotensin receptor blocker who have elective surgery, what proportion take it on the morning of surgery?** (new)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists and British and Irish Hypertension Society, Measurement and management of adult blood pressure in the peri-operative period (Anaesthesia 2026), recommendation 7: "Patients should normally take their antihypertensive therapy, including angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors and angiotensin receptor blockers, on the day of surgery (moderate recommendation based on moderate-quality evidence)." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.70082
- Pass: The admission drug chart or medicines reconciliation shows the ACE inhibitor or ARB dose taken on the morning of surgery, or a documented clinical reason to omit it.. Target: ≥85% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible elective patients over 2–3 weeks.
- Change: Update the pre-assessment medication instruction sheet and the electronic instruction list so ACE inhibitors and ARBs default to 'take on the morning of surgery'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-061.csv`
- Pitfalls: Some anaesthetists still prefer to omit → share the guideline and allow a documented individual decision.; Old leaflets remain in circulation → recall and replace all printed stock.; Whether the dose was taken is not recorded → add it to the admission checklist.; Hypotension after induction worries staff → record pre-induction BP and report it.
- Pearls: Recruit a pre-assessment pharmacist.; Check all instruction sources (leaflet, letter, electronic list) before cycle 1.; Present the national guideline change as the reason, not local opinion.; Name a consultant owner for the medication instruction sheet.

**NNA-062. Of elective operations cancelled on the day for high blood pressure, what proportion had a blood pressure below 180/120 mmHg?** (new)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists and British and Irish Hypertension Society, Measurement and management of adult blood pressure in the peri-operative period (Anaesthesia 2026): "A blood pressure measurement should be performed in the pre-operative clinic using best practice as outlined in this guideline. Surgery should proceed unless the blood pressure is ≥ 180/120 (stage 3 hypertension)." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.70082
- Pass: A cancellation for blood pressure is appropriate only if repeated clinic blood pressure was ≥180/120 mmHg or there was another documented clinical reason.. Target: ≥90% of blood pressure cancellations appropriate; fewer cancellations overall. Sample: All such cancellations over the last 12 months (expect 20–50 in a DGH).
- Change: Put the national blood pressure 'traffic light' chart in every admissions area and anaesthetic room, and require consultant anaesthetist agreement for any blood pressure cancellation below 180/120.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-062.csv`
- Pitfalls: Reason codes are vague ('medical') → search the anaesthetic notes for BP values.; Anxiety readings on the day → require repeated readings and record them.; Anaesthetists feel second-guessed → present as a department standard agreed at a meeting.; Low monthly numbers → use 12 months for cycle 1 and 6 months for cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit the theatre manager; cancellation data are theirs.; Present minutes of theatre time lost.; Ask pre-assessment to request GP readings for anyone at 160/100 or above.; Name a consultant for the second-opinion rule.

**NNA-063. In adults taking a GLP-1 receptor agonist (e.g. semaglutide, tirzepatide) who have elective surgery or sedation, what proportion have a documented pulmonary aspiration risk assessment?** (new)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists, CPOC, RCoA and partners, Elective peri-operative management of adults taking GLP-1 RAs, GIP/GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors: consensus statement (Anaesthesia 2025): "Individualised pulmonary aspiration risk assessment should be completed, accounting for drug, patient and procedural factors." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.16541
- Pass: The anaesthetic pre-op note or pre-assessment record documents the GLP-1 drug and an aspiration risk assessment (e.g. dose phase, GI symptoms, planned technique or mitigation).. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive patients identified from pre-assessment medication lists over 1–2 months.
- Change: Add a 'GLP-1/GIP agonist: yes/no → aspiration risk (low/raised), plan' prompt to the pre-assessment and anaesthetic pre-op templates.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-063.csv`
- Pitfalls: Weight-loss injections bought privately are missing from GP lists → ask directly at pre-assessment.; Staff still stop the drug for a week → record 'stopped' separately and share the new guidance.; Definitions of 'assessment' vary → agree minimum content before cycle 1.; Few cases in cycle 1 → extend the time window, not the inclusion criteria.
- Pearls: Recruit a pre-assessment pharmacist to search medication lists.; Include endoscopy sedation lists; they see many of these patients.; Share the national consensus infographic with the change.; Name a consultant who owns the local guide.

**NNA-064. In adults taking an SGLT2 inhibitor (e.g. dapagliflozin, empagliflozin) who have elective surgery, what proportion omit it on both the day before and the day of the procedure?** (new)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists, CPOC, RCoA and partners, Elective peri-operative management of adults taking GLP-1 RAs, GIP/GLP-1 RAs and SGLT2 inhibitors: consensus statement (Anaesthesia 2025): "Sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitors should be omitted the day before and the day of a procedure." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.16541
- Pass: Pre-assessment instruction to omit on the day before and the day of surgery, and the admission record confirms no dose was taken on either day.. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over 1–2 months.
- Change: Add SGLT2 inhibitors (by drug name, all indications) to the pre-assessment electronic 'omit' list with an automatic day-before and day-of instruction letter.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-064.csv`
- Pitfalls: Heart failure patients are not flagged as 'diabetic' → search by drug name, not diagnosis.; Only day-of omission advised → check the wording of every leaflet.; Whether the day-before dose was taken is not asked → add it to the admission checklist.; Cardiologists worry about stopping → the omission is only 2 days; share the consensus.
- Pearls: Recruit a pharmacist to run a drug-name search.; Include day-surgery units.; Add restart advice at discharge in the same change.; Keep the drug list up to date as new SGLT2 inhibitors appear.

**NNA-065. In adults on a variable rate intravenous insulin infusion during surgery and recovery, what proportion have a capillary blood glucose checked at least every hour?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists and Joint British Diabetes Societies for Inpatient Care, Peri-operative management of diabetes mellitus: consensus statement (Anaesthesia 2026): "Check a capillary blood glucose every hour for patients who are placed on a variable rate intravenous insulin infusion." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.70181
- Pass: No gap longer than 60 minutes between glucose readings from the start of anaesthesia to discharge from recovery while the insulin infusion runs.. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over 1–2 months.
- Change: Add hourly glucose prompt rows to the anaesthetic and recovery charts that appear when an insulin infusion is started, and link the glucose meters to the EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-065.csv`
- Pitfalls: Meter readings are not transcribed → download from the meter to check.; Short cases under an hour → need only one intra-operative reading; define rules before cycle 1.; Recovery nurses unaware of VRII → include recovery staff in the change.; Few eligible patients → extend the time window.
- Pearls: Recruit the inpatient diabetes specialist nurse.; Ask point-of-care testing for meter data by location.; Report hypoglycaemia and hyperglycaemia episodes as outcomes.; Use the same gap rule in both cycles.

**NNA-066. In adults having general anaesthesia with at least one PONV risk factor, what proportion receive prophylaxis with two antiemetic interventions?** (new)
- Standard: Fifth Consensus Guidelines for the Management of Postoperative Nausea and Vomiting: Executive Summary (Gan et al., Anesth Analg 2026;143:497–513), Guideline 3: "Guideline 3. Administer PONV prophylaxis using 2 interventions in adults at any risk for PONV ... Since most adult patients undergoing surgery and anesthesia will have at least one risk factor for PONV, a general multimodal approach is recommended for administering PONV prophylaxis." https://doi.org/10.1213/ANE.0000000000007816
- Pass: Two prophylactic interventions from different classes (e.g. dexamethasone plus ondansetron, or one drug plus TIVA) recorded on the anaesthetic chart.. Target: ≥85% of eligible cases. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible cases from mixed day-case and inpatient lists over 2 weeks.
- Change: Add a default 'PONV prophylaxis: dexamethasone + ondansetron (untick with reason)' bundle to the electronic anaesthetic chart.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-066.csv`
- Pitfalls: Dexamethasone avoided in diabetes → record the reason; a documented alternative counts.; Apfel score not documented → derive it from the notes and keep the same method in both cycles.; Anaesthetists resist 'recipe' anaesthesia → keep an untick-with-reason option.; PONV outcome under-recorded in recovery → add a yes/no field to the recovery chart.
- Pearls: Recruit the recovery sister to record PONV and rescue.; Present day-case unplanned admissions due to PONV if available.; Pharmacy can confirm drug stock in each anaesthetic room.; Run cycle 1 on the same lists you will re-audit.

**NNA-067. In adults who vomit or feel sick after surgery despite prophylaxis, what proportion receive their first rescue antiemetic from a different drug class to the prophylaxis?** (new)
- Standard: Fifth Consensus Guidelines for the Management of Postoperative Nausea and Vomiting: Executive Summary (Gan et al., Anesth Analg 2026;143:497–513), Guideline 4: "Patients should receive rescue treatment from a different pharmacological class to the prophylactic agents administered. If more than 6 hours has elapsed, administration of a second dose of a 5-HT3 receptor antagonist or butyrophenone may be considered if no alternatives are available." https://doi.org/10.1213/ANE.0000000000007816
- Pass: First rescue antiemetic (in recovery or within 24 hours on the ward) is from a class not used for prophylaxis, or a repeat 5-HT3 antagonist given more than 6 hours after the prophylactic dose.. Target: ≥85% of rescue episodes. Sample: 40 consecutive patients who received rescue antiemetics, from EPMA/recovery records over 3–4 weeks.
- Change: Replace the generic 'ondansetron PRN' recovery and ward order set with a PONV rescue ladder that lists the drugs given in theatre and suggests a different class first (e.g. cyclizine, prochlorperazine, droperidol).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-067.csv`
- Pitfalls: Recovery nurses only have ondansetron under a PGD → update the PGD or order set before cycle 2.; Prophylaxis not recorded clearly → use the electronic chart as the only source.; Droperidol availability varies → offer two alternatives in the ladder.; Ward and recovery rescue differ → report both settings separately.
- Pearls: Recruit a recovery nurse and a surgical pharmacist.; Collect second-rescue rates; they show the benefit.; Show the ladder on the recovery drug trolley.; Pair this audit with the prophylaxis audit for one presentation.

**NNA-068. For tracheal intubations outside theatres (ICU, emergency department and wards), what proportion have waveform capnography confirmation of tube placement documented?** (new)
- Standard: Project for Universal Management of Airways and international airway societies (including DAS), Preventing unrecognised oesophageal intubation: consensus guideline (Anaesthesia 2022): "Exhaled carbon dioxide monitoring and pulse oximetry should be available and used for all episodes of airway management. ... Sustained exhaled carbon dioxide should be used to confirm alveolar ventilation following passage of a tracheal tube" https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.15817
- Pass: The intubation record documents a sustained waveform capnography trace (or end-tidal CO2 value after ≥7 breaths) as confirmation of tube placement.. Target: 100% of intubations. Sample: 40 consecutive out-of-theatre intubations over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Sustained exhaled CO2 confirmed (waveform): yes/no' line to the out-of-theatre intubation checklist and airway record.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-068.csv`
- Pitfalls: Capnography used but not written down → the checklist line fixes documentation; say so when presenting.; Cardiac arrest forms lack the field → update the resuscitation record too.; Low trace in cardiac arrest confuses staff → include the PUMA 'sustained CO2' criteria on the checklist.; Small numbers per month → collect from ICU, ED and arrest calls together.
- Pearls: Recruit an ICU airway lead and an ED consultant.; Check every emergency airway trolley has a working capnography line during weeks 5–12.; Record videolaryngoscope use at the same time.; Report any recognised oesophageal intubations anonymously.

**NNA-069. After thyroid surgery, what proportion of adults have observations including wound inspection recorded at least hourly for the first 6 hours?** (new)
- Standard: DAS, BAETS and ENT UK, Management of haematoma after thyroid surgery: consensus guidelines (Anaesthesia 2022): "Thus, routine patient observations should be carried out at least hourly for first 6 h postoperatively ... Minimum monitoring includes wound inspection, early warning and pain scoring, as well as awareness for more subtle signs (agitation; anxiety; difficulty in breathing; discomfort)." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.15585
- Pass: Observations (NEWS2, pain score and wound/neck check) recorded with no gap over 60 minutes from recovery discharge until 6 hours after end of surgery.. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: All thyroid operations over the last 6 months (expect 30–50 in a DGH with a thyroid surgeon).
- Change: Add a 'post-thyroid surgery' observation schedule to the e-observations system (hourly for 6 hours with a neck/wound check field and DESATS prompts), triggered by the procedure code.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-069.csv`
- Pitfalls: Low volume → use 6–12 months for cycle 1 and keep collecting until 20 cases for cycle 2.; Obs recorded but no wound check → the field must be separate.; Patients moved to wards far from the nursing station → record bed location too.; Night staffing pushback → hourly obs apply only to the first 6 hours.
- Pearls: Recruit the ENT or endocrine ward sister.; Check for the post-thyroid emergency box at the same time.; Invite the thyroid surgeon to co-present.; Use the DAS/BAETS/ENT UK post-thyroid surgery review chart as the template.

**NNA-070. What proportion of adults with a BMI over 40 are flagged as such on the published theatre list?** (new)
- Standard: Society for Obesity and Bariatric Anaesthesia, Airway management in patients living with obesity: best practice recommendations (Anaesthesia 2025): "Operating theatre lists should flag patients with a BMI > 40 kg.m-2 (Grade D, strong recommendation)." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.16647
- Pass: The final published theatre list shows BMI or an obesity flag for the patient.. Target: ≥95% of patients with BMI >40. Sample: 40 consecutive patients with BMI >40 over 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Make BMI a mandatory field when booking a case in the theatre system and display it automatically on the printed and electronic list, with a flag above 40.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-070.csv`
- Pitfalls: BMI missing for emergency cases → allow 'estimated' and record weight on admission.; Patients object to BMI on public screens → show a neutral flag icon rather than the number.; Booking clerks bypass mandatory fields → ask IT to block list publication without BMI.; Definitions change (BMI >35 vs >40) → keep 40 in both cycles.
- Pearls: Recruit the theatre scheduling manager.; Check emergency lists separately; they fail most.; Show a near miss (equipment not ready) to explain why.; Record team brief discussion as a second measure.

**NNA-071. For central venous catheters inserted in theatre or intensive care, what proportion have witnessed documentation that the guidewire was removed?** (new)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists guidelines: safe vascular access 2025 (Anaesthesia 2025): "All possible procedures should be put in place to prevent retained guidewires, including operator vigilance; guidewire counts; mandatory witnessed documentation of guidewire removal; and where available, the use of guidewire anti-retention devices." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.16727
- Pass: The CVC insertion record or LocSSIP checklist states the guidewire was removed and names or signs a second person as witness.. Target: 100% of insertions. Sample: 40 consecutive insertions over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Replace free-text line notes with a vascular access LocSSIP sticker/EPR form that has a 'Guidewire removed and shown to: ___ (signature)' box.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-071.csv`
- Pitfalls: Theatre lines documented on the anaesthetic chart only → accept the chart if the witness is named.; Assistants not trained to witness → brief ODPs and ICU nurses before the change.; Paper stickers run out → keep stock in every line trolley and add an EPR version.; Emergency lines out of hours are missed → audit day and night separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the ICU nurse educator and the theatre ODP lead.; Record ultrasound use too; it is in the same guideline.; Keep a blank form on the CVC trolley.; Present any Never Event learning nationally to show relevance.

**NNA-072. In adults having major elective surgery with blood cross-matched or a transfusion anticipated, what proportion have a documented pre-operative discussion about transfusion and its alternatives?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Association of Anaesthetists guidelines: the use of blood components and their alternatives (Anaesthesia 2025), recommendation 2: "Where blood transfusion is anticipated, this and alternatives to transfusion should be discussed with the patient before surgery, and their consent should be documented according to local protocols." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.16542
- Pass: Consent form, pre-assessment or anaesthetic note documents a discussion of transfusion risks, benefits and alternatives, and the patient's decision, before the day of surgery or before induction.. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add a transfusion section (risks, benefits, alternatives, leaflet given, decision) to the consent form or anaesthetic pre-op template for procedures with a cross-match on the maximum surgical blood ordering schedule.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-072.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgeons and anaesthetists each assume the other consents → agree who owns it before cycle 1.; Consent recorded only when transfusion happens → sample from the cross-match list, not the transfusion list.; Jehovah's Witness patients distort results → include them; an advance decision counts as a documented discussion.; Section left blank on the form → make it mandatory for listed procedures.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion practitioner.; Use the NHS Blood and Transplant patient leaflet.; Link the change to the maximum surgical blood ordering schedule.; Report alternatives offered; it supports patient blood management.

**NNA-073. In adults using CPAP or home non-invasive ventilation who have elective surgery, what proportion are told at pre-assessment to bring their machine on the day of surgery?** (new)
- Standard: Society for Obesity and Bariatric Anaesthesia, Airway management in patients living with obesity: best practice recommendations (Anaesthesia 2025): "Patients on CPAP/non-invasive ventilation should be instructed to bring their machine on the day of surgery (Grade C, strong recommendation)." https://doi.org/10.1111/anae.16647
- Pass: Pre-assessment record or letter documents the instruction to bring the machine, and the admission record shows it arrived.. Target: ≥90% instructed; ≥80% machines brought. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add 'Uses CPAP/NIV: bring machine and mask on day of surgery' as an automatic line in the pre-assessment admission letter when CPAP is ticked.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-073.csv`
- Pitfalls: CPAP use not asked at pre-assessment → add a yes/no question first.; Patients forget despite instruction → add a text reminder the day before.; Wards lack power points or staff familiar with CPAP → involve the respiratory nurse.; Small numbers → extend the collection window.
- Pearls: Recruit the sleep or respiratory physiology team.; Include day-surgery units.; Record post-op CPAP use as an outcome.; Keep the same inclusion in both cycles.

## Emergency medicine

**NNA-074. What proportion of adults at high risk from sepsis (NEWS2 7 or more with suspected infection) receive IV antibiotics within 1 hour of their first ED NEWS2 score?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG253 rec 1.8.3 (November 2025, updated September 2026): "Give people aged 16 or over who are at high risk of severe illness or death from sepsis broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotic treatment, within 1 hour of calculating the person's NEWS2 score on initial assessment in the emergency department or on ward deterioration. Only give antibiotics if they have not been given before for this episode of sepsis (see recommendations 1.7.7 and 1.7.8 on managing the condition while awaiting transfer)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Managing-suspected-sepsis
- Pass: First IV antibiotic dose recorded as given on the drug chart within 60 minutes of the first ED NEWS2 time; or pre-hospital antibiotics already given for this episode.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible ED attendances over the last 2 months.
- Change: Build an automatic ED alert on the e-observations system: a NEWS2 of 7 or more with an 'infection suspected' tick opens a sepsis order set with a pre-filled antibiotic and a 60-minute countdown on the tracking board.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-074.csv`
- Pitfalls: Time zero recorded differently between cycles → agree in advance that time zero is the first ED NEWS2 time stamp, not triage or doctor review.; Prescription time used instead of administration time → take only the 'given' time from EPMA.; Medium-risk patients with a 3-point single parameter caused by infection are missed → add a column and decide inclusion rules before cycle 1.; Nursing staff see the audit as blame → share results by shift pattern, not by name.; Gains fade after rotation → make the alert part of the system, not a poster.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED sepsis nurse and the EPR analyst on day 1; they can pull NEWS2 time stamps in bulk.; Record the reason for each delay; the commonest cause tells you which fix to pick.; Use a run chart of weekly compliance, not a single percentage.; Hand the monthly report to a consultant owner before you rotate.

**NNA-075. What proportion of adults at high risk from sepsis who receive IV fluid in the ED are given an initial bolus of 250 ml?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG253 recs 1.8.6 and 1.8.8 (November 2025, updated September 2026): "1.8.6 Give an initial bolus of 250 ml. Ideally, give this over 10 to 15 minutes. [2025] ... 1.8.8 Reassess after each fluid bolus. [2025]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Managing-suspected-sepsis
- Pass: First resuscitation bolus prescribed and given as 250 ml of crystalloid, with a documented reassessment (observations or clinical note) before any further bolus.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible attendances over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Replace the sepsis order set default from '500 ml bolus' to '250 ml over 10–15 minutes, reassess' and reprint the sepsis sticker with the same wording.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-075.csv`
- Pitfalls: Old 500 ml bags or stickers left in resus → remove old stock labels and stickers on the change date.; Paramedic pre-alert fluids counted as the first bolus → record pre-hospital fluid separately.; Clinicians object that 250 ml is too little in shock → share the NG253 wording and stress repeat boluses up to 1,000 ml.; Reassessment not written down → accept a timed set of observations as reassessment.
- Pearls: Agree definitions with the ED sepsis lead before cycle 1.; Ask pharmacy to change the order set default; it is quick and lasts beyond rotation.; Pair the audit with the antibiotic timing audit to share data collection.; Report total fluid in the first hour too, to show no one is under-resuscitated.

**NNA-076. What proportion of adults at medium risk from sepsis (NEWS2 5 or 6 with suspected infection) are reviewed by an FY2-level or more senior clinician, with the venous lactate result, within 1 hour?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG253 rec 1.8.16 (November 2025, updated September 2026): "For people aged 16 or over with a medium risk of severe illness or death from sepsis: carry out a venous blood test, including for: blood gas, including glucose and lactate measurement ... arrange for a clinician with core competencies in the care of acutely ill patients (FY2 level or above) to review the person's condition and venous lactate results within 1 hour of the person being assessed as at medium risk. [2024, amended 2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Managing-suspected-sepsis
- Pass: A timed clinical note by an FY2 or more senior clinician, mentioning the lactate result, within 60 minutes of the first NEWS2 of 5 or 6.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible attendances over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a 'medium sepsis risk' flag to the ED tracking board that starts a 60-minute timer and prompts an automatic venous gas at triage.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-076.csv`
- Pitfalls: Grade of reviewer not recorded → use the rota or login name to confirm grade.; Lactate done on the arterial gas instead → accept any blood gas lactate.; Patients escalate to high risk and drop out → record them and analyse separately.; Night-time staffing skews results → report by time of day.
- Pearls: Get the ED nurse in charge to agree triage-led venous gas before cycle 1.; Pull NEWS2 5–6 plus infection codes from the EPR to find cases fast.; Record the antibiotic decision to show safe deferral, not just speed.; Present results with the high-risk antibiotic audit as one sepsis story.

**NNA-077. What proportion of children under 16 with suspected sepsis who meet a NICE high risk criterion receive antibiotics within 1 hour of meeting that criterion in the ED?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG254 rec 1.7.3 (November 2025): "Ensure urgent assessment mechanisms are in place to deliver antibiotics when any high risk criteria are met in a person under 16 in secondary care (within 1 hour of meeting a high risk criterion in an acute hospital setting). [2016]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng254/chapter/Managing-suspected-sepsis
- Pass: First antibiotic dose given (IV or IO) within 60 minutes of the first recorded high risk criterion.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive eligible children over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add a paediatric sepsis 'grab box' with pre-drawn antibiotic doses by weight band and an IO needle, and a sepsis flag on the PEWS chart that starts a 60-minute timer.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-077.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers in a DGH → extend the look-back to 6 months or share with a neighbouring ED.; Criterion time hard to find → use the first PEWS chart entry showing the criterion.; Cannulation delays blamed → record access method and attempts.; Paediatric and ED teams dispute ownership → run it as a joint audit with named leads from each.
- Pearls: Recruit a paediatric nurse champion to check the grab box daily.; Use the ED coding for 'sepsis' and 'fever' to find cases.; Agree what counts as the criterion time before cycle 1.; Give weight-band dosing cards to reduce prescribing delay.

**NNA-078. What proportion of children under 5 with fever seen in the ED have temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate and capillary refill time all recorded at first assessment?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG143 rec 1.2.8 (2019, updated 2021): "Measure and record temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate and capillary refill time as part of the routine assessment of a child with fever. [2007]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng143/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: All four values recorded in the triage or first clinician observations.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive attendances over 2 weeks.
- Change: Make capillary refill time a mandatory field in the paediatric triage screen on the ED system (cannot save without it).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-078.csv`
- Pitfalls: Triage staff resist more mandatory fields → agree with the nurse lead and show baseline data first.; CRT entered as '<2' by default → check for copy-forward values.; Only initial observations audited → keep to first assessment to avoid mixed definitions.; Streamed children seen in UTC are missed → include UTC or state exclusion.
- Pearls: Recruit a paediatric ED nurse to co-lead.; Collect traffic light category too; it adds value at no extra time.; Show missing CRT by time of day to target training.; A mandatory field keeps working after rotation.

**NNA-079. What proportion of adults with a head injury and a NICE 1-hour CT risk factor have their CT head scan within 1 hour of that risk factor being identified?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.5.8 (May 2023): "For people 16 and over who have sustained a head injury, do a CT head scan within 1 hour of any of these risk factors being identified: a GCS score of 12 or less on initial assessment in the emergency department; a GCS score of less than 15 at 2 hours after the injury on assessment in the emergency department; suspected open or depressed skull fracture; any sign of basal skull fracture (haemotympanum, 'panda' eyes, cerebrospinal fluid leakage from the ear or nose, Battle's sign); post-traumatic seizure; focal neurological deficit; more than 1 episode of vomiting. [2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: CT acquisition time within 60 minutes of the first documented risk factor time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Introduce a 'NICE 1-hour head CT' request option on the radiology system that flags as urgent on the CT worklist and alerts the radiographer by bleep.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-079.csv`
- Pitfalls: Risk factor time is not written → use the first note describing it and record how often it is missing.; Radiology feels blamed → run it jointly with a radiology lead.; Request made late, not scan late → record request time to split ED and radiology delay.; Changing inclusion between cycles → use the same list of 7 risk factors.
- Pearls: Get CT acquisition times in bulk from RIS.; Split the timeline into identify → request → scan → report.; Recruit a CT superintendent radiographer early.; Show the worst delays as short case stories at governance.

**NNA-080. What proportion of adults on anticoagulants with a head injury and no other CT indication have a documented CT decision with a reason?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.5.13 (May 2023): "For people who have sustained a head injury and have no other indications for a CT head scan, but are on anticoagulant treatment (including vitamin K antagonists, direct-acting oral anticoagulants (DOACs), heparin and low molecular weight heparins) or antiplatelet treatment (excluding aspirin monotherapy), consider doing a CT head scan: within 8 hours of the injury (for example, if it is difficult to do a risk assessment or if the person might not return to the emergency department if they have signs of deterioration) or within the hour if they present more than 8 hours after the injury." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Notes record a CT decision (scan or no scan) and a reason; if scanned, CT within 8 hours of injury (or within 1 hour if presenting after 8 hours).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add an 'anticoagulated head injury' box to the ED head injury proforma asking: CT yes/no, reason (difficult assessment, unable to return, other) and time of injury.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-080.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients with other risk factors slip in → screen each case against recs 1.5.8 and 1.5.9 first.; Aspirin alone included by mistake → exclude aspirin monotherapy.; Clinicians fear missing a bleed → present yield and the NICE rationale together.; Injury time missing → record 'unknown' and treat as presenting after 8 hours.
- Pearls: Pull cases from the ED head injury code plus medication list.; Report the CT yield; it helps the department agree a local stance.; Get a consultant to sign off the proforma wording.; Keep the box short so it gets used.

**NNA-081. What proportion of children with a head injury and exactly one NICE risk factor are observed for at least 4 hours from the time of injury?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.5.12 (May 2023): "Observe people under 16 who have sustained a head injury but have only 1 of the risk factors in recommendation 1.5.11 for a minimum of 4 hours from the time of injury. If, during observation, any of the following risk factors are identified, do a CT head scan within 1 hour: a GCS score of less than 15; further vomiting; a further episode of abnormal drowsiness. If none of these risk factors occur during observation, use clinical judgement to determine whether a longer period of observation is needed. [2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Documented observations continue until at least 4 hours after the time of injury before discharge.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible children over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a 'discharge not before' time box to the paediatric head injury proforma, calculated as injury time plus 4 hours, and show it on the tracking board.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-081.csv`
- Pitfalls: Injury time not recorded → make it a required field; record 'unknown' in cycle 1.; Children observed in a separate area with paper charts → check both paper and electronic records.; Breach pressure pushes early discharge → share with the site team that observation counts as care.; Multi-factor children included by mistake → screen for 1.5.10 and 1.5.11 factors first.
- Pearls: Recruit a paediatric ED nurse to check the 'discharge not before' box.; Report deterioration during observation to show why the rule matters.; Use the tracking board so the time is visible to all.; Agree the proforma change with the paediatric lead before cycle 1.

**NNA-082. What proportion of children discharged from the ED after a head injury have a letter sent to their GP and to their health visitor or school nurse within 48 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.10.12 (May 2023): "For anyone who has attended the emergency department with a head injury, write to their GP within 48 hours of discharge, giving details of clinical history and examination. Also share this letter with health visitors (for preschool children) and school nurses (for school-age children and young people). If appropriate, provide a copy of the letter for the person with a head injury, and their family or carers, custodial staff or social worker. [2014]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: GP letter sent within 48 hours of ED discharge and copied to the health visitor (under 5) or school nurse (school age).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive discharges over 2–4 weeks.
- Change: Set the ED system to auto-copy every paediatric head injury discharge letter to the paediatric liaison inbox, which forwards to the health visitor or school nurse.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-082.csv`
- Pitfalls: Letters generated but not sent → check the sent log, not the draft.; Health visitor contact unknown → route via the paediatric liaison team.; IT change takes months → start with a manual liaison list while the build is done.; Adults included by mistake → restrict to under 16.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric liaison health visitor as co-lead.; The safeguarding lead will back this; ask early.; Audit copy recipients, not just timing.; Keep a small weekly spot check after the change.

**NNA-083. What proportion of adults aged 65 or over discharged from the ED after a fall-related head injury are referred for a falls assessment?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.10.13 (May 2023): "Refer people with a head injury to investigate its causes and manage contributing factors, if appropriate. This could include, for example, referral for a falls assessment or to safeguarding services. [2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Documented referral to a falls service, frailty team or GP falls review, or a documented reason why not appropriate.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 50 consecutive discharges over 2–4 weeks.
- Change: Add a falls referral tick box to the ED discharge letter for patients 65 or over with a fall, which sends an electronic referral to the community falls service.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-083.csv`
- Pitfalls: No falls service capacity → agree the referral route with the service before cycle 1.; GP letter wording counted as referral → define what counts before cycle 1.; Patients from care homes already known → record as 'already under falls team'.; Tick box ignored → make it a required field on discharge.
- Pearls: Recruit the frailty or falls nurse lead early.; Report CFS alongside to show need.; Show the number of repeat attendances with falls to motivate staff.; Keep the referral one click.

**NNA-084. What proportion of adults treated in the ED for suspected anaphylaxis have two correctly timed mast cell tryptase samples?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG258 rec 1.1.4 (May 2026): "After suspected anaphylaxis in an adult or young person aged 16 years or older, take timed blood samples for mast cell tryptase testing as follows: a sample as soon as possible after emergency treatment has started; a second sample ideally within 1 to 2 hours (but no later than 4 hours) from the onset of symptoms. [2011]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng258/chapter/Timing-of-blood-samples
- Pass: First tryptase sample taken after treatment started, and a second sample taken no later than 4 hours from symptom onset, both with times recorded.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive cases over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Add a timed tryptase order set ('0 h and 1–2 h') to the ED anaphylaxis pathway, which prints two labelled tubes and adds a 1-hour task on the tracking board.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-084.csv`
- Pitfalls: Onset time unknown → use the ambulance call time and note it.; Allergic reactions without adrenaline included → restrict to IM adrenaline cases.; Small numbers → use 12 months look-back and report as counts.; Second sample forgotten at handover → put the task on the tracking board.
- Pearls: Ask the lab to report sample collection time, not receipt time.; Recruit the local allergy consultant; they want this data.; Pair with the auto-injector and referral audit to share case finding.; Build the order set once; it outlasts rotation.

**NNA-085. What proportion of patients discharged from the ED after anaphylaxis leave with two in-date adrenaline auto-injectors?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG258 rec 1.1.15 (May 2026): "At discharge following emergency treatment for suspected or known anaphylaxis, ensure the adult, young person or child has 2 in-date adrenaline auto-injectors (via a prescription, if needed) and knows when and how to use them, unless the anaphylaxis was due to a drug allergy and the drug can be easily avoided. [2011, amended 2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng258/chapter/Discharge-from-AE-or-inpatient-care
- Pass: Two auto-injectors supplied or prescribed (or two in-date devices confirmed), and training documented; or an easily avoided drug trigger documented.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive discharges over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Create an ED anaphylaxis discharge bundle on EPMA: two auto-injectors as a default TTO, device training checkbox and an automatic allergy clinic referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-085.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients already carrying devices not checked for expiry → add 'in date' to the checkbox.; Out-of-hours pharmacy cannot supply → keep two devices as ED pre-packs.; Drug-trigger exemptions overused → require the drug name.; Training not recorded → accept a nursing note naming the device brand.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED pharmacist to build the default TTO.; Keep brand-specific trainer pens at the nurse station.; Record allergy referral in the same pass; NG258 rec 1.1.12 asks for it.; Share results with the allergy clinic to secure their support.

**NNA-086. What proportion of adults investigated in the ED for suspected pulmonary embolism have a 2-level PE Wells score documented before the D-dimer or CTPA request?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 rec 1.1.17 (2020, updated 2023): "If PE is suspected, use the 2‑level PE Wells score (table 2) to estimate the clinical probability of PE. [2012]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A PE Wells score (number or likely/unlikely) documented before the first D-dimer or CTPA request time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive patients over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Make the PE Wells score a required field on the CTPA and D-dimer request forms from the ED, with the result steering the order set.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-086.csv`
- Pitfalls: Wells written after the test → compare time stamps.; PERC used instead of Wells in low-risk patients → record PERC use as a separate field.; Radiology vetting already enforces it informally → check with radiology to avoid double work.; Required field filled with random numbers → spot check 10 scores for accuracy.
- Pearls: Report CTPA positive rate; it is the outcome consultants care about.; Recruit a radiology registrar to share data pulls.; Include the age-adjusted D-dimer question as a secondary measure.; Agree the form change with the IT team before cycle 1.

**NNA-087. What proportion of adults with a likely DVT Wells score seen in the ED get either an ultrasound result within 4 hours or interim therapeutic anticoagulation?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 recs 1.1.3 and 1.1.4 (2020, updated 2023): "1.1.3 Offer people with a likely DVT Wells score (2 points or more): a proximal leg vein ultrasound scan, with the result available within 4 hours if possible (if the scan result cannot be obtained within 4 hours follow recommendation 1.1.4) ... 1.1.4 If a proximal leg vein ultrasound scan result cannot be obtained within 4 hours, offer people with a DVT Wells score of 2 points or more: a D‑dimer test, then interim therapeutic anticoagulation (see the section on interim therapeutic anticoagulation for suspected DVT or PE) and a proximal leg vein ultrasound scan with the result available within 24 hours. [2012, amended 2020]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Ultrasound result within 4 hours of assessment, or a therapeutic anticoagulant dose given before the patient leaves the ED; or a documented contraindication.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a pre-printed interim anticoagulation line (DOAC or LMWH by weight and renal function) to the ED DVT pathway, triggered when no scan slot is available within 4 hours.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-087.csv`
- Pitfalls: Wells not written → count as fail and report separately.; Ambulatory clinic records live on paper → include both sources.; Clinicians worry about bleeding → include contraindication as a pass route.; Weekend scan availability changes between cycles → record day of week.
- Pearls: Recruit the anticoagulation nurse specialist as co-lead.; Report time of day; out-of-hours is where it fails.; Check baseline bloods are sent (NG158 1.3.4).; Keep the pre-printed line simple, with a renal dose table on the back.

**NNA-088. What proportion of adults seen in the ED after self-harm have a discharge or referral decision made without a risk score or a low, medium or high risk rating?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG225 recs 1.6.2 and 1.6.4 (September 2022): "1.6.2 Do not use risk assessment tools and scales to determine who should and should not be offered treatment or who should be discharged. ... 1.6.4 Do not use global risk stratification into low, medium or high risk to determine who should be offered treatment or who should be discharged." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: ED record shows no risk scale score (for example SAD PERSONS) and no low/medium/high rating used to decide discharge or referral.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive attendances over 3–4 weeks.
- Change: Remove the risk score and low/medium/high boxes from the ED self-harm triage and clerking template and replace them with the NG225 1.7.12 needs-based questions.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-088.csv`
- Pitfalls: Liaison psychiatry notes still use ratings → include them and discuss with the mental health trust.; Staff feel the old score protects them → explain the NICE rationale and offer a needs-based alternative.; Template change takes months → start with a paper version.; Hard to judge 'used for decision' → count any rating as fail in both cycles.
- Pearls: Co-lead with a liaison psychiatry nurse.; Use the RCEM mental health toolkit wording in the new template.; Also record whether liaison referral was offered.; Get the mental health trust to agree the change before cycle 1.

**NNA-089. What proportion of adolescents attending the ED with a mental health problem have an emergency clinician-initiated psychosocial assessment documented?** (new)
- Standard: RCEM Adolescent Mental Health QIP 2026, Standard 1 (published November 2025): "All adolescent patients attending the emergency department with mental health problems should have an emergency clinician initiated psychosocial assessment documented." https://rcem.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/AMH-QIP-pack-Analysis-plan-FINAL-15.12.25-v3.pdf
- Pass: ED notes contain a structured psychosocial assessment (for example HEADSSS) by an ED clinician before referral or discharge.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive attendances over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a HEADSSS template to the ED system that opens automatically for 12–17-year-olds with a mental health presenting complaint.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-089.csv`
- Pitfalls: Adolescents seen in the adult ED are missed → search both areas.; CAMHS assessment counted as ED assessment → count only ED clinician notes.; ED doctors lack HEADSSS confidence → add a prompt card with questions.; Data entry doubles with the RCEM portal → use the same fields as the QIP.
- Pearls: Register with the RCEM QIP to benchmark nationally.; Recruit the paediatric mental health lead nurse.; Record CAMHS response time as a secondary measure.; Share anonymised good examples with the team.

**NNA-090. What proportion of adults discharged from the ED with atrial fibrillation have a CHA2DS2-VASc score documented?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG196 rec 1.2.1 (2021): "Use the CHA2DS2-VASc stroke risk score to assess stroke risk in people with any of the following: symptomatic or asymptomatic paroxysmal, persistent or permanent atrial fibrillation; atrial flutter; a continuing risk of arrhythmia recurrence after cardioversion back to sinus rhythm or catheter ablation. [2021]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng196/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: CHA2DS2-VASc score documented in ED notes or discharge letter, with an anticoagulation plan (started, referred or reason not).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive discharges over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a CHA2DS2-VASc and ORBIT calculator to the ED AF discharge template, with a DOAC start option and a referral to the AF clinic.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-090.csv`
- Pitfalls: AF with rapid rate admitted → restrict to discharges only.; Known AF on anticoagulant included → exclude.; ED staff say it is the GP's job → the NICE wording covers any setting; share the stroke risk.; Score calculated but wrong → spot check 10 scores.
- Pearls: Recruit a cardiology or AF nurse specialist.; Record the plan, not just the score.; Include ORBIT as a secondary measure.; Put the calculator in the template, not on a poster.

**NNA-091. What proportion of adults discharged from the ED after transient loss of consciousness have driving advice documented?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG109 recs 1.5.2.1 and 1.5.2.2 (2010, updated 2023): "1.5.2.1 Give advice about eligibility to drive when a person first presents with TLoC. Please refer to the Driver and Vehicle Licensing Agency (DVLA) guidance on neurological disorders: assessing fitness to drive. 1.5.2.2 Advise all people who have experienced TLoC that they must not drive while waiting for a specialist assessment. Following specialist assessment, the healthcare professional should advise the person of their obligations regarding reporting the TLoC event to the DVLA." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg109/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Driving status asked and driving advice documented in the ED notes or discharge letter.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive discharges over 2–3 weeks.
- Change: Add a required 'driving status and advice given' line to the ED discharge template for syncope, collapse and seizure diagnoses, with a link to DVLA guidance.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-091.csv`
- Pitfalls: Advice given verbally but not written → only count written advice in both cycles.; Staff unsure of DVLA rules → include a one-page summary with the template.; Simple vasovagal faints have different rules → record diagnosis so advice can be checked.; Non-drivers counted as fails → record 'does not drive' as pass if asked.
- Pearls: Pair with a 12-lead ECG check; same notes, extra value.; Recruit an ED consultant with an interest in syncope.; Link the template to the DVLA web page.; A required field keeps working after rotation.

**NNA-092. What proportion of adults with a staggered or uncertain-time paracetamol overdose start acetylcysteine within 1 hour of ED arrival?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, September 2012 (vol 6, issue 2: A1): "where there is doubt over the timing of paracetamol ingestion including when ingestion has occurred over a period of one hour or more – 'staggered overdose' – acetylcysteine should be given without delay (the nomogram should not be used)." https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/treating-paracetamol-overdose-with-intravenous-acetylcysteine-new-guidance
- Pass: Acetylcysteine infusion started within 60 minutes of arrival, without waiting for the paracetamol level (local definition of 'without delay').. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive cases over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add a 'staggered or unknown-time overdose' route to the ED paracetamol pathway that prescribes acetylcysteine at triage by weight band.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-092.csv`
- Pitfalls: Overdose type not clear in notes → record 'unclear' and treat as unknown time.; Waiting for weight → use weight-band tables.; Pharmacy prescribing delays at night → keep weight-band bags in ED.; Small numbers → extend the look-back.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED pharmacist.; Check dose accuracy at the same time.; Use TOXBASE wording in the pathway.; Share with liaison psychiatry to align the timing of assessment.

**NNA-093. What proportion of fluoroquinolone prescriptions issued from the ED have a documented reason why first-line antibiotics were inappropriate?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, January 2024 (vol 17, issue 6: 2): "the UK indications for systemic fluoroquinolones have been updated so they must only be used in situations when other antibiotics, that are commonly recommended for the infection, are inappropriate; situations in which other antibiotics are considered to be inappropriate and where a fluoroquinolone may be indicated are where: there is resistance to other first-line antibiotics recommended for the infection; other first-line antibiotics are contraindicated in an individual patient; other first-line antibiotics have caused side effects in the patient requiring treatment to be stopped; treatment with other first-line antibiotics has failed" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/fluoroquinolone-antibiotics-must-now-only-be-prescribed-when-other-commonly-recommended-antibiotics-are-inappropriate
- Pass: One of the four MHRA reasons documented, and the patient given fluoroquinolone safety advice.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive prescriptions over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'reason first-line inappropriate' drop-down (the four MHRA reasons) to all fluoroquinolone prescriptions on EPMA in the ED.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-093.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper FP10 prescriptions missed → include ED pharmacy TTO records.; Microbiology advice verbal → accept 'discussed with micro' plus reason.; Drop-down filled without thought → spot check 10 against the notes.; Pushback from urology or ENT pathways → agree exemptions with specialty leads.
- Pearls: Recruit the antimicrobial pharmacist; they can pull a dispensing list in minutes.; Report by indication; UTI and prostatitis dominate.; Print the MHRA patient sheet into the discharge pack.; Share results with the stewardship committee for Trust-wide spread.

**NNA-094. What proportion of adults having procedural sedation in the ED have continuous capnography documented?** (new)
- Standard: RCEM Best Practice Guideline: Procedural Sedation in the Emergency Department, recommendation 11 (August 2022): "Monitoring during procedural sedation should include: 3 lead ECG, oxygen saturations, continuous capnography, non-invasive blood pressure." https://rcem.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2022/08/RCEM_BPC_Procedural_Sedation_Final_Aug_22.pdf
- Pass: Sedation record documents continuous capnography plus 3-lead ECG, SpO2 and NIBP.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive sedations over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a capnography line with a tick box and trace sticker space to the ED sedation proforma, and fit capnography modules to every resus monitor.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-094.csv`
- Pitfalls: Capnography modules missing → check equipment before cycle 1.; Proforma not used → count free-text notes too and report proforma use.; Sedation lead not involved → ask them to co-lead.; Paediatric sedation mixed in → keep to adults or audit separately.
- Pearls: Ask the ED sedation lead to present the results and own the proforma.; Record adverse events as a balancing measure.; Keep capnography lines with each monitor.; Build it into sedation competency.

**NNA-095. What proportion of patients with Parkinson's disease in the ED receive their time-critical medication within 30 minutes of their usual dose time?** (under-audited)
- Standard: RCEM Time Critical Medications QIP 2025 (Year 2) Information Pack, Standard 2 (January 2025): "A patient's TCM should be administered according to their usual regime whilst they are in the ED" https://rcem.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2025/01/TCM-QIP-2024-25-Information-Pack-Year-2-V4.pdf
- Pass: Every Parkinson's dose due during the ED stay given (or self-administered and recorded) within 30 minutes of the usual time.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive attendances over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a 'time-critical medication' question at triage that flags the patient on the tracking board and prompts self-administration or prescribing with usual times.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-095.csv`
- Pitfalls: Usual dose times not known → ask patient or carer and record at triage.; Self-administered doses not recorded → add a self-administration entry on EPMA.; Short ED stays with no dose due → exclude them.; Pharmacy stock gaps → agree an ED stock list with pharmacy.
- Pearls: Register with the RCEM QIP to benchmark.; Recruit a Parkinson's nurse specialist.; Use Parkinson's UK 'Get it on time' materials.; Report missed doses as counts; they are powerful at governance.

## Acute and specialty medicine

**NNA-096. What proportion of adults with acute kidney injury have a urine dipstick result documented within 24 hours of the AKI being detected?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG148 rec 1.4.2 (2019, updated 2024): "Perform urine dipstick testing for blood, protein, leucocytes, nitrites and glucose in all people as soon as acute kidney injury is suspected or detected. Document the results and ensure that appropriate action is taken when results are abnormal. [2013]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng148/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Dipstick result (all five analytes) documented in the notes or EPR within 24 hours of the first AKI alert, with a stated action if abnormal.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive AKI alerts on medical wards over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'urine dipstick result' field to the EPR AKI care bundle that opens with the AKI alert, and add dipstick to the nursing AKI checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-096.csv`
- Pitfalls: Dipsticks done by nurses but not written anywhere → agree before cycle 1 where the result is recorded (observations chart or EPR) and search both.; Catheterised or anuric patients skew results → exclude anuric patients and record catheter status as a note.; Small numbers of stage 2–3 AKI → report all stages together and stage 2–3 separately.; The bundle field gets ticked without a real result → count a pass only if analyte values are recorded.
- Pearls: Recruit the AKI specialist nurse or outreach team; they already review alerts daily.; Ask the ward managers to stock dipsticks and pots at the nurses' station on each medical ward.; Pair the change with a one-line rule on the AKI alert: 'Dip the urine now'.; Hand the dashboard to the renal link nurse so it survives the rotation.

**NNA-097. What proportion of adults with cirrhosis who present with upper GI bleeding receive intravenous antibiotics before endoscopy?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG50 rec 1.9.1 (2016, updated 2023); NICE CG141 rec 1.5.2: "NG50 1.9.1: Offer prophylactic intravenous antibiotics for people with cirrhosis who have upper gastrointestinal bleeding. [2016] CG141 1.5.2: Offer prophylactic antibiotic therapy at presentation to patients with suspected or confirmed variceal bleeding." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng50/chapter/Managing-complications
- Pass: First IV antibiotic dose administered before the start of endoscopy (or within 6 hours of arrival if endoscopy was later).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive patients over the last 6–12 months (cirrhotic bleeds are less common; use a longer window).
- Change: Add IV antibiotic (per local microbiology choice) and terlipressin as pre-ticked items in a 'cirrhosis with GI bleed' EPR order set, triggered from the Blatchford/GI bleed pathway.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-097.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few cases → extend the retrospective window and consider a regional collaboration.; Antibiotic given but not charted in ED → check the ED drugs record and nursing notes.; Cirrhosis not known at arrival → record whether cirrhosis was known and report both groups.; Order set ignored at night → ask the ED lead to include it in the GI bleed triage card.
- Pearls: Recruit the hepatology nurse specialist to identify cases.; Use the BSG decompensated cirrhosis bundle as the vehicle, since many trusts already have it.; Agree the antibiotic choice with microbiology first so the order set is unarguable.; Report infection rate alongside compliance.

**NNA-098. What proportion of adults with cirrhosis and ascites on long-term antibiotic prophylaxis for spontaneous bacterial peritonitis meet a NICE indication for it?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG50 recs 1.8.1–1.8.2 (2016, updated 2023): "1.8.1 Do not routinely offer antibiotics to prevent spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) in people with cirrhosis and ascites. [2023] 1.8.2 Consider antibiotics to prevent SBP only if: the person is at high risk of developing SBP because they have severe liver disease (for example, they have an ascitic protein of 15 g per litre or less, a Child–Pugh score of more than 9, or a MELD score of more than 16) or the consequences of an infection could seriously impact the person's care, for example, if it could affect their wait for a transplant or a transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic stent insertion (TIPS). [2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng50/chapter/Managing-complications
- Pass: Patient on SBP prophylaxis has a documented qualifying reason (ascitic protein ≤15 g/L, Child–Pugh >9, MELD >16, transplant or TIPS work-up, or previous SBP as secondary prophylaxis).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients on SBP prophylaxis (discharge prescriptions or hepatology clinic lists) over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add an indication field (drop-down of the NICE criteria plus 'secondary prophylaxis') to the SBP prophylaxis prescription in e-prescribing or the hepatology discharge template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-098.csv`
- Pitfalls: Previous SBP (secondary prophylaxis) wrongly failed → record it and count it as indicated.; Child–Pugh or MELD not calculable from records → collect raw values and calculate centrally.; Hepatologists disagree with the 2023 change → agree the standard at the MDT before cycle 1.; Stopping prophylaxis worries GPs → include a line in the clinic letter explaining the change.
- Pearls: Recruit a hepatology pharmacist; they can pull all ciprofloxacin/co-trimoxazole repeat prescriptions for liver patients.; Report fluoroquinolone exposure avoided as a stewardship win.; Run the audit from clinic lists so you can act at the next appointment.; Add a stop date or review date to every prophylaxis prescription.

**NNA-099. What proportion of adults started on anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation in hospital have an ORBIT bleeding risk score documented?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG196 rec 1.2.2 (2021, updated June 2021): "Assess the risk of bleeding when: considering starting anticoagulation in people with atrial fibrillation and reviewing people already taking anticoagulation. Use the ORBIT bleeding risk score because evidence shows that it has a higher accuracy in predicting absolute bleeding risk than other bleeding risk tools." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng196/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: ORBIT score (number or category) documented in the notes, EPR or discharge summary before or on the day anticoagulation was first prescribed.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive new AF anticoagulation starts over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add an ORBIT calculator (auto-filling haemoglobin, age and eGFR) to the EPR AF/anticoagulation initiation template, replacing HAS-BLED.
