- Pitfalls: Consultant review not time-stamped → the template field fixes this; in cycle 1 use the note time and mark 'untimed' separately.; Night admissions reviewed at a morning ward round after 14 hours → analyse by admission hour to support rota discussions.; Registrar reviews on behalf of consultants → only count staff on the consultant rota, as RCPCH defines.; Short-stay discharges by registrars → record separately (standard 4 covers them).
- Pearls: Involve the clinical director early; the fix may be rota change, not documentation.; Include weekends in both cycles.; Plot hours-to-review by admission hour.; Share results with the RCPCH regional lead as part of Facing the Future benchmarking.

## Obstetrics, gynaecology and sexual health

**NNA-193. Of pregnant women with at least one high-risk factor for pre-eclampsia, what proportion were advised to take aspirin 75–150 mg daily, with a prescription issued, by 16+0 weeks?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG133 rec 1.1.2 (2019, updated 2023): "Advise pregnant women at high risk of pre-eclampsia to take 75 mg to 150 mg of aspirin daily from 12 weeks until the birth of the baby. Women at high risk are those with any of the following: hypertensive disease during a previous pregnancy; chronic kidney disease; autoimmune disease such as systemic lupus erythematosus or antiphospholipid syndrome; type 1 or type 2 diabetes; chronic hypertension." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng133/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: High-risk factor recorded, aspirin advice documented and a prescription (hospital or GP) for 75–150 mg daily issued by 16+0 weeks, or a documented contraindication or decline.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible women who reached 20 weeks in the last 6 months
- Change: Make the aspirin decision a mandatory field in the electronic booking risk assessment, which triggers a prescription task when a high-risk factor is ticked.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-193.csv`
- Pitfalls: Aspirin issued by the GP is invisible in hospital notes → agree access to the shared care record or GP summary before cycle 1.; Moderate-risk women muddy the denominator → audit high-risk women only; run moderate risk as a second cycle.; Staff tick the field but no prescription follows → count a prescription, not a tick, as a pass.; Some units prescribe 150 mg, others 75 mg → accept 75–150 mg; record the dose to show variation.
- Pearls: Recruit the digital midwife early; the EPR change is the fix.; Agree the 16-week cut-off with the lead obstetrician before cycle 1 and keep it for cycle 2.; Ask community pharmacy leads to confirm aspirin cannot be bought over the counter for this use, and put this in the leaflet.; Hand over the dashboard metric to the antenatal clinic matron before rotation.

**NNA-194. Of women who took methyldopa in pregnancy, what proportion had it stopped within 2 days after the birth?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG133 rec 1.4.12 (2019, updated 2023): "If a woman has taken methyldopa to treat gestational hypertension, stop within 2 days after the birth and change to an alternative treatment if necessary (for choice of antihypertensive during the postnatal period, see the section on antihypertensive treatment during the postnatal period, including during breastfeeding)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng133/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Last methyldopa dose given no later than 48 hours after the time of birth (recs 1.3.19 and 1.5.18 give the same advice for chronic hypertension and pre-eclampsia).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive women over the last 6–12 months
- Change: Add a postnatal hypertension order set to e-prescribing that ends methyldopa at 48 hours and offers enalapril or nifedipine.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-194.csv`
- Pitfalls: Numbers fall as fewer units start methyldopa → extend the look-back to 12 months if needed.; Methyldopa still on the discharge letter even when stopped on the ward → record both; the letter drives GP repeats.; Physicians object to switching when blood pressure is stable → the order set allows a documented reason to override.; Paper charts in some areas → check both systems during the pilot week.
- Pearls: Ask the maternity pharmacist to run the list of methyldopa administrations; it saves hours.; Measure hours from birth, not calendar days, and fix the definition before cycle 1.; Link the change to the postnatal depression screen so midwives see the point.; Give the order set a named owner in pharmacy so it survives rotation.

**NNA-195. Of women who reached 36 weeks, what proportion had a carbon monoxide reading and smoking status recorded at the 36-week appointment?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Saving Babies' Lives care bundle v3, element 1, intervention 1.1 and 1.3 (NHS England, 2023): "1.1 CO testing offered to all pregnant women at the antenatal booking and 36-week antenatal appointment. 1.3 Whenever CO testing is offered, it should be followed up with an enquiry about smoking status with the CO result and smoking status recorded." https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/saving-babies-lives-version-3/
- Pass: CO reading in ppm and smoking status both recorded at the 36-week contact (35+0 to 37+6 weeks), or a documented decline.. Target: ≥95% (bundle ambition 1.14b). Sample: 50 consecutive women with a 36-week appointment in the last 2 months
- Change: Make CO reading and smoking status mandatory fields on the 36-week contact template in the EPR, with an auto-referral when CO is 4 ppm or more.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-195.csv`
- Pitfalls: CO monitors missing in community bags → check monitor stock and calibration with the team lead before cycle 1.; 36-week contacts done by phone → record setting; exclude remote contacts from the denominator only if the template allows no CO.; Mandatory field filled with '0' to get past it → spot-check 10 records against the monitor log.; Gains fade after the audit → put the rate on the monthly dashboard.
- Pearls: Recruit a community midwife champion; most 36-week contacts are in the community.; Agree the 35+0 to 37+6 window before cycle 1.; Pair the audit with the tobacco dependence adviser so positive readings reach treatment.; Use the same EPR report for both cycles.

**NNA-196. Of babies born at 22+0 to 33+6 weeks, what proportion had a full course of antenatal corticosteroids completed within the 7 days before birth?** (new)
- Standard: Saving Babies' Lives care bundle v3, element 5, intervention 5.19 (NHS England, 2023): "Antenatal corticosteroids should be offered to women who birth a baby between 22 and 33 weeks' (22+0 to 33+6) gestation within 1 week prior to delivery. A steroid-to-birth interval of >7 days should be avoided if possible and repeat courses of steroids should be avoided where possible." https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/saving-babies-lives-version-3/
- Pass: Second steroid dose given 0 to 7 days before birth. Record a partial course and >7 days separately.. Target: ≥80% (set with the Local Maternity and Neonatal System). Sample: All eligible births over the last 12 months (typically 30–50 in a district general hospital)
- Change: Add a preterm birth risk tool (for example, QUiPP) to the triage pathway so steroids are given when birth within 7 days is likely.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-196.csv`
- Pitfalls: Numbers are small → use 12 months per cycle and report counts, not only percentages.; Steroids given at another unit before transfer → check transfer letters.; Clinicians object that some births cannot be predicted → report 'no opportunity' cases separately.; Definitions drift between cycles → fix 'full course' as two doses 24 hours apart.
- Pearls: Pull the list from the neonatal dataset, which already records steroid status.; Recruit a neonatal registrar; steroid timing matters to them too.; Present each case as a short timeline; it drives change better than a rate.; Combine with the magnesium audit to share one data pull.

**NNA-197. Of babies born before 30+0 weeks, what proportion had magnesium sulfate given to the mother in the 24 hours before birth?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG25 rec 1.10.2 (2015, updated 2022): "Offer intravenous magnesium sulfate for neuroprotection of the baby to women between 24+0 and 29+6 weeks of pregnancy who are: in established preterm labour, or having a planned preterm birth within 24 hours." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng25/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Intravenous magnesium sulfate started within the 24 hours before birth, or a documented reason (for example, birth too rapid, maternal contraindication).. Target: ≥85% (local target; reported as Saving Babies' Lives v3 process indicator 5c). Sample: All eligible births over the last 12–24 months
- Change: Put a pre-made magnesium sulfate neuroprotection pack and prescription in the labour ward preterm box, triggered by a triage prompt for gestation under 30 weeks.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-197.csv`
- Pitfalls: Very small numbers → audit 12–24 months and present each missed case.; Infusion started but not charted → check the infusion chart and anaesthetic record.; Precipitate births skew the rate → record 'no opportunity' cases separately.; Toxicity worries → include the monitoring chart in the pack.
- Pearls: Neonatal data already hold this field; ask the neonatal data clerk.; Recruit a labour ward coordinator to own the preterm box.; Agree how to count births within 1 hour of arrival before cycle 1.; Use a simulation drill to launch the box.

**NNA-198. Of babies born before 37+0 weeks, what proportion had cord clamping delayed for at least 60 seconds?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG25 rec 1.14.1 (2015, amended 2022): "Wait at least 60 seconds before clamping the cord of preterm babies unless there are specific maternal or fetal conditions that need earlier clamping." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng25/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Time of cord clamping recorded and at least 60 seconds after birth, or a documented reason for earlier clamping.. Target: ≥80% of eligible births (local target). Sample: 40 consecutive preterm births over the last 3–4 months
- Change: Add a mandatory 'time of cord clamping (seconds)' field to the birth record and the caesarean theatre record, with a timer prompt.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-198.csv`
- Pitfalls: Timing not recorded → count missing time as a fail and fix the form first.; Neonatal team worry about delaying resuscitation → include clamping with the cord intact or resuscitation at the bedside as passes where available.; Late preterm births outnumber very preterm → report by gestation band.; Caesarean births recorded in a separate system → check both.
- Pearls: Recruit a neonatal consultant and an anaesthetist; clamping happens between teams.; Agree what counts as 'documented reason' before cycle 1.; Use the theatre team brief to say 'deferred clamping planned' out loud.; Report monthly to keep the gain.

**NNA-199. Of women with preterm prelabour rupture of membranes, what proportion were prescribed oral erythromycin 250 mg four times a day for no more than 10 days?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG25 rec 1.4.1 (2015, amended 2022): "As prophylaxis for intrauterine infection, offer women with P-PROM oral erythromycin 250 mg 4 times a day for a maximum of 10 days or until the woman is in established labour (whichever is sooner)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng25/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Erythromycin 250 mg four times a day (or an oral penicillin if erythromycin is not tolerated) started, and stopped by day 10 or established labour; no co-amoxiclav.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive women over the last 6–12 months
- Change: Add a preterm prelabour rupture of membranes order set to e-prescribing with erythromycin preset at 250 mg four times a day and an automatic 10-day stop date.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-199.csv`
- Pitfalls: Diagnosis uncertain (no pooling, test used) → include only women managed as preterm rupture of membranes by the team.; Antibiotics continued at home without a stop date → check the discharge prescription.; Stewardship team may prefer another agent locally → agree the standard with them before cycle 1.; Small numbers → extend to 12 months.
- Pearls: Recruit the maternity pharmacist and microbiologist early.; Use a 10-day auto-stop, not a reminder.; Count co-amoxiclav separately; it is the headline safety issue.; Give the order set to the triage lead to own.

**NNA-200. Of women with group B streptococcus found in the current pregnancy who laboured, what proportion received intravenous antibiotics during labour?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG195 rec 1.6.1 (2021, updated 2026): "Offer antibiotics during labour to women, trans men and non-binary people who: are in pre-term labour or have group B streptococcal colonisation, bacteriuria or infection during the current pregnancy or have had group B streptococcal colonisation, bacteriuria or infection in a previous pregnancy, and have not had a negative test for group B streptococcus by enrichment culture or polymerase chain reaction (PCR) on a rectovaginal swab samples collected between 35 and 37 weeks' gestation or 3 to 5 weeks before the anticipated delivery date in the current pregnancy or have had a previous baby with an invasive group B streptococcal infection or have a clinical diagnosis of chorioamnionitis." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng195/chapter/Prevention-and-risk-reduction
- Pass: First dose of intravenous benzylpenicillin (or the allergy alternative in NG195 table 1) given in labour. Record whether it was 4 hours or more before birth.. Target: ≥95% given; report the ≥4-hour rate. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible women over the last 6 months
- Change: Link positive group B streptococcus results to an automatic alert on the maternity EPR front sheet and a pre-written intrapartum antibiotic prescription.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-200.csv`
- Pitfalls: Results reported after booking never reach the notes → check the lab system against the EPR alert for every case.; Rapid births give no chance → report 'birth within 1 hour of arrival' separately.; Penicillin allergy labels vary → record the agent used.; Home-birth women with GBS → include only if they laboured in hospital.
- Pearls: Ask microbiology for a list of positive antenatal results; it is the easiest denominator.; Agree the alert wording with the digital midwife.; Show neonatal colleagues the babies who needed 36 hours of observation because antibiotics were missed.; Hand the monthly report to the labour ward manager.

**NNA-201. Of women having a caesarean birth, what proportion received prophylactic antibiotics before skin incision?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG192 rec 1.4.43 (2021, updated 2025): "Offer women prophylactic antibiotics before skin incision for caesarean birth, choosing antibiotics that are effective against endometritis, urinary tract and wound infections." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng192/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Antibiotic administration time recorded and before the skin incision time; not co-amoxiclav (rec 1.4.45).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive caesarean births over the last 1 month
- Change: Add 'antibiotic given before incision? time' to the obstetric WHO safety checklist time-out.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-201.csv`
- Pitfalls: Antibiotic time and incision time on different clocks → use the theatre system time for both where possible.; Category 1 births make timing hard → report them apart; still count them.; Antibiotic given but not charted → check the anaesthetic chart and drug record.; Anaesthetists see this as their domain → run the audit jointly with the obstetric anaesthesia lead.
- Pearls: Recruit an obstetric anaesthetist as co-lead.; Agree the data source for incision time before cycle 1.; Keep the checklist line short so it gets said aloud.; Feed results into the surgical site infection surveillance programme.

**NNA-202. Of category 1 caesarean births, what proportion had the baby born within 30 minutes of the decision?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG192 rec 1.4.3 (2021, updated 2025): "Perform category 1 caesarean birth as soon as possible, and in most situations within 30 minutes of making the decision." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng192/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Decision time and birth time both recorded, and birth within 30 minutes of the decision.. Target: ≥90% within 30 minutes; 100% with decision time recorded. Sample: All category 1 caesarean births over the last 6 months (typically 30–50)
- Change: Introduce a category 1 caesarean call sheet that records the decision time at the moment of the call, held by the coordinator.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-202.csv`
- Pitfalls: Decision time written from memory later → take it from the call sheet or emergency bleep time.; Staff feel blamed → present delays as system causes (theatre busy, anaesthetist elsewhere).; Category misclassified (category 2 labelled 1) → record the indication and check against the NICE definition.; Small numbers → pool 6 months.
- Pearls: Recruit the labour ward coordinator; they hold the times.; Agree 'decision time' is the time the obstetrician states the category aloud.; Report the median and the longest times, not only the pass rate.; Link cases to neonatal outcome for context.

**NNA-203. Of women having a caesarean birth, what proportion received carbetocin by slow intravenous injection to prevent postpartum haemorrhage?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG235 rec 1.10.13 (2023, updated 2026): "For women who have had a caesarean birth, offer carbetocin by slow intravenous injection for the prevention of postpartum haemorrhage." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng235/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Carbetocin given by slow intravenous injection at birth, or a documented reason for another uterotonic.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive caesarean births over the last 1 month
- Change: Replace oxytocin with carbetocin in the caesarean drug tray and the anaesthetic chart pre-print.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-203.csv`
- Pitfalls: Carbetocin not on formulary or in stock → confirm with pharmacy before cycle 1.; Cost objection → show NICE's cost-effectiveness rationale and the extra oxytocin infusions avoided.; Pre-eclampsia or cardiac disease need care → accept a documented reason as a pass.; Uterotonic recorded only as 'given' → count missing drug name as a fail.
- Pearls: Recruit the lead obstetric anaesthetist and the theatre pharmacist.; Change the tray, not only the guideline.; Record blood loss to show safety.; Keep the pre-printed chart line after rotation.

**NNA-204. Of women who had regional analgesia or anaesthesia for birth, what proportion had a straight leg raise checked and recorded by 4 hours after the last dose?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG235 rec 1.12.2 (2023, updated 2026): "Check that women who have had regional analgesia or anaesthesia can perform a straight leg raise by 4 hours after the last anaesthetic dose. If not, contact the obstetric anaesthetist for urgent review." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng235/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Straight leg raise result recorded at or before 4 hours after the last epidural or spinal dose; if absent, anaesthetist contacted.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive women over the last 1 month
- Change: Add a timed 'straight leg raise at 4 hours' box to the postnatal observation chart, prefilled with the due time at handover from theatre or labour ward.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-204.csv`
- Pitfalls: Last dose time unclear with continuous epidurals → use the time the infusion or top-up stopped.; Checked but not written → the chart box fixes this; spot-check.; Women transferred to the ward before 4 hours → the due time must move with the handover.; Night shifts miss checks → report results by time of day.
- Pearls: Recruit a postnatal ward midwife champion and the obstetric anaesthesia lead.; Agree 'last dose' definition before cycle 1.; Put the due time on the handover sheet, not only the chart.; Audit a small monthly sample to keep the gain.

**NNA-205. Of women who had a first- or second-degree tear repaired, what proportion were offered a rectal NSAID after the repair?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG235 rec 1.12.22 (2023, updated 2026; recommendation dated 2007): "Offer rectal non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs routinely after perineal repair of first- and second-degree trauma provided these drugs are not contraindicated." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng235/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Rectal NSAID (for example, diclofenac 100 mg) given or offered and declined after repair, or a documented contraindication.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive women over the last 1 month
- Change: Add 'rectal NSAID offered/given/contraindicated' as a required line in the perineal repair record, linked to a PGD for midwives.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-205.csv`
- Pitfalls: No PGD, so midwives wait for a doctor → set up the PGD before cycle 2.; Contraindications not recorded → the required line includes a 'contraindicated' option.; Pre-eclampsia and haemorrhage cases → count as passes only if documented.; Birth-centre births recorded on paper → include them.
- Pearls: Recruit a senior midwife from the birth centre and labour ward.; Ask pharmacy to prepare the PGD early; it takes weeks.; Ask women about pain at the first postnatal check to show impact.; Keep the required line after rotation.

**NNA-206. Of women who had a third- or fourth-degree tear repaired, what proportion were prescribed a laxative after the repair?** (new)
- Standard: RCOG Green-top Guideline No. 29, section 10.1 (2015): "The use of postoperative laxatives is recommended to reduce the risk of wound dehiscence." https://www.rcog.org.uk/media/5jeb5hzu/gtg-29.pdf
- Pass: Laxative (for example, lactulose) prescribed after the repair and included on the discharge prescription; no bulking agent alone.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All cases over the last 12 months (typically 30–50)
- Change: Create an obstetric anal sphincter injury order set with antibiotics and lactulose preset, including a 10-day discharge supply.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-206.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers → take 12 months.; Laxative on the ward chart but not on discharge → check both.; Grade recorded differently in theatre and birth records → use the operation note.; Local protocol differs on agent → accept any laxative; record which.
- Pearls: Recruit the perineal specialist midwife; they hold a case list.; Build the order set with the maternity pharmacist.; Add the follow-up clinic booking to the same order set.; Report with the antibiotic rate for a fuller picture.

**NNA-207. Of women transferred home after birth, what proportion had a face-to-face midwife visit within 36 hours of transfer?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG194 rec 1.1.14 (2021, updated 2026): "Ensure that the first postnatal visit by a midwife takes place within 36 hours after transfer of care from the place of birth or after a home birth. The visit should be face-to-face and usually at the woman's home, depending on her circumstances and preferences." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng194/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First face-to-face midwife contact recorded within 36 hours of discharge time (or of a home birth).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive women over the last 1 month
- Change: Send an automatic electronic referral to the community team at discharge, with the '36-hour due' time shown on their daily worklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-207.csv`
- Pitfalls: Telephone contacts counted as visits → count face-to-face only.; Weekend discharges drive delay → report by discharge day.; Discharge time not recorded → use the EPR discharge timestamp.; Staffing pressure blamed → show the delay pattern to support a rota change.
- Pearls: Recruit the community matron.; Agree the clock start (discharge timestamp) before cycle 1.; Break results down by locality and day of week.; Keep the worklist field after the audit.

**NNA-208. Of women with gestational diabetes whose glucose returned to normal after birth, what proportion had a fasting plasma glucose or HbA1c test 6 to 13 weeks after the birth?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG3 rec 1.6.11 (2015, updated 2020): "For women who were diagnosed with gestational diabetes and whose blood glucose levels returned to normal after the birth: offer lifestyle advice (including weight control, diet and exercise); offer a fasting plasma glucose test 6 to 13 weeks after the birth to exclude diabetes (for practical reasons this might take place at the 6‑week postnatal check); after 13 weeks offer a fasting plasma glucose test if this has not been done earlier, or an HbA1c test if a fasting plasma glucose test is not possible; do not routinely offer a 75-g 2‑hour OGTT." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng3/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Fasting plasma glucose done 6–13 weeks after birth (or HbA1c after 13 weeks if fasting test not possible, within 6 months); no routine OGTT.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive women who gave birth 4–9 months ago
- Change: Add a standard paragraph to the maternity discharge letter asking the GP to arrange a fasting glucose at 6–13 weeks, with the due date filled in automatically.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-208.csv`
- Pitfalls: Tests done in primary care not visible → confirm the pathology system shows GP requests before cycle 1.; Letters change but tests do not follow → measure both the letter and the test.; Re-audit too soon to see tests → re-audit letters at week 13, tests at 6 months.; OGTT still used by some practices → record it; it is a fail against the standard.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes specialist midwife and a GP lead.; Pull the cohort from the diabetes clinic list.; Put the due date in the letter, not only 'at 6 weeks'.; Link to the NHS Diabetes Prevention Programme referral.

**NNA-209. Of women treated medically for missed miscarriage, what proportion received 200 mg oral mifepristone followed 48 hours later by 800 micrograms misoprostol?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG126 rec 1.11.1 (2019, updated 2026): "For the medical management of missed miscarriage offer: 200 mg oral mifepristone and 48 hours later, 800 micrograms misoprostol (vaginal, oral or sublingual) unless the gestational sac has already been passed." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng126/chapter/Management-of-miscarriage
- Pass: Mifepristone 200 mg given, then misoprostol 800 micrograms about 48 hours later (unless the sac passed first); no mifepristone for incomplete miscarriage.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive women over the last 3–4 months
- Change: Replace the early pregnancy unit's medical management prescription with a pre-printed mifepristone-then-misoprostol pack and a take-home pregnancy test.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-209.csv`
- Pitfalls: Mifepristone not stocked in the unit → confirm supply with pharmacy before cycle 1.; Two visits needed → offer home misoprostol where local policy allows.; Incomplete and missed miscarriage mixed → use the scan report to classify.; Surgical rates are the real outcome → record them for context.
- Pearls: Recruit the early pregnancy unit lead nurse.; Ask pharmacy for a monthly issue list of mifepristone.; Include the 3-week pregnancy test in the pack (rec 1.11.7).; Keep the pack after rotation by naming an owner.

**NNA-210. Of RhD-negative women managed for miscarriage or ectopic pregnancy at up to 11+6 weeks, what proportion were correctly not given anti-D immunoglobulin?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG126 rec 1.18.1 (2019, updated 2026): "Do not offer anti-D immunoglobulin prophylaxis as a treatment for ectopic pregnancy or miscarriage to women, trans men and non-binary people for an ectopic pregnancy, miscarriage or threatened miscarriage up to and including 11+6 weeks' gestation. If there is a discrepancy between length of gestation as measured from ultrasound and that calculated from last menstrual period, use the findings from ultrasound to guide management." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng126/chapter/Anti-D-immunoglobulin-prophylaxis
- Pass: No anti-D given at up to 11+6 weeks by scan. For a comparison group at 12+0 to 12+6 weeks having medical or surgical management, anti-D at least 250 IU given (rec 1.18.2).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive RhD-negative women over the last 6 months (after the 2026 update)
- Change: Update the early pregnancy unit anti-D flowchart and blood bank request form with the 11+6 week cut-off.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-210.csv`
- Pitfalls: Gestation by dates used instead of scan → take gestation from the scan report.; Older guideline copies in circulation → remove them from the unit and intranet.; Blood bank issues anti-D on request without checking → involve the transfusion practitioner.; Small numbers of RhD-negative women → take 6 months.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion practitioner; the blood bank log is the fastest data source.; Audit only cases after the 2026 update date.; Show the number of unnecessary doses avoided.; Update the patient leaflet at the same time.

**NNA-211. Of women with bleeding in early pregnancy, a scan-confirmed intrauterine pregnancy and a previous miscarriage, what proportion were offered vaginal micronised progesterone 400 mg twice daily?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG126 rec 1.9.2 (2021, updated 2026): "Offer vaginal micronised progesterone 400 mg twice daily to women with an intrauterine pregnancy confirmed by a scan, if they have vaginal bleeding and have previously had a miscarriage." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng126/chapter/Management-of-miscarriage
- Pass: Progesterone 400 mg vaginal twice daily prescribed or offered and declined, with a plan to continue to 16 weeks if a heartbeat is seen (rec 1.9.3).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible women over the last 3 months
- Change: Add a 'previous miscarriage? → offer progesterone' prompt to the early pregnancy unit assessment template, with a standard GP letter for continuation to 16 weeks.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-211.csv`
- Pitfalls: Previous miscarriage history not asked → the template prompt makes it a required question.; GPs decline to continue prescribing → agree shared care with the ICB before cycle 2.; Wrong preparation or dose → record the product used.; Started before intrauterine location confirmed → count as a fail.
- Pearls: Recruit the early pregnancy unit lead and the ICB pharmacist.; Agree the 'eligible' definition with the scan report wording.; Include the continuation plan in every GP letter.; Keep the prompt in the template after rotation.

**NNA-212. Of women referred to gynaecology with heavy menstrual bleeding, what proportion had a full blood count done?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG88 rec 1.2.6 (2018, updated 2026): "Carry out a full blood count test for all women with HMB, in parallel with any HMB treatment offered." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng88/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Full blood count result available within 3 months before or at the first gynaecology appointment; if anaemic, iron treatment documented.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive new referrals over the last 2 months
- Change: Add 'FBC done? Hb? iron started?' fields to the heavy menstrual bleeding clinic template, with a pre-clinic blood test order for referrals without a recent result.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-212.csv`
- Pitfalls: Results from primary care not visible → confirm the pathology system shows GP requests.; Test done but anaemia not treated → the template asks about iron too.; Advice-and-guidance referrals missed → include them if seen in clinic.; Unneeded hormone tests continue → record them as a secondary measure.
- Pearls: Recruit the gynaecology outpatient lead and a GP referral lead.; Agree the 3-month window before cycle 1.; Offer iron at the first visit to show benefit to patients.; Keep the template field after rotation.

**NNA-213. Of women under 40 diagnosed with premature ovarian insufficiency, what proportion had a raised FSH on 2 blood samples taken 4 to 6 weeks apart?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG23 recs 1.7.2–1.7.3 (2015, updated 2026): "Diagnose premature ovarian insufficiency in women, trans men and non-binary people registered female at birth who are under 40 based on: menopause-associated symptoms, including no or infrequent periods (taking into account whether the person has had a hysterectomy) and elevated follicle stimulating hormone (FSH) levels on 2 blood samples taken 4 to 6 weeks apart. Do not diagnose premature ovarian insufficiency on the basis of a single blood test." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng23/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Two raised FSH results 4–6 weeks apart before the diagnosis, and hormone treatment (HRT or combined hormonal contraception) offered unless contraindicated (rec 1.7.6).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All new diagnoses over the last 24 months (typically 30–40)
- Change: Add a premature ovarian insufficiency diagnosis checklist to the clinic letter template (2 FSH dates, gap in weeks, treatment offered).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-213.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few cases → search 24 months of coding and letters.; Coding is poor → also search letters for 'premature ovarian insufficiency' and 'premature menopause'.; Second FSH done by the GP → check the pathology system for GP requests.; AMH used as a shortcut → record it as a secondary measure.
- Pearls: Recruit the menopause or reproductive medicine lead.; Ask the lab for a list of patients under 40 with raised FSH to cross-check.; Put the checklist in the letter template, not a separate form.; Pair with a bone health check as a later cycle.

**NNA-214. Of women referred to gynaecology with suspected endometriosis, what proportion had a transvaginal ultrasound scan offered before or at the first appointment?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG73 rec 1.5.2 (2017, updated 2024): "Offer a transvaginal ultrasound scan to all women or people with suspected endometriosis, even if pelvic or abdominal examination is normal, to: identify ovarian endometriomas and deep endometriosis, including that involving the bowel, bladder or ureter; identify or rule out other pathology which may be causing symptoms; guide management options and enable referral to an appropriate service, depending on the ultrasound findings." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng73/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Transvaginal scan done or requested by the first gynaecology appointment, or offered and declined with a transabdominal scan considered (rec 1.5.3).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new referrals over the last 3 months
- Change: Add 'transvaginal ultrasound' to the endometriosis referral form so it is requested at triage, before the first clinic.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-214.csv`
- Pitfalls: Scan waiting lists are long → report time to scan as well as whether it was done.; Women who have never had sex may decline transvaginal scan → accept an offered-and-declined record.; Radiology capacity objection → agree the extra demand with radiology before cycle 2.; Mixed referral reasons → define 'suspected endometriosis' by referral wording.
- Pearls: Recruit a sonographer and the gynaecology triage consultant.; Agree the referral form change with primary care leads.; Show the number of endometriomas found to justify capacity.; Keep the triage rule written in the referral guidance.

**NNA-215. Of people attending for emergency contraception, what proportion were offered a copper intrauterine device?** (new)
- Standard: FSRH Clinical Guideline: Emergency Contraception (March 2017, amended July 2023): "All women requiring EC should be offered a Cu-IUD if appropriate as it is the most effective method of EC." https://www.cosrh.org/Common/Uploaded%20files/documents/fsrh-guideline-emergency-contraception03dec2020-amendedjuly2023-11jul.pdf
- Pass: Documented offer of a copper IUD (fitted, declined or referred), or a documented reason it was not appropriate.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive emergency contraception attendances over the last 1 month
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Cu-IUD offered? outcome' field to the emergency contraception template in the sexual health EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-215.csv`
- Pitfalls: No same-day fitter → record referrals and time to fitting; fix access alongside.; 'Offered' ticked without discussion → spot-check free-text notes.; Pharmacy and online EC outside the audit → state the setting clearly.; Fitting rates stay low even when offered → report offer and uptake separately.
- Pearls: Recruit a senior nurse fitter to hold emergency coil slots.; Agree what counts as 'not appropriate' before cycle 1.; Offer a leaflet comparing EC methods.; Keep the field after the audit.

**NNA-216. Of people diagnosed with gonorrhoea, what proportion had a culture taken for antibiotic susceptibility before treatment?** (under-audited)
- Standard: BASHH UK national guideline for the management of infection with Neisseria gonorrhoeae (2025), auditable outcome measures: "All individuals with gonorrhoea should have cultures taken for susceptibility testing prior to treatment (performance standard 97%)." https://www.bashh.org/_userfiles/pages/files/fiferetal2025britishassociationofsexualhealthandhivuknationalguidelineforthemanagementofinfection_1.pdf
- Pass: Culture swab from each positive site taken before the first antibiotic dose.. Target: ≥97%. Sample: 50 consecutive new diagnoses over the last 1–2 months
- Change: Add a 'culture taken from all positive sites?' hard stop to the gonorrhoea treatment template before ceftriaxone can be recorded.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-216.csv`
- Pitfalls: Recall visits after NAAT results often skip culture → the hard stop is at the treatment step.; Transport delays make cultures fail → record lab failures separately.; Pharyngeal sites missed → count each positive site.; Nurses and doctors use different templates → change both.
- Pearls: Recruit the lead health adviser and the microbiologist.; Check culture positivity rates too; a taken swab that never grows is a lab issue.; Agree the site-by-site pass rule before cycle 1.; Link to test-of-cure decisions under the 2025 guideline.

**NNA-217. Of people treated for confirmed syphilis, what proportion had an RPR titre recorded 6 months after treatment?** (new)
- Standard: BASHH UK national guidelines on the management of syphilis (2024), auditable outcomes: "The percentage of confirmed syphilis cases having a record of an RPR titre obtained 6 months post-treatment (standard 65% confirmed syphilis cases)." https://www.bashh.org/_userfiles/pages/files/syphilis_2024.pdf
- Pass: RPR titre recorded at treatment and again 5 to 7 months after treatment.. Target: ≥65% (BASHH standard); local stretch ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive cases treated 7–12 months ago
- Change: Set automatic recall text messages at 3, 6 and 12 months when syphilis treatment is recorded in the EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-217.csv`
- Pitfalls: Follow-up takes 6 months, so re-audit must wait → plan the re-audit date at the start.; People tested elsewhere → check shared lab results.; Recall numbers out of date → confirm contact details at treatment.; People living with HIV followed in HIV clinic → include HIV clinic results.
- Pearls: Recruit the lead health adviser; they run recalls.; Record the treatment titre too; without it the 6-month result means little.; Use the same look-back window in both cycles.; Keep the automatic recall owned by the EPR administrator.

**NNA-218. Of people prescribed doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis, what proportion had their eligibility for HIV PrEP assessed and documented?** (new)
- Standard: BASHH UK national guideline for the use of doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis (2025), auditable outcome measures: "All people prescribed doxyPEP should be assessed for eligibility for HIV PrEP (performance standard 97%)" https://www.bashh.org/_userfiles/pages/files/saundersetal2025britishassociationforsexualhealthandhivbashhuknationalguidelinefortheuseof.pdf
- Pass: HIV PrEP eligibility recorded (already on PrEP, living with HIV, eligible and offered, or not eligible) at the doxycycline prescribing visit.. Target: ≥97%. Sample: 50 consecutive new prescriptions over the last 2–3 months
- Change: Build a doxycycline prophylaxis prescribing template with required fields for PrEP status, STI tests, vaccines and information.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-218.csv`
- Pitfalls: Prescribing rules still settling locally → agree the local doxyPEP protocol before cycle 1.; Online and postal prescriptions use another system → include both routes.; People living with HIV counted as fails → mark them as 'not applicable, pass'.; Template fatigue → keep fields to single clicks.
- Pearls: Recruit the PrEP lead nurse.; Audit the other BASHH measures from the same records for little extra effort.; Agree definitions with the health adviser team.; Keep the template owned by the service manager.

**NNA-219. Of HIV post-exposure prophylaxis starter packs given in the emergency department, what proportion fitted within the recommended or consider indications?** (new)
- Standard: BASHH UK guideline for the use of HIV post-exposure prophylaxis (2021, amended 2023), auditable outcome measure 3: "Proportion of PEP prescriptions that fit within recommended indications (consider or recommended): aim 90%" https://www.bashh.org/_userfiles/pages/files/resources/pep2021_2023amendment.pdf
- Pass: Exposure type and source risk recorded, and the prescription matches the guideline table as 'recommended' or 'consider'.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive starter packs over the last 6–12 months
- Change: Replace free-text assessment with an emergency department post-exposure prophylaxis proforma that uses the BASHH risk table and prints the follow-up referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-219.csv`
- Pitfalls: Source HIV status unknown and poorly recorded → the proforma asks for source group.; Starter packs issued without notes → cross-check the pharmacy issue log.; ED staff see it as a sexual health task → run it jointly with the sexual health lead.; Follow-up loss → record the referral as a secondary measure.
- Pearls: Recruit an emergency medicine consultant and a sexual health consultant.; Use the pharmacy log to find every starter pack.; Agree what counts as 'consider' before cycle 1.; Keep the proforma linked to the sexual health referral.

## Diagnostics, prescribing and general practice

**NNA-220. What proportion of red cell transfusions in adults without active bleeding are given as a single unit followed by a clinical reassessment and haemoglobin check before any further unit?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 recs 1.6.1 and 1.6.2 (2015, updated February 2026): "1.6.1 Consider single‑unit red blood cell transfusions for adults (or equivalent volumes calculated based on body weight for children or adults with low body weight) who do not have active bleeding. [2015] 1.6.2 After each single‑unit red blood cell transfusion (or equivalent volumes calculated based on body weight for children or adults with low body weight), clinically reassess and check haemoglobin levels, and give further transfusions if needed. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Red-blood-cell-transfusion
- Pass: One unit prescribed per episode, and a documented clinical review plus a post-transfusion haemoglobin result before any second unit was started.. Target: ≥80% of episodes single-unit with reassessment. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible transfusion episodes over the last 6–8 weeks.
- Change: Change the transfusion prescription (paper or EPR order set) so one unit is the default, with a mandatory free-text reason to order more than one.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-220.csv`
- Pitfalls: Active bleeding is hard to judge from notes → agree a simple definition (drop in Hb with visible bleeding or haemodynamic instability) before cycle 1.; Blood bank issues two units at once for convenience → agree with the lab that the second unit is issued only on request.; Clinicians object in low-weight or very anaemic patients → allow a documented reason as a pass.; Small numbers on one ward → pool across the hospital.; Order-set change delayed by IT → use a paper sticker on the prescription in the interim.
- Pearls: Recruit the hospital transfusion practitioner early; they already have issue data.; Report 'units saved' alongside compliance; it persuades managers.; Pair with the Hb threshold field so you do not double count threshold audits.; Hand the dashboard to the transfusion team so it survives your rotation.

**NNA-221. What proportion of adult platelet transfusions are followed by a platelet count before a further dose is given?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG24 recs 1.8.1 and 1.8.3 (2015, updated February 2026): "1.8.1 Do not routinely transfuse more than a single dose of platelets. [2015] ... 1.8.3 Reassess the person's clinical condition and check their platelet count after each platelet transfusion, and give further doses if needed. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Platelet-transfusion
- Pass: A single adult dose given, and a platelet count taken after it and before any further dose (or within 24 hours if no further dose).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive platelet transfusions over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add an automatic 'post-transfusion platelet count' request to the platelet order set in the EPR (or a sticker on the prescription).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-221.csv`
- Pitfalls: Haematology day-unit patients go home before a count → accept a count at next attendance if documented plan.; Doses are recorded as bags not adult doses → confirm dose conversion with blood bank.; Pre-procedure transfusions often skip the count → note procedure type separately.; Order-set change needs IT time → start with a printed sticker.
- Pearls: The transfusion practitioner can pull issue and count data in one report.; Present the cost per platelet dose; it gets attention.; Flag low-bleeding-risk procedures (NG24 1.7.8) as a secondary finding for a later cycle.

**NNA-222. What proportion of adults transfused during an admission have a discharge summary that tells the GP they were transfused and why?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.14.4 (2015, updated February 2026): "Provide the person and their GP with copies of the discharge summary or other written communication that explains: the details of any transfusions they had the reasons for the transfusion any adverse events that they are no longer eligible to donate blood. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Patient-information
- Pass: Discharge summary states that a transfusion was given, the component, the reason, any reaction (or none) and that the patient can no longer donate blood.. Target: ≥90% all four items. Sample: 40 consecutive transfused adults discharged over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a 'Blood products given this admission' section to the electronic discharge template, auto-populated from blood bank data where possible, with fixed text on donation.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-222.csv`
- Pitfalls: Transfusion in theatre is missed by ward doctors → pull cases from LIMS, not notes.; IT will not auto-populate → a free-text heading still works.; Donation wording seen as irrelevant in older patients → keep it; it is part of the standard.; Gains fade after rotation → make the heading mandatory.
- Pearls: The transfusion practitioner can send the list weekly.; Ask the GP practice liaison for one example of a missed transfusion; it makes the case.; Keep the fixed text short: one line per item.

**NNA-223. What proportion of adult inpatients on intravenous antibiotics have a documented review decision within 72 hours of the first dose?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG15 rec 1.1.39 (2015); UKHSA Start Smart Then Focus toolkit (2023): "Consider reviewing intravenous antimicrobial prescriptions at 48–72 hours in all health and care settings (including community and outpatient services). Include response to treatment and microbiological results in any review, to determine if the antimicrobial needs to be continued and, if so, whether it can be switched to an oral antimicrobial." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng15/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A dated note or chart entry between 48 and 72 hours recording one of: stop, switch to oral, change, continue with review date, or OPAT.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over 2 weeks (point-prevalence style across several wards).
- Change: Add a mandatory 48–72 hour 'antimicrobial review' prompt in the e-prescribing system (or a review sticker on the paper chart) with the five decision options.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-223.csv`
- Pitfalls: Review happens verbally on rounds but is not written → count only written decisions and say so.; Weekend starts miss the window → record day of start and analyse separately.; Prompt fatigue if the pop-up fires daily → set it once at 48 hours.; Definitions drift between cycles → fix the 48–72 hour window in advance.
- Pearls: Partner with the AMS pharmacist; they already run point-prevalence sweeps.; Use the Start Smart Then Focus five decisions as the drop-down list.; Feed back ward-level results; competition helps.

**NNA-224. What proportion of patients on repeat antibiotic prescriptions for more than 6 months have a documented review of the need to continue in the last 6 months?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG15 rec 1.1.37 (2015): "Do not issue repeat prescriptions for antimicrobials unless needed for a particular clinical condition or indication. Avoid issuing repeat prescriptions for longer than 6 months without review and ensure adequate monitoring for individual patients to reduce adverse drug reactions and to check whether continuing an antimicrobial is really needed." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng15/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Coded or written medication review in the last 6 months that names the antibiotic and records the decision to continue or stop, with the indication.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All patients found by the search (usually 30–60 in a mid-sized practice).
- Change: Set a 6-month maximum review date on every antibiotic repeat template and add an antibiotic line to the practice repeat-prescribing protocol for the clinical pharmacist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-224.csv`
- Pitfalls: Specialist letters say 'continue' without a review → accept only if dated in last 6 months.; Patients object to stopping acne antibiotics → offer a topical plan at review.; Search misses acute issues repeated manually → search issues, not only templates.; Gains fade → the review date on the template is the lasting fix.
- Pearls: Recruit the PCN clinical pharmacist; this is squarely their work.; Run the search before and after with the same saved report.; Report the number of antibiotic courses avoided.

**NNA-225. What proportion of adult inpatients with a recorded drug allergy have the reaction and the date it happened documented?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG183 rec 1.2.2 (2014): "If drug allergy status has been documented, record all of the following at a minimum: the drug name the signs, symptoms and severity of the reaction (see recommendation 1.1.1) the date when the reaction occurred." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg183/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: For every listed drug allergy: drug name, reaction with severity, and date (year acceptable) recorded in the allergy field.. Target: ≥90% all items. Sample: 40 consecutive patients with a listed drug allergy on 3–4 wards over 1 week.
- Change: Make reaction, severity and date mandatory fields in the EPR allergy entry (or add three boxes to the paper drug chart allergy panel).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-225.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients cannot recall the date → accept year or 'childhood' and pre-agree this.; Mandatory fields invite 'unknown' → audit the rate of 'unknown' separately.; IT change takes months → use pharmacy reconciliation to complete fields meanwhile.; Old allergy imports from primary care lack detail → count them but analyse separately.
- Pearls: Ward pharmacists can complete the data and the fix; recruit them.; Show one real near-miss where 'penicillin' meant nausea; it lands well.; Use penicillin labels as the seed for a de-labelling project.

**NNA-226. What proportion of oral methotrexate prescriptions state the day of the week to take it in full?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, 'Methotrexate once-weekly for autoimmune diseases: new measures to reduce risk of fatal overdose' (23 September 2020): "decide with the patient which day of the week they will take their methotrexate and note this day down in full on the prescription" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/methotrexate-once-weekly-for-autoimmune-diseases-new-measures-to-reduce-risk-of-fatal-overdose-due-to-inadvertent-daily-instead-of-weekly-dosing
- Pass: Prescription directions name the weekday in full (e.g. 'once a week on MONDAY').. Target: 100%. Sample: All practice patients on oral methotrexate (usually 30–50), or 40 consecutive hospital prescriptions.
- Change: Edit every methotrexate repeat template (or the EPR order sentence) so the weekday must be picked from a drop-down before issue.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-226.csv`
- Pitfalls: Hospital shared-care letters override GP templates → check both.; Mixed 2.5 mg and 10 mg strengths → record as a secondary safety flag.; Prescribers write 'weekly' and think it enough → the standard needs the day.; Template edits lost when a new strength is added → recheck at re-audit.
- Pearls: Practice pharmacists can fix templates in an afternoon.; Ask community pharmacy to report prescriptions without a day.; The same search works for weekly oral bisphosphonates.

**NNA-227. What proportion of isotretinoin follow-up appointments record a mental health patient-reported outcome score and a question about sexual function?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, 'Isotretinoin: changes to prescribing guidance and additional risk minimisation measures' (22 January 2026): "prescribers should monitor patients for side effects including mental health and sexual function side effects at each follow up appointment including objective mental health patient reported outcome measures" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/isotretinoin-changes-to-prescribing-guidance-and-additional-risk-minimisation-measures
- Pass: Each follow-up entry records a validated mental health PROM score (e.g. PHQ-9, GAD-7, PHQ-A) and a documented question about sexual function.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive follow-up appointments (face-to-face or remote) over 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Build a one-page isotretinoin follow-up template in the EPR with required PROM score and sexual function fields.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-227.csv`
- Pitfalls: Remote appointments skip the questionnaire → send it electronically before the call.; Nurse-led clinics use a different form → include all prescribers in the template.; Clinicians see PROMs as box-ticking → agree an action pathway for high scores.; National guidance may change again → note the version used in each cycle.
- Pearls: Recruit the acne nurse specialist; they see most follow-ups.; Use the same PROM at every visit so change can be tracked.; Share results with the BAD audit lead to avoid duplicate work.

**NNA-228. What proportion of adults newly started on an opioid for non-cancer pain in primary care have a documented treatment plan with a review or stop date?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, 'Opioids: risk of dependence and addiction' (23 September 2020): "before starting treatment with opioids, agree with the patient a treatment strategy and plan for end of treatment" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/opioids-risk-of-dependence-and-addiction
- Pass: Consultation at initiation records the treatment goal and a review or stop date (or a planned duration), plus a note that dependence risk was discussed.. Target: ≥80% all three items. Sample: 40 consecutive new starts over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add an opioid initiation template (goal, planned duration, review date, risk discussed) that opens when a new opioid is prescribed, and set new opioids to acute issue only.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-228.csv`
- Pitfalls: Out-of-hours and hospital starts are not captured → note the source and include only practice starts.; Clinicians see the template as extra clicks → keep it to four fields.; Short courses under 7 days inflate pass rates → record days' supply.; Codeine co-preparations are missed by searches → include combination products.
- Pearls: Use the TARGET-style patient leaflet in the template.; The PCN pharmacist can run the search each month.; Track 'on repeat at 3 months' as the outcome that matters.

**NNA-229. What proportion of adults started on, or changed to a new, lipid-lowering drug have a full lipid profile and liver transaminase within 2 to 3 months?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG238 rec 1.11.1 (December 2023): "Measure liver transaminase and full lipid profile at 2 to 3 months after starting or changing lipid-lowering treatment. [May 2023, amended December 2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng238/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Full lipid profile and ALT/AST resulted 6–14 weeks after the start or change date.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive starts or changes, 4–7 months ago (to allow time for the test).
- Change: Add an automatic 10-week recall and pre-booked lipid/LFT blood form to the practice's statin initiation template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-229.csv`
- Pitfalls: Re-audit window is long → pick a sample started right after the change and look back at week 13–14 or extend to 20 weeks.; Hospital-started statins have no practice recall → include them and code the source.; Patients do not attend → count a recall sent plus two reminders as a separate measure.; Changing the definition of 'change' between cycles → fix it in advance (drug or dose).
- Pearls: Recruit the practice phlebotomy lead to pre-book the blood test at initiation.; Report the proportion reaching >40% non-HDL reduction as a bonus outcome.; Use one saved search for both cycles.

**NNA-230. What proportion of adults newly diagnosed with hypertension had the diagnosis confirmed by ambulatory or home blood pressure monitoring?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG136 recs 1.2.3 and 1.2.4 (2019, updated February 2026): "1.2.3 If clinic blood pressure is between 140/90 mmHg and 180/120 mmHg, offer ambulatory blood pressure monitoring (ABPM) to confirm the diagnosis of hypertension. ... [2019] 1.2.4 If ABPM is unsuitable or the person is unable to tolerate it, offer home blood pressure monitoring (HBPM) to confirm the diagnosis of hypertension. [2019]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng136/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: ABPM daytime average or HBPM average recorded before the hypertension code was added.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new diagnoses.
- Change: Add a hypertension diagnosis template that asks for the ABPM/HBPM average before the code can be saved, with a link to the practice home BP loan and diary.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-230.csv`
- Pitfalls: HBPM values held in free text → search both codes and free text.; Monitor shortage → a loan scheme or pharmacy BP check service solves it.; Clinicians code 'raised BP' then 'hypertension' later → use the first hypertension code date.; Small practices may have few cases → pool across the PCN.
- Pearls: Recruit the healthcare assistants who fit ABPM.; Report how many were not hypertensive after ABPM; it justifies the time.; Use a standard HBPM diary with average calculation.

**NNA-231. What proportion of adults with type 2 diabetes and heart failure are prescribed an SGLT-2 inhibitor?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG28 recs 1.14.1 and 1.14.2 (2015, updated February 2026): "1.14.1 For adults with type 2 diabetes and heart failure offer: modified-release metformin, and an SGLT-2 inhibitor. [2026] 1.14.2 If metformin is contraindicated or not tolerated, offer monotherapy with an SGLT-2 inhibitor. [2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng28/chapter/Initial-medicines
- Pass: SGLT-2 inhibitor issued in the last 3 months, or a documented reason not to (contraindication, intolerance, declined).. Target: ≥80% prescribed or reason documented. Sample: All patients found by the search (usually 30–60 per practice).
- Change: Run a monthly practice search with a pop-up alert on the records of eligible patients not on an SGLT-2 inhibitor, actioned by the practice pharmacist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-231.csv`
- Pitfalls: Heart failure is under-coded → also search for loop diuretics plus echo letters.; Hospital cardiology may have stopped it → accept a documented reason.; Genital infection and DKA concerns → include sick-day advice as a field.; Supply problems change practice → note any shortage.
- Pearls: The practice pharmacist can do most of the reviews.; Start with HFrEF, where evidence is strongest, then widen.; Pair with the eGFR field so renal limits are checked.

**NNA-232. What proportion of adults with severe psoriasis on systemic treatment or phototherapy have a documented cardiovascular risk assessment within the last 5 years?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG153 rec 1.2.3.1 (2012, updated September 2017): "Offer adults with severe psoriasis of any type (defined as either needing treatment with phototherapy or systemic treatments, or needing hospital admission in the studies underpinning this recommendation), a cardiovascular risk assessment at presentation using a validated risk estimation tool. Offer further assessment of cardiovascular risk every 5 years, or more frequently if indicated following assessment." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg153/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: QRISK3 (or other validated score) recorded in dermatology or GP records within the last 5 years, with the score in the notes.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients from the biologic/systemic or phototherapy clinic list.
- Change: Add a QRISK3 field to the dermatology systemic therapy review proforma, with a standard GP letter paragraph asking for statin review if ≥10%.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-232.csv`
- Pitfalls: QRISK done in primary care is not visible → check the summary care record.; Dermatologists feel CV risk is the GP's job → the GP letter paragraph shares the load.; Phototherapy lists are nurse-led → include nurses in the proforma change.; Very young patients have low scores → still counts as assessed.
- Pearls: QRISK3 has a free online calculator; build the link into the proforma.; Nurse specialists can record it at the monitoring visit.; Report the number with QRISK ≥10% not on a statin.

**NNA-233. What proportion of adults with myeloma starting thalidomide, lenalidomide or pomalidomide with steroids are prescribed VTE prophylaxis?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG89 rec 1.6.2 (2018, updated August 2019): "Consider pharmacological VTE prophylaxis for people with myeloma who are receiving chemotherapy with thalidomide, pomalidomide or lenalidomide with steroids. Choose either: aspirin (75 mg or 150 mg) or LMWH. [2018]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng89/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Aspirin or LMWH prescribed from cycle 1 day 1 (or already on anticoagulation), or a documented reason for none.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive IMiD regimen starts over the last 12 months.
- Change: Add VTE prophylaxis as a default line (aspirin or LMWH, with a 'not required – reason' option) in the IMiD regimen protocol on the chemotherapy prescribing system.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-233.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few starts per month → look back 12 months for cycle 1.; Aspirin bought over the counter is not on the chart → check clinic letters.; Thrombocytopenia contraindicates aspirin → record platelet count.; Protocol change needs pharmacy approval → involve the chemo pharmacist early.
- Pearls: The haematology pharmacist can pull all IMiD starts in one report.; Record VTE events as an outcome; it strengthens the case.; Align with the local myeloma protocol wording.

**NNA-234. What proportion of adults treated for proximal DVT or PE have a documented review of whether to continue, stop or change anticoagulation at 3 months?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 rec 1.4.1 (2020, updated August 2023): "Assess and discuss the benefits and risks of continuing, stopping or changing the anticoagulant with people who have had anticoagulation treatment for 3 months (3 to 6 months for people with active cancer) after a proximal DVT or PE." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A dated clinic, virtual or GP entry between 10 and 16 weeks (up to 6 months with active cancer) recording provoked/unprovoked status and a decision to stop, continue or change.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive diagnoses made 5–7 months ago.
- Change: Book a 3-month anticoagulation review (virtual clinic or GP task with a standard template) at the point of diagnosis, with the provoked/unprovoked status recorded on the discharge letter.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-234.csv`
- Pitfalls: The review happens in primary care and is not visible → check GP records via shared care record.; Provoked status is never recorded → make it a field at diagnosis.; Re-audit window is long → sample diagnoses from the first 2 weeks after the change.; Haematology capacity for clinics → a virtual or GP template works.
- Pearls: Recruit the anticoagulation nurse; they often run the DVT clinic.; Use the HAS-BLED field for unprovoked cases as a quality marker.; Report days of anticoagulation avoided.

**NNA-235. What proportion of adults aged 65 and over assessed for suspected UTI are managed without a urine dipstick test?** (under-audited)
- Standard: UKHSA, 'Diagnosis of urinary tract infections: quick reference tools for primary care' (last updated 7 July 2025), adults over 65 years: "Do not perform urine dipsticks (see rationale). Urine dipsticks are less reliable with increasing age over 65 years due to increasing levels of asymptomatic bacteriuria (ASB) in older adults." https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/urinary-tract-infection-diagnosis/diagnosis-of-urinary-tract-infections-quick-reference-tools-for-primary-care
- Pass: No dipstick result used for the UTI diagnosis; decision based on symptoms and signs, with culture sent before antibiotics if treated.. Target: ≥80% no dipstick. Sample: 40 consecutive patients coded or treated for UTI over 2–4 weeks.
- Change: Remove dipstick from the ED/AMU nursing triage protocol for patients ≥65 and add a UKHSA over-65 symptom checklist to the UTI clerking or GP template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-235.csv`
- Pitfalls: Nurses dipstick by habit at triage → the triage protocol must change, not just doctors' practice.; Clinicians fear missing sepsis → emphasise symptom-based assessment and culture.; Dipstick results are not always recorded → check obs charts and ask the ward.; Fit, community-dwelling women over 65 may use the under-65 tool → record and analyse separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED nurse educator and the AMS pharmacist.; Remove dipsticks from resus/triage trolleys for the pilot.; Count antibiotic courses avoided.

**NNA-236. What proportion of patients consulting with acute sore throat have a FeverPAIN or Centor score recorded?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG84 rec 1.1.3 (2018): "Use FeverPAIN or Centor criteria to identify people who are more likely to benefit from an antibiotic and manage in line with recommendations 1.1.4 to 1.1.13." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng84/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A FeverPAIN or Centor score (total or all components) recorded in the consultation, and management matches the score band.. Target: ≥80% scored. Sample: 40 consecutive consultations over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a FeverPAIN template (auto-scoring) to the practice sore throat consultation, linked to the TARGET leaflet.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-236.csv`
- Pitfalls: eConsult and phone contacts have no exam → record contact type and analyse separately.; Locums do not use the template → include in locum pack.; Winter peaks change case mix → keep the same season if possible.; Coding varies (URTI vs sore throat) → fix the code list in advance.
- Pearls: Pharmacy First handles many sore throats; include their referrals in context.; Auto-scoring templates cut the effort to one click per item.; Report antibiotic prescription rate by score band.

**NNA-237. What proportion of non-urgent outpatient contrast CT requests record a kidney risk assessment (an eGFR from the past 6 months or the NICE screening questions)?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG148 recs 1.1.8 to 1.1.10 (2019, updated October 2024): "1.1.8 Before requesting a non-urgent iodine-based contrast media CT scan, assess whether the person has pre-existing kidney disease. [2024] 1.1.9 If available, use an eGFR measurement from the past 6 months to support decision making about the use of iodine-based contrast media. ... [2024] 1.1.10 If no eGFR is available from the past 6 months, ask the person, or their family members and carers if appropriate, the following screening questions: do they have kidney disease or a kidney transplant? have they seen or are waiting to see a kidney specialist, or a kidney surgeon or urologist? do they have symptoms of acute illness likely to cause acute kidney injury such as diarrhoea, vomiting, fever, hypovolaemia, infection or difficulty passing urine? [2024]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng148/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Request or radiographer checklist shows an eGFR dated within 6 months, or all three screening questions answered, before the scan.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive scans over 1–2 weeks.
- Change: Replace the RIS request eGFR box with the three NICE screening questions as mandatory fields when no eGFR from the last 6 months is shown.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-237.csv`
- Pitfalls: Local SOP still demands eGFR for everyone → update the SOP with the radiology lead first.; Radiographers ask verbally and do not record → add the checklist to the RIS.; Measuring over-testing and under-assessment together confuses the message → keep pass criterion single; report delays as secondary.; GP requests come by paper → include the questions on the paper form.
- Pearls: Recruit the CT superintendent radiographer.; Report scans delayed or cancelled for bloods; it shows the benefit.; Use the NICE wording verbatim on the form.

**NNA-238. What proportion of patients discharged from hospital have medicines reconciliation completed by the GP practice within 1 week of the practice receiving the discharge summary?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG5 rec 1.3.3 (2015): "In primary care, carry out medicines reconciliation for all people who have been discharged from hospital or another care setting. This should happen as soon as is practically possible, before a prescription or new supply of medicines is issued and within 1 week of the GP practice receiving the information." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng5/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Coded medicines reconciliation (or documented review of discharge medicines against repeats) within 7 days of the discharge summary arriving, and before the next repeat issue.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive discharge summaries received over the last 1–2 months.
- Change: Route all discharge summaries with medicine changes to the practice pharmacist's workflow queue with a 5-working-day task.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-238.csv`
- Pitfalls: Receipt date is not the discharge date → use workflow receipt date.; Reconciliation is done but not coded → agree one code in advance.; Pharmacist leave stalls the queue → set a cover rule.; Summaries arrive without medicines → exclude and report separately to the trust.
- Pearls: The practice pharmacist is the natural owner.; Report discrepancies found; it proves value.; Feed poor-quality summaries back to the trust.

**NNA-239. What proportion of outpatient CT scans on a faster diagnosis cancer pathway are reported within 3 days of the scan?** (new)
- Standard: NHS England, 'Diagnostic imaging reporting turnaround times' (9 August 2023), Table 1: "Table 1: TAT by referral category for imaging services (Imaging service | Referral category | Maximum TAT) ... Cross-sectional imaging: – CT, including cone beam CT – MRI | Outpatient faster diagnosis standard cancer pathway | 3 days" https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/diagnostic-imaging-reporting-turnaround-times/
- Pass: Final (verified) report time minus scan completion time ≤3 calendar days.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive flagged scans over 1–2 weeks.
- Change: Create a dedicated 'FDS cancer pathway' RIS worklist with the flag set at vetting, so these scans sit at the top of reporters' lists.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-239.csv`
- Pitfalls: Cancer flag missing on requests → check the pathway via the cancer tracker as well.; Outsourced reports have different timestamps → confirm which field is 'final'.; Weekends inflate calendar days → report working days too.; Radiologist workload objection → show the numbers are small.
- Pearls: Recruit the RIS manager to pull timestamps in bulk.; Cancer MDT coordinators know which scans were FDS.; Present time lost per patient against the 28-day standard.

**NNA-240. What proportion of mobile chest X-rays taken to check central line position on intensive care have a clinician's evaluation written in the notes?** (under-audited)
- Standard: RCR, 'IR(ME)R: Implications for clinical practice in diagnostic imaging, interventional radiology and diagnostic nuclear medicine', second edition (August 2026), chapter 11: "Regulation 12(9) says that the employer must ensure that a clinical evaluation, including the outcome and implications, is recorded for each exposure. ... The outcome of the clinical evaluation may be recorded directly in the patient's electronic records or written clinical notes. ... Exposures that are not clinically evaluated are not justified." https://www.rcr.ac.uk/media/s1sdu4cq/rcr-publications-ir-me-r-implications-for-clinical-practice-in-diagnostic-imaging-interventional-radiology-and-diagnostic-nuclear-medicine-second-edition.pdf
- Pass: A dated, signed note by an entitled clinician stating line tip position and whether it is safe to use (and any pneumothorax), before the line is used or a formal report is issued.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive line-check films over 3–4 weeks.
- Change: Add a 'CXR clinical evaluation' box (tip position, pneumothorax, safe to use, name, time) to the ICU line insertion sticker or e-form.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-240.csv`
- Pitfalls: ICU staff are not all entitled operators → check the entitlement list with the RPS first.; Evaluation made verbally on the round → only written entries count.; Radiology reports quickly in some units → exclude films reported before line use.; Sticker not stocked → keep it in the CVC packs.
- Pearls: Work with the radiation protection supervisor; this fills their audit requirement.; Put the box on the existing CVC insertion sticker, not a new form.; Same method works for NG tube and chest drain films.

**NNA-241. What proportion of women aged 40 and over with a primary care CA125 at or above the NICE age-specific threshold have an urgent pelvic ultrasound requested within 2 weeks?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 recs 1.5.8 and 1.5.9 (2015, updated April 2026): "1.5.8 For women, and trans men and non-binary people with female reproductive organs who are aged 40 or over with persistent symptoms that suggest ovarian cancer (see recommendations 1.5.1 to 1.5.5), measure CA125 in primary care. [2026] 1.5.9 Arrange an urgent, direct access ultrasound scan of the abdomen and pelvis depending on age and serum CA125 according to the thresholds in table 1. [2026] (Table 1: 40 to 49, 35 IU/ml or greater; 50 to 59, 31 IU/ml or greater; 60 to 69, 24 IU/ml or greater; 70 to 79, 25 IU/ml or greater; 80+, 31 IU/ml or greater)" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: Urgent abdominal and pelvic ultrasound requested within 14 days of the CA125 result.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive qualifying results from the lab over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Add an automatic lab comment to CA125 reports stating the NICE age-specific threshold and 'urgent USS recommended', and flag results above threshold to the requesting practice.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-241.csv`
- Pitfalls: The 50–79 thresholds are below the old 35 → results between old and new cut-offs are the ones most likely missed; flag them.; Ultrasound done privately or elsewhere → check shared record.; Lab comment change needs pathology approval → involve the duty biochemist early.; Some women are symptomatic of another cause → note documented alternative diagnosis.
- Pearls: Recruit the clinical biochemist; the lab comment is the lasting fix.; The ICB cancer GP lead can circulate results.; Count cancers found in the 'new threshold' band.

**NNA-242. What proportion of primary care serum protein electrophoresis requests for suspected myeloma also include serum free light chains?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.10.4 (2015, amended 2025): "Offer the following to assess for myeloma in people aged 60 and over with persistent bone pain, particularly back pain, or unexplained fracture: a full blood count, and blood tests for: calcium plasma viscosity or erythrocyte sedimentation rate paraprotein, using serum protein electrophoresis free light chains contained in serum. If serum free light chain testing is not available, use a Bence–Jones test to check for free light chains contained in urine. [2015, amended 2025]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: Serum free light chains (or urine Bence–Jones if SFLC unavailable) requested on the same or next-day sample as the SPEP, with FBC, calcium and ESR/PV.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive qualifying SPEP requests over 2–4 weeks.
- Change: Create a 'suspected myeloma' test set on the GP electronic requesting system (ICE) that orders SPEP, SFLC, FBC, calcium and ESR together, and set the lab to add SFLC to lone SPEP requests from primary care.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-242.csv`
- Pitfalls: SFLC costs more → agree with the lab that lab add-on is cheaper than delayed diagnosis.; Many SPEP requests are not for myeloma → use clinical details to classify.; Urine samples never arrive → the serum test set removes this problem.; Test set not used by all practices → check uptake by practice.
- Pearls: The clinical biochemist can build the test set and add-on rule.; Report time to diagnosis for any myeloma found.; Share practice-level uptake with the ICB.

## Older people, stroke, neurology and palliative care

**NNA-243. In adults treated in hospital for an acute migraine attack, what proportion receive an opioid?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG150 rec 1.3.16 (2012, guideline updated June 2025): "Do not offer ergots or opioids for the acute treatment of migraine." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg150/chapter/Recommendations
