- Pass: No opioid (codeine, dihydrocodeine, tramadol, morphine, oxycodone or others) given in hospital or prescribed on discharge for the migraine attack.. Target: ≥90% of attendances opioid-free. Sample: 40 consecutive coded migraine attendances over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add a migraine order set to the ED and acute medical unit electronic prescribing system with NSAID, triptan and metoclopramide or prochlorperazine as defaults, and require a documented reason before an opioid can be prescribed for a migraine diagnosis.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-243.csv`
- Pitfalls: Migraine is under-coded or coded as 'headache' → search both codes and confirm the diagnosis in the notes before including; Clinicians say the triptan or NSAID was contraindicated → record contraindications and report opioid use with and without them; Opioids given before the diagnosis was made → record the time of diagnosis and count only doses given for the migraine; Pushback that patients 'expect' strong analgesia → share the NICE wording and the medication overuse risk, and include re-attendance as a balancing measure
- Pearls: Recruit an ED consultant and a neurology or headache nurse as joint leads; Ask pharmacy to run the opioid report for migraine diagnoses; it takes minutes; Give patients a one-page migraine discharge leaflet with triptan advice and medication overuse warning; Track re-attendance within 7 days to show that the change is safe

**NNA-244. In adults seen in neurology clinic with chronic headache, what proportion have the number of days per month they take painkillers or triptans recorded?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG150 rec 1.2.7 (2012, guideline updated June 2025): "Be alert to the possibility of medication overuse headache in people whose headache developed or worsened while they were taking the following drugs for 3 months or more: triptans, opioids, ergots or combination analgesic medications on 10 days per month or more or paracetamol, aspirin or an NSAID, either alone or in any combination, on 15 days per month or more." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg150/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The clinic letter records the number of days per month of acute headache medication use (analgesics, triptans or opioids).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new chronic headache clinic letters over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Acute medication days per month' line, with the NICE thresholds, to the neurology headache clinic letter template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-244.csv`
- Pitfalls: Letters say 'uses painkillers frequently' without a number → count only a days-per-month figure as a pass; Consultants use personal templates → agree one shared template line at the department meeting before cycle 2; Referral letters lack the information → add the question to the GP referral form as well
- Pearls: Ask patients to bring a headache diary to the first visit; the clinic letter invitation can request it; Recruit the headache specialist nurse to champion the template line; Report how many patients met the overuse threshold; it shows the value of asking

**NNA-245. In adults with MS who agree to start a disease-modifying therapy, what proportion receive their first dose within 12 weeks of the treatment decision?** (new)
- Standard: National Neurosciences Advisory Group, Optimal clinical care pathway for adults: Multiple Sclerosis (2024), Standards and metrics: "People with a confirmed diagnosis of MS who are eligible and want to start treatment start DMT within 12 weeks of shared treatment decision" https://www.mssociety.org.uk/sites/default/files/2024-12/NNAG%20MS%20optimal%20pathway%202024.pdf
- Pass: First dose given 84 days or fewer after the documented shared treatment decision.. Target: ≥90% within 12 weeks. Sample: All disease-modifying therapy starts in the last 12 months (typically 30–60 in a regional MS service).
- Change: A disease-modifying therapy start checklist in the clinic letter template, so screening bloods, vaccination review and the funding form are all requested at the same appointment as the decision, and a named coordinator tracks each start to first dose.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-245.csv`
- Pitfalls: The decision date is unclear in letters → agree before cycle 1 that it is the clinic date when the person agreed to start; Deferred starts inflate delays → record patient-chosen deferral separately and exclude it from the pass rate; Infusion-unit capacity sits outside the MS team → invite the day-unit manager to the results meeting; Small numbers per drug → report overall and by route, not by individual drug
- Pearls: Recruit the MS nurse or treatment coordinator as co-lead; they hold most of the dates; Chart each step (bloods, funding, homecare, dose) to show where the weeks go; Request screening bloods at the diagnosis visit when treatment is likely; Share the result with the homecare provider; their set-up time is often the longest step

## Radiology, pathology and diagnostics

**NNA-246. What proportion of imaging reports carrying a CANCER alert have a documented action by the referring team within 7 days of the report?** (new)
- Standard: Academy of Medical Royal Colleges and RCR, Alerts and notification of imaging reports: recommendations (October 2022), recommendations on cancer imaging alerts 1 and 2: "1. All imaging reports with expected or unexpected new cancer diagnosis or newly detected cancer recurrence should be notified to the referrers with a CANCER alert in the report via a digital platform immediately upon completion of the reports. 2. Referring teams must ensure a valid contact detail (or through a pre-agreed system) is available, including out of hours, on all imaging referrals. They must also ensure that all reports are read, acknowledged and acted upon (or plan to act)." https://www.rcr.ac.uk/media/44sfqlbi/rcr-publications_alerts-and-notification-of-imaging-reports-recommendations_october-2022.pdf
- Pass: The report is electronically acknowledged by the referring team and an action or plan (referral, clinic, MDT, patient told) is recorded within 7 calendar days of report authorisation.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive CANCER-alert reports from the last 2 months.
- Change: Switch on automatic escalation: an unacknowledged CANCER-alert report triggers a daily reminder to the referrer and, at 96 hours, an email to the clinical lead and the results coordinator.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-246.csv`
- Pitfalls: Acknowledgement is clicked without action → count a pass only when an action or plan is recorded.; Reports on GP patients sit outside the EPR → include GP-referred cases and ask the practice or use the ICE/GP results log.; Alert codes applied inconsistently by reporters → agree the code list with radiologists before cycle 1 and keep it fixed.; Escalation emails go to a generic inbox → route them to named leads and the results coordinator.
- Pearls: Recruit the RIS manager early; most systems can export alert and acknowledgement times.; Show two or three real delayed cases (anonymised) at governance; they move people more than percentages.; Give the results coordinator ownership of the monthly figure so it survives trainee rotation.

**NNA-247. What proportion of imaging reports with a CRITICAL alert record the name of the person who received the verbal message?** (new)
- Standard: Academy of Medical Royal Colleges and RCR, Alerts and notification of imaging reports: recommendations (October 2022), recommendations on critical imaging alerts 2 and 4: "2. If immediate intervention is required, the alert should be supplemented by direct verbal communication. 4. The verbal communication including the name of the person who receives the results should be documented in the imaging report or patient record." https://www.rcr.ac.uk/media/44sfqlbi/rcr-publications_alerts-and-notification-of-imaging-reports-recommendations_october-2022.pdf
- Pass: The report or patient record names the person who received the verbal message and the time of the call.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive CRITICAL-alert reports from the last 1–2 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Critical result phoned to: name / role / time' field that appears when a reporter selects the CRITICAL alert code in the RIS.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-247.csv`
- Pitfalls: Outsourced reporters use a different template → agree the field with the teleradiology provider before cycle 1.; Calls made but written only on paper → accept the EPR or report addendum only, and say so in advance.; Radiologists see it as extra typing → make the field a short pick-list plus a name box.; Small numbers in one month → pool 2 months if fewer than 40 alerts.
- Pearls: Recruit a consultant radiologist who chairs the discrepancy meeting as co-lead.; Also record minutes from report to call; it is a useful secondary measure.; Ask the PACS team to build a monthly report of CRITICAL alerts without a named receiver.

**NNA-248. What proportion of inpatient and ED imaging requests include a working contact number for the referring team that covers out of hours?** (new)
- Standard: Academy of Medical Royal Colleges and RCR, Alerts and notification of imaging reports: recommendations (October 2022), recommendation on critical imaging alerts 7: "7. Referrers must ensure a valid contact detail is available, including out of hours, on all imaging referrals. They must also ensure that all reports are read, acknowledged and acted upon (or plan to act)." https://www.rcr.ac.uk/media/44sfqlbi/rcr-publications_alerts-and-notification-of-imaging-reports-recommendations_october-2022.pdf
- Pass: The request shows a bleep, extension or team phone number that is answered 24 hours a day (or a pre-agreed out-of-hours route), and the number works when tested.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive requests over 1 week, split across day and night.
- Change: Make a 24-hour team contact a mandatory field on the electronic imaging request, pre-filled from the ward or specialty on-call bleep list kept by switchboard.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-248.csv`
- Pitfalls: Personal mobile numbers entered → count only numbers that switch with the rota.; Pre-filled numbers go out of date at changeover → give switchboard ownership of the list.; Checking numbers is slow → test a random 10 by phone, not all 50.; IT change takes months → use a hard-stop free-text field as an interim step.
- Pearls: Ask radiologists how many calls failed last week; it wins support quickly.; Agree what counts as 'valid' with the radiology lead before cycle 1.; Include night requests; that is where the gap usually is.

**NNA-249. What proportion of abnormal radiology reports on patients discharged from the ED have a recorded clinician review within 72 hours of the report?** (new)
- Standard: RCEM Best Practice Guideline: Management of Investigation Results in the Emergency Department (revised May 2020), summary of recommendations: "a. The process of review and action taken should be identifiable and traceable, and completed in a timely fashion. This should be ‘real-time’ for time critical investigation results, and within 72 hours for non-urgent results. ... All reports of abnormal radiological investigations requested by the Emergency Department team must be reviewed by a clinician, taking the clinical scenario into account, and necessary actions that may be required." https://rcem.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2021/10/RCEM_BPC_InvestigationResults_200520.pdf
- Pass: An ED clinician's review is recorded (e-sign-off or note) with the action taken (recall, letter, fracture clinic, no action with reason) within 72 hours of report authorisation.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive abnormal reports over 2 weeks.
- Change: Create a daily ED results work-list in the EPR that shows every unsigned abnormal report older than 24 hours, owned by a named senior clinician on the rota.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-249.csv`
- Pitfalls: Weekend reports wait until Monday → include weekend days in the rota slot or the 72 hours will fail.; Reviews done but not recorded → count only EPR sign-off or a written note.; Radiology and ED disagree on what is 'abnormal' → use the radiology alert or discrepancy code as the definition.; Work-list grows at busy times → report backlog size weekly to the ED lead.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED consultant who already does results review; they know the pain points.; Record patient contact outcome; it supports Duty of Candour reporting.; Keep the work-list slot in the rota, not in a person's goodwill, so it survives rotation.

**NNA-250. What proportion of critical potassium results (6.5 mmol/L or more, or 2.5 mmol/L or less) are phoned to the clinical team within 2 hours of the result being available?** (new)
- Standard: RCPath G158 Best practice recommendations: the communication of critical and unexpected pathology results (version 2, May 2026), biochemistry table and notes: "A = rapid communication within 2 hours, usually by telephone ... K mmol/L 2.5 6.5 A A Exclude haemolysis/old samples/EDTA contamination first." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/g158-bpr-the-communication-of-critical-and-unexpected-pathology-results.html
- Pass: The LIMS records a call to a named clinician (or agreed receiver) within 2 hours of result validation.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive critical potassium results over 2 weeks.
- Change: Set a LIMS timer that flags any critical result not marked as phoned within 60 minutes to the duty biomedical scientist, with a named out-of-hours GP route on the flag.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-250.csv`
- Pitfalls: GP results out of hours cannot be phoned → agree the out-of-hours GP route before cycle 1 and count calls to it.; Call time not typed into the LIMS → make the call record a required step to release the comment.; Different thresholds for renal patients → list agreed exceptions in advance and exclude them in both cycles.; Lab staff feel blamed → present the data as a system measure with the biochemistry lead.
- Pearls: Recruit the duty biochemist and a senior biomedical scientist.; Report time to call by hour of day; night-time delays usually show a staffing issue.; Use the same 2026 limits in both cycles.

**NNA-251. What proportion of blood cultures that turn positive after the patient has left hospital are telephoned to a clinician in the last team that cared for them?** (new)
- Standard: RCPath G158 Best practice recommendations: the communication of critical and unexpected pathology results (version 2, May 2026), recommendations for microbiology and virology departments: "Blood cultures that become positive after a patient has been discharged must be telephoned to the last team to care for that patient. For example, if a patient was discharged from a medical ward, then that team must be notified of the blood culture. If the patient had a blood culture taken in the emergency department (ED) and subsequently discharged without admission, then a clinician in ED must be telephoned of the result." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/g158-bpr-the-communication-of-critical-and-unexpected-pathology-results.html
- Pass: The LIMS or EPR records a telephone call to a named clinician from the last team (or ED) that cared for the patient.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a 'patient discharged' flag to the LIMS positive-culture screen that shows the last ward and the agreed contact (ED results clinician or ward team bleep).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-251.csv`
- Pitfalls: Microbiology phones the consultant's secretary → count only calls to a clinician in the last team.; Discharge time missing in the LIMS → pull it from PAS.; Contaminants inflate the denominator → agree the contaminant list before cycle 1.; Ward teams have rotated → use the team bleep, not an individual.
- Pearls: Recruit a microbiology registrar; they make these calls.; Record what happened to the patient; recalled cases make a strong governance story.; Ask the ED to name one clinician per shift as the results contact.

**NNA-252. What proportion of adult blood culture sets taken in the ED grow a likely skin contaminant?** (under-audited)
- Standard: UK SMI S 12: Sepsis and systemic or disseminated infections (UKHSA, issue 1.1, April 2025), blood culture contamination: "Contamination of blood cultures complicates interpretation and may lead to unnecessary antimicrobial therapy and increased costs. In general, contamination target rates are set at less than 3%." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/uk-smi-s-12i1-1-sepsis-and-systemic-or-disseminated-infection-april-2025-pdf.html
- Pass: The set does not grow a likely contaminant (coagulase-negative staphylococci, Cutibacterium, Micrococcus, Bacillus species other than B. anthracis, or diphtheroids in one set only).. Target: <3% of sets contaminated. Sample: All ED sets over 2 weeks (usually 150–300); a full count comes straight from the LIMS.
- Change: Introduce a standard ED blood culture pack (chlorhexidine applicator, bottle-top wipes, checklist sticker) with collector name and site recorded on the label.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-252.csv`
- Pitfalls: Denominator is small if you use only positives → count all sets taken, not just positive ones.; Definition of contaminant drifts between cycles → fix the organism list with microbiology before cycle 1.; Packs not restocked at night → give ownership to the ED stock manager.; Teaching alone fades → rely on the pack and feedback rather than sessions.
- Pearls: Recruit a microbiologist and an ED nurse educator together.; Show the cost and extra antibiotic days per contaminated set.; Feed back rates by shift or area monthly; it keeps the change alive.

**NNA-253. What proportion of adults with suspected sepsis have two blood culture sets taken within 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: UK SMI S 12: Sepsis and systemic or disseminated infections (UKHSA, issue 1.1, April 2025), section 5.3 number: "Take 2 consecutive sets from 2 separate venepuncture sites during any 24 hour period for each septic episode." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/uk-smi-s-12i1-1-sepsis-and-systemic-or-disseminated-infection-april-2025-pdf.html
- Pass: Two sets (each one aerobic and one anaerobic bottle) received by the lab within 24 hours of the first set.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive episodes over 2 weeks.
- Change: Change the EPR sepsis order set to request two blood culture sets by default, printing two labels.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-253.csv`
- Pitfalls: Second set delays antibiotics → state that both sets can be taken in one draw, as SMI S 12 allows, and antibiotics must not wait.; Lab counts bottles, not sets → agree how the LIMS shows a set before cycle 1.; Contamination may rise with more sets → track contamination as a balancing measure.; Order set change needs IT time → start with a printed two-label pack.
- Pearls: Pair with the contamination audit; both use the same LIMS extract.; Recruit an ED sepsis nurse lead.; Show single-set cases later labelled 'possible contaminant' to explain why two sets matter.

**NNA-254. What proportion of blood culture bottles are loaded onto the analyser within 4 hours of collection?** (new)
- Standard: UK SMI S 12: Sepsis and systemic or disseminated infections (UKHSA, issue 1.1, April 2025), section 5.4 optimal transport and storage conditions: "Specimens should be transported and processed as soon as possible. Inoculated bottles should be loaded onto automated blood culture analyser (or incubated in the case of non-automated analysers) without delay and ideally within a maximum of 4 hours from time of collection." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/uk-smi-s-12i1-1-sepsis-and-systemic-or-disseminated-infection-april-2025-pdf.html
- Pass: Analyser loading time is 4 hours or less after the collection time on the request.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All bottles over 1 week (usually 300+), from the LIMS and analyser log.
- Change: Place a blood culture analyser module (or 24-hour loading point) at the site with the longest delays, or add a timed courier run for blood cultures overnight.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-254.csv`
- Pitfalls: Collection time missing or defaulted to label print time → report missing times separately and fix the label workflow.; Hub-and-spoke labs blame transport → split delays into collection-to-receipt and receipt-to-load.; Capital cost of a new module → start with courier timing, which is cheaper.; Seasonal volume changes → re-audit in the same season if possible.
- Pearls: Recruit the microbiology laboratory manager; they own the analyser log.; Plot delays by hour of collection; the night peak is usually obvious.; Use the same week-day pattern in both cycles.

**NNA-255. What proportion of adults with an incidental lung nodule of 5 mm or more on CT have follow-up arranged in line with BTS guidance?** (new)
- Standard: BTS quality standards for the investigation and management of pulmonary nodules (BMJ Open Resp Res 2018), quality statement 4: "People with pulmonary nodules confirmed on CT are offered discharge, further surveillance, further workup or treatment according to BTS Guidelines" https://www.brit-thoracic.org.uk/document-library/quality-standards/pulmonary-nodules/bts-quality-standards-for-the-investigation-and-management-of-pulmonary-nodules/
- Pass: Within 4 weeks of the report, a surveillance CT is requested at the BTS interval or the patient is referred to the nodule/lung clinic, as matches the nodule size and risk.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive reports over the last 3 months.
- Change: Use a standard nodule code in the report that auto-refers the case to the lung nodule MDT list, so follow-up does not depend on the requesting team.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-255.csv`
- Pitfalls: Free-text search misses nodules → search several terms and check a sample of chest CTs by hand.; Reports do not state size → ask radiologists to use the BTS size bands in the code.; Respiratory team fears extra workload → show that most small nodules are discharged by the MDT, not seen in clinic.; Follow-up requested but never done → record whether the CT happened as a secondary measure.
- Pearls: Recruit the lung nodule MDT lead and a chest radiologist.; Fix the size cut-offs to the BTS guideline in both cycles.; Give the nodule nurse the tracking list; it survives trainee rotation.

**NNA-256. What proportion of inpatients with an eGFR below 30 who receive iodine-based contrast have a documented decision about intravenous volume expansion?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG148 Acute kidney injury: prevention, detection and management, rec 1.2.8 (2019, updated October 2024): "For inpatients having iodine-based contrast media, consider intravenous volume expansion with either isotonic sodium bicarbonate or 0.9% sodium chloride if they are at particularly high risk, for example, if: they have an eGFR less than 30 ml/min/1.73 m2; they have had a renal transplant; a large volume of contrast medium is being used (for example, higher than the standard diagnostic dose or repeat administration within 24 hours); intra-arterial administration of contrast medium with first-pass renal exposure is being used. [2019]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng148/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: IV volume expansion is prescribed around the scan, or the notes record a reason not to give it (e.g. fluid overload, emergency scan).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive scans over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add an 'eGFR under 30: IV fluids plan (give / not giving because...)' prompt to the RIS vetting screen, visible to the vetting radiologist and the ward.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-256.csv`
- Pitfalls: Emergency scans cannot wait for fluids → accept a documented 'not given: emergency' as a pass.; Clinicians argue the evidence is weak → NICE says 'consider', so the pass is a documented decision, not fluid itself.; eGFR from months earlier → use the latest eGFR before the scan and state this rule in advance.; Heart failure patients → record the reason; do not push fluids.
- Pearls: Recruit a renal registrar and a CT vetting radiologist.; Report AKI after scans as a secondary measure; it keeps the audit balanced.; Keep the prompt short; a one-line pick list is used, a paragraph is not.

**NNA-257. What proportion of inpatient and ED MRI requests have the referrer's MRI safety screening questions fully completed?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Safety guidelines for magnetic resonance imaging equipment in clinical use (version 5.0, July 2026), section 5.3.4 screening prior to examination, figure 5 stage 1: "Referrer completes targeted MRI screening questions as part of the MRI referral, designed to highlight any potential contraindications to MRI." https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/safety-guidelines-for-magnetic-resonance-imaging-equipment-in-clinical-use
- Pass: Every targeted MRI safety question on the request (e.g. pacemaker or other implant, metal fragments, previous surgery, pregnancy) is answered, none left blank or 'unknown' without a plan.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive requests over 2 weeks.
- Change: Make the MRI safety questions mandatory fields on the electronic MRI request, with 'unknown' allowed only when a named person is chasing the information.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-257.csv`
- Pitfalls: Mandatory fields invite ticking 'no' without asking → compare the request answers with the radiographer's full screen.; Unconscious patients cannot answer → allow 'unknown: collateral being sought' and record who is chasing.; IT change is slow → use a required free-text line as an interim.; MRI staff and referrers blame each other → present the data jointly with the MR safety expert.
- Pearls: Recruit the MR safety expert and a senior MRI radiographer.; Measure delays caused by safety queries; they show the cost.; Keep the question list identical in both cycles.

**NNA-258. Is the median dose-length product of adult CT head scans for suspected stroke at or below the national diagnostic reference level of 790 mGy cm?** (new)
- Standard: IR(ME)R 2017 regulation 12(3)(c) and UKHSA National Diagnostic Reference Levels (updated 11 December 2025), table 1 adult CT: "(3) Without prejudice to paragraphs (1) and (2), the operator must select equipment and methods to ensure that for each exposure the dose of ionising radiation to the individual undergoing the exposure is as low as reasonably practicable and consistent with the intended diagnostic or therapeutic purpose and in doing so must have regard, in particular to— ... (c) adherence to such diagnostic reference levels for radiodiagnostic examinations falling within regulation 3(a), (b), (e) and (f) as the employer may have established, as set out in the employer’s procedures. UKHSA table 1: Head, Acute stroke, All sequences, CTDIvol 47 mGy, DLP 790 mGy cm, adopted 2022." https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/diagnostic-radiology-national-diagnostic-reference-levels-ndrls/ndrl
- Pass: Department level: median DLP ≤790 mGy cm per scanner. Patient level: DLP ≤790 mGy cm for the whole examination.. Target: Median ≤790 mGy cm on every scanner. Sample: 50 consecutive examinations per scanner over 2 weeks.
- Change: Review the stroke CT head protocol with the medical physics expert (tube current modulation, scan length, iterative reconstruction) and lock the optimised protocol on every scanner.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-258.csv`
- Pitfalls: Mixing stroke and trauma protocols → filter by clinical indication, not protocol name alone.; Repeat series inflate DLP → record them separately so the protocol is judged fairly.; One scanner drives the result → report per scanner.; Re-education alone gives small gains → change the protocol, not only behaviour.
- Pearls: Recruit the medical physics expert first; they can pull dose data quickly.; Use the NDRL version adopted in 2022 in both cycles.; Check image quality with a neuroradiologist after any protocol change.

**NNA-259. What proportion of CT aortograms for suspected acute aortic dissection from the ED are ECG-gated?** (new)
- Standard: RCEM Best Practice Guideline: Diagnosis of Thoracic Aortic Dissection in the Emergency Department (March 2025), introduction: "The diagnostic modality of choice is CT Aortogram (CTA) which has high diagnostic accuracy in discriminating the various components of TAD. Initial non-contrast acquisition should form part of the scanning protocol for its value in assessment of intramural haematoma. ... Arterial phase acquisition should routinely be performed with ECG synchronisation (ECG-gated scan) with the aim of producing motion-free images of the aortic root." https://www.rcr.ac.uk/media/hwubc3le/diagnosis-of-thoracic-aortic-dissection-in-the-ed-_updated-march-2025.pdf
- Pass: The arterial phase is ECG-gated (prospective or retrospective) as recorded in the scan protocol or DICOM series description.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive scans over the last 3 months.
- Change: Set a single 'CT aorta – suspected dissection' protocol on every scanner that is ECG-gated by default, with gating leads stocked in the CT room.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-259.csv`
- Pitfalls: Out-of-hours radiographers unfamiliar with gating → include night scans and give them a one-page set-up card.; Fast or irregular heart rates → record the reason when gating is not possible and accept it as a documented exception.; Series names vary by scanner → agree how gating is identified with the CT superintendent before cycle 1.; Small numbers → pool 3 months.
- Pearls: Recruit a cardiothoracic or vascular radiologist and the CT superintendent.; Report motion artefact as a secondary measure; it shows why gating matters.; Make the gated protocol the default so it survives staff turnover.

**NNA-260. What proportion of blood gas analyser tests in the ED are run under an operator ID with in-date recorded competence?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Management and use of IVD point of care test devices (updated 14 July 2026), section 4.2 training: "Only staff whose training and competence has been established and recorded should be permitted to carry out POCT. They should also receive continued support and regular updates." https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/in-vitro-diagnostic-point-of-care-test-devices/management-and-use-of-ivd-point-of-care-test-devices
- Pass: The test was run under a personal operator ID that is on the POCT training register with an in-date competence on the test date.. Target: ≥98%. Sample: All patient tests over 1 week (usually several hundred), from middleware.
- Change: Remove all shared operator IDs and switch on middleware lock-out for expired competence, with a 30-day reminder email to the user.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-260.csv`
- Pitfalls: Lock-out blocks a doctor during an emergency → keep a short-term emergency ID that is audited weekly.; Training capacity cannot meet demand → agree extra training sessions with the POCT team before the lock-out date.; Doctors rotating in without training → add POCT training to ED induction.; Middleware data unfamiliar to clinicians → ask the POCT coordinator to run the extract.
- Pearls: Recruit the POCT coordinator; they own the register and middleware.; Announce the lock-out date early and publish weekly counts of expired users.; Keep the same analysers in both cycles.

**NNA-261. What proportion of urine pregnancy tests done in the ED have a complete point-of-care test record in the patient notes?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Management and use of IVD point of care test devices (updated 14 July 2026), section 4.10 record keeping: "It is imperative to keep accurate records of patient results from POCT devices. These records should include: date; device type; batch numbers; result; operator identity; patient identity" https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/in-vitro-diagnostic-point-of-care-test-devices/management-and-use-of-ivd-point-of-care-test-devices
- Pass: The patient record shows all six items: date, test type, batch number, result, tester identity and patient identity.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive tests over 2 weeks.
- Change: Add a structured 'urine pregnancy test' entry in the ED EPR with required fields for result, batch number and tester, and move testing to one area with the kits.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-261.csv`
- Pitfalls: Tests done but not documented → count only what is in the record; say so in advance.; Batch number seen as bureaucracy → share the MHRA recall case study.; Kits in several places → put them in one place next to the EPR terminal.; Paper and electronic records mixed → check both in cycle 1 and cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED senior nurse and the POCT coordinator.; Use the same age band and exclusion rules in both cycles.; Stick the batch number label on the kit box to make copying easy.

**NNA-262. What proportion of repeat liver function test requests on adult inpatients are made less than 72 hours after the previous one?** (under-audited)
- Standard: RCPath G147 National minimum retesting intervals in pathology (version 2, March 2021), B-L2 liver, acute inpatient setting: "B-L2 Acute inpatient setting — Testing at 72-hour intervals in acute setting (apart from those in L4). B-L4 Acute poisoning (e.g. paracetamol), TPN, liver unit, acute liver injury and ITU patients — May require more frequent monitoring." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/g147-minimum-retesting-intervals-in-pathology.html
- Pass: The repeat LFT is 72 hours or more after the previous LFT, or the patient is in a B-L4 group.. Target: ≥80% of repeat requests at 72 hours or more (or an exception). Sample: 100 consecutive repeat LFT requests over 1 week (from the LIMS).
- Change: Add a minimum retesting interval rule in the electronic order system: a repeat LFT within 72 hours shows the last result and needs a reason from a pick-list.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-262.csv`
- Pitfalls: LFTs bundled in a 'daily bloods' order set → change the order set as well as the rule.; Hard blocks anger clinicians → use a soft stop with a reason, not a block.; Exception groups not flagged in the LIMS → check exclusions against the EPR for a sample.; Gains fade after rotation → keep the rule, not only teaching.
- Pearls: Recruit the duty biochemist and a digital clinical safety officer.; Show the cost and carbon of avoidable tests per month.; Report by ward; it makes the change local.

**NNA-263. What proportion of ED blood samples for urea and electrolytes are reported as haemolysed?** (new)
- Standard: Local standard: "No more than 5% of ED urea and electrolyte samples should be reported as haemolysed (haemolysis index above the laboratory's potassium reporting limit)." 
- Pass: The sample is not flagged as haemolysed above the potassium reporting limit.. Target: ≤5%. Sample: All ED samples over 1 week (from the LIMS), with a notes review of 50 haemolysed samples for collection method.
- Change: Stock and default to vacuum tube collection through a needle or butterfly (no syringe transfer), and send ED samples by pneumatic tube at the lower speed setting agreed with the lab.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-263.csv`
- Pitfalls: Lab changes the haemolysis threshold between cycles → fix the threshold before cycle 1.; Collection method rarely documented → use a short 2-week sticker on the request to capture it.; Pneumatic tube settings are outside ED control → involve estates and the lab early.; Staff feel blamed → report by area, not by person.
- Pearls: Recruit the lab quality manager and an ED nurse educator.; Measure delay to a valid potassium; it shows the patient impact.; Feed back the rate monthly on the ED safety board.

**NNA-264. What proportion of unexpected cancers found in routine surgical specimens have a documented action by the requesting team within 14 days of the report?** (new)
- Standard: RCPath G133 Best practice recommendations: communication of unexpected findings, urgent reports, delayed reports and the use of alert systems in diagnostic cellular pathology (version 2, February 2026), sections 2 and 4: "Service users sending specimens should have systems in place to ensure that all histopathology reports are read by someone who is suitably trained and qualified to read and interpret them correctly, in the context of the patient’s clinical details. ... In participating in the use of a coding or alert system, pathologists and their employing authorities should make it clear that it is the responsibility of the requesting clinician to have systems in place to ensure timely reading and acting upon of a laboratory report." https://www.rcpath.org/resourceLibrary/g133-bpr-communication-of-unexpected-findings-urgent-reports-delayed-reports-and-the-use-of-alert-systems-.html
- Pass: Within 14 days of report authorisation the notes show an action: MDT referral, clinic appointment, or letter to patient and GP with a plan.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive cases over the last 6–12 months (SNOMED search).
- Change: Pathologists add an 'unexpected malignancy' alert code that places the case on the relevant MDT list automatically and sends a copy to the MDT coordinator.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-264.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers per month → use a 6–12 month retrospective window for cycle 1.; Pre-malignant lesions debated → list the included diagnoses with a pathologist before cycle 1.; Surgeon on leave when report arrives → auto-listing to the MDT removes the single point of failure.; Letters sent but no plan → count only actions that state what happens next.
- Pearls: Recruit a consultant histopathologist and an MDT coordinator.; Include specialties beyond general surgery; gynaecology and urology specimens are often forgotten.; Track the time to MDT discussion as a secondary measure.

## Patient safety and nursing care

**NNA-265. What proportion of adult ward inpatients with a NEWS2 score of 5 or more have their next full set of observations recorded within 1 hour?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Royal College of Physicians, NEWS2 Chart 4: Clinical response to the NEWS trigger thresholds (2017): "Total 5 or more — Urgent response threshold — Frequency of monitoring: Minimum 1 hourly" https://www.rcp.ac.uk/media/mrllczrj/news2-chart-4_clinical-response-to-news-trigger-thresholds_0.pdf
- Pass: The next complete NEWS2 set (all seven parameters) is timed within 60 minutes of the first score of 5 or more.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes of a first NEWS2 score of 5 or more across 3–4 wards over the last 4 weeks (one episode per patient).
- Change: Set the electronic observations system to schedule and alert the next set at 1 hour automatically whenever NEWS2 is 5 or more, with an overdue alert to the nurse in charge.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-265.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper and electronic records both in use → audit one ward type only, or confirm every ward uses the electronic system before cycle 1.; Night-time repeats look worse because of staffing → report day and night separately so the fix is aimed correctly.; Nurses object that the alert adds noise → set the alert only for scores of 5 or more, not for every set.; A partial set (e.g. saturations only) gets counted as a repeat → define 'full set' as all seven NEWS2 parameters before cycle 1.; Gains fade when the alert is switched off after an upgrade → name the system owner who keeps the setting.
- Pearls: Recruit the critical care outreach lead; they already review these reports.; Pull the trigger list from the observations system; it takes minutes, not hours.; Agree what counts as 'next set' with the ward sisters before collecting.; Share each ward's result with its own sister, not just the trust total.

**NNA-266. What proportion of adult ward inpatients at high risk from sepsis (suspected infection and NEWS2 of 7 or more) have NEWS2 recalculated within 30 minutes?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG253 rec 1.6.5 (2024; guideline published 2025, updated 2026): "Recalculate the NEWS2 score and re-evaluate risk of sepsis periodically, in line with the AoMRC statement on the initial antimicrobial treatment of sepsis (2022): every 30 minutes, for those at high risk of severe illness or death from sepsis" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Evaluating-risk
- Pass: A complete NEWS2 is recorded within 30 minutes of the first qualifying score of 7 or more.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a 30-minute repeat observation timer to the sepsis screening tool in the electronic observations system that starts when a high-risk screen is completed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-266.csv`
- Pitfalls: Suspected infection is not always written down → use the sepsis screen or the first antibiotic prescription as the marker, agreed before cycle 1.; Patients moved to ED resus or critical care drop out of the ward record → exclude transfers within 30 minutes and count them.; Small numbers per ward → pool all adult wards.; Staff see the re-score as a nursing task only → make the timer visible to the doctor reviewing the patient too.
- Pearls: Recruit the sepsis nurse or outreach team; they already hold the list of high-risk screens.; Collect time-to-antibiotics as a context field, but keep the re-score as the single measure.; Agree that the first qualifying score is time zero before starting.

**NNA-267. What proportion of adult inpatients started on intravenous antibiotics for suspected sepsis have blood cultures taken before the first dose?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG253 rec 1.8.1 (2016, amended 2024; guideline published 2025, updated 2026): "For people in hospital who have suspected infections: start looking for the source of infection (see the section on finding and controlling the source of infection) take microbiological and blood samples before giving an antimicrobial." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Managing-suspected-sepsis
- Pass: Blood culture collection time is earlier than or the same as the first antibiotic administration time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add blood culture bottles and a 'cultures taken? time' box to the sepsis grab bag and the electronic sepsis screen, so the antibiotic order set asks for the culture time before the first dose is signed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-267.csv`
- Pitfalls: Staff fear delaying antibiotics → state that culture must not delay the first dose beyond the NICE time limit.; Collection time on the lab system is the label print time → check a few against the notes before cycle 1.; Out-of-hours shortages of culture bottles → check stock in the grab bags as part of the fix.; Contamination rises when cultures are rushed → record contaminants so the fix does not trade one problem for another.
- Pearls: Recruit a microbiologist; they can pull collection times in bulk.; Pair with the pharmacy antibiotic report to find cases fast.; Keep the grab bag checklist owned by the ward sister, not the audit team.

**NNA-268. What proportion of adult ward inpatients at medium risk from sepsis (suspected infection and NEWS2 of 5 or 6) who are given antibiotics receive the first dose within 3 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG253 rec 1.8.17 (2024; guideline published 2025, updated 2026): "For people at medium risk of severe illness or death from sepsis, a clinician with core competencies in the care of acutely ill patients (FY2 level or above) should consider: deferring administration of a broad-spectrum antibiotic treatment for up to 3 hours after calculating the person's first NEWS2 score on initial assessment in the emergency department or on ward deterioration and using this time to gather information for a more specific diagnosis (see recommendations on finding and controlling the source of infection and choice of antibiotic therapy) discussing with a senior clinical decision maker. Once a decision is made to give antibiotics, do not delay administration any further." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Managing-suspected-sepsis
- Pass: First antibiotic dose given within 3 hours of the first NEWS2 of 5 or 6 with suspected infection.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Make the first antibiotic dose for suspected sepsis a 'stat' order in electronic prescribing that alerts the nurse in charge if not given within 30 minutes of prescribing.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-268.csv`
- Pitfalls: Time zero is unclear when the score drifts up and down → use the first 5 or 6 with a documented infection concern, agreed before cycle 1.; Clinicians read 3 hours as a target → present the delay after decision as a second number.; Patients who never needed antibiotics get included → only include those given antibiotics.; The stat alert is ignored → route it to the nurse in charge, not a generic inbox.
- Pearls: Recruit an antimicrobial pharmacist; they can pull first-dose times.; Show the result as a run chart of minutes, not only a percentage.; Keep medium and high risk separate so each gets its own standard.

**NNA-269. What proportion of inpatients aged 65 or over with a postural drop in blood pressure have a documented medication review within 48 hours of the reading?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG249 rec 1.2.3 (2025): "Ensure that the person's individual risk factors identified in the comprehensive falls assessment are promptly addressed with appropriate interventions to reduce their risk of falls. These interventions can be offered in the same service or involve an appropriate referral." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng249/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Within 48 hours of the reading, the notes record a review of blood-pressure-lowering and other falls-risk medicines, with each stopped, reduced or kept with a reason.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients with a postural drop over the last 2–3 months on care of older people and acute medical wards.
- Change: Add an automatic 'postural drop: medication review needed' task to the doctors' ward task list when a postural drop is entered on the electronic observations chart.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-269.csv`
- Pitfalls: Readings taken with the wrong technique create false drops → record the standing time and exclude readings without it.; Doctors say review happened but was not written → count only written reviews and agree this before cycle 1.; Pharmacist and doctor reviews overlap → accept either, as long as a decision per drug is recorded.; The automatic task is dismissed without action → audit the notes, not the task completion log.
- Pearls: Recruit the ward pharmacist; they often do the review anyway.; Pull cases from the observations system rather than reading every chart.; Keep the 48-hour window; same-day is unrealistic at weekends.

**NNA-270. What proportion of inpatients who fall have a documented check for fracture and spinal injury before they are moved from the floor?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS86 statement 4 (2015, updated 2025): "People who fall during a hospital stay are checked for signs or symptoms of fracture and potential for spinal injury before they are moved. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs86/chapter/Quality-statement-4-Checks-for-injury-after-an-inpatient-fall
- Pass: Nursing post-fall record states that the patient was checked for signs of fracture and spinal injury before being moved.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive reported inpatient falls over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Replace free-text post-fall notes with a one-page post-fall nursing checklist (paper or EPR) whose first two tick boxes are fracture check and spinal check before moving.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-270.csv`
- Pitfalls: Incident reports are completed late and by a different person → use the contemporaneous nursing note as the source.; Unwitnessed falls where the patient is already standing → record separately and exclude from the denominator.; Nurses feel audited as individuals → present by ward, never by name.; Checklist becomes one more form → replace the existing post-fall note rather than add to it.
- Pearls: Recruit the falls prevention nurse lead early.; Agree with the ward sisters exactly what counts as a documented check.; Put the checklist in the post-fall grab folder on each ward.

**NNA-271. What proportion of inpatients who hit their head in a fall have neurological observations recorded half-hourly for the first 2 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG232 rec 1.9.11 (2003, current guideline 2023); applied to inpatients by the local post-fall protocol: "Carry out and record observations on a half-hourly basis until there is a GCS score of 15. Observations for people with a GCS score of 15 should start after the initial assessment in the emergency department and the minimum frequency should be: half-hourly for 2 hours, then 1 hourly for 4 hours, then 2 hourly. [2003]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng232/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Four recorded neurological observation sets (GCS, pupils, limb movements) in the first 2 hours after the fall, none more than 40 minutes apart.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive falls with head strike or unwitnessed falls over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a neurological observation schedule (half-hourly for 2 hours, then hourly for 4 hours) to the electronic observations system that is started from the post-fall checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-271.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper neuro charts get lost → confirm where charts are filed before cycle 1.; Night falls with one nurse per bay → report night falls separately.; Unwitnessed falls without clear head injury inflate the denominator → apply one definition in both cycles.; Staff wake patients repeatedly and families object → explain why the first 6 hours matter; frequency drops after that.
- Pearls: Recruit the falls lead and a ward sister from a high-falls ward.; Use the incident system's 'head injury' tick box to find cases quickly.; Record anticoagulant use; it makes the case for the fix.

**NNA-272. What proportion of adults admitted to hospital have a pressure ulcer risk assessment completed within 6 hours of arriving on the ward?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS89 statement 1 (2015): "People admitted to hospital or a care home with nursing have a pressure ulcer risk assessment within 6 hours of admission." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs89/chapter/Quality-statement-1-Pressure-ulcer-risk-assessment-in-hospitals-and-care-homes-with-nursing
- Pass: A validated risk assessment (e.g. Waterlow, Braden or PURPOSE-T) is time-stamped within 6 hours of ward arrival.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions across 3–4 wards over 2 weeks.
- Change: Make the pressure ulcer risk assessment a mandatory field in the EPR nursing admission document, with a ward-level overdue list at 4 hours.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-272.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients waiting many hours in ED → decide before cycle 1 whether the clock starts at ED arrival or ward arrival, and keep it the same.; Assessments copied from the ED record → accept an ED assessment only if timed within the window.; Nurses see it as a tick box → show the ulcer incidence alongside the result.; Mandatory field filled with defaults → spot-check 5 scores against the patient's condition.
- Pearls: Recruit the tissue viability nurse; they often hold ward data.; EPR timestamps make this a 1-hour data pull.; Feed results back to each ward sister within a week.

**NNA-273. What proportion of adults at high risk of pressure ulcers who cannot reposition themselves are repositioned at least every 4 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG179 rec 1.1.9 (2014): "Encourage adults who have been assessed as being at high risk of developing a pressure ulcer to change their position frequently and at least every 4 hours. If they are unable to reposition themselves, offer help to do so, using appropriate equipment if needed. Document the frequency of repositioning required." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg179/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Over a 24-hour period, no gap longer than 4 hours between recorded repositions, and the required frequency is documented.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 patients on 3–4 wards: one 24-hour period each, over 2 weeks.
- Change: Add a repositioning due-time alert to the electronic SSKIN chart that shows on the ward whiteboard or handheld devices when a turn is overdue.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-273.csv`
- Pitfalls: Charts completed retrospectively at shift end → look for identical times and flag them.; Night gaps to let patients sleep → the pass criterion allows a documented clinical reason; agree this before cycle 1.; Air mattresses used as a reason not to turn → record mattress type but do not exempt.; Healthcare assistants feel blamed → present by ward and involve them in designing the alert.
- Pearls: Recruit a healthcare assistant champion on each ward.; Pick one fixed 24-hour window per patient to keep collection fast.; Ask the tissue viability nurse to co-present the results.

**NNA-274. What proportion of adult inpatients with a MUST score of 2 or more have a nutrition care plan documented within 48 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS24 statement 2 (2012): "People who are malnourished or at risk of malnutrition have a management care plan that aims to meet their complete nutritional requirements." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs24/chapter/Quality-statement-2-Treatment
- Pass: Within 48 hours of the MUST score of 2 or more, a written nutrition care plan is in the notes (e.g. food record chart, fortified diet or supplements prescribed, with a review date).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients with MUST 2 or more over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Set the EPR so that a MUST score of 2 or more automatically sends a dietitian referral and opens a nutrition care plan for the nurse to complete.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-274.csv`
- Pitfalls: MUST scores calculated wrongly → check a sample of scores against weight and BMI.; Dietitian capacity worries → agree triage with the dietetic lead before switching on referrals.; Supplements prescribed but not given → record administration as a context field.; Weekend admissions miss the 48-hour window → report weekend admissions separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the lead dietitian; they can pull referral times.; Use the EPR MUST report to find cases.; Share results at the ward nurse huddle.

**NNA-275. What proportion of adults with delirium started on an antipsychotic in hospital have it stopped within 7 days, or a documented reason to continue?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG103 rec 1.7.4 (2010, guideline updated 2023): "If a person with delirium is distressed or considered a risk to themselves or others, and verbal and non-verbal de-escalation techniques are ineffective or inappropriate, consider giving short-term haloperidol (usually for 1 week or less). Start at the lowest clinically appropriate dose and titrate cautiously according to symptoms." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The antipsychotic is stopped by day 7, or the notes on day 7 record a senior review with a reason to continue and a stop date.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive patients over the last 3 months.
- Change: Set a default 7-day stop date on antipsychotic prescriptions with the indication 'delirium' in e-prescribing, so continuing needs an active re-prescription with a reason.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-275.csv`
- Pitfalls: Delirium not written as the indication → search e-prescribing for all new antipsychotic starts in people aged 65 or over and confirm from notes.; Psychiatry liaison continued the drug → accept their documented reason and stop date as a pass.; As-required doses never given are counted → record doses given, but judge the prescription.; Default stop dates annoy prescribers → allow a one-click extension with a reason.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward pharmacist and a liaison psychiatrist.; Include the discharge prescription; that is where harm spreads.; Report the number still on an antipsychotic at discharge as a second figure.

**NNA-276. What proportion of adult ward inpatients on intravenous fluids have a fluid balance chart totalled for the previous 24 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG174 rec 1.2.4 (2013, updated 2017): "All patients continuing to receive IV fluids need regular monitoring. This should initially include at least daily reassessments of clinical fluid status, laboratory values (urea, creatinine and electrolytes) and fluid balance charts, along with weight measurement twice weekly." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg174/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The chart for the previous full 24 hours shows a total input, a total output and a net balance.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 patients on 4 wards on 2 separate audit days (point prevalence).
- Change: Switch to an electronic fluid balance chart that totals input, output and net balance automatically at a set time each day (or, on paper, add a printed 24-hour total box signed by the night nurse in charge).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-276.csv`
- Pitfalls: Charts started for patients who do not need them dilute effort → audit only patients on IV fluids.; Oral intake is not recorded, so totals look low → note if oral intake is missing but still count the total.; Night staff blamed → agree who totals the chart before cycle 1.; Gains fade after the audit [9398] → build the total into the chart itself, not into reminders.
- Pearls: Recruit the AKI nurse specialist.; Audit on two different weekdays to avoid one bad day skewing results.; Photograph paper charts (no identifiers) to show what 'totalled' looks like.

**NNA-277. What proportion of inpatients with an indwelling urinary catheter have a documented reason for it that is still valid on the audit day?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG139 rec 1.2.2.2 (2003, guideline updated 2017): "The patient's clinical need for catheterisation should be reviewed regularly and the urinary catheter removed as soon as possible. [2003]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg139/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes or catheter care plan record an indication from an agreed list (e.g. retention, hourly output monitoring in unstable patients, sacral wound healing, end-of-life comfort, urology plan), and that indication still applies on the audit day.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 catheterised patients across medical and surgical wards on 2 audit days (point prevalence).
- Change: Add a daily 'catheter still needed?' question to the nursing care rounds in the EPR, with a nurse-led removal protocol when no valid indication is ticked.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-277.csv`
- Pitfalls: Doctors and nurses disagree on what counts as valid → agree the indication list with urology and infection prevention before cycle 1.; Nurses worry about removing catheters without a doctor → the protocol must name who can remove.; Point prevalence misses short catheters → run two audit days a week apart.; Retention after removal worries staff → include a bladder scan step in the protocol.
- Pearls: Recruit the continence nurse and an infection prevention nurse.; A ward walk-round finds catheters faster than the EPR.; Use the catheter passport if the trust already has one.

**NNA-278. What proportion of adults with an indwelling urinary catheter and suspected urinary infection are diagnosed without using a urine dipstick?** (new)
- Standard: NICE QS90 statement 2 (2015, updated 2023): "Adults with indwelling urinary catheters do not have dipstick testing to diagnose urinary tract infections (UTIs). [2015, updated 2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs90/chapter/Quality-statement-2-Diagnosing-urinary-tract-infections-in-adults-with-catheters
- Pass: No urine dipstick result is recorded or cited in the decision to diagnose or treat the suspected UTI.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a hard stop to the EPR urinalysis entry for patients flagged as catheterised ('Dipstick not valid with a catheter: send culture if symptomatic'), and remove dipsticks from the catheter trolley.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-278.csv`
- Pitfalls: Dipsticks done but not written down → ask nurses on the audit wards and record 'unknown' honestly.; Doctors worry about missing sepsis → the fix sends a culture; it does not stop treatment.; Catheter status not flagged in the EPR → the fix needs a catheter flag first.; Result improves only on the pilot ward → roll out the EPR stop trust-wide once shown to work.
- Pearls: Recruit an antimicrobial pharmacist and the continence nurse.; Pull cases from the lab 'catheter specimen' list.; Record antibiotic starts; the saving makes the case to managers.

**NNA-279. What proportion of peripheral cannulas in place on adult wards have been used in the previous 24 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS61 statement 5 (2014): "People who need a vascular access device have their risk of infection minimised by the completion of specified procedures necessary for the safe insertion and maintenance of the device and its removal as soon as it is no longer needed." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs61/chapter/Quality-statement-5-Vascular-access-devices
- Pass: The cannula has been used for an IV drug, fluid or blood product in the previous 24 hours, or a documented reason to keep it (e.g. high risk of deterioration) is recorded.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40–50 cannulas across 4 wards on 2 audit days (point prevalence).
- Change: Add a daily 'cannula needed?' prompt to the VIP score entry in the EPR, with nurse-led removal if unused for 24 hours and no reason recorded.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-279.csv`
- Pitfalls: Doctors keep cannulas 'just in case' → allow a documented reason; the aim is conscious decisions.; Oral switch not done, so cannula looks used → note IV antibiotics that could have been switched as a context finding.; Point prevalence misses short stays → use two days a week apart.; Nurses unsure they may remove cannulas → confirm the trust policy allows nurse-led removal.
- Pearls: Recruit the infection prevention nurse; they may already run a cannula audit.; Do the walk-round at mid-morning after drug rounds.; Show the number of idle cannula days per month; it is a striking figure.

**NNA-280. What proportion of adult ward inpatients receiving oxygen have it signed for on the drug chart at every drug round?** (under-audited)
- Standard: British Thoracic Society guideline for oxygen use in adults in healthcare and emergency settings, summary of recommendations (2017), section 4: "Oxygen should be prescribed and a signature should be entered on the drug chart on each drug round." https://www.brit-thoracic.org.uk/document-library/guidelines/emergency-oxygen/bts-guideline-for-oxygen-use-in-healthcare-and-emergency-settings-summary-of-recommendations/
- Pass: Oxygen signed (or electronically recorded as administered, with device and flow) at every drug round in the previous 24 hours.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 patients across 4–5 wards on 2 audit days (point prevalence).
- Change: Make oxygen a scheduled administration on e-prescribing at each drug round (or a pre-printed oxygen box on the paper chart) so it appears on the nurse's medicines round list.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-280.csv`
- Pitfalls: Oxygen treated as 'not a drug' by staff → show the BTS wording at ward huddles.; E-prescribing lists oxygen as 'as required', so it never appears on the round → change it to scheduled with the pharmacy system team.; Patients who stopped oxygen mid-day confuse counts → count only rounds while on oxygen.; Gains fade after the audit → build it into the system, not a poster.
- Pearls: Recruit a respiratory nurse specialist and the e-prescribing pharmacist.; Collect device and target range as context; they show the wider picture.; Audit on two different weekdays.

**NNA-281. What proportion of DNACPR decisions made in hospital record a discussion with the patient or those close to them, or the reason no discussion took place?** (under-audited)
- Standard: BMA, Resuscitation Council UK and RCN, Decisions relating to cardiopulmonary resuscitation, 3rd edition (1st revision) 2016, section 15: "Good documentation includes the following, on the CPR decision form or in the main health record or in some cases both: … details of any discussions about the decision with the patient, with those close to them, and (for a person who does not have capacity) with any person with authority to make decisions on their behalf and/ or with an IMCA • where no discussions have taken place the reasons for this" https://www.resus.org.uk/sites/default/files/2020-05/20160123%20Decisions%20Relating%20to%20CPR%20-%202016.pdf
- Pass: The form or notes record who the decision was discussed with and when, or state a specific reason why no discussion took place (e.g. patient lacks capacity and no family reachable, with a plan to contact them).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive new decisions over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Make 'discussed with' and 'reason not discussed' mandatory fields that must be completed before an electronic DNACPR or ReSPECT form can be signed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-281.csv`
- Pitfalls: Discussions happen but are written in a different part of the notes → check both the form and the notes.; Decisions made at night with family unreachable → a clear reason and plan counts as a pass.; Clinicians feel their judgement is being questioned → audit the record, not the decision.; Mandatory fields filled with 'N/A' → spot-check 5 forms for meaningful content.
- Pearls: Recruit the resuscitation officer; they hold the register.; Involve a palliative care consultant in presenting the results.; Share one anonymised good example with every audited team.

**NNA-282. What proportion of DNACPR decisions made by a doctor other than the responsible consultant are endorsed by that consultant within 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: BMA, Resuscitation Council UK and RCN, Decisions relating to cardiopulmonary resuscitation, 3rd edition (1st revision) 2016, section 15: "if that is not the senior responsible clinician, the name and position of the senior responsible clinician, who should review and if appropriate endorse the decision at the earliest opportunity" https://www.resus.org.uk/sites/default/files/2020-05/20160123%20Decisions%20Relating%20to%20CPR%20-%202016.pdf
- Pass: Consultant endorsement (signature or documented review) dated within 24 hours of the decision (local operational limit for 'at the earliest opportunity').. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive qualifying decisions over the last 1–2 months.
- Change: Add unendorsed DNACPR decisions to the daily post-take and board-round list generated by the EPR, so the consultant sees and signs them on the next ward round.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-282.csv`
- Pitfalls: Weekend decisions wait until Monday → report weekday and weekend separately; agree 24 hours includes weekends.; Endorsement recorded only as a signature without a date → count undated signatures as fail and fix the form.; Consultants feel second-guessed → frame it as protecting junior colleagues.; ReSPECT and DNACPR forms coexist → include both and record which.
- Pearls: Recruit an acute medicine consultant as sponsor.; The resuscitation officer's register makes case-finding quick.; Present decisions changed at review; they show why endorsement matters.

**NNA-283. What proportion of infants who are not independently mobile and attend the emergency department with a bruise have a safeguarding assessment by a paediatrician before discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG89 rec 1.1.2 (2009, updated 2025): "Suspect child maltreatment if there is bruising or petechiae (tiny red or purple spots) that are not caused by a medical condition (for example, a causative coagulation disorder) and if the explanation for the bruising is unsuitable. Examples include: bruising in a child who is not independently mobile" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg89/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A paediatrician's safeguarding assessment (history, full skin examination and documented outcome, including social care check or referral) is recorded before the infant leaves the ED or paediatric unit.. Target: 100%. Sample: All qualifying attendances over the last 6 months (expect 20–40 in a district general hospital).
- Change: Add a mandatory 'mobile? bruise?' question to ED triage for children under 12 months that auto-refers any bruise in a non-mobile infant to the paediatric registrar.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-283.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers → use a 6-month window and report every case, not just a percentage.; Bruise mentioned only in the examination, not triage → search free text as well as codes.; Mobility not recorded → record 'not recorded' as its own category; it is a finding.; Parents' distress makes staff reluctant to refer → the triage rule removes individual judgement.
- Pearls: Recruit the named doctor for safeguarding children as co-lead.; Ask the ED IT lead for a free-text search of 'bruise' in under-1s.; Use the local safeguarding partnership bruising protocol for the referral steps.

## Oncology, haematology and transfusion

**NNA-284. What proportion of adults with suspected neutropenic sepsis receive their first dose of intravenous antibiotics within 1 hour of arrival at hospital?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG151 rec 1.4.1.1 (2012, updated 2024): "Treat suspected neutropenic sepsis as an acute medical emergency and offer empiric antibiotic therapy immediately." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First dose of intravenous empiric antibiotic given (administration time on the drug chart or EPR) within 60 minutes of the arrival time, or of the time the patient became unwell if already an inpatient. The 60-minute limit is the audit's measure of 'immediately'.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 3–4 months (ED, acute medical unit, oncology/haematology wards and assessment unit).
- Change: Introduce a patient group direction so the triage or assessment-unit nurse gives the first dose of piperacillin with tazobactam without waiting for a doctor or a neutrophil count.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-284.csv`
- Pitfalls: Arrival time and administration time sit in different systems → agree before cycle 1 which timestamp counts, and use administration time only.; Patients who were never neutropenic get excluded later, changing the denominator → include all treated as suspected neutropenic sepsis and report confirmed cases separately.; ED nurses may object to a PGD they have not been trained on → agree the PGD with pharmacy and the ED nurse educator before launch.; Gains fade when the audit team rotates → hand the dashboard to the acute oncology nurse team.
- Pearls: Recruit the acute oncology nurse; they already see most of these patients within 24 hours.; Stock piperacillin with tazobactam in the ED resus cupboard so the dose is not delayed by pharmacy.; Ask the chemotherapy unit to give patients an alert card to show at triage.; Report median minutes as well as the percentage so small improvements are visible.

**NNA-285. What proportion of adults treated for suspected neutropenic sepsis receive no aminoglycoside in their initial empiric antibiotic regimen, unless a reason is documented?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG151 rec 1.4.3.2 (2012, updated 2024): "Do not offer an aminoglycoside, either as monotherapy or in dual therapy, for the initial empiric treatment of suspected neutropenic sepsis unless there are patient-specific or local microbiological indications." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No aminoglycoside prescribed in the first 24 hours of empiric treatment, or an aminoglycoside given with a patient-specific or microbiological reason written in the notes (e.g. penicillin allergy regimen, known resistant organism, septic shock per local policy).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Remove gentamicin from the neutropenic sepsis antibiotic order set and the sepsis page of the local antimicrobial app, leaving piperacillin with tazobactam alone as the default with a separate penicillin-allergy option.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-285.csv`
- Pitfalls: Local policy may still recommend gentamicin for shock or allergy → agree the local exceptions with microbiology before cycle 1 and count them as passes.; ED sepsis protocols override the oncology guideline → change the ED sepsis page at the same time.; Small numbers if few patients get gentamicin → report the count as well as the percentage.; Microbiologists may object without local resistance data → bring the latest bacteraemia sensitivity report to the meeting.
- Pearls: Recruit the oncology or antimicrobial pharmacist; they can check every new episode within 24 hours.; Check the penicillin-allergy regimen at the same time so the fix does not leave a gap.; Share one-page results with ED and acute medicine, where most first doses are prescribed.; Build the order set so the default is the correct regimen with one click.

**NNA-286. What proportion of adults with confirmed neutropenic sepsis have a validated risk score (such as MASCC) documented within 24 hours of presentation?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG151 rec 1.5.1.1 (2012, updated 2024): "A healthcare professional with competence in managing complications of anticancer treatment should assess the patient's risk of septic complications within 24 hours of presentation to secondary or tertiary care, basing the risk assessment on presentation features and using a validated risk scoring system." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A named validated score (MASCC or CISNE) with its total written in the notes or EPR within 24 hours of arrival.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive confirmed episodes over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory MASCC score field to the acute oncology assessment form in the EPR, completed at the first acute oncology review.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-286.csv`
- Pitfalls: Scores written by the medical team before neutrophils are known → count only scores recorded after the count is available.; Weekend admissions without acute oncology cover → record day of admission and report weekends separately.; Staff tick 'low risk' without a number → a pass needs the named score and its total.; The field is added but nobody acts on it → track step-down to oral antibiotics in the re-audit.
- Pearls: Recruit the acute oncology nurse specialist to own the field.; Print the MASCC score on the back of the neutropenic sepsis pathway so it is scored at the bedside.; Agree with consultants before cycle 1 which score counts as validated.; Link the score to a one-line step-down plan so it has a purpose.

**NNA-287. What proportion of adults starting a new course of chemotherapy have documented written and verbal information on neutropenic sepsis and the 24-hour oncology helpline before cycle 1?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG151 rec 1.1.1.1 (2012, updated 2024): "Provide patients having anticancer treatment and their carers with written and oral information, both before starting and throughout their anticancer treatment, on: neutropenic sepsis; how and when to contact 24-hour specialist oncology advice; how and when to seek emergency care." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The pre-treatment consultation, consent form or chemotherapy nurse assessment records that neutropenic sepsis was explained, a written leaflet or alert card with the 24-hour helpline number was given, and this was dated before the first dose.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive new starts over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a required 'neutropenic sepsis information and alert card given' tick box, with date, to the chemotherapy pre-assessment template that must be completed before cycle 1 is released.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-287.csv`
- Pitfalls: Information is given but written in a place you do not search → list every document type before cycle 1 (consent form, nurse assessment, clinic letter).; Oral chemotherapy started in clinic by a different team → include oral starts and record the team.; Nurses object to another tick box → replace a free-text field rather than adding a new page.; Leaflet stock runs out → ask the unit manager to keep alert cards with the consent packs.
- Pearls: Recruit the chemotherapy nurse lead; they run pre-assessment.; Use the UKONS or local alert card so the helpline number is always the same.; Check oral chemotherapy clinics as well; they are often missed.; Ask two patients at the re-audit to show you their card; it makes the result real for staff.

**NNA-288. What proportion of adults starting rituximab for a haematological cancer have hepatitis B surface antigen and core antibody results available before the first dose?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG165 rec 1.5.48 (2013, updated 2017): "In people who are HBsAg negative and anti-HBc positive (regardless of anti-HBs status) and are starting rituximab or other B cell-depleting therapies: offer prophylaxis with lamivudine; start prophylaxis before beginning immunosuppressive therapy and continue for a minimum of 6 months after stopping immunosuppressive therapy." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg165/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Both HBsAg and anti-HBc results reported on the results system before the date of the first rituximab dose.. Target: 100%. Sample: 40 consecutive new starts over the last 6 months.
- Change: Make HBsAg and anti-HBc results a hard-stop item on the cycle 1 pre-treatment checklist in the chemotherapy e-prescribing system, checked by the pharmacist before release.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-288.csv`
- Pitfalls: Serology done at another hospital is not visible → check referral letters and record 'external result' as a pass only if the result is written.; Low numbers in a small unit → include all new starts over 6 months.; Clinicians argue that anti-HBc is not needed → quote CG165 1.5.48; prophylaxis decisions depend on it.; The hard stop is overridden → report overrides at the re-audit.
- Pearls: Recruit the haematology pharmacist who verifies cycle 1.; Add HBV serology to the lymphoma new-patient blood order set.; Check that positive results trigger a hepatology or virology referral as a secondary measure.; Ask pharmacy to keep the hard stop on the checklist after the audit team leaves.

**NNA-289. What proportion of adults starting fluorouracil or capecitabine have a DPD deficiency test result documented before the first dose?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, October 2020: "Patients with complete or partial dihydropyrimidine dehydrogenase (DPD) deficiency are at increased risk of severe and fatal toxicity during treatment with these medicines. All patients should be tested for DPD deficiency before initiation to minimise the risk of these reactions." https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/5-fluorouracil-intravenous-capecitabine-tegafur-dpd-testing-recommended-before-initiation-to-identify-patients-at-increased-risk-of-severe-and-fatal-toxicity
