- Pass: A DPYD genotype (or DPD phenotype) result is on the results system or in the notes, dated before the first dose of intravenous fluorouracil or capecitabine.. Target: 100%. Sample: 40 consecutive new starts over the last 3 months.
- Change: Make a DPYD result a hard-stop field on the cycle 1 prescription in the chemotherapy e-prescribing system, so the pharmacist cannot verify cycle 1 without it.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-289.csv`
- Pitfalls: Urgent starts before the result returns → record the documented reason and report them separately.; Result sent to a genetics portal the team cannot see → confirm where results live before cycle 1.; Oncologists may fear delays → share the laboratory turnaround time and request the test at the first clinic visit.; Variant found but dose not reduced → collect dose adjustment as a secondary field.
- Pearls: Recruit the oncology pharmacist; they verify every cycle 1.; Add the DPYD test to the colorectal and breast new-patient blood order sets.; Ask the laboratory for the median turnaround so clinics can plan.; Present one near-miss case at the meeting; it wins support fast.

**NNA-290. What proportion of adults given a non-urgent red cell transfusion on medical or haematology wards have a documented discussion of the reason, risks and alternatives before the transfusion starts?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 recs 1.14.2 and 1.14.3 (2015, updated 2026): "Provide verbal and written information to people who may have or who have had a transfusion, and their family members or carers (as appropriate), explaining: the reason for the transfusion; the risks and benefits; the transfusion process; any transfusion needs specific to them; any alternatives that are available, and how they might reduce their need for a transfusion; that they are no longer eligible to donate blood; that they are encouraged to ask questions. [2015] Document discussions in the patient's notes. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Patient-information
- Pass: Before the first unit is started, the notes or EPR record the reason for transfusion, the risks discussed and any alternatives discussed (or a note that the patient lacked capacity and a best-interests decision was made).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive transfusion episodes over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add a short consent section (reason, risks including circulatory overload, alternatives, leaflet given) to the transfusion prescription or EPR blood order, which the prescriber completes before the order is sent.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-290.csv`
- Pitfalls: Consent written after the unit started → record both times and count only discussions before the start.; Out-of-hours transfusions have poorer documentation → report in-hours and out-of-hours separately.; Juniors say it takes too long → keep the section to four tick boxes and one free-text line.; Patients without capacity are counted as fails → accept a documented best-interests decision.
- Pearls: Recruit the hospital transfusion practitioner; they already audit transfusion records.; Keep leaflets with the transfusion packs on each ward.; Include circulatory overload in the risk box; the 2024 national alert asks for it.; Ask the blood bank to reject e-orders with an empty consent section only after the pilot has worked.

**NNA-291. What proportion of adult red cell transfusions have a completed TACO (transfusion-associated circulatory overload) risk assessment before the first unit starts?** (under-audited)
- Standard: National Patient Safety Alert NatPSA/2024/004/MHRA, action 1b (2024): "A TACO risk assessment is undertaken utilising the SHOT risk assessment tool prior to transfusions" https://www.gov.uk/drug-device-alerts/national-patient-safety-alert-reducing-risks-for-transfusion-associated-circulatory-overload-natpsa-slash-2024-slash-004-slash-mhra
- Pass: The SHOT TACO risk assessment is fully completed (all questions answered) and dated before the first unit starts; if any risk is present, a mitigation (e.g. single unit, weight-adjusted dose, slower rate, diuretic plan) is written.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive transfusion episodes over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Build the SHOT TACO risk assessment into the red cell prescription (paper or EPR) as a mandatory section that must be completed before the prescription is valid.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-291.csv`
- Pitfalls: The form is ticked without thought → count as a pass only if every question is answered and a mitigation is written when risk is present.; Paper forms get separated from the notes → check the transfusion record and the EPR.; Doctors object to another form → replace an existing section of the prescription rather than adding a sheet.; Gains fade after rotation → include the form in junior doctor induction and keep the transfusion practitioner's monthly spot check.
- Pearls: Recruit the hospital transfusion practitioner; the NatPSA already requires them to audit this.; Use the current SHOT 2024 version of the tool, which includes severe chronic anaemia.; Pair results with any local TACO incidents to show why it matters.; Ask blood bank staff to prompt when an order arrives without the assessment.

**NNA-292. Of red cell transfusions given to adult medical inpatients who are not bleeding, what proportion are given at a haemoglobin below 70 g/L?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 recs 1.5.1 and 1.5.2 (2015, updated 2026): "Use restrictive red blood cell transfusion thresholds for people who need red blood cell transfusions and who do not: have major haemorrhage or have acute coronary syndrome or need regular blood transfusions for chronic anaemia. [2015] When using a restrictive red blood cell transfusion threshold, consider a threshold of 70 g/litre and a haemoglobin concentration target of 70 to 90 g/litre after transfusion. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Red-blood-cell-transfusion
- Pass: The most recent haemoglobin before the first unit was below 70 g/L, or a patient-specific reason for a higher threshold is documented (e.g. symptomatic anaemia with a written clinical reason).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive transfusion episodes over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Make the pre-transfusion haemoglobin and an indication code mandatory fields on the electronic blood request, with a prompt showing the 70 g/L threshold when the value entered is higher.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-292.csv`
- Pitfalls: Symptomatic anaemia is used loosely as a reason → agree before cycle 1 what counts as a documented reason.; The pre-transfusion haemoglobin is days old → record its date and require one within 24 hours.; Consultants disagree with a single threshold in frail patients → report results by ward and invite them to the meeting.; Changing exclusions between cycles → fix the exclusion list in writing before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion practitioner and a haematology consultant to present with you.; Look at out-of-hours transfusions separately; they are more often outside threshold.; Pair the threshold with single-unit dosing on the same request screen.; Share each ward's results with its matron, not just the doctors.

**NNA-293. Of prophylactic platelet transfusions given to adults who are not bleeding and not having a procedure, what proportion are given at a platelet count below 10×10⁹/L?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.7.3 (2015, updated 2026): "Offer prophylactic platelet transfusions to people with a platelet count below 10×10⁹ per litre who are not bleeding or having invasive procedures or surgery, and who do not have any of the following conditions: chronic bone marrow failure; autoimmune thrombocytopenia; heparin-induced thrombocytopenia; thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Platelet-transfusion
- Pass: The most recent platelet count before the transfusion (within 24 hours) was below 10×10⁹/L, or a higher threshold is justified in the notes (e.g. sepsis or fever per local policy).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive prophylactic platelet transfusions over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Make the pre-transfusion platelet count and an indication code mandatory on the platelet request, and have the blood bank query any prophylactic request above 10×10⁹/L before issue.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-293.csv`
- Pitfalls: Bleeding status is not recorded → agree to classify from nursing notes and exclude unclear cases consistently.; Haematology units use higher thresholds for fever → write these exceptions into the pass rule before cycle 1.; Laboratory staff are uneasy querying senior clinicians → give them a written script and haematology backing.; Numbers are small outside haematology → collect over a longer period rather than cutting the sample.
- Pearls: Recruit a haematology consultant to agree the exceptions list.; Use the blood bank issue list; it captures every unit.; Show the cost and shelf-life of platelets on the results slide.; Pair with the existing library audit on post-transfusion platelet counts for the next cycle.

**NNA-294. Of adults with a platelet count between 10 and 50×10⁹/L who have a central venous catheter inserted or a bone marrow biopsy, what proportion receive no prophylactic platelet transfusion for the procedure?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.7.8 (2015, updated 2026): "Do not offer prophylactic platelet transfusions to people having procedures with a low risk of bleeding, such as adults having central venous cannulation or any people having bone marrow aspiration and trephine biopsy. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Platelet-transfusion
- Pass: No platelet transfusion was given in the 12 hours before the procedure with the procedure as the stated reason.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive procedures over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add a line to the platelet request screen: 'Central line or bone marrow biopsy only? Platelets not indicated (NICE NG24 1.7.8)', with the request blocked unless a senior overrides it with a reason.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-294.csv`
- Pitfalls: Local policies (and some society guidance) still use a threshold of 20×10⁹/L for lines → agree the local rule with haematology and anaesthesia before cycle 1 and report counts below 20 separately.; Line insertions are poorly logged → use the blood bank issue list to find platelet units linked to procedures.; Operators may refuse without platelets → pair the change with ultrasound-guided insertion by an experienced operator.; Small numbers → extend the collection period rather than the population.
- Pearls: Recruit an anaesthetist who inserts lines and a haematologist who does biopsies.; Record ultrasound guidance; it reassures sceptics.; Report bleeding complications alongside compliance to show safety.; Ask ICU to join; they insert most lines in thrombocytopenic patients.

**NNA-295. What proportion of fresh frozen plasma transfusions in adults are given for bleeding or before an invasive procedure with a risk of significant bleeding?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.9.2 (2015, updated 2026): "Do not offer fresh frozen plasma transfusions to correct abnormal coagulation in people who: are not bleeding (unless they are having invasive procedures or surgery with a risk of clinically significant bleeding); need reversal of a vitamin K antagonist. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Fresh-frozen-plasma-transfusion
- Pass: FFP was given to a patient with clinically significant bleeding, or within 12 hours before a named invasive procedure or surgery with a risk of significant bleeding, and was not given to reverse warfarin.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive FFP episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Require an indication code (bleeding or named procedure) on every FFP request, with the blood bank referring any other indication to the on-call haematologist before issue.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-295.csv`
- Pitfalls: 'Procedure planned' but never done → record whether the procedure happened within 12 hours.; Major haemorrhage packs inflate numbers → exclude protocol activations.; ICU and liver teams may object → invite them to agree the exceptions before cycle 1.; Warfarin reversal with FFP hides the lack of PCC stock → check PCC availability as part of the fix.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion laboratory manager; they see every request.; Show the cost per unit and the TRALI/TACO risk on one slide.; Ask the on-call haematologist to log each referral for the re-audit.; Keep PCC stocked in ED so warfarin reversal never needs FFP.

**NNA-296. Of adults on warfarin with severe bleeding outside the brain, what proportion receive prothrombin complex concentrate within 1 hour of the bleed being recognised?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.12.3 (2015, updated 2026): "Offer immediate prothrombin complex concentrate transfusions for the emergency reversal of warfarin anticoagulation in people with either: severe bleeding or head injury with suspected intracerebral haemorrhage. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Prothrombin-complex-concentrate-transfusion
- Pass: PCC administration started within 60 minutes of the time severe bleeding was first documented (the audit's measure of 'immediate').. Target: ≥80%. Sample: All eligible cases over the last 6–12 months (aim for 30), from the blood bank PCC issue log.
- Change: Stock PCC in the ED with a weight- and INR-based dosing chart, so it can be given without phoning haematology or the blood bank.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-296.csv`
- Pitfalls: Time of recognition is vague → use the first documented time of 'major bleed' or haemodynamic instability, agreed before cycle 1.; Few cases per month → collect retrospectively over 6–12 months and report each case.; Haematology may want to approve each dose → agree a dosing chart they sign off in advance.; Stock expires unused in ED → agree rotation with the blood bank.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion laboratory manager and an ED consultant.; Write the dosing chart on the PCC box itself.; Record IV vitamin K as a secondary field; it should be given with PCC.; Present one delayed case as a short timeline; it persuades faster than percentages.

**NNA-297. What proportion of adults given prothrombin complex concentrate to reverse warfarin have their INR rechecked within 1 hour of the dose?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.12.6 (2015, updated 2026): "Monitor the international normalised ratio (INR) to confirm that warfarin anticoagulation has been adequately reversed, and consider further prothrombin complex concentrate.[2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Prothrombin-complex-concentrate-transfusion
- Pass: A repeat INR sample was taken within 60 minutes of the end of the PCC dose (the audit's local measure of monitoring).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: All eligible cases over the last 6–12 months (aim for 30), from the PCC issue log.
- Change: Add 'repeat INR 30 minutes after PCC' as a pre-printed step on the PCC prescription and dosing chart, with the sample request generated at the same time as the PCC order.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-297.csv`
- Pitfalls: Sample taken but time not recorded → use the laboratory collection time, not the report time.; Small numbers → include all cases for 6–12 months and present case by case.; Teams recheck only if still bleeding → explain that surgery and endoscopy decisions depend on it.; Changing the time window between cycles → fix the 60-minute window before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Collect this at the same time as the PCC timing audit; the cases are the same.; Recruit the blood bank; they can remind the ward when PCC is issued.; Report INR values as well as timing so the committee sees reversal adequacy.; Put the step on the PCC box label.

**NNA-298. What proportion of red cell units have pulse, blood pressure, temperature and respiratory rate recorded before the start, at 15 minutes and at the end of the unit?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG24 rec 1.13.1 (2015, updated 2026): "Monitor the person's condition and vital signs before, during and after blood transfusions, to detect acute transfusion reactions that may need immediate investigation and treatment. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng24/chapter/Patient-safety
- Pass: All four vital signs recorded within 60 minutes before the start, 15 minutes (±10 minutes) after the start, and within 60 minutes after the end of the unit.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive units on medical and surgical wards over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Add a transfusion observation schedule to the e-observations system that is triggered when a unit is started and alerts the nurse at 15 minutes and at the expected end time.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-298.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper and electronic observations are split → check both for every unit.; Night transfusions have fewer staff → report day and night separately and avoid non-urgent night transfusions.; Nurses object to alerts → agree the alert wording with ward managers.; The 15-minute window is argued over → fix it (5–25 minutes) before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the transfusion practitioner and a ward matron.; Report by ward; ward-level results drive nursing change.; Pair with a push to avoid starting non-urgent units after 20:00.; Share any reaction found late as a learning case.

**NNA-299. What proportion of people with an acute painful sickle cell episode receive their first analgesia within 30 minutes of arriving at hospital?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG143 rec 1.1.4 (2012, updated 2026): "Offer analgesia within 30 minutes of presentation to all patients presenting at hospital with an acute painful sickle cell episode (see also the recommendations on primary analgesia). [2012]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg143/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First analgesic dose administered (time on the drug chart or ED record) within 30 minutes of arrival or booking time.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive attendances over the last 6–12 months (collect across sites if volume is low).
- Change: Introduce a patient group direction for triage nurses to give the first dose of the patient's care-plan opioid (or a standard opioid) at triage for people with known sickle cell disease.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-299.csv`
- Pitfalls: Low attendance numbers in a DGH → collect over 12 months or pool with a neighbouring hospital.; Arrival time and triage time differ → agree which time counts before cycle 1.; ED staff may doubt pain severity → include the patient's care plan and triage pain score in the fix.; Frequent attenders dominate the sample → report per attendance and the number of individual patients.
- Pearls: Recruit the haemoglobinopathy specialist nurse and a patient from the local sickle cell society.; Put individual care plans on the EPR alert so triage can see the patient's usual opioid.; Report median minutes as well as the percentage.; Include children; paediatric ED often has different barriers.

**NNA-300. What proportion of people treated with a strong opioid for an acute painful sickle cell episode have a sedation score recorded every hour for the first 6 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG143 rec 1.1.16 (2012, updated 2026): "Monitor patients taking strong opioids for adverse events, and perform a clinical assessment (including sedation score): every 1 hour for the first 6 hours; at least every 4 hours thereafter. [2012]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg143/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A sedation score recorded at least once in each of the 6 hours after the first strong opioid dose.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Add a sickle cell opioid monitoring schedule to the e-observations system that sets hourly sedation and respiratory rate checks for 6 hours when a strong opioid is given.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-300.csv`
- Pitfalls: Sedation scores recorded on a separate chart → check all charts before marking a fail.; Transfer from ED to ward breaks the schedule → include both areas and note where gaps occur.; Nurses see hourly checks as excessive → share the acute chest syndrome risk and agree a stop point at 6 hours.; Low numbers → pool 12 months of data.
- Pearls: Recruit the haemoglobinopathy nurse and the ward matron.; Build the schedule into the PCA chart as well.; Report the gap by hour; it shows exactly where monitoring drops.; Include respiratory rate as a secondary measure; it catches hypoventilation earlier.

**NNA-301. What proportion of people with an acute painful sickle cell episode have their pain reassessed with a pain score within 30 minutes of the first analgesic dose?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG143 rec 1.1.12 (2012, updated 2026): "Assess the effectiveness of pain relief: every 30 minutes until satisfactory pain relief has been achieved, and at least every 4 hours thereafter; using an age-appropriate pain scoring tool; by asking questions, such as: How well did that last painkiller work? Do you feel that you need more pain relief? [2012]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg143/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A pain score recorded within 30 minutes (±5 minutes) after the first analgesic dose.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 6–12 months (can share cases with the time-to-analgesia audit).
- Change: Add an automatic 30-minute pain reassessment task to the ED and ward e-observations that is triggered when an opioid is recorded for a patient with a sickle cell alert.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-301.csv`
- Pitfalls: Reassessment is done but not scored → count only numeric or age-appropriate scale scores.; Busy EDs cannot manage 30-minute checks → assign the task to the nurse giving the dose.; Patients moved between areas → track across ED and ward.; Children need age-appropriate tools → list accepted tools before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Run this alongside the time-to-analgesia audit; the same notes give both.; Recruit a patient representative to review the results.; Report the proportion who needed a second dose; it shows why reassessment matters.; Put the reassessment reminder on the sickle cell care plan alert.

**NNA-302. Of adults with active cancer and a new proximal DVT or PE, what proportion are started on a DOAC or have a documented reason why a DOAC is unsuitable?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 recs 1.3.16 and 1.3.17 (2020, updated 2023): "When choosing anticoagulation treatment for people with active cancer and confirmed proximal DVT or PE, take into account the tumour site, interactions with other drugs including those used to treat cancer, and the person's bleeding risk. [2020] Consider a direct-acting oral anticoagulant (DOAC) for people with active cancer and confirmed proximal DVT or PE. [2020]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Discharged on a DOAC, or on LMWH or warfarin with a written reason (e.g. GI or urothelial tumour with bleeding risk, drug interaction, renal function, patient choice).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive cases over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add a cancer-associated thrombosis section to the VTE treatment proforma or EPR order set that asks tumour site, interacting drugs and bleeding risk, and defaults to a DOAC if none apply.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-302.csv`
- Pitfalls: Cancer status is unclear in the notes → use the NICE definition of active cancer and check oncology letters.; Oncologists prefer LMWH for some tumours → any documented reason counts as a pass.; Outpatient PE pathways are missed → include ambulatory emergency care cases.; Drug interactions with SACT are not checked → ask the oncology pharmacist to review the proforma.
- Pearls: Recruit the anticoagulation nurse and oncology pharmacist.; Add a short interaction list for common SACT drugs to the proforma.; Capture the 3–6 month review date at discharge for the next audit cycle.; Share results with acute oncology; they see these patients first.

**NNA-303. What proportion of adults with a central venous line treated for suspected neutropenic sepsis receive no empirical vancomycin or teicoplanin, unless a reason is documented?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG151 rec 1.4.3.3 (2012, updated 2024): "Do not offer empiric glycopeptide antibiotics to patients with suspected neutropenic sepsis who have central venous access devices unless there are patient-specific or local microbiological indications." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg151/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No vancomycin or teicoplanin in the first 48 hours, or a documented patient-specific or microbiological reason (e.g. line site infection, MRSA carriage, Gram-positive bacteraemia, septic shock per local policy).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 6 months.
- Change: Change the neutropenic sepsis section of the antimicrobial app and order set so that glycopeptides appear only under named indications (line site infection, MRSA, Gram-positive blood culture), not as an add-on for all line patients.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-303.csv`
- Pitfalls: Haematology units may have local policies adding glycopeptides → agree local indications with microbiology before cycle 1.; Glycopeptides started after 48 hours for a positive culture are appropriate → only count the first 48 hours.; Small numbers in solid tumour units → include haematology.; The order set is bypassed with free-text prescribing → check free-text orders at re-audit.
- Pearls: Recruit the antimicrobial pharmacist and a microbiologist.; Collect with the aminoglycoside audit; the same drug charts answer both.; Report days of glycopeptide therapy saved.; Ask the line team to record line-site signs at admission so exceptions are clear.

## Medicines safety and antimicrobial stewardship

**NNA-304. What proportion of adults on IV antibiotics who meet the national IV-to-oral switch criteria at 48 hours are switched to oral antibiotics within the next 24 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: UKHSA National antimicrobial intravenous-to-oral switch (IVOS) criteria for prompt switch for adults (updated October 2024): "IVOS should be considered within 48 hours of the first dose of IV antimicrobial being administered. If no IVOS within first 48 hours, review daily thereafter. [...] Clinical signs and symptoms of infection are improving. [...] Temperature has been between 36 to 38°C for the past 24 hours. [...] Gastrointestinal tract must be functioning with no evidence of malabsorption. Safe swallow or enteral tube administration." https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/antimicrobial-intravenous-to-oral-switch-criteria-for-early-switch/national-antimicrobial-intravenous-to-oral-switch-ivos-criteria-for-early-switch
- Pass: Oral antibiotic given (or IV stopped) within 24 hours of the first time the patient met all IVOS criteria at or after 48 hours, or a documented special consideration (e.g. endocarditis, meningitis, undrained abscess) or microbiology advice to stay IV.. Target: ≥80% switched within 24 hours of meeting criteria. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible adults from the pharmacy or EPMA IV antibiotic list over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add an IVOS prompt to the EPMA IV antibiotic order that fires at 48 hours and asks the prescriber to switch, continue with a reason, or stop.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-304.csv`
- Pitfalls: Criteria judged loosely from notes → pull temperature and NEWS2 straight from e-obs and agree the exact criteria list before cycle 1.; Senior clinicians object that the patient 'looks unwell' → accept a documented reason as a pass; count only undocumented delays as fails.; Weekend gaps inflate delays → record the day of week and report weekday and weekend separately.; Oral option not stocked on the ward → check the formulary oral equivalents with pharmacy before the change goes live.; The EPMA prompt gets clicked through → audit the reason text in cycle 2, not just prompt completion.
- Pearls: Recruit an antimicrobial pharmacist; they already review IV lists daily.; Report IV days saved and cannulas avoided; managers respond to bed-days and line infections.; Use the national IVOS decision aid as the poster so wording matches the standard.; Hand the monthly IVOS report to the stewardship team so it outlives your rotation.

**NNA-305. What proportion of adults given once-daily IV gentamicin have a gentamicin level result available before the second dose is given?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Gentamicin 40 mg/ml Solution for Injection or Infusion, Summary of Product Characteristics section 4.2 (Wockhardt UK, revised April 2024): "Serum concentration monitoring of gentamicin is recommended, especially in elderly, in newborns and in patients with impaired renal function. Samples are taken at the end of a dosing interval (trough level). Trough levels should not exceed 2 µg/ml administering gentamicin twice daily and 1 µg/ml for a once daily dose." https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/2394/smpc
- Pass: A gentamicin level taken at the time set by the local protocol after the first dose, with the result documented or acknowledged before the second dose is given.. Target: ≥90% with a result before the second dose. Sample: 40 consecutive gentamicin courses from the pharmacy or EPMA list over the last 6–8 weeks.
- Change: Build a gentamicin order set in EPMA that auto-requests the level at the protocol time and blocks the second dose until the result is acknowledged.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-305.csv`
- Pitfalls: Local protocols use different sampling windows (e.g. 6–14 hours) → quote your Trust protocol in the report and keep it fixed between cycles.; Lab turnaround is the real delay at night → record sample and result times separately so ward and lab delays are not confused.; Doses delayed to wait for levels harm septic patients → count a dose held for a pending level as a pass only if documented.; EPMA build takes months → use a gentamicin chart sticker with a level time box in the interim.; Small numbers on one ward → pool courses across the hospital.
- Pearls: Recruit the antimicrobial pharmacist and a lab lead; both own part of the pathway.; Agree the exact protocol window with microbiology before cycle 1.; Record renal function too; it explains most high levels.; Ask pharmacy to run the report monthly after you rotate.

**NNA-306. What proportion of adults on intermittent IV vancomycin have their first level taken on the second day of treatment, immediately before the next dose?** (new)
- Standard: Vancomycin 1000 mg powder for concentrate for solution for infusion, Summary of Product Characteristics section 4.2 (hameln pharma, revised January 2026): "In patients with normal renal function, the serum concentration of vancomycin should be monitored on the second day of treatment immediately prior to the next dose." https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/15737/smpc
- Pass: First vancomycin level sampled on day 2 of treatment within 1 hour before the next scheduled dose.. Target: ≥85% of first levels on day 2 within 1 hour before the dose. Sample: 40 consecutive vancomycin courses from pharmacy or EPMA over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add an automatic day-2 pre-dose level request to the vancomycin EPMA order, linked to the second-day dose time.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-306.csv`
- Pitfalls: Phlebotomy rounds miss the pre-dose slot → agree that nurses take the level at dose time before cycle 1.; Dose times move after the first dose → record the actual next scheduled time, not the planned one.; Units move to continuous infusion mid-audit → exclude continuous infusions in both cycles.; The request fires but the sample is not labelled 'pre-dose' → add sample time to the lab request.
- Pearls: Recruit ward nurses early; they give the dose and can take the level.; Pair with the gentamicin audit to share one EPMA build.; Report days with an out-of-range level; it links timing to harm.; Leave the monthly search in the pharmacy TDM report.

**NNA-307. What proportion of adults started on IV vancomycin receive a first dose that matches the weight-based dose in the local guideline?** (new)
- Standard: Vancomycin 1000 mg powder for concentrate for solution for infusion, Summary of Product Characteristics section 4.2 (hameln pharma, revised January 2026): "The initial dose should be based on total body weight. Subsequent dose adjustments should be based on serum concentrations to achieve targeted therapeutic concentrations. [...] In seriously ill patients, a loading dose of 25-30 mg/kg of body weight can be used to facilitate rapid attainment of target trough serum vancomycin concentration." https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/15737/smpc
- Pass: A weight recorded in this admission before the first dose, and a first dose within the local guideline band for that weight (rounded as the guideline allows).. Target: ≥90% of first doses match the guideline. Sample: 40 consecutive new vancomycin courses over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Put a vancomycin dose calculator in EPMA that pulls the recorded weight and suggests the guideline first dose.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-307.csv`
- Pitfalls: Stated or estimated weights hide the error → record the weight source and report it.; Obese patients need capped doses → apply the guideline cap and do not count capped doses as fails.; ED gives the first dose before weighing → include ED doses and involve the ED pharmacist.; Calculator is bypassed with free text → check free-text orders in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit ED and acute medicine; most first doses start there.; Put chair or bed scales where the first dose is given.; Agree the dose table with microbiology before cycle 1 so it does not change.; Pharmacist screening can keep the check alive after rotation.

**NNA-308. What proportion of adult inpatients started on a DOAC receive the licensed dose for their Cockcroft–Gault creatinine clearance?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update volume 13 issue 3 (October 2019): Prescribing medicines in renal impairment: "creatinine clearance (CrCl) should be calculated using the Cockcroft-Gault formula (see below) to determine dosage adjustments for: direct-acting oral anticoagulants (DOACs) [...] In particular, CrCl should always be used to guide dose adjustment for direct-acting oral anticoagulants (DOACs; apixaban, dabigatran etexilate, edoxaban▼, and rivaroxaban▼). Use of eGFR for dosing of DOACs is known to increase risk of bleeding events as a consequence of overestimating renal function." https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/prescribing-medicines-in-renal-impairment-using-the-appropriate-estimate-of-renal-function-to-avoid-the-risk-of-adverse-drug-reactions
- Pass: Creatinine clearance calculated by Cockcroft–Gault (actual body weight, current creatinine) and the prescribed dose matches the product licence for the indication, age, weight and clearance.. Target: ≥95% at the licensed dose. Sample: 40 consecutive new DOAC starts from pharmacy or EPMA over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory Cockcroft–Gault calculator and licensed-dose table to the EPMA DOAC order sentence.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-308.csv`
- Pitfalls: No weight recorded → count as a fail; the clearance cannot be calculated.; Deliberate off-licence dosing by haematology → accept a documented specialist reason.; Creatinine changes during the stay → use the value nearest the first dose and keep that rule in both cycles.; Staff object to extra clicks → show how many patients were under- or over-dosed.
- Pearls: Recruit the anticoagulation pharmacist; they know the licence criteria by heart.; Report under-dosing as well as over-dosing; strokes persuade as much as bleeds.; Use one printed dose table for all four DOACs.; Build the check into pharmacy screening so it survives rotation.

**NNA-309. What proportion of adults prescribed treatment-dose enoxaparin have the dose calculated from a weight measured during this admission?** (new)
- Standard: Clexane pre-filled syringes, Summary of Product Characteristics section 4.2 (Sanofi, revised February 2022): "Enoxaparin sodium can be administered SC either as a once daily injection of 150 IU/kg (1.5 mg/kg) or as twice daily injections of 100 IU/kg (1 mg/kg). The regimen should be selected by the physician based on an individual assessment including evaluation of the thromboembolic risk and of the risk of bleeding." https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/4499/smpc
- Pass: A measured (not stated or estimated) weight recorded this admission before the first treatment dose, and the dose within the local dose band for that weight.. Target: ≥90% dosed from a measured weight. Sample: 40 consecutive new treatment-dose courses over the last 6–8 weeks.
- Change: Make 'measured weight and date' a mandatory field on the EPMA treatment-dose enoxaparin order, pre-filled from the observations chart.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-309.csv`
- Pitfalls: Immobile patients cannot stand on scales → list where hoist or bed scales are kept before cycle 1.; ED gives the first dose before weighing → include ED starts and agree a weigh-in-ED rule.; Dose-banding tables differ between wards → use the single Trust table in both cycles.; Mandatory field filled with a guessed number → record the weight source in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward sister; nurses own the weighing.; Report how many doses were more than 10% off; it makes the risk concrete.; Link to the paracetamol weight audit; one weighing fix serves both.; Ask the thrombosis nurse to keep the monthly check.

**NNA-310. What proportion of adults starting anticoagulation for a DVT or PE in hospital are given an anticoagulant alert card before discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 rec 1.5.2 (2012, guideline updated August 2023): "Give people who are having anticoagulation treatment information and an 'anticoagulant alert card' that is specific to their treatment. Advise them to carry the 'anticoagulant alert card' at all times. [2012]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Documented issue of an alert card specific to the anticoagulant prescribed (DOAC, warfarin or LMWH) before or at discharge.. Target: ≥90% with an alert card documented. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients from the anticoagulation or ambulatory care list over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add an 'anticoagulant alert card given' tick box to the discharge letter template, linked to any new anticoagulant on the discharge prescription.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-310.csv`
- Pitfalls: Cards are given but not documented → accept a pharmacy counselling record as evidence.; Ambulatory patients leave without a discharge letter → include the ambulatory proforma as a data source.; Wards run out of cards → check stock on each ward before cycle 1.; Drug changes after discharge → record the drug at discharge only.
- Pearls: Recruit the thrombosis nurse and ward pharmacists; they already counsel.; Keep cards with the discharge medicines, not in an office.; Ask a few patients at follow-up if they still carry the card; it strengthens the report.; Leave the tick box report with the thrombosis team.

**NNA-311. What proportion of adults discharged on prednisolone 5 mg a day or more (or equivalent) for 4 weeks or longer have a Steroid Emergency Card documented as issued or already held?** (new)
- Standard: NHS England National Patient Safety Alert NatPSA/2020/005/NHSPS (13 August 2020), with the eligibility criteria in the Society for Endocrinology and RCP guidance (Clin Med 2020): "All organisations that initiate steroid prescriptions should review their processes/policies and their digital systems/software and prompts to ensure that prescribers issue a Steroid Emergency Card to all eligible patients, as outlined in new guidance. [...] Patients taking prednisolone 5 mg/day or equivalent for 4 weeks or longer across all routes of administration (oral, topical, inhaled or intranasal) should be issued with an NHS Steroid Emergency Card." https://www.england.nhs.uk/wp-content/uploads/2020/08/NPSA-Emergency-Steroid-Card-FINAL-2.3.pdf
- Pass: Discharge letter or notes record that a Steroid Emergency Card was issued, or that the patient already holds one.. Target: ≥90% of eligible discharges. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges from EPMA over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add an EPMA prompt that fires when a qualifying steroid dose is on the discharge prescription and asks the prescriber to confirm the card was issued.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-311.csv`
- Pitfalls: Duration is unclear from notes → use the GP record or drug history and keep the same rule in both cycles.; Short COPD courses are counted by mistake → exclude courses under 4 weeks unless repeated.; Staff assume the GP will issue it → agree with pharmacy that the card is given before discharge.; Cards run out → check ward and pharmacy stock before the change.
- Pearls: Recruit respiratory and rheumatology; they discharge most eligible patients.; Ask pharmacy to hand the card with the medicines at discharge.; Add inhaled high-dose steroids in a later cycle once oral is reliable.; Leave the EPMA report with the medication safety officer.

**NNA-312. What proportion of adults admitted as an emergency on long-term oral steroids receive every due dose of their steroid in the first 72 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NHS England National Patient Safety Alert NatPSA/2020/005/NHSPS (13 August 2020), action 3: "Omission of steroids for patients with adrenal insufficiency can lead to adrenal crisis; a medical emergency which if left untreated can be fatal. [...] Providers that treat patients with acute physical illness or trauma, or who may require emergency or elective surgical or other invasive procedures, including day patients, should review their admission/assessment/examination/clerking documentation to ensure it includes prompts to check for risk of adrenal crisis and to establish if the patient has a Steroid Emergency Card." https://www.england.nhs.uk/wp-content/uploads/2020/08/NPSA-Emergency-Steroid-Card-FINAL-2.3.pdf
- Pass: Every dose of the usual steroid (or a prescribed higher or parenteral equivalent) due in the first 72 hours after arrival was given, with none omitted or delayed by more than 2 hours.. Target: ≥90% receive every due dose. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible emergency admissions over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'on long-term steroids? card held?' question to the admission clerking template that triggers a steroid prescription prompt with the IV/IM alternative when nil by mouth.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-312.csv`
- Pitfalls: Steroid use missed at clerking → search EPMA for any steroid on the drug history as well as the chart.; Nurses record 'nil by mouth' and omit → include an IV/IM alternative on the prompt.; Deliberate stops look like omissions → accept a documented clinical decision.; EPMA delay codes are used loosely → agree what counts as an omission before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit acute medicine nursing leads; omissions happen on the first night.; Use the Trust omitted-doses report if one exists to find cases fast.; Tell one real near-miss story at the presentation; it changes practice.; Put the check into the ward pharmacist's daily screening.

**NNA-313. What proportion of adults on a variable rate intravenous insulin infusion who normally take a long-acting insulin continue to receive it while on the infusion?** (under-audited)
- Standard: JBDS-IP guideline JBDS 09: The use of variable rate intravenous insulin infusion (VRIII) in medical inpatients (current version, October 2014), Chapter 3 key message 2: "Continue patients' usual basal* subcutaneous insulin whilst on VRIII and stop rapid acting or mixed insulins" https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_09_IP_VRIII.pdf
- Pass: Every dose of the usual basal insulin due while the VRIII was running was prescribed and given.. Target: ≥90% of eligible episodes. Sample: 30–40 consecutive VRIII episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a 'continue usual basal insulin' line with a prescribing prompt to the VRIII order set or chart.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-313.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgical patients follow a different pathway → keep to medical inpatients in both cycles, or audit surgery separately.; Nurses hold basal insulin when the patient is nil by mouth → include this in ward teaching and on the chart line.; Small numbers → extend the look-back period rather than mixing in DKA.; Prompt added but insulin not prescribed → check prescription and administration separately in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes specialist nurses; they see most VRIIIs.; Use the NaDIA harms data if your Trust submits it.; Record rebound hyperglycaemia after stopping; it shows the harm.; Hand the audit to the inpatient diabetes team for yearly repeat.

**NNA-314. What proportion of adults on a variable rate intravenous insulin infusion on a general ward have a glucose-containing substrate fluid running throughout the infusion?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS-IP guideline JBDS 09: The use of variable rate intravenous insulin infusion (VRIII) in medical inpatients (current version, October 2014), sections 3.7 and 3.7.1: "Avoid hypoglycaemia by providing substrate at a steady rate for the insulin infusion [...] Do not infuse insulin without substrate unless in HDU/ITU setting" https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_09_IP_VRIII.pdf
- Pass: A glucose-containing substrate fluid prescribed from the start of the VRIII and recorded as running on the fluid chart at every check while the insulin ran.. Target: ≥95% of episodes. Sample: 30–40 consecutive VRIII episodes over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Replace the separate fluid prescription with a combined VRIII and substrate fluid order set so the insulin cannot be prescribed alone.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-314.csv`
- Pitfalls: Fluid charts are incomplete → treat unrecorded periods as fails and agree this before cycle 1.; Fluid-overloaded patients need less volume → accept 10% glucose at a lower rate as a pass.; Pumps run out overnight → record gaps and their length, not just yes/no.; Order-set change needs IT time → use a printed combined chart in the interim.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes nurses and the IV fluids lead.; Check that the recommended bag is actually stocked on each ward.; Pair with the basal insulin audit; one data pull covers both.; Ask the diabetes team to repeat it yearly.

**NNA-315. What proportion of adults discharged from general surgical wards with a newly started opioid have a planned stop date or duration in the discharge letter?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update: Opioids: risk of dependence and addiction (23 September 2020): "opioid medicines (opioids) provide relief from serious short-term pain; however long-term use in non-cancer pain (longer than 3 months) carries an increased risk of dependence and addiction [...] before starting treatment with opioids, agree with the patient a treatment strategy and plan for end of treatment" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/opioids-risk-of-dependence-and-addiction
- Pass: The discharge letter to the GP states a stop date, number of days, or a tapering plan for the new opioid.. Target: ≥90% with a stop date or duration. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Make 'planned duration / stop date' a mandatory field on the discharge prescription when an opioid not on the admission drug history is added.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-315.csv`
- Pitfalls: Pre-admission opioid use is unclear → check the GP record before counting as new.; Short supply implies a stop but no date is written → count only an explicit plan as a pass.; Surgeons object to extra fields → keep it to one mandatory line.; Mandatory fields are filled with 'as required' → review free text in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit a surgical pharmacist and one consultant champion.; Share the result with the local primary care prescribing team; they see the downstream harm.; Record modified-release supplies; they are the highest risk.; Build the check into the pharmacy discharge screen so it outlasts the rotation.

**NNA-316. What proportion of inpatient 'when required' opioid prescriptions state a maximum dose or number of doses in 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG46 rec 1.5.3 (2016): "When prescribing 'when required' controlled drugs: • document clear instructions for when and how to take or use the drug in the person's care record • include dosage instructions on the prescription (with the maximum daily amount or frequency of doses) so that this can be included on the label when dispensed • ask about and take into account any existing supplies the person has of 'when required' controlled drugs." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng46/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The prescription states a maximum total dose in 24 hours or a maximum number of doses in 24 hours, plus a minimum interval.. Target: ≥95% of as-required opioid prescriptions. Sample: 50 consecutive as-required opioid prescriptions across medical and surgical wards, collected over 2–3 audit days.
- Change: Change the EPMA 'when required' opioid order sentences so a maximum dose in 24 hours and a minimum interval are mandatory.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-316.csv`
- Pitfalls: Order sentences already include a maximum but are free-texted over → audit free-text orders separately.; Palliative teams object to caps → exclude end-of-life anticipatory prescriptions or allow a stated range.; Paper charts on some wards → include them and note the chart type.; Snapshot days miss weekend prescribing → collect across different days of the week.
- Pearls: Recruit the controlled drugs pharmacist; they run CD audits already.; Agree the order sentences with the acute pain team before the change.; Show one example where repeated doses caused sedation.; Leave the spot check with the CD accountable officer's team.

**NNA-317. What proportion of discharge prescriptions for Schedule 2 or 3 controlled drugs state the total quantity in both words and figures?** (new)
- Standard: The Misuse of Drugs Regulations 2001, regulation 15(1)(f): "(f)specify the dose to be taken and— (i)in the case of a prescription containing a controlled drug which is a preparation, the form and, where appropriate, the strength of the preparation, and either the total quantity (in both words and figures) of the preparation or the number (in both words and figures) of dosage units, as appropriate, to be supplied; (ii)in any other case, the total quantity (in both words and figures ) of the controlled drug to be supplied;" https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2001/3998/regulation/15
- Pass: Total quantity or number of dosage units written in both words and figures on the discharge prescription as first submitted to pharmacy.. Target: 100% legally complete on first submission. Sample: 50 consecutive discharge controlled drug prescriptions from the pharmacy dispensary over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Configure the EPMA discharge template so controlled drug items auto-generate the total quantity in words and figures.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-317.csv`
- Pitfalls: Pharmacy corrects errors silently → use the prescription as first submitted, not the dispensed version.; Gabapentinoids are missed as Schedule 3 → give auditors a schedule list.; IT cannot auto-generate words → use an EPMA pop-up reminder in the interim.; Different pharmacists record returns differently → agree the returned-prescription log fields first.
- Pearls: Recruit the dispensary lead; they keep the returns log.; Report discharge delay hours; bed managers will back the fix.; Add a one-slide example to FY1 induction.; Ask pharmacy to keep the monthly returns metric.

**NNA-318. What proportion of hospital discharge letters record the patient's drug allergy status?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG183 recs 1.2.1 and 1.2.7 (2014): "1.2.1 Document people's drug allergy status in their medical records using 1 of the following: • 'drug allergy' • 'none known' • 'unable to ascertain' (document it as soon as the information is available). 1.2.7 Ensure that information about drug allergy status is updated and included in all: • GP referral letters • hospital discharge letters." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg183/chapter/1-Recommendations
- Pass: The discharge letter states 'drug allergy' (naming the drug and reaction), 'none known' or 'unable to ascertain', and it matches the inpatient drug chart.. Target: ≥95% of letters. Sample: 50 consecutive discharge letters across medical and surgical wards over the last 2 weeks.
- Change: Make the discharge letter template pull allergy status automatically from the EPMA allergy record as a locked field.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-318.csv`
- Pitfalls: Letters say 'see drug chart' → count this as a fail; GPs cannot see it.; Allergy recorded in EPMA but not pulled through → check the IT mapping before cycle 1.; New allergies during admission are missed → record these separately; they matter most.; Specialties use different templates → sample across specialties in both cycles.
- Pearls: Recruit the EPR team early; the fix is an IT change.; Ask two local GPs how often letters lack allergies; quote them.; Record new in-hospital reactions as a sub-measure.; Hand the dashboard measure to the discharge quality lead.

**NNA-319. What proportion of adults admitted as an emergency at the weekend have medicines reconciliation completed within 24 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG5 rec 1.3.1 (2015): "In an acute setting, accurately list all of the person's medicines (including prescribed, over‑the‑counter and complementary medicines) and carry out medicines reconciliation within 24 hours or sooner if clinically necessary, when the person moves from one care setting to another – for example, if they are admitted to hospital." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng5/chapter/1-Recommendations
- Pass: A documented medicines reconciliation (at least two sources, with discrepancies resolved or noted) completed by a pharmacist, pharmacy technician or doctor within 24 hours of the admission time.. Target: ≥80% within 24 hours at weekends. Sample: 40 consecutive weekend emergency admissions over the last 4 weekends; optionally 40 weekday admissions for comparison.
- Change: Give the weekend on-call pharmacy team an EPMA worklist of admissions without a reconciliation, sorted by time since admission.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-319.csv`
- Pitfalls: Doctors' drug histories are counted as reconciliation → agree the definition (two sources, discrepancies addressed) before cycle 1.; Pharmacy managers see it as a staffing complaint → frame it as prioritising the highest-risk patients.; Timestamps are missing on paper → use EPMA only, or record time from notes consistently.; Bank holiday weekends skew data → note them and exclude or report separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the chief pharmacist early and share weekday data too.; Record critical medicines missed before reconciliation; that shows harm.; Use the Summary Care Record as a second source to speed it up.; Leave the weekend worklist report with the pharmacy duty manager.

**NNA-320. What proportion of adults admitted with sepsis, vomiting or diarrhoea who take an ACE inhibitor or ARB have a documented decision to withhold or continue it within 24 hours of admission?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG148 rec 1.2.14 (2013, guideline updated October 2024): "Consider temporarily stopping ACE inhibitors and ARBs in adults, children and young people with diarrhoea, vomiting or sepsis until their clinical condition has improved and stabilised. [2013]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng148/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Within 24 hours of admission the notes or EPMA record a decision to withhold the ACE inhibitor or ARB, or to continue it with a stated reason.. Target: ≥90% with a decision within 24 hours. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions to acute medicine over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add a 'sick day medicines' line to the acute medical clerking template listing ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics, metformin, SGLT2 inhibitors and NSAIDs with a withhold/continue choice.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-320.csv`
- Pitfalls: Heart failure teams object to stopping → a documented reason to continue is a pass.; 'Held' in notes but still given → check the administration record, not only the plan.; Sepsis diagnosis is vague → use the clerking diagnosis and keep the rule in both cycles.; Template line is ignored → check completion rate in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit an acute physician and the AKI nurse.; Record AKI at 72 hours; even small differences make the case.; Keep the line short so it is filled in.; Ask the AKI team to repeat it yearly.

**NNA-321. What proportion of intravenous potassium doses given on general wards are given as a ready-mixed (pre-diluted) infusion bag?** (new)
- Standard: NHS England Never Events list 2018 (updated February 2021), Never Event 4: Mis-selection of a strong potassium solution: "Mis-selection refers to: when a patient is intravenously given a strong potassium solution rather than the intended medication. Setting: All settings providing NHS-funded care. National safety requirement: Patient Safety Alert – Potassium chloride concentrate solutions (2002; updated 2003). [...] ≥10% potassium w/v (eg ≥0.1 g/mL potassium chloride, 1.3 mmol/mL potassium chloride)." https://www.england.nhs.uk/wp-content/uploads/2020/11/2018-Never-Events-List-updated-February-2021.pdf
- Pass: The IV potassium dose was given from a ready-mixed bag; no strong potassium concentrate (≥10% w/v) was used on a general ward.. Target: 100% ready-mixed on general wards. Sample: 50 consecutive IV potassium administrations over the last 4 weeks, plus a stock check of every general ward.
- Change: Remove potassium concentrate from all general ward stock and list the ready-mixed bags as the only EPMA options for ward prescribing.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-321.csv`
- Pitfalls: Product not recorded in the administration record → combine EPMA with ward stock and issue data.; Some wards need higher strength bags → agree a restricted list with pharmacy before cycle 1.; Concentrate reappears in stock after the audit → add it to the yearly stock check.; Staff fear running out of bags → confirm top-up levels with pharmacy.
- Pearls: Recruit the medication safety officer; this is a Never Event area.; Do the stock check with a ward pharmacist.; Report the stock check as a simple ward list.; Leave the yearly check with pharmacy.

**NNA-322. What proportion of IV paracetamol prescriptions for adults weighing under 50 kg use a weight-based dose of no more than 15 mg/kg?** (under-audited)
- Standard: SPS: Prescribing and administering intravenous paracetamol safely (updated June 2026), with MHRA Drug Safety Update (July 2010) dose table: "Deaths have occurred in adults with low body weight due to incorrect IV paracetamol dosing. [...] Use actual body weight when calculating IV paracetamol doses in very low weight children and adults. [MHRA:] Children, adolescents, and adults weighing >33 kg and <50 kg Dose (per administration): 1 intravenous infusion of 15 mg/kg; i.e., 1·5 mL solution per kg Maximum daily dose: 60 mg/kg (i.e., 6 mL/kg) without exceeding 3 g (i.e., 300 mL) in total" https://www.sps.nhs.uk/articles/prescribing-and-administering-intravenous-paracetamol-safely/
- Pass: A weight recorded this admission, each IV dose ≤15 mg/kg of actual body weight, and a total daily dose ≤60 mg/kg (and ≤3 g).. Target: 100% of low-weight adults dosed at ≤15 mg/kg. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible prescriptions over the last 2–3 months (search EPMA for IV paracetamol and weight <50 kg or blank).
- Change: Set the EPMA IV paracetamol order to require a current weight and default to 15 mg/kg when the weight is under 50 kg.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-322.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients with no weight recorded are missed by the search → include blank weights and count them as fails.; Oral and IV doses are added together → record the total from all routes.; Elderly frail patients just over 50 kg are also at risk → note hepatotoxicity risk factors for later cycles.; Default dose rule is overridden → check overrides in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit ward pharmacists; they catch most errors now.; Pair with the enoxaparin weight audit to share one weighing fix.; Show the SPS safety wording on one slide.; Leave the EPMA report with the medication safety officer.

## General practice and community care

**NNA-323. What proportion of women and girls of childbearing potential on repeat valproate have a signed Annual Risk Acknowledgement Form from the last 12 months in their GP record?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, April 2019 (valproate materials updated June 2025): "It is the prescriber’s responsibility to ensure women and girls of childbearing potential (from menarche to menopause) who are taking a valproate medicine, irrespective of indication, fulfil all the requirements of the Pregnancy Prevention Programme. These responsibilities include that the patient (or responsible person) and their specialist must complete the Annual Risk Acknowledgement Form at each year’s annual review." https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-medicines-and-serious-harms-in-pregnancy-new-annual-risk-acknowledgement-form-and-clinical-guidance-from-professional-bodies-to-support-compliance-with-the-pregnancy-prevention-programme
- Pass: A signed Annual Risk Acknowledgement Form (ARAF) dated within the 12 months before the audit date is filed or coded in the GP record. A specialist-signed ARAF section stating the patient is not at risk of pregnancy, countersigned by the patient, also counts.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible patients in the practice; if fewer than 30, include every practice in the primary care network (PCN).
- Change: Add a valproate alert to every repeat template that shows the date of the last ARAF at re-authorisation, backed by a practice register that triggers a letter to the specialist when a form is more than 11 months old.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-323.csv`
- Pitfalls: The ARAF is signed in clinic but never reaches the practice → request a copy before counting a fail, and record 'requested' separately.; Small numbers in one practice → pool across the PCN and report counts as well as percentages.; A clinic letter that mentions the programme is counted as the form → count a pass only for the signed form or its scanned copy.; Specialists object that the ARAF is their job → frame the audit as the practice checking the safety net, not doing the specialist's review.
- Pearls: Recruit the PCN clinical pharmacist; valproate is already on their high-risk drug list.; Agree with the local neurology and mental health teams before cycle 1 that every ARAF is copied to the GP.; Code each form on receipt so the search finds it next time.; Hand the monthly register to the practice medicines lead so it survives the audit team's rotation.

**NNA-324. What proportion of women of childbearing potential on repeat topiramate have highly effective contraception recorded in their GP record?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, June 2024: Topiramate (Topamax): introduction of new safety measures, including a Pregnancy Prevention Programme: "topiramate should not be used in women of childbearing potential unless the conditions of the Pregnancy Prevention Programme are fulfilled. This aims to ensure that all women of childbearing potential: are using highly effective contraception; have a pregnancy test to exclude pregnancy before starting topiramate; are aware of the risks from use of topiramate" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/topiramate-topamax-introduction-of-new-safety-measures-including-a-pregnancy-prevention-programme
- Pass: A highly effective method (contraceptive implant, copper intrauterine device, levonorgestrel intrauterine system, or sterilisation) is recorded as current at the audit date, or a documented reason that the patient is not at risk of pregnancy.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible patients in the practice; if fewer than 30, include every practice in the PCN.
- Change: Add a topiramate Pregnancy Prevention Programme alert to the repeat template, and send each woman without a recorded highly effective method a recall letter for a contraception review.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-324.csv`
- Pitfalls: Contraception fitted elsewhere (sexual health clinic) is not coded → check letters and ask the patient at recall before counting a fail.; Oral contraceptives are counted as highly effective → agree the list of highly effective methods before cycle 1 and keep it the same for the re-audit.; Topiramate is an enzyme inducer and can reduce hormonal contraceptive effect → flag this in the recall letter so the review covers method choice.; Patients feel accused by a letter about pregnancy → use neutral wording and the MHRA Patient Guide.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice's contraception lead nurse to run the recall clinic.; Use the MHRA Patient Guide and Risk Awareness Form so every review follows the same script.; Run the same search for valproate at the same time; the work is shared.; Put topiramate on the PCN high-risk drug list so the check survives rotation.

**NNA-325. What proportion of patients on stable repeat methotrexate had their full monitoring blood set (blood count, liver tests, renal function) in the 12 weeks before their latest prescription?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Specialist Pharmacy Service (SPS), Methotrexate monitoring (last updated January 2026): "Ongoing once stable. Required. Every 2 -3 months: Full blood count; Liver function tests; Serum creatinine (for creatinine clearance) or Estimated glomerular filtration rate. Rheumatology patients: At least every 12 weeks." https://www.sps.nhs.uk/monitorings/methotrexate-monitoring/
- Pass: A full blood count, liver function tests and a creatinine or eGFR, all dated within 84 days before the most recent methotrexate issue.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients (or all, if fewer) with a methotrexate issue in the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Limit methotrexate repeats to 12 weeks' supply and add a re-authorisation alert that shows the dates of the last FBC, LFT and eGFR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-325.csv`
- Pitfalls: Bloods taken in hospital clinics are not visible in the GP record → check the shared results viewer before counting a fail.; Dermatology and gastroenterology intervals differ → record the specialty and use the SPS interval for each; keep the definition fixed for the re-audit.; Staff issue 'one more month' without bloods → the alert must show dates, not just a reminder.; The SPS entry is being updated to the 2025 BSR guideline → check the page before cycle 2 and keep the cycle 1 definition if it changes.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice's repeat prescribing clerks; they see every request first.; Agree with rheumatology which blood results they will copy to the practice.; Report the median days since the last FBC as well as the pass rate.; Hand the monthly search to the PCN pharmacist so it continues after the audit.

**NNA-326. What proportion of adults aged 75 and over on a DOAC for atrial fibrillation are on the licensed dose for their Cockcroft-Gault creatinine clearance?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, October 2019: Prescribing medicines in renal impairment: "In particular, CrCl should always be used to guide dose adjustment for direct-acting oral anticoagulants (DOACs; apixaban, dabigatran etexilate, edoxaban▼, and rivaroxaban▼). Use of eGFR for dosing of DOACs is known to increase risk of bleeding events as a consequence of overestimating renal function." https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/prescribing-medicines-in-renal-impairment-using-the-appropriate-estimate-of-renal-function-to-avoid-the-risk-of-adverse-drug-reactions
- Pass: The prescribed dose matches the Summary of Product Characteristics dose for atrial fibrillation, using creatinine clearance calculated by Cockcroft-Gault from a weight and a creatinine both from the last 12 months (and, for apixaban, the age, weight and creatinine criteria).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients (alphabetical from the search), or all if fewer.
- Change: Add a DOAC review template that calculates Cockcroft-Gault creatinine clearance from the latest weight and creatinine and shows the licensed dose, triggered at each annual re-authorisation.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-326.csv`
- Pitfalls: No recent weight, so creatinine clearance cannot be calculated → count 'cannot calculate' as a fail and fix it by adding weight to the AF review.; Auditors disagree on which body weight to use → agree actual body weight (or the local policy) before cycle 1 and keep it for cycle 2.; Deliberate off-label dosing by cardiology → record it as a documented exception, not a fail.; Clinicians change doses on one creatinine during an acute illness → use a stable result, not one taken during an admission.
- Pearls: Recruit the PCN pharmacist; DOAC reviews are a standard pharmacist task.; Use one online Cockcroft-Gault calculator for all auditors.; Report the number of patients on too high a dose; it is the figure that moves partners.; Link the template to the annual AF review so it runs without the audit team.

**NNA-327. What proportion of adults on long-term oral prednisolone 5 mg a day or more have a Steroid Emergency Card issue recorded in their GP record?** (new)
- Standard: National Patient Safety Alert NatPSA/2020/005/NHSPS, Steroid Emergency Card (13 August 2020), action 2: "Prescribers undertaking standard/scheduled reviews (eg in clinics or when authorising repeat prescriptions) should review their processes/policies and their digital systems/software and prompts to ensure all eligible patients prescribed steroids have been assessed, and where necessary issue a Steroid Emergency Card." https://www.england.nhs.uk/2020/08/steroid-emergency-card-to-support-early-recognition-and-treatment-of-adrenal-crisis-in-adults/
- Pass: A coded or free-text record that a Steroid Emergency Card was issued (by the practice, hospital or pharmacy) at any time during the current course.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients from the search, or all if fewer.
- Change: Add a prompt to every oral glucocorticoid repeat template: 'Steroid Emergency Card issued? Code it', with cards stocked at reception and the dispensary.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-327.csv`
- Pitfalls: Cards given in hospital are never coded → check hospital letters and ask at the next contact before counting a fail.; Dose equivalents confuse the search → agree a conversion table (e.g. dexamethasone, hydrocortisone) before cycle 1.; The prompt is clicked through → count only coded issues, and show the override rate at the practice meeting.; Inhaled and topical steroid users are also eligible in some cases → keep this audit to oral steroids so the cycles stay comparable.
- Pearls: Recruit the dispensary or community pharmacy team; they can hand out cards when they supply prednisolone.; Order a stock of cards from NHS Forms before cycle 1.; Add card issue to the polymyalgia and COPD review templates, which cover most long-term users.; Name a medicines safety lead to own the prompt after the audit.

**NNA-328. What proportion of denosumab 60 mg injections given in general practice are given within 6 months and 4 weeks of the previous dose?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, August 2020: Denosumab 60mg (Prolia): increased risk of multiple vertebral fractures after stopping or delaying ongoing treatment (6-month-and-4-week window is a local standard): "an increased risk of multiple vertebral fractures has been reported in patients within 18 months of stopping or delaying ongoing denosumab 60mg treatment for osteoporosis; cases have been reported in patients in the UK. patients should not stop denosumab without specialist review. if you miss a prescribed dose of denosumab, the missed injection should be administered as soon as possible. After this, your next injection will be scheduled 6 months from the date of your last injection" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/denosumab-60mg-prolia-increased-risk-of-multiple-vertebral-fractures-after-stopping-or-delaying-ongoing-treatment
- Pass: The injection is given no more than 6 months and 4 weeks (211 days) after the previous injection.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: The last 40 consecutive denosumab injections given by the practice (or PCN).
- Change: Book the next injection at the time of each dose, and run a monthly recall search for anyone more than 6 months since their last injection, owned by a named practice nurse.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-328.csv`
- Pitfalls: Injections given elsewhere (hospital or community nurses) are missing → check letters and ask the patient before counting a delay.; Small numbers in one practice → pool injections across the PCN.; The recall list is run once and then forgotten → put it on the nurse lead's monthly task list.; Blood tests delay the injection → agree that calcium is checked in the month before the due date, not on the day.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice nurse who gives most injections; she knows who is overdue.; Give every patient a card with their next due date.; Report the number of patients more than 7 months since their last dose; it shows the fracture risk plainly.; Share the results with the fracture liaison service, which may help fund recall.

**NNA-329. In adults with chronic kidney disease newly started on an ACE inhibitor or ARB in general practice, what proportion have potassium and eGFR checked 1 to 2 weeks after starting?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG203 rec 1.6.15 (2021): "Measure serum potassium concentrations and estimate the GFR before starting renin–angiotensin system antagonists in adults with CKD. Repeat these measurements between 1 and 2 weeks after starting renin–angiotensin system antagonists and after each dose increase. [2008]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng203/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A serum potassium and an eGFR from the same sample, taken 7 to 14 days after the first prescription.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive new starters from the search, or all in 6 months if fewer.
- Change: Add an initiation protocol to the ACE inhibitor and ARB templates that creates a blood request and a reception task to book it 10 days ahead at the time of prescribing.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-329.csv`
- Pitfalls: A 7–14 day window is strict and patients test late → record the actual days and report 15–28 days separately.; Bloods taken by hospital clinics are not visible → check the shared results viewer.; Small numbers of new starters with CKD → widen to 6 months or pool across the PCN.; The booking task is created but ignored → give reception a weekly list of outstanding tasks.
- Pearls: Recruit the phlebotomy lead and reception manager; the fix is a booking step.; Agree with prescribers that the 'bloods in 10 days' task is part of the template, not optional.; Report any potassium above 6 found at follow-up; it makes the case.; Keep the protocol in the practice's prescribing policy so it survives staff changes.

**NNA-330. What proportion of adults with diabetes and chronic kidney disease with a urine ACR of 3 mg/mmol or more are prescribed an ACE inhibitor or ARB?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG203 rec 1.6.6 (2021): "For adults with CKD and diabetes (type 1 or type 2) offer an ARB or an ACE inhibitor (titrated to the highest licensed dose that the person can tolerate) if ACR is 3 mg/mmol or more. [2021]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng203/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: An ACE inhibitor or ARB issued in the last 3 months, or a documented contraindication, intolerance or informed decline.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients from the search, or all if fewer.
- Change: Add a prompt to the diabetes annual review template that fires when ACR is 3 mg/mmol or more and no ACE inhibitor or ARB is prescribed, plus a monthly search for the diabetes nurse.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-330.csv`
- Pitfalls: One raised ACR during illness is counted → require a confirmed result as in NICE, and keep the rule for cycle 2.; Patients stopped the drug for good reasons that were never written down → count a documented reason as a pass and ask prescribers to record it.; Clinicians fear hyperkalaemia → pair the change with the 1–2 week blood check protocol.; Numbers are small in one practice → pool across the PCN.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes specialist nurse and the practice's diabetes GP lead.; Agree the ACR confirmation rule before cycle 1.; Show the list of untreated patients by name to the diabetes clinic team; it turns into action quickly.; Add the search to the existing diabetes recall so it runs after the audit.

**NNA-331. What proportion of patients treated in an emergency care setting for an asthma attack are reviewed by their GP practice within 2 working days of discharge?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS25 statement 4 (2013, updated 2018): "People who receive treatment in an emergency care setting for an asthma attack are followed up by their general practice within 2 working days of discharge. [2013, updated 2018]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs25/chapter/Quality-statement-4-Follow-up-by-general-practice-after-emergency-care
- Pass: A documented clinical review (face to face, video or telephone) by a GP, nurse or pharmacist within 2 working days of discharge, or of the practice receiving notification if later.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive notifications over the last 3–6 months (pool across the PCN if needed).
- Change: Add a workflow rule so any discharge or ED letter mentioning asthma goes the same day to a named nurse task: 'Book asthma review within 2 working days'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-331.csv`
- Pitfalls: Letters arrive days late → measure from the date the practice receives the letter as well as the discharge date.; Workflow staff miss asthma in long letters → include 'wheeze' and 'salbutamol' in the search terms and train the workflow team.; Parents do not answer calls → count two documented attempts as a separate category, not a pass.; Out-of-hours notifications are missed → include the out-of-hours system report in the case search.
- Pearls: Recruit the document workflow team; the fix happens at their desk.; Offer a telephone review; it is enough for the standard and easier to fit in.; Check inhaler technique and preventer adherence in the same call.; Share results with the local ED; faster letters help both sides.

**NNA-332. What proportion of people aged 12 and over on the asthma register who were issued a reliever inhaler in the last 12 months were also issued an inhaled corticosteroid?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG245 (BTS/NICE/SIGN) rec 1.7.7 (2024): "Change treatment for people with confirmed asthma who are currently using a short-acting beta2 agonist (SABA) only to a low-dose ICS/formoterol combination inhaler used as needed (as-needed AIR therapy).
