[BTS/NICE/SIGN 2024]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng245/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: At least one inhaler containing an inhaled corticosteroid (including ICS/formoterol as AIR or MART) issued in the same 12 months.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: The whole eligible list from the search (usually hundreds); audit notes for a random 40 of those who fail.
- Change: Stop adding SABA as a stand-alone repeat for asthma: switch the practice asthma formulary and template default to as-needed ICS/formoterol, and invite everyone on SABA only for a review.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-332.csv`
- Pitfalls: Old, inactive registrants inflate the list → exclude patients with no asthma contact or issue in 2 years, and keep the rule for cycle 2.; Some people on the register do not have asthma → record diagnosis doubt as a reason rather than switching them.; Patients decline steroids → record informed decline, and report it separately.; Community pharmacy continues to supply old SABA repeats → update the repeat template, not just the formulary.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice asthma nurse and the PCN pharmacist.; Use the NICE/BTS/SIGN pathway diagram in every review so the message is the same.; Target people with 3 or more SABA inhalers first; they carry the most risk.; Add the search to the quarterly respiratory dashboard.

**NNA-333. What proportion of adults added to the COPD register in the last 2 years have post-bronchodilator spirometry with an FEV1/FVC below 0.7 recorded?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG115 rec 1.1.5 (2018, updated 2019): "Measure post-bronchodilator spirometry to confirm the diagnosis of COPD. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng115/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A post-bronchodilator FEV1/FVC below 0.7 recorded in the GP record, from a test done before or within 3 months after the diagnosis code was added.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new COPD diagnoses, or all if fewer.
- Change: Make the COPD diagnosis code available only through a diagnostic template that asks for the post-bronchodilator FEV1/FVC, or creates a spirometry referral task if none is recorded.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-333.csv`
- Pitfalls: Spirometry access in the area is limited → record 'referred, awaiting test' separately so the audit shows the service gap.; Pre-bronchodilator results are counted → check the report and keep the definition for cycle 2.; Older adults with FEV1/FVC just above 0.7 are argued over → follow the NICE threshold and note borderline results.; Hospital diagnoses lack results in the record → request the report before counting a fail.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice's respiratory nurse and the local community spirometry hub.; Agree a 'suspected COPD' code for use while waiting for spirometry.; Report the number of patients on inhalers without a confirmed diagnosis.; Put the template in the practice coding policy so new staff use it.

**NNA-334. What proportion of adults aged 26 and over newly started on an antidepressant in general practice have a documented review within 2 weeks?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG222 rec 1.4.11 (2022): "When prescribing antidepressant medication, ensure people have information about: ... when their first review will be; this will usually be within 2 weeks to check their symptoms are improving and for side effects, or 1 week after starting antidepressant medication if a new prescription is for a person aged 18 to 25 years or if there is a particular concern for risk of suicide" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng222/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A documented clinical review (face to face, video or telephone) of symptoms or side effects within 14 days of the first prescription.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive new starters.
- Change: Add a 'first review booked' step to the antidepressant initiation template that creates a telephone review task at day 10–14, and set the first issue to 14 days' supply.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-334.csv`
- Pitfalls: Reviews by practice pharmacists or talking therapy workers are not coded as reviews → agree what counts before cycle 1 and search all staff groups.; Restarts are counted as new starters → require no antidepressant issue in the previous 12 months.; Patients do not answer the review call → record two documented attempts separately.; 'Usually within 2 weeks' is read as optional → set the local target at 80%, not 100%.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice's mental health practitioner or care coordinator to run the calls.; Book the review before the patient leaves the first consultation.; A 14-day first supply builds the review in.; Hand the template to the practice mental health lead after the audit.

**NNA-335. What proportion of adults with suspected heart failure and an NT-proBNP above 2,000 ng/L from a GP request have specialist assessment with echocardiography within 2 weeks of the result?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG106 rec 1.2.3 (2018, updated 2025): "Because very high levels of NT-proBNP carry a poor prognosis, refer people with suspected heart failure and an NT-proBNP level more than 2,000 nanogram per litre (236 picomole per litre) urgently, to have specialist assessment and transthoracic echocardiography within 2 weeks. [2018]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng106/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Specialist heart failure assessment with transthoracic echocardiography completed within 14 days of the NT-proBNP result date.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive results (pool across the PCN or use the laboratory list for all local practices).
- Change: Agree with the laboratory an automatic comment on every NT-proBNP above 2,000 ng/L ('Refer urgently: specialist assessment and echo within 2 weeks') and a direct e-referral form to the heart failure one-stop clinic.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-335.csv`
- Pitfalls: The clinic's capacity, not the practice, causes delay → record referral date so practice delay and service delay are shown separately.; Tests requested for other reasons (e.g. known heart failure) are counted → exclude known heart failure at the search stage.; Small numbers per practice → use the laboratory list for the whole PCN or locality.; The lab comment is ignored in busy result workflows → pair it with a pop-up in the results workflow.
- Pearls: Recruit the community heart failure nurse; they often run the one-stop clinic.; Ask the laboratory for the monthly list of results above 2,000 ng/L; it makes case finding easy.; Report the number admitted as an emergency before assessment; it makes the case for capacity.; Agree the pathway in writing with cardiology so it survives staff changes.

**NNA-336. What proportion of women aged 55 and over presenting to general practice with unexplained post-menopausal bleeding are referred on a suspected cancer pathway?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.5.12 (2015, amended 2026): "Refer women, and trans men and non-binary people with female reproductive organs using a suspected cancer pathway referral for endometrial cancer if they are aged 55 and over with unexplained post-menopausal bleeding that cannot be attributed to hormone replacement therapy (HRT). [2015, amended 2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: A suspected cancer pathway referral for endometrial cancer (or the local direct-to-test equivalent) sent within 2 working days of the consultation.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive presentations (pool across the PCN if needed).
- Change: Link the post-menopausal bleeding code to a pop-up that opens the gynaecology suspected cancer referral form with the NICE criteria.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-336.csv`
- Pitfalls: Uncoded presentations are missed → also search free text for 'PMB' and 'post-menopausal bleed'.; Bleeding on HRT is counted → record HRT use and exclude it, as NICE does.; Local pathways send patients straight to ultrasound → agree before cycle 1 which routes count as the suspected cancer pathway.; Patients who decline are counted as fails → record informed decline separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice cancer lead and the PCN cancer care coordinator.; Use the national referral form so criteria appear on screen.; Review every non-referred case at the practice cancer meeting; it is quick and educational.; Keep the pop-up in the practice's clinical system policy.

**NNA-337. What proportion of adults aged 45 and over with unexplained visible haematuria in general practice are referred on a suspected cancer pathway?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.6.4 (2015): "Refer people using a suspected cancer pathway referral for bladder cancer if they are: aged 45 and over and have: unexplained visible haematuria without urinary tract infection, or visible haematuria that persists or recurs after successful treatment of urinary tract infection, or aged 60 and over and have unexplained non-visible haematuria and either dysuria or a raised white cell count on a blood test. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: A urology suspected cancer pathway referral sent within 2 working days of the consultation (or of a negative urine culture, if one was sent).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive presentations (pool across the PCN if needed).
- Change: Add a haematuria template that asks about age, infection and anticoagulation and opens the urology suspected cancer referral form when the NICE criteria are met.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-337.csv`
- Pitfalls: Haematuria is put down to anticoagulants → state in the template that anticoagulation does not explain it.; Presumed UTI without culture delays referral → record whether a culture was sent and the date.; Uncoded presentations are missed → search free text for 'blood in urine' and 'haematuria'.; Declined referrals are counted as fails → record them separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice cancer lead and a urology consultant to agree the template wording.; Report the median days from consultation to referral.; Discuss each non-referral at the cancer meeting; it teaches without blame.; Share the template across the PCN once it works.

**NNA-338. What proportion of men with possible prostate cancer symptoms and a PSA above the NICE age-specific threshold have a suspected cancer referral or a documented reason not to refer?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.6.3 and table 2 (2021): "Consider referring men, and trans women and non-binary people with male reproductive organs who have possible symptoms of prostate cancer, as specified in recommendation 1.6.2, using a suspected cancer pathway referral for prostate cancer if their PSA levels are above the threshold for their age in table 2. Take into account their preferences and any comorbidities when making the decision. [2021]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: A urology suspected cancer referral sent within 2 weeks of the result, or a documented decision not to refer that names the reason (patient preference or comorbidity). NICE table 2 thresholds (micrograms/litre): 40–49 more than 2.5; 50–59 more than 3.5; 60–69 more than 4.5; 70–79 more than 6.5.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive raised results, or all in 12 months if fewer.
- Change: Add a laboratory comment to every PSA above the age threshold ('Above NICE threshold: consider suspected cancer referral') and a results-workflow rule that sends it back to the requesting clinician.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-338.csv`
- Pitfalls: Tests done for screening without symptoms are included → record symptoms and report asymptomatic tests separately.; Repeat PSA before referral is common local practice → agree before cycle 1 whether a repeat within 2 weeks counts, and keep it fixed.; Men over 79 inflate the list → NICE leaves them to clinical judgement; exclude them.; Filing by a clinician who did not request the test → record who filed it.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice cancer lead and the laboratory's duty biochemist.; Put the age thresholds on a card beside every screen.; Review every unactioned result at the monthly cancer meeting.; Make the results-workflow rule part of the practice's results protocol.

**NNA-339. What proportion of GP-requested chest X-rays reported as suggesting lung cancer lead to a suspected lung cancer referral or direct CT within 7 days of the report?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.1.1 (2015); 7-day timeframe is a local standard: "Refer people using a suspected cancer pathway referral for lung cancer if they: have chest X-ray findings that suggest lung cancer, or are aged 40 and over with unexplained haemoptysis. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-organised-by-site-of-cancer
- Pass: A suspected lung cancer referral sent, or a CT chest booked through a direct-to-CT pathway, within 7 days of the report date.. Target: 100%. Sample: All eligible reports in 12 months across the PCN (typically 20–50).
- Change: Agree with radiology a 'suspicious for cancer' alert code that routes the report to the practice's urgent results workflow the same day, and keep a failsafe register until each patient is seen.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-339.csv`
- Pitfalls: Radiology already books CT directly → record this and count it as a pass, but still check the patient was told.; Keyword searches miss unusual wording → agree the phrase list with a radiologist before cycle 1.; Small numbers → use a full year and the whole PCN.; Reports are filed by a locum who then leaves → the failsafe register must name the practice, not a person.
- Pearls: Recruit the radiology lead for GP reporting; they can add the alert code.; Treat any fail as a significant event and review it at the practice meeting.; Put the failsafe register on the shared practice drive with a named administrator.; Combine with the practice's other cancer failsafe checks to save time.

**NNA-340. What proportion of patients referred on a suspected cancer pathway from general practice have it documented that they were told the referral is to a cancer service?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.14.1 (2015): "Explain to people who are being referred with suspected cancer that they are being referred to a cancer service. Reassure them, as appropriate, that most people referred will not have a diagnosis of cancer, and discuss alternative diagnoses with them. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations-on-patient-support-safety-netting-and-the-diagnostic-process
- Pass: The consultation or referral records that the patient was told the referral is to a cancer (suspected cancer) service.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive suspected cancer referrals.
- Change: Add a mandatory tick-box to the practice's suspected cancer referral template: 'Told this is a referral to a cancer service; leaflet given', with the national patient leaflet linked.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-340.csv`
- Pitfalls: Clinicians say they explained but did not write it → the fix is a template field, not more teaching.; The tick-box is ticked by default → make it blank and mandatory.; Missed appointments have other causes → record them for interest, not as the pass criterion.; Clinicians worry about frightening patients → the NICE wording includes reassurance; build it into the template.
- Pearls: Recruit the PCN cancer care coordinator; they can phone patients after referral.; Link the national patient leaflet in the template.; Check availability for the next 2 weeks in the same step.; Keep the template in the practice's referral policy.

**NNA-341. What proportion of patients aged 65 and over coded with severe frailty have had a medication review in the last 12 months?** (new)
- Standard: NHS (General Medical Services Contracts) Regulations 2015, Schedule 3 paragraph 7A, inserted by SI 2017/908: "(3) Where the contractor identifies a patient aged 65 years or over who is living with severe frailty, the contractor must— (a) undertake a clinical review in respect of the patient which includes— (i) an annual review of the patient’s medication, and (ii) where appropriate, a discussion with the patient about whether the patient has fallen in the last 12 months;" https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2017/908/regulation/3/made
- Pass: A documented medication review (coded, with a note of medicines considered and any changes or a statement that none were needed) dated within 12 months of the audit date. Re-authorisation of repeats alone does not count.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 randomly selected patients from the severe frailty list.
- Change: A frailty review template (medication review plus falls question) and a monthly search of severe frailty patients without a review in 12 months, assigned to the PCN pharmacist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-341.csv`
- Pitfalls: Re-authorisation is coded as a medication review → count a pass only when the note shows medicines were considered.; The eFI over-labels frailty → accept the coded list but record the frailty source.; Care home reviews by another team are not in the record → check care home pharmacist letters.; Pharmacist capacity runs out → prioritise those on 10 or more medicines.
- Pearls: Recruit the PCN clinical pharmacist; structured medication reviews are part of their role.; Agree what counts as a medication review before cycle 1.; Report the number of medicines stopped; it shows value.; Tie the search to the care home and frailty ward rounds so it continues.

**NNA-342. What proportion of practice patients whose cervical screening result needs colposcopy have colposcopy attendance recorded in their GP record within 12 weeks of the result?** (new)
- Standard: NHS Cervical Screening Programme, Good practice guidance for sample takers, section 14.4 (updated August 2026); 12-week timeframe is a local standard: "GPs that provide cervical screening services in accordance with the GMS contract are responsible for making sure that the test result is known and followed up appropriately and refer a person for further investigation and treatment where necessary (for example, a person needing colposcopy who has moved from another area, or a person who has been discharged following a previous non-attendance at colposcopy). All laboratories operate a direct referral system for colposcopy in conjunction with their local colposcopy clinics. The GP still has a responsibility to make sure that colposcopy has taken place" https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/nhs-cervical-screening-programme-good-practice-guidance-for-sample-takers/nhs-cervical-screening-programme-good-practice-guidance-for-sample-takers
- Pass: A colposcopy clinic letter, attendance code or outcome filed in the GP record within 12 weeks of the screening result date.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All eligible results in 12 months across the practice or PCN (typically 20–60).
- Change: Keep a colposcopy failsafe register: every result needing colposcopy is added on receipt and checked monthly by a named administrator until a colposcopy letter is filed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-342.csv`
- Pitfalls: Colposcopy letters are slow → wait 12 weeks before counting a fail, and check the hospital system.; Patients treated privately or elsewhere → record them and ask for letters.; The register is started and then dropped → name the administrator and a deputy.; Numbers are small → include the whole PCN and 12 months.
- Pearls: Recruit the practice's cervical screening administrator; they already receive results.; Agree with the colposcopy clinic that DNA letters are copied to the practice.; Treat any patient lost to follow-up as a significant event.; Combine with the sample-taker's 'every sample has a result' register for one failsafe routine.

## Head, neck, eye and skin

**NNA-343. What proportion of adults with sudden hearing loss that began within the last 30 days are seen by ENT within 24 hours of referral?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG98 rec 1.1.2 (2018, updated 2023): "Refer adults with sudden onset or rapid worsening of hearing loss in one or both ears, which is not explained by external or middle ear causes, as follows. If the hearing loss developed suddenly (over a period of 3 days or less) within the past 30 days, refer immediately (to be seen within 24 hours) to an ear, nose and throat service or an emergency department." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng98/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Seen face to face by an ENT clinician (any grade) within 24 hours of the time the referral was made (GP, emergency department, optometrist, NHS 111 or other).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible adults over the last 12 months (aim for 30–40; sudden hearing loss is uncommon).
- Change: Create a 'sudden hearing loss' hot slot in the daily ENT emergency clinic that GPs and the emergency department book directly through one bleep or email, with a same-day audiogram request built into the booking.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-343.csv`
- Pitfalls: Referral time missing for telephone referrals → log the time of the first call in the ENT referral book and use the same source in both cycles.; Small numbers make percentages jump → use a 12-month look-back first, re-audit prospectively over 6 months and report counts as well as percentages.; Conductive or middle ear causes included by mistake → confirm otoscopy and audiogram findings before counting a case.; Hot slot filled with routine cases → print the NG98 criteria on the booking form and let the ENT registrar veto misuse.
- Pearls: Recruit the audiology lead early: a same-day audiogram is usually the bottleneck.; Agree with the emergency department that out-of-hours cases get the next-morning slot, not a routine letter.; Put the pathway on the GP referral guidance page so it survives trainee rotation.

**NNA-344. When adults or children in the emergency department are given an oral antibiotic for sore throat or tonsillitis, what proportion receive phenoxymethylpenicillin (or clarithromycin or erythromycin if allergic to penicillin)?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG84 rec 1.3.1 and table 1 (2018): "When prescribing an antibiotic for acute sore throat: follow table 1 for adults aged 18 years and over; follow table 2 for children and young people under 18 years. [Table 1] First-choice oral antibiotic: Phenoxymethylpenicillin: 500 mg four times a day or 1,000 mg twice a day for 5 to 10 days … Alternative first choice for penicillin allergy or intolerance (for people who are not pregnant): Clarithromycin: 250 mg to 500 mg twice a day for 5 days." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng84/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The oral antibiotic given is phenoxymethylpenicillin; or clarithromycin (erythromycin in pregnancy) when a penicillin allergy or intolerance is recorded. Any other antibiotic is a fail.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive eligible attendances over the last 2 months.
- Change: Build a 'sore throat' order set in the emergency department discharge prescribing system that defaults to phenoxymethylpenicillin at the NG84 dose, and shows clarithromycin or erythromycin only when an allergy is recorded.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-344.csv`
- Pitfalls: Parents object to four-times-daily liquid → offer the twice-daily regimen NG84 allows and explain it on the leaflet.; Pre-pack cupboard holds amoxicillin only → agree stock with pharmacy before cycle 1.; Unverified penicillin allergy labels → count clarithromycin as a pass only when an allergy is recorded; report the label rate separately.; Order set bypassed by free-text prescribing → check in week 6 how many prescriptions used it and remind the team.
- Pearls: Recruit the emergency department pharmacist; they own the pre-pack stock and the order set.; Agree the diagnosis codes to search with the coding team before cycle 1 and keep them the same.; Ask the stewardship team to report the measure quarterly so it outlives the audit team.

**NNA-345. What proportion of suspected skin cancer referrals for a pigmented lesion record a weighted 7-point checklist score?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG12 rec 1.7.1 (2015, updated 2026): "Refer people using a suspected cancer pathway referral for melanoma if they have a suspicious pigmented skin lesion with a weighted 7‑point checklist score of 3 or more." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng12/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The referral records a total weighted 7-point checklist score (or each feature scored so the total can be read).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive referrals over the last 2 months.
- Change: Replace free-text skin cancer referrals with a regional e-referral form on which the 7-point checklist is a required field that calculates the score.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-345.csv`
- Pitfalls: The form needs primary care agreement → take it through the cancer alliance or ICB clinical lead, not the hospital alone.; Scores ticked without real assessment → compare referral scores with the clinic findings in a sample of cases.; Referrals arrive by several routes → agree one extraction route before cycle 1 and keep it the same.; Worry that a low score will stop urgent referral → state on the form that clinical concern can still override the score.
- Pearls: Recruit a GP with a special interest in dermatology to champion the form.; Feed back each practice's scores and diagnoses; it motivates more than reminders.; Add a prompt to attach a dermoscopic photograph; it also supports teledermatology triage.

**NNA-346. What proportion of wide local excisions for stage I or II melanoma use the NICE clinical margin (1 cm for stage I, 2 cm for stage II)?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG14 rec 1.5.3 (2015, updated 2022): "Use a clinical margin of: 1 cm when excising stage I melanoma or when a 2 cm excision margin would cause unacceptable disfigurement or morbidity; 2 cm when excising stage II melanoma. The clinical margin should be around the histological biopsy scar and take into account the primary melanoma margin." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng14/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The operation note records a clinical margin around the biopsy scar of 1 cm for stage I or 2 cm for stage II; or 1 cm for stage II with a documented reason that 2 cm would cause unacceptable disfigurement or morbidity.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive wide local excisions over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Add a required 'stage and planned clinical margin (cm)' box to the skin surgery operation note template, pre-filled from the MDT outcome.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-346.csv`
- Pitfalls: Stage not known at the time of the op note → take stage from the MDT outcome and record it before listing.; Margin written as 'WLE' without a number → make the numeric field required in the template.; Face and hand cases pull the rate down → count a documented reason for a reduced margin as a pass, as NICE allows.; Different surgeons read 'margin' differently → define it as the distance from the scar edge, agreed before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit one dermatologic surgeon and one plastic surgeon as joint leads so both services own it.; Ask the MDT coordinator to add the recommended margin to the MDT outcome letter.; Record the site; head and neck cases usually explain most variation.

**NNA-347. For isotretinoin prescriptions issued to patients who can get pregnant, what proportion have a negative pregnancy test documented 3 days or less before the prescription is issued?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, Oral retinoids: pregnancy prevention – reminder of measures to minimise teratogenic risk (2013, republished 2014; read with the 2023 and 2026 isotretinoin updates): "Pregnancy must be excluded before treatment with oral retinoids … Pregnancy test results (with a minimum sensitivity of 25 mIU/mL) must be documented 3 days or less before the prescription is issued" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/oral-retinoids-pregnancy-prevention-reminder-of-measures-to-minimise-teratogenic-risk
- Pass: A negative, medically supervised pregnancy test (in person or remote) with its date and result recorded, dated 3 days or less before the prescription date.. Target: 100%. Sample: 50 consecutive prescriptions over the last 3 months.
- Change: Make the isotretinoin prescription form (paper or electronic) require the pregnancy test date and result, with pharmacy screening set to refuse to dispense if the test is more than 3 days older than the prescription.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-347.csv`
- Pitfalls: Remote tests make dates unclear → record the date the test was done, not the date the result was uploaded.; Prescriptions are signed days before the clinic → define the prescription date as the date it is issued to the patient or pharmacy.; Childbearing status mislabelled → review the PPP status in every case, not only those on the PPP list.; Pharmacy pushback on refusing to dispense → agree the rule with the chief pharmacist before the change.
- Pearls: Recruit the dermatology specialist nurse who runs the PPP clinic; they hold the test log.; Align fields with the BAD national audit so one data set serves both.; Check homecare and community pharmacy prescriptions as well as hospital ones.

**NNA-348. Of adults who have taken hydroxychloroquine for more than 5 years, what proportion had retinal monitoring in the last 12 months?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Royal College of Ophthalmologists, Hydroxychloroquine and Chloroquine Retinopathy: Recommendations on Monitoring (December 2020), section 2.2: "All individuals who have taken hydroxychloroquine for greater than five years should receive annual monitoring for retinopathy. … It is the responsibility of the prescribing physician (as per GMC guidelines) to refer patients eligible for monitoring to the local hospital eye service." https://www.rcophth.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/Hydroxychloroquine-and-Chloroquine-Retinopathy-.pdf
- Pass: A retinal monitoring visit (SD-OCT, with fundus autofluorescence or visual fields as the local protocol requires) recorded in the hospital eye service within the last 12 months (allow up to 13 months).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 patients randomly selected from the rheumatology and dermatology hydroxychloroquine lists.
- Change: Run a monthly report from the DMARD database of patients reaching 5 years on hydroxychloroquine and send each an RCOphth-style standard referral to the eye service.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-348.csv`
- Pitfalls: Start dates unknown when GPs started treatment → use the earliest prescription found and state the rule.; Eye service capacity objects to a surge of referrals → agree numbers and imaging slots with the medical retina lead before sending.; Patients monitored elsewhere are counted as fails → check community optometry or other trusts before scoring.; Lists miss patients on homecare or shared care → merge DMARD, homecare and pharmacy lists.
- Pearls: Recruit a rheumatology specialist nurse to own the monthly report.; Use a standard referral proforma with dose, weight, eGFR and tamoxifen use so the eye service can risk-stratify.; Include dermatology and lupus clinics, which are often missed.

**NNA-349. What proportion of adults with chronic open angle glaucoma are seen within the review interval their clinician set at the previous visit?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG81 rec 1.5.13 and table 3 (2017, updated 2022): "For people with COAG: use clinical judgement to assess risk of COAG progression to sight loss, and reassess according to table 3. … Use clinical judgement to decide when the next appointment should take place within the recommended interval, including the need to assess the impact of any new treatments started." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng81/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Seen by the date requested at the previous visit plus a tolerance of 15% of the requested interval (for example, a 6-month interval is met by 6.9 months).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 50 patients randomly selected from those due in the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a required risk-of-harm rating (high, medium, low) to the glaucoma clinic outcome form and run a weekly overdue report that books high-risk patients first.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-349.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients who did not attend are counted as service failures → record the reason and report both rates.; Requested interval not recorded → make the interval a required field on the outcome form.; Capacity limits make the target look impossible → pair the audit with a capacity-and-demand count so managers see the gap.; Tolerance changed between cycles → fix the 15% rule before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the outpatient booking lead and the service manager; they own the fix.; Present the number of patients now overdue, not only the percentage.; Use nurse-led or virtual glaucoma clinics for stable, low-risk patients to free consultant slots.

**NNA-350. What proportion of ward patients with a tracheostomy or laryngectomy have a complete emergency airway equipment set at the bedside?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NCEPOD, Tracheostomy Care: On the Right Trach? (2014), recommendation 14: "All hospitals should adhere to recommendations already made by the National Tracheostomy Safety Project to maintain an essential box of equipment which is sufficiently portable to be moved around with the patient." https://www.ncepod.org.uk/2014report1/downloads/OnTheRightTrach_Summary.pdf
- Pass: Every item on the hospital's tracheostomy or laryngectomy bedside checklist (based on the National Tracheostomy Safety Project list) is present and in date, including spare tubes of the same size and one size smaller for tracheostomies.. Target: 100%. Sample: Point-prevalence checks twice a week for 2 weeks until at least 30 patient-checks are made (a patient may be checked more than once).
- Change: Introduce a sealed, tagged tracheostomy 'grab box' issued by critical care outreach with every ward transfer, with a once-a-shift check line added to the nursing safety checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-350.csv`
- Pitfalls: Checklist differs between wards → agree one hospital list with the tracheostomy lead before cycle 1.; Staff feel policed at the bedside → check with the nurse in charge and report by ward, not by name.; Very few patients on some days → spread checks across the fortnight and report counts.; Box emptied after use and not restocked → use numbered tags so an opened box is easy to spot.
- Pearls: Recruit critical care outreach; they see every ward tracheostomy and can issue the boxes.; Include laryngectomy patients; they are often on ENT or oncology wards without trache-trained staff.; Pair with the bedhead-sign check so one visit covers both.

**NNA-351. Of patients discharged from critical care to a ward with a cuffed tracheostomy tube, what proportion have the reason for keeping a cuffed tube documented?** (new)
- Standard: NCEPOD, Tracheostomy Care: On the Right Trach? (2014), recommendation 12: "Particularly careful consideration should be made at discharge from the critical care unit as to whether a cuffed tube is still indicated, and reasons must be documented. If it is, then there must be equipment and competences available on the ward for cuff pressure measurement." https://www.ncepod.org.uk/2014report1/downloads/OnTheRightTrach_Summary.pdf
- Pass: The critical care discharge summary or ward-transfer note states why a cuffed tube is still needed (for example aspiration risk or need for ventilation).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All eligible discharges over the last 12 months (aim for 30–40).
- Change: Add a required 'cuffed tube still needed? yes/no and reason' field to the critical care discharge summary template for any patient with a tracheostomy.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-351.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers → use a 12-month look-back and report counts.; Reason given in the ward round note but not in the discharge summary → agree before cycle 1 which documents count and keep them fixed.; Staff see it as paperwork → show the NCEPOD ward-death findings when presenting cycle 1.; Field filled with 'as per plan' → define acceptable reasons in the template note.
- Pearls: Recruit the critical care tracheostomy lead and outreach team as co-leads.; Link the field to an automatic outreach referral so the ward gets support on day 1.; Record whether a cuff manometer was on the receiving ward; it often explains ward incidents.

**NNA-352. In children under 5 attending the emergency department with a burn or scald, what proportion have the notes record whether the explanation fits the injury?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG89 rec 1.1.6 (2009, updated 2025): "Suspect child maltreatment if a child has burn or scald injuries: if the explanation for the injury is absent or unsuitable or if the child is not independently mobile or on any soft tissue area that would not be expected to come into contact with a hot object in an accident (for example, the backs of hands, soles of feet, buttocks, back) or in the shape of an implement (for example, cigarette, iron) or that indicate forced immersion …" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg89/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The emergency department notes contain an explicit statement that the explanation is, or is not, consistent with the injury and the child's development (a completed burns safeguarding checklist counts).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive attendances over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a required five-question burns safeguarding box (explanation, consistency, mobility, site and pattern, previous attendances) to the emergency department paediatric burns template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-352.csv`
- Pitfalls: Staff fear that asking implies blame → use neutral wording in the box and explain it is asked of every child.; Checklist in paper notes gets lost → build it into the electronic template.; Paediatric liaison already reviews cases → agree that their note does not count unless it is made during the attendance.; Changing the age range between cycles → fix under 5 before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the named safeguarding nurse or doctor as co-lead.; Use the child's development stage (mobility) prompt; it catches most non-mobile scalds.; Include urgent treatment centres, where many small scalds are seen.

**NNA-353. Of patients who reach the emergency department within 3 hours of a thermal burn, what proportion have 20 minutes of cool running water documented?** (new)
- Standard: London and South East of England Burn Network (LSEBN), Initial management of burn wounds (2026): "Cool with running tap water for 20 minutes within 3 hours of injury. If limited water supply, apply a cool compress, change frequently over 20-minute period. … Do not use ice/iced water/ice packs" https://www.lsebn.nhs.uk/clinical-guidelines
- Pass: The notes record at least 20 minutes of cool running water in total (before arrival plus in the department), started within 3 hours of injury.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive eligible attendances over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a 'cooling' section to the emergency department triage burns template (minutes before arrival, time cooling started and stopped in the department), with triage sending any patient short of 20 minutes to a designated sink or shower.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-353.csv`
- Pitfalls: Pre-hospital cooling not written down → ask about it at triage and record 'unknown' rather than guessing.; Children get cold → cool the burn and keep the child warm, as the guidance says; exclude large burns.; Busy triage skips cooling → make the sink location and a timer part of the triage step.; Hydrogel used instead of water → record it separately; it does not count as cooling.
- Pearls: Recruit the triage nurse lead; they own the template.; Put a clock and a laminated '20 minutes' sign above the designated sink.; Share results with the ambulance service; they can change the handover template too.

**NNA-354. In patients with a chemical injury to the eye, what proportion have the eye pH rechecked both straight after irrigation stops and again 30 minutes later?** (new)
- Standard: LSEBN, Initial management of ocular burns (2026, developed with Moorfields Eye Hospital): "Check corneal ph (mean ph of tears is 7.6) … In chemical injuries, repeat the pH test soon after the irrigation has ceased and again 30 minutes later" https://www.lsebn.nhs.uk/clinical-guidelines
- Pass: Two post-irrigation pH readings are documented with times: one soon after irrigation stops (within 5 minutes) and one at least 30 minutes later.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive attendances over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Stock a chemical eye injury pack at triage (pH strips, topical anaesthetic, Morgan lens or giving set, fluorescein) with a sticker chart for pH before irrigation, at the end of irrigation and 30 minutes later.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-354.csv`
- Pitfalls: pH strips not stocked → check stock before cycle 1 and add them to the pack list.; Patient leaves or moves to eye casualty before the 30-minute check → allow the eye unit's documented check to count.; Small numbers → use a 12-month look-back and report counts.; Staff unsure what 'normal' is → print the target pH range on the sticker.
- Pearls: Recruit an emergency nurse practitioner who sees most minor eye injuries.; Keep the pack sealed and tagged so used packs are easy to spot and restock.; Include industrial and cleaning-product injuries from occupational health reports to find cases.

**NNA-355. Of children discharged after a burn, what proportion have toxic shock syndrome safety-netting advice documented?** (new)
- Standard: LSEBN, Toxic Shock guideline (January 2026), section 2.3: "Provide TSS safety netting advice (verbal and written where possible)" https://www.lsebn.nhs.uk/clinical-guidelines
- Pass: The discharge note records that toxic shock advice was given (verbally or with a leaflet) covering fever, rash, diarrhoea or vomiting, and when to return.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive discharges over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add toxic shock advice to the emergency department paediatric burns discharge template as a required tick-box that prints the network toxic shock leaflet.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-355.csv`
- Pitfalls: Advice given but not written down → make the tick-box part of the discharge template, not free text.; Leaflet not stocked → print it from the template instead of relying on paper stock.; Children seen again in dressing clinic are double-counted → count the first discharge only.; Parents with limited English → include translated or pictorial versions of the leaflet.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric emergency nurse lead and the burns outreach nurse.; Ask the burns unit to check advice was given at their follow-up call.; Keep the advice short: fever, rash, diarrhoea or vomiting, drowsiness, return straight away.

**NNA-356. Of patients with a facial burn seen in the emergency department, what proportion have a fluorescein eye examination documented?** (new)
- Standard: LSEBN, Facial Burns management (March 2026): "Check eyes with fluorescein … Consider ophthalmology review if required" https://www.lsebn.nhs.uk/clinical-guidelines
- Pass: A fluorescein staining result is documented for each eye (or a documented reason it could not be done, with ophthalmology review arranged).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive attendances over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add 'eyes: fluorescein result right and left' to the emergency department burns template, triggered whenever the face is shaded on the body map, and keep fluorescein strips and a blue light in the burns bay.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-356.csv`
- Pitfalls: Swollen lids make examination hard → allow a documented reason plus ophthalmology review to count as a pass.; Equipment kept only in the eye room → stock strips and a blue light in the burns bay.; Face shaded on the body map but not coded as facial burn → search the body map as well as diagnosis codes.; Intubated patients missed → include them; eye checks matter most when patients cannot report symptoms.
- Pearls: Recruit an ophthalmology registrar to give a 10-minute demonstration at the start.; Link the fluorescein field to the facial burn leaflet so both are done together.; Report corneal injuries found; they persuade staff more than percentages.

**NNA-357. Of patients with a burn and at least one sign of inhalation injury, what proportion are reviewed by a senior anaesthetist in the emergency department?** (new)
- Standard: LSEBN, Burn Inhalation Injury protocol (2026): "Review by senior anaesthetist and consider early intubation if there is a suspicion of BII: early intubation may decrease pulmonary-related mortality in patients with inhalation injury." https://www.lsebn.nhs.uk/clinical-guidelines
- Pass: A documented airway assessment by a senior anaesthetist (grade agreed locally before cycle 1, for example ST5 or above, specialty doctor or consultant) while the patient is in the emergency department.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 12–24 months (aim for 30).
- Change: Add 'suspected inhalation injury' to the emergency department burns template and trauma call criteria as an automatic trigger for a senior anaesthetist call.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-357.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few cases each year → use a 24-month look-back if needed and report counts.; 'Senior' means different grades → fix the definition before cycle 1 and keep it.; Anaesthetist review happens but is written only on the anaesthetic chart → search both records.; Burn team asks for a review only when stridor appears → the trigger must use the full feature list, not stridor alone.
- Pearls: Recruit an anaesthetic consultant as co-lead; they can change the call criteria.; Measure carboxyhaemoglobin in every enclosed-space fire; it helps identify cases.; Share each case in joint emergency and anaesthetic morbidity meetings.

**NNA-358. Of patients taking warfarin who have a tooth extraction, what proportion have their INR checked no more than 24 hours before (or 72 hours if their INR is stable)?** (under-audited)
- Standard: SDCEP, Management of Dental Patients Taking Anticoagulants or Antiplatelet Drugs, 2nd edition (2022), section 6: "Ensure that the patient’s INR has been checked, ideally no more than 24 hours before the procedure. If the patient has a stable INR, checking the INR no more than 72 hours before is acceptable." https://www.sdcep.org.uk/media/4gslzda1/sdcep-management-of-dental-patients-taking-anticoagulants-or-antiplatelet-drugs-2nd-edition.pdf
- Pass: An INR result with its date and time is recorded no more than 24 hours before the extraction, or no more than 72 hours before if the INR is stable (no weekly monitoring and no INR above 4 in the last 2 months).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive procedures over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Use a point-of-care INR meter in the oral surgery clinic on the day of the procedure, with the result entered in a required field on the extraction safety checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-358.csv`
- Pitfalls: INR from the anticoagulation clinic has no time → record date and time from the pathology system or meter log.; Stability is unknown → count as unstable unless the anticoagulation record shows otherwise.; Meter quality control lapses → assign one named nurse and follow the manufacturer's checks.; Patients told by others to stop warfarin → record interruptions separately; they are a harm too.
- Pearls: Recruit the clinic nurse lead, who will run the meter.; Send patients the SDCEP warfarin instruction sheet with their appointment letter.; Agree with the anticoagulation clinic how to share recent INR results.

**NNA-359. What proportion of adults starting radiotherapy for head and neck cancer had a specialist dental assessment before treatment began?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Royal College of Surgeons of England, Faculty of Dental Surgery, The Oral & Dental Management of Patients Before, During and After Cancer Therapy (February 2026), section 5.2: "Patients who are due to undergo surgery and/or radiotherapy to the head and neck region, should be assessed by the specialist dental team that works with the head and neck surgeon and oncologist in the multidisciplinary team" https://www.rcseng.ac.uk/-/media/Files/RCS/FDS/Publications/The-oral-and-dental-management-of-patients-before-during-and-after-cancer-therapy.pdf
- Pass: A documented assessment by the specialist (restorative or hospital) dental team dated before the first radiotherapy fraction.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over the last 6 months.
- Change: Make 'referred to specialist dental team' a required outcome field on the head and neck MDT form for any patient with radiotherapy in the plan, generating the referral automatically.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-359.csv`
- Pitfalls: Edentulous patients skipped → include them; dentures and bone still need checking.; Assessment done at another hospital → accept any specialist dental record if it is in the notes before radiotherapy.; Fast-tracked treatment starts before dental review → flag these at MDT and record the reason.; Dental team capacity → agree a ring-fenced weekly slot for MDT referrals before the change.
- Pearls: Recruit the restorative dentistry consultant who sits on the MDT.; Ask the MDT coordinator to add dental status to the MDT outcome letter.; Record days from extraction to radiotherapy too; it shows whether the pathway is early enough.

**NNA-360. Of adults treated in the emergency department for nosebleeds that did not stop with first aid, what proportion have nasal cautery attempted before a nasal pack is inserted?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Local standard: "Adults with an anterior nosebleed that continues after 10–15 minutes of first-aid pressure should have the nose examined with a headlight after topical vasoconstrictor and local anaesthetic, and have silver nitrate cautery of any visible bleeding point attempted before a nasal pack is inserted. If a pack is inserted without cautery, the reason is documented." 
- Pass: Cautery attempted before any pack; or a documented reason for packing without cautery (no bleeding point seen after examination with a headlight and vasoconstrictor, or bleeding too heavy to see).. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 50 consecutive eligible attendances over the last 3 months.
- Change: Stock an emergency department nosebleed trolley (headlight, nasal speculum, co-phenylcaine, silver nitrate sticks, packs) with a laminated step-by-step pathway attached, checked daily.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-360.csv`
- Pitfalls: No headlight or silver nitrate available → stock the trolley before cycle 1 and check it daily.; Doctors unsure how to cauterise → pair the trolley with a short hands-on session from ENT.; Posterior bleeds counted as failures → accept 'no bleeding point seen' as a pass when examination is documented.; ENT and emergency department disagree on who treats first → agree the pathway jointly before the change.
- Pearls: Recruit an ENT registrar and an emergency department nurse practitioner as joint leads.; Report packs and admissions avoided; managers care about bed days.; Keep the pathway on the trolley itself so it survives staff rotation.

**NNA-361. Of adults attending the emergency department with an eye problem, what proportion have visual acuity recorded for each eye?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Local standard: "Every adult attending the emergency department with an eye complaint or eye injury has visual acuity recorded for each eye (with glasses or a pinhole) at triage or at first clinician assessment, before any dilating or anaesthetic drops, unless a documented reason prevents it." 
- Pass: Visual acuity recorded for both eyes (Snellen or equivalent, with glasses or pinhole), or a documented reason why it could not be measured.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive eligible attendances over the last month.
- Change: Make right and left visual acuity required fields in the emergency department triage template whenever the presenting complaint is an eye problem, and mount a Snellen chart at a marked distance in triage.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-361.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper proformas are ignored → build the field into the electronic triage screen instead.; No chart or pinhole at triage → install both before cycle 1.; Near-vision cards used without recording → accept any recorded method but note which.; Busy periods lead to 'not done' → allow a documented reason, then track how often it is used.
- Pearls: Recruit the triage nurse lead and the IT template owner early.; Mark the floor at the correct distance for the chart; it removes an excuse.; Share one missed diagnosis case at the start; it convinces staff.

**NNA-362. What proportion of adults listed for cataract surgery have their chosen refractive outcome (for example distance, near or monovision) recorded before the day of surgery?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG77 rec 1.5.3 (2017): "At the preoperative assessment: discuss the refractive implications of different intraocular lenses with the person; base the choice of intraocular lens on the person's chosen refractive outcome; record the discussion and the person's choices in their medical notes." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng77/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes record the agreed refractive target for the eye being operated on (for example emmetropia for distance, a myopic target for near, or monovision), dated before the day of surgery.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive operations over the last month.
- Change: Add a required 'agreed refractive target' field to the cataract pre-assessment e-form that carries through to the lens selection screen and the WHO cataract checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-362.csv`
- Pitfalls: Target written only on the biometry printout → agree before cycle 1 which records count.; Surgeons assume 'distance' by default → the field should force an explicit choice.; Second-eye surgery copies the first-eye target without discussion → count each eye separately.; Independent-sector records not accessible → limit the sample to in-house lists.
- Pearls: Recruit the cataract lead nurse or optometrist who runs pre-assessment.; Link the field to the lens-ordering step so it cannot be skipped.; Audit the second eye separately if numbers allow; errors cluster there.

## Gastroenterology, hepatology, renal and diabetes

**NNA-363. What proportion of adults admitted with acute upper GI bleeding have a Glasgow-Blatchford score documented at their first assessment?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG141 rec 1.1.1 (2012, updated 2016): "Use the following formal risk assessment scores for all patients with acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding: the Blatchford score at first assessment, and the full Rockall score after endoscopy." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg141/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A numeric Glasgow-Blatchford score is written in the ED or admission clerking (or EPR score field) before the decision to admit, discharge or refer for endoscopy.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults with suspected upper GI bleeding over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory Glasgow-Blatchford calculator to the ED and acute take GI-bleed proforma (EPR form or printed sticker), so the score auto-calculates from the bloods and observations already entered.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-363.csv`
- Pitfalls: Score calculated but written only in the endoscopy request → search ED notes, clerking and the request, and count it only if it is timed before the disposition decision.; ED staff see the score as the medical team's job → agree before cycle 1 which team owns it (usually the first clinician) and put that on the proforma.; Missing urea or haemoglobin at first assessment → allow a score recorded once the first bloods return, and note the time.; Small numbers of score-0 patients → report score-0 discharges as context only, not as a pass criterion.; Gains fade when the proforma is bypassed → track use of the proforma alongside the pass rate.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED consultant and a gastroenterologist as joint sponsors before cycle 1.; Build the calculator so it fills from the observations and blood results already in the EPR; manual scoring is where it fails.; Ask the endoscopy booking team to return requests without a score: this keeps the change alive after the audit team rotates.; Feed back monthly run charts to the ED huddle for the first 3 months.

**NNA-364. What proportion of adults with suspected variceal bleeding receive terlipressin before endoscopy?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG141 rec 1.5.1 (2012, updated 2016): "Offer terlipressin to patients with suspected variceal bleeding at presentation. Stop treatment after definitive haemostasis has been achieved, or after 5 days, unless there is another indication for its use." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg141/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First dose of terlipressin given (administration time on the drug chart) before the start of endoscopy, in a patient in whom variceal bleeding was suspected at presentation.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive adults with suspected variceal bleeding over the last 6–12 months (variceal bleeds are less frequent).
- Change: Add terlipressin as a pre-selected item in a 'suspected variceal bleed' EPR order set (with IV antibiotic) and stock it in the ED emergency drug cupboard so the first dose is not delayed by pharmacy supply.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-364.csv`
- Pitfalls: Suspected variceal bleed is judged after the event → define 'suspected' before cycle 1 as known cirrhosis, portal hypertension or stigmata of chronic liver disease in the clerking.; Drug given in endoscopy is recorded late → use the endoscopy start time from the endoscopy system and the administration time on the drug chart, not the prescription time.; Small numbers → extend the look-back window rather than dropping below 30 cases, and keep the same window length in cycle 2.; Pharmacy objects to ED stock because of cost → show the cost of one extra ITU day; agree a small stock with expiry checks.
- Pearls: Recruit a hepatology consultant and the ED pharmacist before cycle 1.; Pair terlipressin with antibiotics in one order set so both happen together.; Put a laminated 'suspected variceal bleed' action card on the ED resus wall.; Ask endoscopy nurses to check terlipressin status at the pre-procedure safety pause.

**NNA-365. What proportion of adults taking low-dose aspirin for secondary prevention who are admitted with upper GI bleeding have aspirin continued once bleeding has stopped?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG141 rec 1.6.1 (2012, updated 2016): "Continue low-dose aspirin for secondary prevention of vascular events in patients with upper gastrointestinal bleeding in whom haemostasis has been achieved." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg141/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Aspirin is on the inpatient drug chart or discharge prescription once haemostasis is documented (endoscopic or clinical), or a documented reason not to restart is recorded by the responsible consultant.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive eligible adults over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add a 'secondary-prevention aspirin: continue once haemostasis achieved (NICE CG141 1.6.1)' tick box to the endoscopy report template and the GI-bleed post-endoscopy plan.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-365.csv`
- Pitfalls: Primary-prevention aspirin mixed in → record the indication from medicines reconciliation and exclude primary prevention.; Clinicians fear rebleeding → record rebleeding as a safety measure and share it with the endoscopists.; Haemostasis time unclear → agree before cycle 1 that the endoscopy report or first 'no further bleeding' note is the time point.; Aspirin restarted in hospital but dropped from the discharge letter → check both the inpatient chart and the discharge prescription.
- Pearls: Recruit the endoscopy lead and a ward pharmacist.; Put the prompt where the decision is made: the endoscopy report.; Share the rebleeding data; it usually reassures sceptical colleagues.; Ask pharmacists to flag stopped secondary-prevention aspirin at discharge screening.

**NNA-366. What proportion of adults with harmful or dependent drinking admitted with an acute illness receive parenteral thiamine within 24 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG100 rec 1.2.1.3 (2010, updated 2017): "Offer prophylactic parenteral thiamine followed by oral thiamine to harmful or dependent drinkers: if they are malnourished or at risk of malnourishment or if they have decompensated liver disease and in addition they attend an emergency department or are admitted to hospital with an acute illness or injury." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg100/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First dose of parenteral thiamine (e.g. Pabrinex) administered within 24 hours of admission, as shown by the administration record.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible adults over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Build an 'alcohol admission' EPR order set that pre-selects IV Pabrinex followed by oral thiamine whenever alcohol dependence is recorded in the clerking.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-366.csv`
- Pitfalls: Prescribed but not given → count administration time, not prescription time.; Eligibility unclear from notes → agree the malnourishment definition (MUST ≥1 or intake poor) before cycle 1 and keep it for cycle 2.; Pharmacy supply delays on wards → check ward stock of Pabrinex before cycle 1.; Order set used for everyone, including low-risk drinkers → report total use as a balancing measure.
- Pearls: Recruit the alcohol care team nurse as co-lead; they already track these patients.; Link the order set to the alcohol question in the clerking so it fires automatically.; Put Pabrinex in the acute medical unit and ED stock lists.; Re-brief each new FY1 intake at induction with a one-slide reminder.

**NNA-367. What proportion of adults treated in hospital for acute alcohol withdrawal are prescribed a symptom-triggered benzodiazepine regimen?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG100 rec 1.1.3.4 (2010, updated 2017): "Follow a symptom-triggered regimen for drug treatment for people in acute alcohol withdrawal who are: in hospital or in other settings where 24‑hour assessment and monitoring are available." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg100/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Benzodiazepine for withdrawal is prescribed as doses linked to a withdrawal score (e.g. CIWA-Ar or GMAWS), and at least one score is recorded before the first as-needed dose.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Replace the free-text withdrawal prescription with a symptom-triggered EPR order set linked to a CIWA-Ar or GMAWS observation chart that tells nurses the dose for each score.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-367.csv`
- Pitfalls: Nurses unfamiliar with scoring → train ward nursing leads before the change, not doctors alone.; Fixed regimens still appropriate for some (e.g. history of seizures) → allow 'fixed with documented reason' as a separate category and report it.; Scores recorded on paper and not in EPR → agree before cycle 1 where scores are found and search both.; Over-sedation fears → track total benzodiazepine dose and sedation events as balancing measures.
- Pearls: Make the alcohol care team and a ward matron co-owners.; Choose one tool (CIWA-Ar or GMAWS) Trust-wide and keep it.; Put the score-to-dose table on the chart itself.; Show the drop in total benzodiazepine dose; it wins over sceptical staff.

**NNA-368. What proportion of adults with cirrhosis and ascites admitted as an emergency have a diagnostic ascitic tap within 24 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: BSG/BASL guidelines on the management of ascites in cirrhosis, Gut 2021;70:9–29, recommendation 2.1: "Diagnostic paracentesis should be carried out without a delay to rule out spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) in all cirrhotic patients with ascites on hospital admission." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7788190/
- Pass: Ascitic fluid sample taken (neutrophil count sent) within 24 hours of the admission time. The 24-hour limit is the local measure of 'without a delay'.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Add a 'diagnostic ascitic tap within 24 hours' step with a pre-filled ascitic fluid order set to the decompensated cirrhosis admission bundle in the EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-368.csv`
- Pitfalls: Taps done in ED not linked to the admission → search laboratory records by patient number from the ED arrival time.; Ascites too small to tap → record ultrasound findings and exclude only when documented.; Clinicians worry about bleeding with a high INR → share the BSG advice that routine clotting checks and blood products are not needed before paracentesis.; Weekend admissions dominate failures → report weekday and weekend results separately.
- Pearls: Recruit a hepatology consultant and the acute medicine lead.; Keep ascitic tap packs and blood-culture bottles on the acute medical unit.; Offer a short supervised tap session for resident doctors in the first month.; Make the bundle the default admission document for anyone coded with decompensated cirrhosis.

**NNA-369. What proportion of adults with cirrhosis having a large-volume paracentesis of more than 5 litres receive 8 g of albumin per litre drained?** (new)
- Standard: BSG/BASL guidelines on the management of ascites in cirrhosis, Gut 2021;70:9–29, recommendation 6.1: "Albumin (as 20% or 25% solution) should be infused after paracentesis of >5 L is completed at a dose of 8 g albumin/L of ascites removed." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7788190/
- Pass: Total 20% or 25% albumin given for the procedure is within ±10% of 8 g per litre of the total volume drained (e.g. 7 L drained → 50–62 g).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive large-volume paracenteses over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a dose table (litres drained → grams and bottles of 20% albumin) to the paracentesis procedure record so the dose is read off, not calculated.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-369.csv`
- Pitfalls: Volume drained not recorded when the drain is removed → make total volume a required field in the procedure record.; Albumin given in 100 ml bottles makes exact dosing impossible → allow ±10% and round to whole bottles.; Albumin given for drains under 5 L (in ACLF or high renal risk) → record separately; BSG allows it in these cases.; Blood bank issues recorded but not given → use the administration record, not issue data.
- Pearls: Recruit the day unit sister; they own the procedure record.; Pre-print the dose table on the drain chart.; Involve the transfusion practitioner; albumin usage data are already collected.; Show the cost saved, not only compliance, at the transfusion committee.

**NNA-370. What proportion of adults with acute kidney injury and no identified cause have a urinary tract ultrasound within 24 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG148 rec 1.4.6 (2019, updated 2024): "When adults, children and young people have no identified cause of their acute kidney injury or are at risk of urinary tract obstruction, offer urgent ultrasound of the urinary tract (to be performed within 24 hours of assessment). [2013]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng148/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Ultrasound of the urinary tract performed (PACS time) within 24 hours of the first medical assessment of the AKI.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible AKI alerts over the last 4–8 weeks.
- Change: Create an 'AKI – no cause found / risk of obstruction' ultrasound request option in the RIS that radiology books into a protected next-day slot.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-370.csv`
- Pitfalls: 'No cause identified' is judged in hindsight → decide eligibility from notes written in the first 24 hours only.; Radiology pushes back on extra urgent scans → show the numbers (usually 2–5 a week) and agree the slot in advance.; Scans requested but not done at weekends → report weekday and weekend separately.; Over-requesting when the cause is clear (NICE 1.4.4) → track scans in patients with a clear cause as a balancing measure.
- Pearls: Recruit a nephrologist and an ultrasound lead radiographer before cycle 1.; Use the laboratory AKI alert list; it gives every case with a time stamp.; Put the NICE wording in the RIS request option so vetting radiologists accept it.; Include catheter status; a bladder scan may answer the question first.

**NNA-371. What proportion of adults discharged after stage 2 or 3 acute kidney injury have a discharge letter that states when their creatinine should be rechecked?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG148 rec 1.5.16 (2019, updated 2024): "Monitor serum creatinine after an episode of acute kidney injury. Base the frequency of monitoring on the stability and degree of renal function at the time of discharge. Consider referral to a nephrologist or paediatric nephrologist when eGFR is 30 ml/min/1.73 m2 or less in adults, children and young people who have recovered from an acute kidney injury. [2013]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng148/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The discharge letter names AKI (with stage) and gives a date or time frame for the next creatinine check and who will do it.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 4–8 weeks.
- Change: Make an 'AKI during admission' section with a required 'recheck creatinine by (date)' field in the electronic discharge letter, triggered automatically when an AKI stage 2–3 alert occurred during the stay.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-371.csv`
- Pitfalls: Letters say 'repeat bloods' without a date → count only a date or a time frame as a pass.; Stage 1 AKI would swamp the sample → restrict to stage 2–3 and keep the same rule in cycle 2.; The trigger fires for patients who died or were transferred → exclude them in both cycles.; GPs receive the date but no one books the test → add a line asking the patient to book it, and ask the GP forum for feedback.
- Pearls: Recruit the EPR team early; the trigger is the hard part.; Agree default time frames with the renal team (e.g. 1–2 weeks for stage 3).; Invite a GP to the presentation; they will champion the change.; Use the lab AKI alert list for cases: it is complete and time-stamped.

**NNA-372. What proportion of adult inpatients with a potassium of 6.0 mmol/L or above have a 12-lead ECG recorded within 1 hour of the result?** (new)
- Standard: UK Kidney Association Clinical Practice Guideline: Management of Hyperkalaemia in Adults (updated July 2026), guideline 14.1: "We recommend that all hospitalised patients with a serum K+ level ≥ 6.0 mmol/L have an urgent 12-lead ECG (electrocardiogram) performed and assessed for changes of hyperkalaemia. (1B)" https://ukkidney.org/sites/default/files/documents/FINAL%20VERSION%20-%20UKKA%20CLINICAL%20PRACTICE%20GUIDELINE%20-%20MANAGEMENT%20OF%20HYPERKALAEMIA%20IN%20ADULTS%20-%20UPDATED%20JULY%202026_0.pdf
- Pass: A 12-lead ECG is recorded (ECG machine or EPR time stamp) within 1 hour of the potassium result being available, and the notes state whether hyperkalaemic changes are present. The 1-hour limit is the local definition of 'urgent'.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add 'record a 12-lead ECG now' to the laboratory critical-result phone script and to the EPR hyperkalaemia alert, so the nurse who takes the call records the ECG.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-372.csv`
- Pitfalls: ECG done but not time-stamped in the notes → use the ECG machine or ECG system time.; Pseudo-hyperkalaemia from haemolysed samples → exclude only if the report or repeat confirms it.; Nurses unsure whether they may record an ECG without a doctor → agree before cycle 1 that the ECG is a nurse-initiated step.; Out-of-hours delays → report day and night separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the biochemistry duty lead; the critical-results log gives every case.; Agree a nurse-initiated ECG policy with nursing leadership.; Keep an ECG machine on each acute ward charged and stocked.; Use the same ECG for the calcium decision and write the finding in one line.

**NNA-373. What proportion of adults treated for a potassium of 6.0 mmol/L or above have their potassium rechecked within 2 hours of treatment?** (new)
- Standard: UK Kidney Association Clinical Practice Guideline: Management of Hyperkalaemia in Adults (updated July 2026), guideline 17.1.2: "We suggest that serum K+ is assessed at least 1, 2, 4, 6 and 24 hours after identification and treatment of moderate or severe hyperkalaemia. (2C)" https://ukkidney.org/sites/default/files/documents/FINAL%20VERSION%20-%20UKKA%20CLINICAL%20PRACTICE%20GUIDELINE%20-%20MANAGEMENT%20OF%20HYPERKALAEMIA%20IN%20ADULTS%20-%20UPDATED%20JULY%202026_0.pdf
- Pass: A potassium value (laboratory or blood gas) is available within 2 hours of the first potassium-lowering treatment (insulin-glucose or salbutamol).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive treated episodes over the last 1–2 months.
- Change: Add a pre-timed potassium recheck (blood gas at 1 and 2 hours) to the hyperkalaemia treatment order set, printed on the insulin-glucose prescription.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-373.csv`
- Pitfalls: Blood-gas potassium not in the EPR → agree before cycle 1 how to retrieve analyser results and search both.; Treatment time unclear → use the administration time on the drug chart, not the prescription time.; Patients moved from ED to a ward mid-treatment → follow the patient, not the location.; Staff treat the 2-hour check as the last one → also record the 4–6 hour check as a secondary measure.
- Pearls: Recruit the point-of-care testing lead to help pull blood-gas data.; Put the recheck times on the prescription so nurses can act without a doctor.; Combine the timed potassium and glucose checks on one hyperkalaemia chart.; Present rebound cases as short stories; they persuade better than percentages.

**NNA-374. What proportion of adults treated for hyperkalaemia in hospital leave with a community potassium check arranged?** (new)
- Standard: UK Kidney Association Clinical Practice Guideline: Management of Hyperkalaemia in Adults (updated July 2026), guideline 21.3: "We recommend that community blood monitoring is arranged on discharge for all patients who have required treatment for hyperkalaemia during hospital admission. (1B)" https://ukkidney.org/sites/default/files/documents/FINAL%20VERSION%20-%20UKKA%20CLINICAL%20PRACTICE%20GUIDELINE%20-%20MANAGEMENT%20OF%20HYPERKALAEMIA%20IN%20ADULTS%20-%20UPDATED%20JULY%202026_0.pdf
- Pass: The discharge letter states a date or time frame for a potassium check after discharge and who will do it (GP, hospital clinic or community team).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a required 'hyperkalaemia treated: potassium check by (date)' field to the electronic discharge letter, triggered when a hyperkalaemia treatment was given during the stay.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-374.csv`
- Pitfalls: Identifying treated patients is hard → use pharmacy issues of potassium binders and insulin-glucose order sets, not coding.; 'Bloods with GP' written with no date → count only a date or a time frame.; Hospital clinic follow-up without bloods → check the clinic letter or booking before counting as a pass.; Readmission numbers are small → report them as context, not as the pass measure.
- Pearls: Recruit the renal pharmacist; they already track binder use.; Link the trigger to the same order set used for treatment.; Suggest default time frames (e.g. within 1 week if a RAAS drug is restarted).; Tell the GP which drugs were stopped or changed in the same section.

**NNA-375. What proportion of adults starting planned haemodialysis start with an arteriovenous fistula or graft rather than a catheter?** (under-audited)
- Standard: UK Renal Association Clinical Practice Guideline: Vascular Access for Haemodialysis, 6th edition (2015), guideline 1.1: "We recommend that all patients with end stage kidney disease who commence haemodialysis or are on long-term haemodialysis should dialyse with an arteriovenous fistula as first choice, an arteriovenous graft as second choice, a tunnelled venous catheter as third choice and a non-tunnelled temporary catheter as an option of necessity (1A)." https://ukkidney.org/sites/default/files/vascular-access.pdf
- Pass: The first haemodialysis session uses a working arteriovenous fistula or graft.. Target: ≥65% of planned starters (UK Renal Registry benchmark). Sample: All planned haemodialysis starters in the last 12 months (typically 30–60 in a renal unit).
- Change: Add an automatic prompt in the renal database that lists every patient whose eGFR falls below 15 ml/min/1.73 m2 (or who has chosen haemodialysis) without a vascular access referral, reviewed at the weekly low-clearance MDT.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-375.csv`
- Pitfalls: Late referrals from primary care inflate catheter starts → the 90-day rule excludes true late presenters; keep it for both cycles.; Fistula created but not mature → record the reason; it points to surgical waiting times, not referral.; Small numbers per quarter → use 12 months for cycle 1 and 6 months for cycle 2, and report counts with percentages.; Surgeons see the audit as blame → co-present with them and focus on the referral step.
- Pearls: Recruit the vascular access nurse; they hold most of the dates.; Agree the eGFR trigger with the low-clearance clinic lead before cycle 1.; Track time from referral to surgery as a secondary measure.; Share each catheter start at MDT as a short case review.

**NNA-376. What proportion of adults treated for diabetic ketoacidosis have 10% glucose started within 1 hour of their blood glucose falling below 14 mmol/L?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS 02: The Management of Diabetic Ketoacidosis in Adults (revised March 2023): "If the glucose falls below 14.0 mmol/L, commence 10% glucose given at 125 ml/hour alongside the 0.9% sodium chloride solution. In addition consider reducing the rate of intravenous insulin infusion to 0.05 units/kg/hr." https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_02_DKA_Guideline_with_QR_code_March_2023.pdf
- Pass: 10% glucose infusion running (fluid chart start time) within 1 hour of the first capillary or laboratory glucose below 14.0 mmol/L while on the fixed-rate insulin infusion.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive DKA episodes over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Pre-print (or pre-build in the EPR) a 10% glucose 125 ml/hour prescription on the DKA pathway, marked 'start when glucose <14 mmol/L', so nurses can start it without waiting for a doctor.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-376.csv`
- Pitfalls: Glucose readings recorded on paper and in the meter but not the EPR → agree before cycle 1 which source is used and keep it.; 10% glucose prescribed but not hung → use the fluid chart start time, not the prescription.; Small numbers of DKA → extend the look-back to 12 months and keep the window the same in cycle 2.; Nurses unsure they can start a pre-prescribed fluid → get nursing policy sign-off before the change.
