- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes inpatient specialist nurse as co-lead.; Put the 14 mmol/L trigger in bold on the hourly monitoring chart.; Count hypoglycaemia during treatment; it shows why the step matters.; Brief ED and AMU nurse leads, as most delays happen at handover.

**NNA-377. What proportion of adults admitted with diabetic ketoacidosis are seen by the diabetes specialist team within 1 working day of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: JBDS 02: The Management of Diabetic Ketoacidosis in Adults (revised March 2023), Best Practice Tariff standards table: "People admitted to hospital with diabetic ketoacidosis should be seen by member of the diabetes specialist team within 1 working day of admission" https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_02_DKA_Guideline_with_QR_code_March_2023.pdf
- Pass: A documented review by a diabetes consultant, specialist registrar or diabetes inpatient specialist nurse by the end of the first working day after admission.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive DKA admissions over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Set up an automatic referral to the diabetes inpatient team when a DKA order set or fixed-rate insulin infusion is started in the EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-377.csv`
- Pitfalls: Friday and weekend admissions fail by design → report weekday and weekend separately, and agree how 'working day' is counted before cycle 1.; Reviews not written in the main notes → search the diabetes team's own system too.; Referral happens but review is by phone → count only a face-to-face or documented virtual review.; Short stays discharged before review → record them and give them a follow-up call as part of the fix.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes inpatient lead nurse; they can report their referral log.; Build the referral into the fixed-rate insulin order so it cannot be missed.; Show readmission data to support a business case for weekend cover.; Keep the same working-day rule in both cycles.

**NNA-378. What proportion of adults with hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state and blood ketones of 1.0 mmol/L or less have intravenous insulin held until glucose stops falling on fluids alone?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS 06: The Management of the Hyperosmolar Hyperglycaemic State (HHS) in Adults (February 2022), Insulin Dose and Timing: "if significant ketonaemia is not present do NOT start an intravenous insulin infusion" https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_06_The_Management_of_Hyperosmolar_Hyperglycaemic_State_HHS_%20in_Adults_FINAL_0.pdf
- Pass: No intravenous insulin started until the glucose has stopped falling on intravenous fluids alone, as shown on the hourly glucose chart (or a documented senior reason to start earlier).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible HHS episodes over the last 12–24 months (HHS is less common; aim for 20–30).
- Change: Create a separate HHS pathway document (paper or EPR) that opens with 'Ketones ≤1.0: do NOT start insulin; fluids first' and replaces use of the DKA pathway for HHS.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-378.csv`
- Pitfalls: HHS miscoded as DKA or hyperglycaemia → search both codes and the diabetes team log.; Small numbers → use 12–24 months of cases and report counts, not only percentages.; Senior clinicians start insulin for good reasons (e.g. ketones rising during fluid treatment) → accept a documented senior reason as a pass and report it.; Calculated osmolality not recorded → calculate it from the results as (2×Na) + glucose + urea.
- Pearls: Recruit a diabetes consultant and the ED lead nurse.; Put the HHS pathway next to the DKA pathway in ED so the right one is picked.; Include an osmolality calculator on the chart.; Review every HHS case at the diabetes team meeting for the first 6 months.

**NNA-379. What proportion of adults treated for hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state receive prophylactic low molecular weight heparin within 24 hours of admission?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS 06: The Management of the Hyperosmolar Hyperglycaemic State (HHS) in Adults (February 2022): "Everyone with HHS should receive prophylactic low molecular weight heparin (LMWH) for the full duration of admission unless contraindicated." https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_06_The_Management_of_Hyperosmolar_Hyperglycaemic_State_HHS_%20in_Adults_FINAL_0.pdf
- Pass: First prophylactic LMWH dose given within 24 hours of admission, or a documented contraindication.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All eligible HHS episodes over the last 12–24 months (aim for 20–30).
- Change: Add prophylactic LMWH (weight- and renal-adjusted) as a pre-selected item in the HHS order set, with a required reason field if it is deselected.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-379.csv`
- Pitfalls: LMWH withheld because of AKI → use renal-adjusted dosing, not omission; record the eGFR.; Given the next evening after a late admission → measure from admission time, not the date.; Small numbers → report counts with percentages and extend the look-back if needed.; Generic VTE assessment scores HHS as low risk → the order set should override the generic tool.
- Pearls: Recruit the anticoagulation pharmacist.; Pair this with the HHS insulin-timing audit and one HHS pathway.; Add 'daily foot check' to the same order set; HHS patients are also at high risk of heel ulcers.; Share any VTE events as case stories.

**NNA-380. What proportion of adult inpatients on insulin or a sulfonylurea have an as-needed hypoglycaemia rescue treatment prescribed?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS 01: The Hospital Management of Hypoglycaemia in Adults with Diabetes Mellitus (revised January 2023): "Promote the prescription of hypoglycaemia “rescue” treatment for all people with diabetes in hospital on insulin or sulfonylurea therapy. This could involve a “PRN (pro re nata)” or “as needed” prescription of intravenous (IV) glucose and/or intramuscular (IM) glucagon." https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_01_Hypo_Guideline_with_QR_code_January_2023.pdf
- Pass: An as-needed prescription for IV glucose (10% or 20%) and/or IM glucagon is active on the drug chart on the audit day, or the Trust hypoglycaemia protocol allows nurses to give it without prescription (record which).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 50 consecutive eligible inpatients identified by a point-prevalence sweep across 4–6 wards on one day.
- Change: Add IV glucose and IM glucagon as-needed items that are pre-selected whenever insulin or a sulfonylurea is prescribed in the EPR (a linked 'diabetes order set').
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-380.csv`
- Pitfalls: Some Trusts allow nurses to give glucose under a patient group direction → record this and agree before cycle 1 whether it counts as a pass.; Order sets create alert fatigue → keep the rescue items quiet (pre-selected, no pop-up).; Point-prevalence results vary by ward → sweep the same wards in both cycles.; Glucagon stocks short on some wards → check ward stock alongside prescribing.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes inpatient specialist nurse and the medicines safety pharmacist.; Link the rescue items to the insulin prescription itself.; Check that each ward has an in-date hypo box on the same day.; Report results by ward on one slide; wards compete to improve.

**NNA-381. What proportion of adult inpatients who have a severe hypoglycaemic episode have their glucose-lowering treatment reviewed by a doctor or diabetes nurse within 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS 01: The Hospital Management of Hypoglycaemia in Adults with Diabetes Mellitus (revised January 2023), 'When hypoglycaemia has been successfully treated': "Unless the cause is easily identifiable and both the nursing staff and the person with diabetes is confident steps can be taken to avoid future events, a medical or Diabetes Inpatient Specialist Nurse (DISN) review should be considered. If the hypoglycaemia event was severe or recurrent, or if the person voices concerns then a review is indicated" https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_01_Hypo_Guideline_with_QR_code_January_2023.pdf
- Pass: A documented medical or DISN review within 24 hours of the episode that states the likely cause and a decision on the glucose-lowering dose (changed, or kept with a reason).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive severe episodes over the last 1–2 months.
- Change: Set the point-of-care glucose system to send an automatic electronic referral to the diabetes inpatient team for every reading below 3.0 mmol/L.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-381.csv`
- Pitfalls: Readings below 3.0 from poor technique → exclude only if a repeat within 5 minutes was normal and documented.; Reviews that say 'noted' without a dose decision → count only reviews that state cause and decision.; DISN notes held in a separate system → search it and agree access before cycle 1.; Workload fears from the diabetes team → estimate weekly numbers from cycle 1 before switching on the referral.
- Pearls: Recruit the point-of-care testing manager; the glucose system already holds every reading.; Ask the DISN team to co-own the referral rule.; Track repeat hypos within 48 hours; it shows the benefit quickly.; Add the review to the ward's morning huddle list.

**NNA-382. What proportion of adults at high risk of refeeding syndrome receive thiamine before tube feeding or parenteral nutrition starts?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG32 rec 1.4.8 (2006, updated 2017): "The prescription for people at high risk of developing refeeding problems should consider: … providing immediately before and during the first 10 days of feeding: oral thiamin 200 to 300 mg daily, vitamin B co strong 1 or 2 tablets, 3 times a day (or full dose daily intravenous vitamin B preparation, if necessary) and a balanced multivitamin or trace element supplement once daily" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg32/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First dose of thiamine (oral, enteral or IV vitamin B preparation) given on or before the day enteral tube feeding or parenteral nutrition starts, as shown by the administration record.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive high-risk patients starting feeding over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a refeeding-risk box to the enteral feeding and PN prescription forms that pre-selects thiamine and vitamin B compound strong for 10 days when any high-risk criterion is ticked.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-382.csv`
- Pitfalls: Risk criteria assessed after feeding starts → use the dietetic assessment and admission data to judge risk as it was on the start day.; Thiamine prescribed but not given because the patient is nil by mouth → allow IV vitamin B preparation and count administration, not prescription.; Feed started out of hours before dietetic review → report out-of-hours starts separately.; Doctors assume the dietitian prescribes → agree before cycle 1 who prescribes (usually the medical team on dietetic advice).
- Pearls: Recruit a nutrition support dietitian and the nutrition nurse as co-leads.; Put the risk criteria as tick boxes on the feed prescription, not in a separate document.; Add the same prompt to the out-of-hours NG feeding protocol.; Report phosphate falls; they make the risk real to ward teams.

## Cardiology and respiratory medicine

**NNA-383. What proportion of adults diagnosed with NSTEMI or unstable angina have a formal 6-month mortality risk score (such as GRACE) recorded in their notes within 24 hours of diagnosis?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG185 recs 1.2.7 and 1.2.9 (2020): "1.2.7 As soon as the diagnosis of unstable angina or NSTEMI is made, and aspirin and antithrombin therapy have been offered, formally assess individual risk of future adverse cardiovascular events using an established risk scoring system that predicts 6‑month mortality (for example, Global Registry of Acute Cardiac Events [GRACE]). [2010] 1.2.9 Record the results of the risk assessment in the person's care record. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng185/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A named 6-month mortality score (GRACE or equivalent) with its value or risk band written in the notes or EPR within 24 hours of the first documented NSTEMI or unstable angina diagnosis.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory GRACE field, with a built-in calculator link, to the ACS clerking proforma or EPR chest pain pathway.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-383.csv`
- Pitfalls: Cardiologists may work out the score but not write it down → count only a recorded score, and agree this rule before cycle 1 so the baseline is not disputed.; The diagnosis time is unclear when troponin rises late → use the first note that states NSTEMI or unstable angina, and keep the same rule in both cycles.; Type 2 myocardial infarction coded as NSTEMI inflates the denominator → exclude cases relabelled by cardiology in the discharge diagnosis.; Staff see the score as paperwork → show the link between score, 72-hour angiography decision and length of stay when presenting.; The field is skipped at night → check completion on the next morning's board round for the first 4 weeks.
- Pearls: Recruit the MINAP data clerk; the extract gives the case list in minutes.; Agree with cardiology which scores count before cycle 1 (GRACE 6-month mortality is the default).; Put the calculator link in the proforma so the score takes under a minute.; Ask the chest pain or cardiology nurse to own the field so it survives trainee rotation.

**NNA-384. What proportion of adults with NSTEMI or unstable angina and a predicted 6-month mortality above 3% have coronary angiography within 72 hours of first admission?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG185 rec 1.2.13 (2020): "Consider coronary angiography (with follow‑on PCI if indicated) within 72 hours of first admission for people with unstable angina or NSTEMI who have an intermediate or higher risk of adverse cardiovascular events (predicted 6‑month mortality above 3.0%) and no contraindications to angiography (such as active bleeding or comorbidity)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng185/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Coronary angiography started within 72 hours of arrival at the first hospital. Patients with a documented contraindication or who declined angiography are excluded from the denominator and counted separately.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Send the referral to the PCI centre electronically on the day the diagnosis is made, triggered from the ACS proforma, with a daily 'awaiting angiography' list reviewed at the cardiology board round.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-384.csv`
- Pitfalls: Transferred patients are hard to trace → use the MINAP record, which follows the patient, or the tertiary centre's discharge letter.; Clock start differs between hospitals → use first admission time, as NICE does, in both cycles.; Frail patients managed medically lower the rate → exclude those with a documented contraindication or informed refusal, and report them separately.; Delays sit in the PCI centre's bed capacity → record the reason for each delay so the change targets the right step, and invite the PCI centre lead from the start.
- Pearls: Recruit the MINAP lead and the cardiac network manager before cycle 1.; Split results by weekday and weekend admission; weekend referrals often explain most breaches.; Agree the referral route with the PCI centre before changing it.; Keep the 'awaiting angiography' list on the board-round agenda so it outlives the audit team.

**NNA-385. What proportion of adults admitted with an acute coronary syndrome and a blood glucose above 11.0 mmol/L, without known diabetes, have HbA1c measured before discharge?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG185 rec 1.3.3 (2020): "Offer all people with hyperglycaemia after acute coronary syndrome and without known diabetes tests for: HbA1c levels before discharge; fasting blood glucose levels no earlier than 4 days after the onset of acute coronary syndrome. These tests should not delay discharge. [2011]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng185/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: An HbA1c result dated between admission and discharge.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 3–6 months.
- Change: Add HbA1c to the standard ACS admission blood order set so it is requested with the first troponin.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-385.csv`
- Pitfalls: The laboratory may reject HbA1c added to every ACS panel → agree the order set with the biochemistry lead before cycle 1.; Point-of-care glucose values are missed → search both laboratory and point-of-care results for the glucose threshold.; Known diabetes is not recorded in hospital notes → check the GP summary care record before excluding.; An HbA1c is done but nobody acts on a high result → record the action field so the next step can be fixed if needed.
- Pearls: Recruit a biochemist and the cardiology pharmacist early.; Fix the glucose threshold (above 11.0 mmol/L) before cycle 1 and keep it.; An order-set change is cheap and lasts after trainees rotate.; Share the number of new diabetes diagnoses found; it keeps the change supported.

**NNA-386. What proportion of adults admitted with acute heart failure are reviewed by the specialist heart failure team before discharge?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG187 rec 1.1.2 (2014, updated 2021): "Ensure that all people being admitted to hospital with suspected acute heart failure have early and continuing input from a dedicated specialist heart failure team." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg187/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A documented review (in person or a recorded advice note) by a heart failure specialist nurse, heart failure consultant or cardiologist during the admission, before discharge or death.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Set up an automatic electronic alert to the heart failure team for every inpatient with a raised NT-proBNP (local threshold agreed with the team).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-386.csv`
- Pitfalls: Advice given by phone is not written down → count a dated advice note in the EPR, and agree this before cycle 1.; The heart failure team may fear more referrals than they can see → agree a threshold and triage rule with them before switching on the alert.; Short stays are discharged before review → report stays under 48 hours separately.; Coded diagnoses miss patients admitted with 'breathlessness' → cross-check the NICOR list against the NT-proBNP list.
- Pearls: Recruit the heart failure specialist nurse lead as co-author from the start.; The laboratory can usually build the NT-proBNP alert in a few weeks; ask early.; Report results by admitting ward so wards can see their own gap.; Hand the monthly figure to the heart failure team so it outlasts the audit team.

**NNA-387. What proportion of adults admitted with new suspected heart failure and a raised natriuretic peptide have a transthoracic echocardiogram within 48 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG187 rec 1.2.4 (2014, updated 2021): "In people presenting with new suspected acute heart failure, consider performing transthoracic Doppler 2D echocardiography within 48 hours of admission to guide early specialist management." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg187/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Transthoracic echocardiogram performed (report time) within 48 hours of arrival.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions over the last 3 months.
- Change: Ring-fence daily inpatient echo slots for new suspected heart failure, booked directly from the heart failure referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-387.csv`
- Pitfalls: Echo capacity is the real limit → involve the echo lead before cycle 1 and measure request-to-report time as well.; NICE says 'consider' → present this as a local standard supported by NICE, not a mandate.; Patients with an old echo are counted wrongly → check the echo system for any study in the last 6 months.; Weekend admissions breach most → report weekday and weekend results separately.
- Pearls: Recruit the chief cardiac physiologist; they know where the slots can come from.; Use the laboratory natriuretic peptide list to find cases quickly.; Agree the peptide thresholds before cycle 1.; Link the slot to the heart failure team review so one referral does both.

**NNA-388. What proportion of adults receiving intravenous diuretics for acute heart failure have their weight recorded every day while on intravenous treatment?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG187 rec 1.3.5 (2014, updated 2021): "Closely monitor the person's renal function, weight and urine output during diuretic therapy." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg187/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A weight recorded on every calendar day from the first to the last day of intravenous diuretic (local standard for 'closely monitor weight').. Target: ≥90% of patients. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add an automatic daily weight task to the EPR observation schedule that switches on when intravenous loop diuretic is prescribed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-388.csv`
- Pitfalls: Scales are missing or broken → check scale availability on each ward before cycle 1.; Weights are written on paper and not in the EPR → agree one place to look and keep it for both cycles.; Bed-bound patients cannot be weighed → count a documented reason as an exclusion, not a fail.; Nurses feel blamed → present the results by ward system, not by individual.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward sister from each main medical ward.; Report day-level and patient-level results; day-level shows progress sooner.; Ask the EPR team for the diuretic-linked task early; it is a small build.; Add a weigh-in time to the morning routine so it becomes habit.

**NNA-389. What proportion of adults seen in hospital after transient loss of consciousness with a red flag feature have a specialist cardiovascular assessment referral reviewed within 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG109 rec 1.1.4.2 (2010, updated 2023): "Refer urgently for cardiovascular assessment, with the referral reviewed and prioritised by an appropriate specialist within 24 hours, anyone with TLoC who also has any of the following: an ECG abnormality (see recommendations 1.1.2.2 and 1.1.2.3); heart failure (history or physical signs); TLoC during exertion; family history of sudden cardiac death in people aged younger than 40 years and/or an inherited cardiac condition; new or unexplained breathlessness; a heart murmur." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg109/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Referral to cardiology or a syncope service made and reviewed by a specialist (documented) within 24 hours of first hospital assessment; admission under cardiology within 24 hours also passes.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible attendances over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add the six NICE red flags as tick boxes to the ED TLoC proforma; any ticked box opens an electronic cardiology referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-389.csv`
- Pitfalls: Red flags are not recorded, so eligible patients cannot be found → also count patients with no red flag assessment and report them.; Discharged patients' referrals are reviewed days later → record the review time from the referral system, not the referral time.; Cardiology may worry about extra referrals → share the red flag list and expected numbers before switching on the referral.; Coding misses 'fall' presentations that were blackouts → search free text for 'collapse' and 'blackout' too.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED consultant and a cardiology registrar as joint leads.; Agree what 'reviewed' means (triage decision recorded) before cycle 1.; Report older adults without prodrome separately; NICE says consider referral for them too.; Keep the proforma in the ED EPR so it survives rotation.

**NNA-390. What proportion of adults with acute hypercapnic respiratory failure who need NIV start it within 60 minutes of the blood gas that led to the decision?** (under-audited)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standards for acute NIV in adults, Quality statement 4 (2018): "Patients who meet evidence-based criteria for acute NIV should start NIV within 60 min of the blood gas result associated with the clinical decision to provide NIV and within 120 min of hospital arrival for patients who present acutely." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5890056/
- Pass: NIV start time (from the NIV chart) 60 minutes or less after the time of the qualifying blood gas.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive acute NIV episodes over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Introduce a single NIV activation call (one bleep or phone number) that brings a ventilator and an NIV-trained nurse to the patient once the decision is made.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-390.csv`
- Pitfalls: Blood gas times on paper differ from the analyser → use the analyser time for both cycles.; The qualifying gas is unclear when several are taken → take the gas recorded as prompting the decision; if none, the first acidotic gas after medical treatment.; Teaching alone made delays worse in one hospital → pick a system change, not another e-learning module.; Small monthly numbers swing the rate → use at least 40 episodes and report the median minutes as well.
- Pearls: Recruit the NIV lead nurse and an ED consultant from the start.; Map the steps from gas to mask on one real case before cycle 1; it shows where time goes.; Record the delay reason every time; it tells you what to fix.; Report door-to-mask time to the NIV group monthly so it lasts after the audit.

**NNA-391. What proportion of adults started on acute NIV have an escalation plan documented before NIV begins?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standards for acute NIV in adults, Quality statement 5 (2018): "All patients should have a documented escalation plan before starting treatment with acute NIV." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5890056/
- Pass: A written plan stating whether the patient is for intubation and intensive care or NIV as the ceiling, timed before or at the NIV start time.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive acute NIV episodes over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Put a mandatory escalation plan box on the front of the NIV prescription chart, so NIV cannot be prescribed until it is completed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-391.csv`
- Pitfalls: Plans are written but not timed → accept the note time in the EPR, and agree this before cycle 1.; Juniors feel unable to set a ceiling at night → allow a registrar plan 'for review by consultant' to pass, and record the grade.; A general TEP form exists but is not NIV-specific → count it if it states the ventilation ceiling clearly.; Staff may object that plans delay NIV → make the box a single line so it takes under a minute.
- Pearls: Recruit the NIV lead consultant and nurse; they own the chart.; Link the box to the existing Trust TEP form rather than creating a new one.; Audit the same episodes for time to NIV; it shows the plan does not slow treatment.; Keep plan completion as a standing item at NIV morbidity and mortality meetings.

**NNA-392. What proportion of adults started on acute NIV have a blood gas taken within 2 hours of starting?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standards for acute NIV in adults, Quality statement 6 (2018): "All patients treated with acute NIV should have blood gas analysis performed within 2 hours of starting acute NIV. Failure of these blood gas measurements to improve should trigger specialist healthcare professional review within 30 min." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5890056/
- Pass: An arterial or arterialised capillary blood gas analysed 2 hours or less after the NIV start time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive acute NIV episodes over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Print the 'repeat blood gas due' time on the NIV prescription chart and set an automatic 2-hour task in the EPR when NIV starts.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-392.csv`
- Pitfalls: Gases taken during a move between wards are missed → search the analyser for all gases on that patient, not the notes.; The NIV start time is not recorded → use the first recorded ventilator settings time and keep the rule for both cycles.; Patients moved to ICU early are counted twice → include only the ward NIV episode.; Nurses cannot take arterial samples → agree who takes the 2-hour gas out of hours before switching on the task.
- Pearls: Recruit the blood gas analyser point-of-care lead; the analyser export gives exact times.; Report the secondary 30-minute review to show why the gas matters.; Add the 2-hour gas to the NIV handover sheet.; Re-audit after the next junior doctor rotation to check it holds.

**NNA-393. What proportion of adults started on acute NIV are reviewed by a consultant trained in NIV within 14 hours of starting?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standards for acute NIV in adults, Quality statement 5 (2018): "Clinical progress should be reviewed by a healthcare professional with appropriate training and competence within 4 hours of starting NIV and by a consultant with training and competence in acute NIV within 14 hours of starting acute NIV." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5890056/
- Pass: A documented review by a consultant with NIV competence (respiratory, intensive care or named acute physician on the NIV rota) 14 hours or less after NIV start.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive acute NIV episodes over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Add every new NIV patient automatically to the on-call respiratory consultant's review list (EPR list or ward NIV board) at the time NIV starts.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-393.csv`
- Pitfalls: Weekend rota has no NIV-trained consultant → agree which consultants count before cycle 1 and report weekends separately.; Reviews by phone are not documented → count only a written review, and say so early.; Consultants feel judged on individual performance → present results by day and time, not by name.; Episodes starting in the evening skew the result → report median hours as well as the rate.
- Pearls: Recruit the respiratory clinical director; the fix is a rota issue.; Start with the list the NIV nurses already keep.; Show that most breaches cluster at weekends; it makes the case for a named weekend reviewer.; Keep the 14-hour figure on the NIV dashboard after the audit ends.

**NNA-394. What proportion of adults admitted with a COPD exacerbation who have no spirometry on record have spirometry requested before discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG115 rec 1.1.5 (2018, updated 2019): "Measure post-bronchodilator spirometry to confirm the diagnosis of COPD. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng115/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Outpatient or community spirometry requested (lung function referral, or an explicit request to the GP in the discharge summary) before discharge, in a patient with no post-bronchodilator spirometry found on record.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: All COPD exacerbation admissions until 40 without spirometry on record are found (usually 2–3 months).
- Change: Add a 'spirometry on record? If not, request now' step with a one-click lung function referral to the COPD discharge bundle.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-394.csv`
- Pitfalls: Old spirometry sits in GP records you cannot see → check the summary care record and ask one local practice to confirm a sample.; Patients are too unwell for spirometry soon after discharge → accept a request timed for 6–8 weeks after recovery.; The lung function lab worries about capacity → agree numbers with them before the change.; Requests are made but never done → record completion at 3 months as a secondary check.
- Pearls: Recruit the respiratory physiologist lead; they can tell you who has had spirometry in seconds.; Use the NACAP case list if your hospital submits; it saves coding searches.; Include a line in the discharge summary so the GP knows why spirometry matters.; Hand the bundle step to the COPD specialist nurses so it outlasts the audit team.

**NNA-395. What proportion of adults discharged after a COPD exacerbation are referred to pulmonary rehabilitation?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG115 rec 1.2.82 (2018, updated 2019): "Make pulmonary rehabilitation available to all appropriate people with COPD (see the recommendation on offering pulmonary rehabilitation), including people who have had a recent hospitalisation for an acute exacerbation. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng115/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A pulmonary rehabilitation referral sent before discharge, or a documented offer that the patient declined.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 2 months.
- Change: Make pulmonary rehabilitation referral a mandatory item on the COPD discharge bundle, with the referral form embedded in the EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-395.csv`
- Pitfalls: Referrals are made by the pulmonary rehabilitation team itself after discharge → check their log, not only the notes.; Patients declining are counted as fails → record documented declines as a pass, as agreed before cycle 1.; The rehabilitation service cannot enrol more people quickly → warn them and measure 30-day enrolment separately.; Non-respiratory wards miss the bundle → report results by discharging team.
- Pearls: Recruit the pulmonary rehabilitation coordinator; they know who was referred.; Ask patients' preference for venue at referral; it lifts uptake.; Use the NACAP data if your hospital submits.; Give the COPD nurses ownership of the bundle item after the audit.

**NNA-396. What proportion of adults discharged after an asthma attack have follow-up with their GP or practice asthma nurse arranged within two working days?** (under-audited)
- Standard: BTS/SIGN British guideline on the management of asthma (SIGN 158), section 9.6.3 (revised 2019, revised edition November 2024, corrections May 2025): "Prior to discharge, follow up should be arranged with the patient’s general practitioner or asthma nurse within two working days and with a hospital specialist asthma nurse or respiratory physician at about one month after admission." https://www.sign.ac.uk/media/2342/sign-158-2024-british-guideline-on-the-management-of-asthma-may-2025.pdf
- Pass: Discharge summary or notes record a GP or practice asthma nurse review booked or requested within two working days of discharge.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'GP/asthma nurse review within 2 working days: requested' field to the asthma discharge template, which sends an electronic task to the practice.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-396.csv`
- Pitfalls: ED discharges have no discharge summary → check the ED letter to the GP and count it only if it asks for review.; 'Two working days' is miscounted over weekends → define working days before cycle 1 and keep the rule.; Practices may not act on an electronic task → agree the route with two local practices first.; Patients told to book themselves may not → record how it was arranged so the change can target it.
- Pearls: Recruit a respiratory nurse and an ED consultant; most asthma attacks leave from ED.; Pair the field with the written asthma action plan to keep discharge simple.; Ask a local GP to confirm a sample of appointments actually happened.; Put the field in the ED template as well as the ward template.

**NNA-397. What proportion of adults with a likely PE Wells score whose CTPA cannot be done immediately receive interim therapeutic anticoagulation before the scan?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 rec 1.1.18 (2020, updated 2023): "For people with a likely PE Wells score (more than 4 points): offer a computed tomography pulmonary angiogram (CTPA) immediately if possible or for people with an allergy to contrast media, severe renal impairment (estimated creatinine clearance less than 30 ml/min) or a high risk from irradiation, assess the suitability of a ventilation/perfusion single photon emission computed tomography (V/Q SPECT) scan or, if a V/Q SPECT scan is not available, a V/Q planar scan, as an alternative to CTPA. If a CTPA, V/Q SPECT or V/Q planar scan cannot be done immediately, offer interim therapeutic anticoagulation (see the section on interim therapeutic anticoagulation for suspected DVT or PE). [2012, amended 2020]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A therapeutic dose of anticoagulant given (drug chart administration time) before the scan, when the scan was more than 1 hour after the Wells score was recorded (local definition of 'not immediately'); a documented contraindication also passes.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a required question to the electronic CTPA and V/Q request: 'Wells above 4 and scan not within 1 hour: interim anticoagulant prescribed? (yes / contraindicated)'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-397.csv`
- Pitfalls: Wells score time is missing → use the CTPA request time as the start and keep it for both cycles.; Radiologists may resist an extra question on the request → keep it to one tick box and agree it with the radiology lead first.; Bleeding-risk patients are counted as fails → count a documented contraindication as a pass.; Scans done within the hour inflate the denominator → exclude them as the standard only applies when imaging is delayed.
- Pearls: Recruit a radiology registrar; RIS gives request and scan times in one export.; Agree the 1-hour definition of 'immediately' with ED and radiology before cycle 1.; Record whether PE was confirmed; untreated confirmed PE makes a strong case.; Keep the request question after the audit; it costs nothing.

**NNA-398. What proportion of pleural aspirations and chest drains for pleural fluid are done with thoracic ultrasound at the time of the procedure?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standard for Pleural Disease, Quality statement 3 (2026): "Peri-procedural thoracic ultrasound must be used for all pleural fluid procedures unless real-time CT imaging is more appropriate." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13202182/
- Pass: Procedure note records thoracic ultrasound performed by the operator at the bedside immediately before or during the procedure (site marking done earlier in radiology does not count).. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive procedures over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Route all non-emergency pleural fluid procedures through a daily pleural list run by ultrasound-trained operators, booked by electronic referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-398.csv`
- Pitfalls: Aspirations done on the ward leave no procedure note → find them from the laboratory pleural fluid list.; Radiology site-marking is counted as ultrasound use → agree before cycle 1 that only bedside ultrasound counts.; Out-of-hours procedures have no trained operator → report in-hours and out-of-hours separately.; Operators feel policed → present by location and time, not by name.
- Pearls: Recruit the pleural lead and the chest drain safety lead.; Check where the ultrasound machines are and who can use them before cycle 1.; Record complications; they make the case for the pleural list.; Keep a simple procedure log in the pleural room so the next audit takes minutes.

**NNA-399. What proportion of pleural procedures have a pleural safety checklist (LocSSIP) completed both before and after the procedure?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standard for Pleural Disease, Quality statement 3 (2026): "A pleural procedures specific safety LocSSIP (Local Safety Standard for Invasive Procedures) should be completed for each patient before and after all pleural procedures." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13202182/
- Pass: Pleural LocSSIP present in the notes or EPR with both the pre-procedure and post-procedure sections completed.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive procedures over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Build the pleural LocSSIP into the EPR pleural procedure note so the note cannot be saved until both sections are complete.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-399.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper checklists are lost after scanning → check scanned notes and the procedure log.; The post-procedure section is skipped when the operator leaves → make the post-check part of the nursing handover.; Aspirations on the ward are missed → use the laboratory pleural fluid list to find them.; Staff see it as box-ticking → share local pleural incidents to show why it matters.
- Pearls: Recruit the Trust NatSSIPs lead; they may already have the EPR form.; Agree what 'complete' means (all fields, signed) before cycle 1.; Put blank LocSSIPs in every chest drain pack while the EPR build is made.; Report procedure-room and ward results separately; wards usually lag.

**NNA-400. What proportion of adults with respiratory infection symptoms and a unilateral pleural effusion have the fluid sampled within 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standard for Pleural Disease, Quality statement 11 (2026): "In the context of respiratory infective symptoms, a unilateral pleural effusion should be promptly recognised as potential pleural infection and sampled within 24 hours if appropriate to confirm or exclude the diagnosis." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13202182/
- Pass: Pleural fluid sample taken within 24 hours of the first imaging report showing the effusion (sample time from laboratory or procedure note), or a documented reason sampling was not appropriate.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 4–6 months.
- Change: Add a 'pneumonia with effusion' step to the pneumonia admission proforma that requires thoracic ultrasound and a pleural team referral within 24 hours.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-400.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small effusions on X-ray are not truly eligible → record ultrasound depth and exclude effusions under 2 cm.; Clock start is disputed → use the first imaging report time in both cycles.; Weekend sampling depends on who can scan → report weekend and weekday results separately.; Numbers are small → extend the look-back period rather than lower the sample.
- Pearls: Recruit the pleural lead and a radiologist to run the report search.; Record pleural infection confirmed; it shows the yield of prompt sampling.; Link to the pleural list or ultrasound audit if run in the same year.; Keep the proforma step after the audit; it takes seconds.

**NNA-401. What proportion of adults with a recurrent spontaneous pneumothorax are offered referral to thoracic surgery for recurrence prevention?** (new)
- Standard: BTS Quality Standard for Pleural Disease, Quality statement 16 (2026): "Patients with spontaneous recurrent pneumothorax* should be considered for elective surgical recurrence prevention if clinically appropriate. *Recurrent pneumothorax includes patients with second ipsilateral or first contralateral pneumothorax." https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13202182/
- Pass: Documented offer of thoracic surgical referral (referral sent, or the patient declined) during the admission or at the first respiratory follow-up.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 12–24 months (aim for 30).
- Change: Add 'Recurrent pneumothorax? Offer thoracic surgery referral' to the pneumothorax discharge proforma, with an electronic link to the regional thoracic surgical referral.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-401.csv`
- Pitfalls: Previous episodes at other hospitals are missed → ask about and search clinic letters for past pneumothorax.; Numbers are small → use a 12–24 month look-back and allow 6 months before re-audit.; Surgeons may not accept some referrals → count the offer as the pass, and record the surgical outcome separately.; Patients may decline surgery → count a documented informed decline as a pass.
- Pearls: Recruit a thoracic surgical registrar from the regional centre.; Agree the recurrence definition (second ipsilateral or first contralateral) before cycle 1.; Put the prompt in both the ED and respiratory discharge templates.; Add the prompt to the first respiratory clinic letter template as a safety net.
